### [United States v. Castillo](../cases/united-states-v-castillo/)

Decision date: January 28, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 92-1279 (the docket caption also names Juan Fernandez; this decision concerns the appeal of Manuel Castillo)  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded. Judge Oakes dissents in part. The mandate has not issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/united-states-v-castillo/events/1994-01-28-decision.md](../cases/united-states-v-castillo/events/1994-01-28-decision.md)

Historical panel: J. Edward Lumbard (Senior Circuit Judge); James L. Oakes (Senior Circuit Judge); J. Daniel Mahoney (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: J. Edward Lumbard (Senior Circuit Judge); James L. Oakes (Senior Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: J. Daniel Mahoney.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Appeal from: United States District Court for the Southern District of New York (Shirley Wohl Kram, District Judge)  
Publication status: Opinion of the court, designated for publication  

#### The case and the result

##### The dispute and how it reached this court

Manuel Castillo appeals from a judgment entered May 19, 1992, in the Southern District of New York after a retrial of one count of using or carrying a firearm in relation to a drug trafficking offense, 18 U.S.C. § 924(c). The judgment imposes an aggregate term of eighty-four months on four convictions, six years of supervised release, and a $200 special assessment. He also challenges the district court's ruling on his delayed motion for a new trial under Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 33, which the district court treated as untimely. The government asks that the judgment be affirmed. Castillo asks for relief from the firearm conviction and the supervisory-role sentencing adjustment, and for a new trial on his trial-rights claims.

In July 1989 Officer Shawn Johnson, working undercover, bought cocaine from Castillo and Juan Fernandez. Johnson testified that Fernandez displayed a firearm at his waistband and partly drew it, while Castillo required Johnson to ingest cocaine. No gun was recovered. Johnson was the only eyewitness who supplied the government's account of a firearm, and his initial arrest report did not mention a gun. The defense attacked that omission and the difference between a gun at the waistband and an encounter described as occurring at gunpoint.

An earlier appeal in this prosecution, *United States v. Castillo*, 924 F.2d 1227 (2d Cir. 1991), reversed the firearm conviction because of prejudicial expert testimony and required reconsideration of a sentencing enhancement on the remaining convictions. At the retrial of the firearm count the government called officers who repeated Johnson's earlier reports. The district court received those statements for rehabilitation, with instructions limiting their substantive use, and Castillo was again convicted under § 924(c).

Castillo also asserts that his attorney refused to let him testify at that trial although he wished to, and that he believed counsel's decision was conclusive. His affidavit says so. He had been told at the first trial that the right to testify was his. A Rule 33 motion raising the point was made after the seven-day period. At sentencing the court applied a two-level adjustment under U.S.S.G. § 3B1.1(c) for an organizing or supervisory role. No testimony from counsel and no post-trial evidentiary record on the disputed conversation is before the court.

Relevant dates: offense and undercover purchase, July 1989; earlier appeal, 1991; judgment under review, May 19, 1992; argument, September 15, 1993; resubmission and decision, January 28, 1994.

The judgment is a final judgment of conviction and sentence, reviewable under 28 U.S.C. § 1291; the district court's jurisdiction rests on 18 U.S.C. § 3231. Nothing in the materials before the panel suggests that the notice of appeal was untimely.

##### The questions and the standards of review

1. Whether the officers' repetition of Johnson's reports was properly received for rehabilitation, whether the limiting instructions and the prosecution's use of the testimony cured any error, and whether any error was harmless. Admission and rehabilitation are reviewed for abuse of discretion, with independent review of the governing evidentiary rule. Castillo objected to the officers' repetition and challenged the limiting instructions and the summation use; Johnson's own initial account was received without objection. Preserved nonconstitutional error is tested under Fed. R. Crim. P. 52(a) and *Kotteakos v. United States*, 328 U.S. 750, 764-65 (1946).
2. Whether the claim that counsel prevented Castillo from testifying can be decided on this appeal. The allocation of the right is a question of law; any ineffective-assistance claim requires the constitutional standard and an adequate factual record, and an appellate court may not find the disputed conversation.
3. Whether the Rule 33 motion was timely. Rule 33's time categories are construed independently; known conduct cannot be turned into newly discovered evidence by a finding.
4. Whether the reasonable-doubt charge shifted the burden of proof. The charge is read as a whole; a genuinely unconstitutional reasonable-doubt instruction is structural error, but disfavored wording alone does not establish one.
5. Whether the § 3B1.1(c) adjustment is sustained. The finding of role is reviewed for clear error and the meaning of the guideline independently.

##### What the court decided

The court held that the officers' repetition of Johnson's earlier reports was not proper rehabilitation as received at the retrial, and that the error was not harmless on a record in which the firearm element depended almost entirely on one witness. The firearm conviction is therefore vacated and the count remanded for a new trial, not an acquittal. Because the eighty-four month aggregate term includes the firearm count, the sentence is vacated and the case remanded for resentencing in light of the eventual disposition of that count, with the § 3B1.1(c) adjustment left in place. The court does not hold the evidence of a firearm legally insufficient and makes no finding that Johnson testified falsely.

The court rejected the remaining claims or declined to decide them on this appeal. It made no finding on the disputed conversation between Castillo and his attorney and granted no relief on that ground, reserving any surviving claim for a proper channel and a developed record. It affirmed the Rule 33 ruling on the ground of untimeliness alone, rejected the challenge to the reasonable-doubt charge, and sustained the supervisory-role adjustment. The convictions on the three other counts are not disturbed.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

LUMBARD, Senior Circuit Judge.

**I. Background.** Manuel Castillo appeals from a judgment of the Southern District of New York entered after a second trial of the firearm count in his prosecution. The facts, which the earlier appeal in this prosecution also recounted in part, are stated above and need only be summarized. Johnson, an undercover officer, was the sole eyewitness to the weapon. No gun was recovered, his first arrest report said nothing of one, and the defense attacked both the silence and the contrast between a gun at the waistband and an encounter at gunpoint. At the retrial the government called officers who repeated Johnson's earlier reports, and the district court received them for rehabilitation under limiting instructions. We take the claims in the order in which they bear on the judgment.

**II. The officers' repetition of Johnson's reports.**

*A. Governing principles.* We review the receipt of evidence offered to rehabilitate a witness for abuse of discretion, but the discretion is exercised within the evidentiary rules, and we review their meaning independently. Because Castillo objected to the officers' testimony and challenged the instructions and the summation use, a nonconstitutional error requires reversal unless it was harmless under Rule 52(a) and *Kotteakos*. The government bears the burden of showing that a preserved error did not prejudice the defendant; that is the contrast the Supreme Court drew with the plain-error rule, under which the defendant bears it. See *United States v. Olano*, 507 U.S. 725 (1993).

Three propositions control. First, the officers' repetition could not come in as substantive evidence under Fed. R. Evid. 801(d)(1)(B). That rule removes a prior consistent statement from the definition of hearsay only when it is offered to rebut a charge of recent fabrication or improper influence or motive and was made before the alleged motive arose. *United States v. Quinto*, 582 F.2d 224, 233-34 (2d Cir. 1978); *United States v. Brennan*, 798 F.2d 581, 587 (2d Cir. 1986). The government concedes that the motive the defense asserts, which is to explain Johnson's ingestion of cocaine, existed when Johnson made his reports.

Second, a prior consistent statement that fails Rule 801(d)(1)(B) may still be received for the limited purpose of rehabilitating a witness, but only if it has probative force bearing on credibility beyond merely showing that the witness has repeated himself. *United States v. Pierre*, 781 F.2d 329, 331-33 (2d Cir. 1986). Such force exists when the statement casts doubt on whether an apparently inconsistent statement was made or is truly inconsistent, or when it clarifies or amplifies that statement. *Id.* at 333; *Brennan*, 798 F.2d at 588-89. A specific attack, such as one resting on an omission or a discrepancy between two descriptions, may be answered in that way; a generalized attack on credibility does not open the door. *Brennan*, 798 F.2d at 588-89. *Pierre* confined *Quinto*'s broader statement that every rehabilitative use must satisfy Rule 801(d)(1)(B), but it did not displace *Quinto*'s refusal to let a party bolster its witness merely by multiplying his accusation through additional witnesses.

Third, a limiting instruction under Fed. R. Evid. 105 is measured against the use actually made of the evidence at trial. And harmlessness under *Kotteakos* does not ask whether other evidence would support the verdict. "The inquiry cannot be merely whether there was enough to support the result, apart from the phase affected by the error." 328 U.S. at 764-65. It asks whether the error itself had substantial influence, so that one cannot say "with fair assurance, after pondering all that happened without stripping the erroneous action from the whole, that the judgment was not substantially swayed by the error." *Id.*

*B. Application.* The government's argument is serious, and we state it at its strongest. The defense did not rest on a general attack on Johnson's honesty. It pointed to a specific omission, a first report that did not mention a gun, and to a specific discrepancy between a gun at the waistband and an encounter at gunpoint. Under *Pierre* and *Brennan* a specific attack of that kind may be answered by a prior statement that has real answering force, and the government says the officers' testimony did that by showing that "gunpoint" described the same threatened display. It adds that the instructions confined the jury to credibility.

We accept the principle and not the application. Rehabilitative force is a function of what a particular statement does, not of the name the proponent gives it. A statement clarifies an inconsistency when its content shows how two accounts fit together, and it answers an omission when it supplies context that bears on why the omitted matter was left out. The officers supplied no account of a weapon of their own; Johnson was the only eyewitness who did. To the extent their testimony did no more than relate Johnson's earlier reports that a gun was displayed at the waistband, it said nothing about why the first report was silent and did nothing to square "waistband" with "gunpoint." It repeated the accusation. Repetition shows that Johnson told the same story more than once, which is precisely what *Pierre* holds is not enough, and a party may not convert it into support by presenting the same account through several witnesses. The repetition was not confined to proper rehabilitation, and receiving it as the district court did was error.

The error was not harmless. The proof of a firearm came from one witness, no weapon was found, and that witness's first report omitted it. On such a record the firearm element turns almost entirely on whether the jury believes Johnson. When officers, speaking with the authority of their office, repeat his accusation, they give the jury the appearance of further sources for it, and the prosecution used the repetition in that way, as support for the proposition that a firearm existed. The limiting instructions told the jury that it could consider the reports only on credibility. But the adequacy of an instruction is judged against the actual use of the evidence, and an instruction to treat repetition as bearing on credibility does little to neutralize a presentation that invites jurors to count several official witnesses in favor of one source on the single disputed element. The line between strengthening Johnson's credibility and corroborating that a gun was shown is not one that we can assume a jury would maintain.

The government's best reply is that the jury could have convicted without the officers' testimony. That is not the test. *Kotteakos* directs attention to the error's influence on the actual verdict, and where the disputed element is supplied principally by the witness being bolstered, we cannot say with fair assurance that the verdict was not substantially swayed. Our 1991 decision reversed this conviction because of prejudicial expert testimony; it does not decide whether a different error at the second trial was harmless, and we decide that question on the second trial's own record.

The firearm conviction therefore cannot stand. The remedy is a new trial and not an acquittal. Our ruling concerns the manner in which the government proved the firearm element. We do not hold that the evidence was legally insufficient, and we make no finding that Johnson testified falsely.

We state the limits of the holding. We do not hold that a prior consistent statement is inadmissible whenever it is offered after a motive to fabricate has arisen, and *Pierre* and *Brennan* remain the law of this circuit. A statement genuinely offered to clarify a specific inconsistency or omission, and shown to do so, may be received under an appropriate instruction. Nor do we decide which of the officers' statements, in what form, might be received at a retrial for such a purpose. On retrial the district court will rule on each offer against the function the statement serves, and the use made of it in argument must stay within that function. A statement is not corroboration merely because several officers heard it from the original witness, and repetition by officers who gave no account of a weapon of their own is not evidence that a weapon existed.

**III. The claim that counsel prevented Castillo from testifying.** Castillo says that he told his attorney he wished to testify, that counsel refused, and that he believed counsel's decision was conclusive. The choice whether to testify belongs to the accused personally. *Rock v. Arkansas*, 483 U.S. 44, 51-53 (1987). It is not counsel's to make. The question here is a different one: whether we can find, on this record, what was said between Castillo and his attorney before the retrial and what Castillo understood. We cannot. The affidavit is not a finding of the district court, and no testimony from counsel or other evidentiary record on the conversation is before us. The advice Castillo received at the first trial shows that he then knew the right was his, but that is evidence of knowledge and not a finding about a later conversation, and it cannot be turned into one. His affidavit, for its part, does not establish that counsel interfered or that any decision not to testify was unknowing. An appellate court does not find such facts, and we make no finding in either direction.

We also do not decide whether a claim of ineffective assistance lies. Such a claim requires both deficient performance and prejudice, neither of which may be assumed. *Strickland v. Washington*, 466 U.S. 668, 687, 694 (1984). A claim that depends on facts outside the trial record is ordinarily presented in a collateral proceeding on a developed record, such as a motion under 28 U.S.C. § 2255. See *United States v. Dukes*, 727 F.2d 34, 39 (2d Cir. 1984). We do not decide whether such a claim is available to Castillo, whether it would succeed, or what bearing this opinion has on it. We hold only that the disputed episode cannot be resolved on this appeal, and that nothing in our disposition gives counsel authority over a defendant's choice to testify or treats earlier advice as a finding about a later conversation. No stand-alone factfinding or collateral order is entered on the strength of the affidavit.

**IV. The Rule 33 motion.** Rule 33, as it then read, gave a defendant two periods. A motion for a new trial on the ground of newly discovered evidence could be made within two years after final judgment, and a motion on any other ground had to be made within seven days after the verdict or finding of guilty, or within such further time as the court fixed during that period. Castillo's motion was made after the seven days. He says his affidavit is newly discovered evidence, that new counsel first showed him the significance of the alleged refusal, and that his earlier lawyer's failure to move sooner should not defeat the motion.

*Dukes* answers the argument. Evidence is newly discovered only if it was found after trial and could not have been found sooner with due diligence, and *Dukes* declined to treat counsel's conduct, already known to the defendant, as newly discovered evidence merely because its significance was recognized later. It reasoned that the contrary rule would let such claims be brought up to two years after judgment, when the trial judge may recall little of counsel's performance, and that the practical difficulty defendants have in recognizing counsel's errors does not justify departing from the language of Rule 33. 727 F.2d at 38-39. Castillo's affidavit recounts an exchange to which he was a party and of which he knew when it occurred. It does not become newly discovered evidence because new counsel presented it. The motion rested on other grounds and was out of time.

This holding concerns timeliness alone. It is not an adjudication that counsel could prohibit a client from testifying, and, as Part III explains, it does not decide whether the alleged events could support relief in a separate constitutional vehicle.

**V. The reasonable-doubt charge.** Castillo contends, relying on *Sullivan v. Louisiana*, 508 U.S. 275, 281-82 (1993), which was cited at argument and in a later letter, that the charge's "abiding conviction" formulation, in its negative phrasing, could suggest that jurors must establish uncertainty before acquitting and so shift the burden to the accused. We read the charge as a whole and ask whether it correctly conveyed the government's burden or required the accused to prove his innocence. *Sullivan* holds that a constitutionally deficient reasonable-doubt instruction is structural error that no harmlessness analysis can save. It does not make a charge deficient. The question is whether this one was.

It was not. In *United States v. Torres*, 901 F.2d 205, 242-43 (2d Cir. 1990), reviewing for plain error, we rejected a challenge to materially similar language because, read where it appeared, it could not fairly be understood to shift the burden, and because the jury there was told repeatedly that the government bore the burden. The charge here is materially the formulation considered in *Torres*, and, read as a whole, it repeatedly places the burden of proof beyond a reasonable doubt on the prosecution and nowhere requires the accused to prove innocence. It does not resemble the "substantial doubt" language condemned in *Cage v. Louisiana*, 498 U.S. 39 (1990) (per curiam). Because the charge is not constitutionally deficient, *Sullivan*'s rule has nothing on which to operate. The challenge fails. We decide the charge actually given and express no view on formulations not used in it.

**VI. The role adjustment.** The district court added two levels under U.S.S.G. § 3B1.1(c), which reaches a defendant who was an organizer, leader, manager, or supervisor in criminal activity. We review the finding of role for clear error and the meaning of the guideline independently. *United States v. Beaulieau*, 959 F.2d 375, 380 (2d Cir. 1992). The government must establish the organizing or supervisory facts by a preponderance of the evidence, and possession of drugs or proceeds is not by itself a basis for the adjustment. The court looks to the discretion the defendant exercised, the nature and degree of his role in planning or organizing the offense, and the control and authority he held over other participants. *Id.* at 379-80.

The government points to evidence that Castillo weighed and packaged the cocaine and held $80 of the $90 purchase money. Castillo answers that handling drugs and money shows custody and not control, and is as consistent with equal participation as with leadership. If the adjustment rested on possession or receipt of proceeds alone, the answer would be sound. It does not. The district court drew from Castillo's operational conduct, weighing and packaging the product and holding most of the purchase money, the inference that he organized the transaction and did not merely take part in it, and weighing and packaging were among the facts supporting the organizer finding in *Beaulieau*, 959 F.2d at 380. The question is not whether we would have drawn that inference in the first instance, but whether the district court's inference is permissible on this record, and it is. It is not clearly erroneous. Our holding is confined to the record described. It is not a rule that receipt of proceeds or possession of drugs supports an inference of supervision, and we announce no general rule about role enhancements. Because the aggregate sentence must be redetermined in light of Part II, the adjustment is carried into that recalculation.

**VII. Disposition.** The conviction under 18 U.S.C. § 924(c) is vacated and the count is remanded for a new trial. The aggregate sentence is vacated and the case is remanded for resentencing in light of the eventual disposition of that count, with the § 3B1.1(c) adjustment left in place. The convictions on the three other counts are not disturbed. The ruling that the Rule 33 motion was untimely is affirmed. No relief is granted on the testimony claim, which is left undecided on its merits for the reasons in Part III, and the challenge to the reasonable-doubt charge is rejected.

---

#### Separate writings

**Judge Stone.** No separate writing. Judge Stone joins the opinion of the court in full.

**Judge Oakes, dissent in part (noted; no separate opinion).** Judge Oakes has noted a dissent in part. No separate opinion by Judge Oakes accompanies this decision, and the following is a summary of his noted position as the finalized decision records it, not a written opinion.

Judge Oakes agrees with the governing principles stated in Part II.A and with Parts III through VI. He dissents from Part II.B and from the parts of Part VII that vacate the firearm conviction and remand for a new trial and resentencing. On his reading, the question under *Pierre* and *Brennan* is answered statement by statement. Reports that answered the omission of a gun from the first arrest report, or that addressed the difference between a gun at the waistband and an encounter described as at gunpoint, were admissible for credibility only, under an instruction confined to credibility, and none was received as substantive evidence under Rule 801(d)(1)(B). He accepts that bare repetition is not rehabilitation and that harmlessness is not measured by the strength of the other evidence; he differs from the majority on whether the officers' testimony as admitted was more than bare repetition and on prejudice. Examining the summation and instructions as actually given, he would find that the repetition did not substantially influence the verdict under *Kotteakos*, and he reaches that conclusion without relying on other evidence or equating sufficiency with harmlessness. He would affirm the judgment in its entirety, with no new trial and no remand for resentencing.

---

#### What the decision does

##### Controlling holding

The opinion of the court is designated for publication and is precedential in this circuit to the extent of its holdings. The holdings, with the judges who support each, are these.

1. **Prior consistent statements, governing principles (Lumbard, Oakes, Stone).** Where the motive to fabricate preceded the statements, they are not substantive evidence under Fed. R. Evid. 801(d)(1)(B). They may be received only for rehabilitation, and only if each has probative force on credibility beyond repetition, such as answering a specific omission or clarifying a specific inconsistency. Bare repetition, including by additional witnesses, is not rehabilitation. Authority: Fed. R. Evid. 801(d)(1)(B) and 105; Fed. R. Crim. P. 52(a); *Pierre*; *Brennan*; *Quinto*; *Kotteakos*. Test and burdens: abuse of discretion with independent review of the rule; the limiting instruction is measured against actual use; harmlessness asks about substantial influence on the verdict, not sufficiency, and the government bears the burden on preserved error. This is Part II.A, with three joins.
2. **Application to this retrial (Lumbard, Stone; Oakes dissenting).** To the extent officers who gave no account of a weapon of their own merely repeated the sole eyewitness's account of a firearm, the repetition lacks rehabilitative force, and where the prosecution used it as corroboration of an unrecovered weapon on a single-witness element the error is not harmless. The remedy is a new trial, not acquittal. Limits: no categorical bar on genuinely clarifying statements; no finding of falsity; no insufficiency holding. This is Part II.B, which states the holding of the court on two joins. Within this circuit, on the described record, repetition of a sole eyewitness's account of a firearm by officers who gave no account of a weapon of their own is not rehabilitation, and its prosecutorial use as corroboration is not harmless under *Kotteakos*. The rule is not a categorical exclusion of clarifying consistent statements.
3. **Right to testify (Lumbard, Oakes, Stone).** The choice to testify is the defendant's. An appellate court does not find a disputed attorney-client conversation, and earlier advice is not a finding about a later one. An ineffective-assistance claim requires *Strickland*'s showing on an adequate record. Authority: *Rock*; *Strickland*; *Dukes*; 28 U.S.C. § 2255 as a possible separate channel. Part III decides nothing about the merits of the alleged attorney-client exchange and adds no rule beyond restating that the choice to testify is the defendant's.
4. **Rule 33 timeliness (Lumbard, Oakes, Stone).** An affidavit recounting the defendant's own previously known dealings with counsel is not newly discovered evidence merely because new counsel later presents it. Authority: Fed. R. Crim. P. 33 as then in force; *Dukes*, 727 F.2d at 38-39.
5. **Reasonable-doubt charge (Lumbard, Oakes, Stone).** The charge as a whole, including the "abiding conviction" formulation, does not shift the burden, and *Sullivan* does not invalidate that formulation by itself. Authority: *Sullivan*; *Cage*; *Torres*. Test: the charge is read as a whole.
6. **Role adjustment (Lumbard, Oakes, Stone).** An organizing-role inference from weighing, packaging and control of most of the purchase money is permissible and not clearly erroneous; it is not a universal inference from receipt of proceeds. Authority: U.S.S.G. § 3B1.1(c); *Beaulieau*. Standard: clear error for the finding, independent review of the guideline; preponderance burden on the government. Part VI is fact-bound.

Parts II.A and III through VI have three joins. The opinion follows and applies *Pierre*, *Brennan*, *Quinto* and *Kotteakos* without overruling or narrowing any of them. It applies *Dukes*, *Torres* and *Beaulieau* as binding circuit authority and *Rock*, *Strickland*, *Sullivan* and *Cage* as binding Supreme Court authority. The law that changes is limited. Parts IV through VI restate settled law applied to this record. The separate position of Judge Oakes is not circuit law.

##### Questions left open

Whether any particular statement by an officer would be admissible at the retrial for a genuinely clarifying purpose; whether Johnson testified falsely (no finding); whether the evidence of a firearm is legally sufficient (not decided); the truth of Castillo's affidavit, counsel's performance, prejudice, and the availability of any particular collateral petition; formulations of the reasonable-doubt charge not used here; and any general rule equating possession of drugs or receipt of proceeds with supervision.

##### Effect on the parties

Party-specific relief is the vacatur of the firearm conviction and aggregate sentence, the new trial on the firearm count, and the resentencing, which bind the parties and the district court on remand. The three convictions left in place by the earlier decision remain undisturbed. The Rule 33 ruling is affirmed on timeliness alone. No relief is granted on the testimony claim, and any surviving claim is reserved for a proper channel and a developed record. No preclusion or law-of-the-case effect extends beyond the parties and this prosecution.

##### Relief and remand instructions

The firearm conviction is vacated and the count is remanded for a new trial. On that trial the district court is to confine evidentiary rulings to the principles stated in Part II and to the function each prior statement actually serves. The aggregate sentence is vacated and the case is remanded for resentencing, in light of the eventual disposition of the firearm count, with the § 3B1.1(c) adjustment left in place. No factfinding remand is ordered on the testimony claim, no collateral-relief order is entered on the strength of Castillo's affidavit, and no acquittal is directed.

##### Mandate status

The mandate has not issued. Under Fed. R. App. P. 40 and 41, as in force on the date of decision, a party may petition for rehearing within the period Rule 40 provides, the mandate does not issue until the period fixed by Rule 41 after the time for rehearing has run, a timely petition stays the mandate until it is decided unless the court orders otherwise, and the court may shorten or enlarge those times by order. The district court's authority to act on the remand begins when the mandate issues. No petition for rehearing, motion to stay the mandate, or other filing is recorded, and none is inferred. Scheduling of the retrial and of resentencing is for the district court after the mandate.

#### Historical comparison

Historically the panel (Lumbard, Oakes, Mahoney) affirmed Castillo's conviction and sentence on 1994-01-28. Judge Mahoney wrote, joined by Judge Oakes; Judge Lumbard dissented, disputing the rehabilitation rationale and the treatment of the sole firearm eyewitness's repeated statements as harmless. The majority admitted the prior statements for rehabilitation, found no reversible prejudice, rejected the testimony and Rule 33 claims, found the reasonable-doubt charge adequate, and sustained the role adjustment; no new trial or resentencing was ordered. Source: United States v. Castillo, 14 F.3d 802 (2d Cir. 1994), No. 92-1279.

The simulated judgment is a changed judgment on the firearm count and sentence: both are vacated and remanded, because officers' repetition is not rehabilitation and not harmless. Judge Stone sits in place of Judge Mahoney; Judge Lumbard writes for himself and Judge Stone, Judge Oakes dissenting in part. The Rule 33, reasonable-doubt and role-adjustment rulings reach the same results, and the testimony claim is left undecided with no relief rather than rejected.

### [United States v. Anderson](../cases/united-states-v-anderson/)

Decision date: February 17, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 93-1097  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Published opinion; precedential to the extent stated below. Term of imprisonment vacated and case remanded for resentencing (two votes to one); finding of violations and order revoking supervised release not disturbed. The mandate has not issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/united-states-v-anderson/events/1994-02-17-decision.md](../cases/united-states-v-anderson/events/1994-02-17-decision.md)

Historical panel: James L. Oakes (Senior Circuit Judge); Amalya L. Kearse (Active Circuit Judge); Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: James L. Oakes (Senior Circuit Judge); Amalya L. Kearse (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Frank X. Altimari.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Opinion of the court by Kearse, Circuit Judge. Judge Oakes concurs in part and dissents in part. Judge Stone-Khoury writes no separate opinion.

#### Decision summary

Cynthia Yvette Anderson pleaded guilty to credit-card fraud under 18 U.S.C. § 1029(a)(2). She was sentenced to six months' imprisonment and three years of supervised release, which began in August 1991. Her conditions restricted controlled substances and firearms and required participation in substance-abuse treatment. The probation officer sought revocation in October 1992. In January 1993 the United States District Court for the District of Connecticut (T. F. Gilroy Daly, District Judge) found violations, and on January 26, 1993 it revoked her supervised release and sentenced her to seventeen months' imprisonment. The Chapter Seven policy statements of the Sentencing Guidelines recommended six to twelve months, and the statutory ceiling was twenty-four months. The district court expressly emphasized the need for structured drug and psychological treatment and access to an intensive institutional program. The record also reflects poor cooperation with supervision.

Anderson appealed and sought resentencing. The United States sought affirmance. She raised two questions:

1. Whether a district court revoking supervised release may select or lengthen the term of imprisonment in order to promote the defendant's rehabilitation.
2. Whether the Chapter Seven recommended range of six to twelve months binds the sentencing court.

The court answered the first question no, by a vote of two to one. When a district court revokes supervised release under 18 U.S.C. § 3583(e) and requires the defendant to serve time in prison, it may not select the length of that imprisonment, or lengthen it, to promote the defendant's rehabilitation or to obtain treatment or a program for the defendant. Because the district court relied on that objective in choosing seventeen months, and the record does not show that the error was harmless, the term of imprisonment is vacated and the case is remanded for resentencing. The court unanimously answered the second question no: the Chapter Seven policy statements must be considered but do not bind, so an otherwise lawful term need not fall within six to twelve months.

The finding of violations and the order revoking supervised release are not disturbed. The court imposes no twelve-month ceiling, no direction to release Anderson, and no appellate finding of fact, and its instructions presuppose no further term of supervised release. Judge Oakes would have affirmed.

##### Result by component

| Component | Result | Judges |
|---|---|---|
| Revocation findings and power to imprison for some period | Stand. Not challenged. | Oakes, Kearse and Stone-Khoury |
| Chapter Seven range as a mandatory twelve-month ceiling | Rejected | Oakes, Kearse and Stone-Khoury, unanimously |
| Rehabilitation as a basis for the length of revocation imprisonment | Impermissible; the district court's reliance on it was error | Kearse and Stone-Khoury in the majority; Oakes dissenting |
| Vacatur of the term and remand for resentencing | Entered | Kearse and Stone-Khoury in the majority; Oakes dissenting and voting to affirm |

The judgment vacating the term and remanding is two votes to one.

##### Writings and joins

- Opinion of the court by Kearse, Circuit Judge. Parts I and III are joined by Oakes and Stone-Khoury. Parts II and IV are joined by Stone-Khoury. Oakes does not join Parts II and IV.
- Opinion of Oakes, Senior Circuit Judge, concurring in part and dissenting in part. No other judge joins it.
- Stone-Khoury, Circuit Judge, writes no separate opinion.

##### Jurisdiction, issues and standards of review

The court has jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1291 and 18 U.S.C. § 3742. The authority to revoke and imprison rests on 18 U.S.C. § 3583(e). Timeliness, forfeiture and mootness are not among the questions presented; the effect of the undisclosed custody status is addressed in Part I of the opinion.

The record supplied to the panel does not include the notice of appeal or docket entries, does not identify Anderson's present custody status, and does not show whether the district court considered the Chapter Seven range.

- Issue 1 (rehabilitation and the length of revocation imprisonment). Standard: independent review of statutory meaning; supported findings of fact receive the deference their nature requires; a legally forbidden objective is not insulated as sentencing discretion.
- Issue 2 (force of the Chapter Seven range). Standard: independent review of the legal force of the policy statements.

##### Dates

Supervised release began August 1991; revocation sought October 1992; violations found January 1993; sentence entered January 26, 1993; argued November 1, 1993; resubmitted and decided February 17, 1994.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

KEARSE, Circuit Judge:

**I. Background and threshold matters** (joined by Oakes, Kearse and Stone-Khoury)

Cynthia Yvette Anderson appeals from the sentence imposed on January 26, 1993, by the United States District Court for the District of Connecticut (T. F. Gilroy Daly, Judge), upon revoking her supervised release. Having pleaded guilty to credit-card fraud under 18 U.S.C. § 1029(a)(2), she began a three-year term of supervised release in August 1991, on conditions that included restrictions on controlled substances and firearms and participation in substance-abuse treatment. The district court found violations in January 1993. The Chapter Seven policy statements recommended six to twelve months' imprisonment; the statutory ceiling was twenty-four months. The court imposed seventeen months, emphasizing Anderson's need for structured drug and psychological treatment and for access to an intensive institutional program. The record also reflects poor cooperation with supervision.

Anderson does not contest the violations or the court's power to imprison her. She argues that the court could not lawfully rely on rehabilitation to fix the length of the term and that the Chapter Seven range capped her sentence at twelve months. The United States asks us to affirm. We have jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1291 and 18 U.S.C. § 3742. The meaning of the statutes and the force of the policy statements are legal questions we decide independently. We defer to supported findings of fact, but a legally impermissible sentencing objective is not insulated from review because the court adopted it in the exercise of discretion.

The papers before us do not disclose Anderson's present custody status or how much of the term she has served. The questions presented do not include mootness, timeliness or forfeiture, and we decide the appeal on the reasons the district court itself gave. The term under review has not been shown to have expired, and a sentence that may still be corrected presents a live controversy. We therefore reach the merits and leave to the district court on remand any effect of intervening events.

**II. Rehabilitation and the length of imprisonment on revocation** (joined by Stone-Khoury)

*A. The sentencing choice.* The question is narrower than a general debate about treatment. Anderson was not merely directed to a program while serving a term chosen for independent reasons. The district court identified the need for structured treatment and an institutional program as a reason for the seventeen months it selected. The issue is whether a court that revokes supervised release may fix the length of the imprisonment it requires in order to obtain rehabilitative services for the defendant. That treatment is desirable, or that the treatment factor in 18 U.S.C. § 3553(a)(2)(D) bears on some decisions in a revocation proceeding, does not answer it. We must ask what Congress permitted a court to do with the instrument of imprisonment.

*B. The statutory text.* Section 3582(a) directs a court that is deciding whether to imprison, and for how long, to consider the applicable factors in § 3553(a), "recognizing that imprisonment is not an appropriate means of promoting correction and rehabilitation." Title 28, section 994(k), expresses the same judgment by directing the Sentencing Commission to ensure that the guidelines reflect "the inappropriateness of imposing a sentence to a term of imprisonment for the purpose of rehabilitating the defendant." The Supreme Court has described the Sentencing Reform Act as one that "rejects imprisonment as a means of promoting rehabilitation." Mistretta v. United States, 488 U.S. 361, 367 (1989). Mistretta describes the design of the Act and does not decide the question before us, so we cite it only as confirmation of what the text says. Our own decisions recognize the line the statute draws. In United States v. Maier, 975 F.2d 944, 947 (2d Cir. 1992), we observed that rehabilitation "may not be a basis for incarceration but must be considered as a basis for a sentence." Maier arose from a departure to probation, and we do not extend its holding beyond that noncustodial setting. Neither Maier nor Bermudez decides whether § 3582(a) governs the length of a revocation term. But Maier's reading of the statutory design is the one we apply.

Nothing in these provisions confines the limitation to an initial sentence. Section 3582(a) speaks of a term of imprisonment and of the determination of its length, and § 994(k) speaks of imprisonment imposed for the purpose of rehabilitating the defendant. A prison term that a court requires a defendant to serve after revocation is a term of imprisonment, and choosing how long it will be is a determination of its length. The policy Congress attached to those decisions is stated in terms of the means employed, not of the procedural label under which the court acts.

*C. The Government's reading.* The United States responds that revocation is governed by § 3583(e). That section authorizes a court, after considering specified § 3553(a) factors that include the need to provide the defendant with "needed educational or vocational training, medical care, or other correctional treatment," § 3553(a)(2)(D), to revoke supervised release and require service in prison of all or part of the term. The Government stresses that § 3583(e) incorporates the treatment factor yet does not cross-reference § 3582(a), and that it speaks of requiring imprisonment rather than imposing it. Because rehabilitation properly informs the choice and length of supervision in the first place, the Government reasons, it remains relevant when supervision fails. It adds that a seventeen-month term below the statutory maximum is lawful and also reflects Anderson's failure to cooperate. The argument is a serious one, but we are not persuaded, for three reasons.

First, the absence of a cross-reference is not a disclaimer. Section 3582(a) is a general direction about how a court is to decide whether and for how long to imprison, and § 3583(e) says nothing exempting revocation terms from it. The difference between requiring and imposing a term is a weak basis for withdrawing a policy Congress attached to decisions about imprisonment itself. A defendant returned to prison after revocation serves a prison term, and the judge who sets its length makes the same decision whatever the label.

Second, reading the two sections together gives the treatment factor in § 3583(e) real work without turning prison into a rehabilitative tool. The factor is not peculiar to revocation. It is part of § 3553(a), and § 3582(a) directs the court to consider the applicable § 3553(a) factors in the very sentence that tells it to recognize that imprisonment is not an appropriate means of promoting rehabilitation. Incorporating § 3553(a)(2)(D) into § 3583(e) therefore cannot be read as a license to pursue that factor by the means § 3582(a) disclaims. Nor is the incorporation idle on our reading. Section 3583(e) lets the court extend a term of supervised release and modify its conditions, and treatment conditions like those in Anderson's own terms remain available in that process. The treatment factor properly guides those choices, as Maier's recognition of rehabilitation's role in nonprison sentencing confirms. What it does not do is authorize the addition of months of imprisonment so that a program may be obtained.

Third, the statutory ceiling answers a different question. That seventeen months is under twenty-four shows the term is not too long, not that the reason for choosing that length was a permissible one. A legally forbidden objective does not become lawful because the sentence it produced lies below the maximum. Nor can we affirm on the Government's alternative ground. Poor cooperation with supervision, protection of the public, deterrence and other lawful considerations that § 3583(e) incorporates may well support imprisonment, and even a term above the Chapter Seven range. But the district court expressly relied on treatment, and we may not uphold the sentence by supposing it would have reached the same length for other reasons while setting aside the reason it gave.

*D. Holding.* We hold that when a district court revokes a term of supervised release under § 3583(e) and requires the defendant to serve time in prison, it may not select the length of that imprisonment, or lengthen it, for the purpose of promoting the defendant's rehabilitation or of obtaining treatment or a program for the defendant. The treatment consideration in § 3583(e) does not authorize adding imprisonment for rehabilitation. It remains relevant to the treatment and supervision choices that are lawfully open to the court, including the conditions, term and structure of supervision, and recommendations for available care when custody rests on other grounds. We do not hold that treatment is irrelevant to revocation, we announce no constitutional right to treatment outside prison, and we direct no particular term. Applied here, the district court's stated reliance on Anderson's need for structured treatment and an institutional program in selecting seventeen months was an impermissible basis for the length of her imprisonment, and the sentence was imposed in violation of law.

**III. The Chapter Seven range** (joined by Oakes, Kearse and Stone-Khoury)

Anderson also contends that the recommended range of six to twelve months capped her sentence. We addressed the force of these statements in United States v. Bermudez, 974 F.2d 12, 14 (2d Cir. 1992), where we described them as "advisory rather than mandatory in nature," while adding that "[c]ourts should nonetheless take the policy statements into account when sentencing for a violation of supervised release." The statements must therefore be considered, but they do not bind, and an otherwise lawful term need not fall within six to twelve months.

Anderson invokes the Supreme Court's treatment of Commission materials in Williams v. United States, 503 U.S. 193 (1992), and Stinson v. United States, 508 U.S. 36 (1993). Those decisions concern policy statements and commentary that operate with a governing guideline. Williams treats a policy statement that prohibits a specified action as "an authoritative guide to the meaning of the applicable Guideline," 503 U.S. at 201, and Stinson concerns commentary that interprets or explains a guideline. Neither converts every policy statement into a mandatory guideline, and neither addresses the stand-alone revocation recommendations of Chapter Seven, as to which Bermudez is controlling circuit authority that a panel must follow.

Our disposition of the first issue does not change that conclusion. Correcting an unlawful sentencing purpose does not make the recommended range mandatory. On resentencing the district court must consider the Chapter Seven statements, and it may select a term within the statutory maximum on lawful grounds, whether inside the recommended range or outside it. The claim that the sentence is limited to twelve months is rejected.

**IV. Remedy** (joined by Stone-Khoury)

When a sentence has been imposed in violation of law, 18 U.S.C. § 3742(f)(1) directs a remand for further sentencing proceedings with such instructions as the court considers appropriate. Where a sentencing court relied on an impermissible consideration, remand is appropriate unless the reviewing court concludes, on the record as a whole, that the error was harmless. Williams, 503 U.S. at 203. Nothing in the record suggests that the district court would have chosen seventeen months without regard to the treatment objective, and its express emphasis on that objective points the other way. We have no basis to treat the error as harmless, and we may neither decide disputed sentencing facts nor choose the sentence ourselves.

We therefore vacate the term of imprisonment and remand for resentencing. We do not disturb the finding of violations or the court's power to revoke and imprison. On remand the district court is to select a term justified by lawful considerations, so that the permissible grounds for whatever term it chooses are apparent, and it may not select additional months because a particular program requires them. It must consider the Chapter Seven policy statements but is not confined to the recommended range. We impose no twelve-month ceiling and no direction to release Anderson. These instructions do not bar consideration of treatment in setting conditions of supervision or recommending care, and they presuppose no further term of supervised release, which this court has held § 3583(e) does not authorize after revocation and imprisonment. Bermudez, 974 F.2d at 13; United States v. Koehler, 973 F.2d 132, 133 (2d Cir. 1992).

The judgment vacating the term of imprisonment is entered, and the case is remanded.

#### Separate opinions

##### Opinion of Judge Oakes, concurring in part and dissenting in part

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.* Joined by no other judge. The final record establishes this writing in the following extent.

Judge Oakes joins Parts I and III of the court's opinion and agrees that the Chapter Seven statements are advisory and must be considered, though they do not bind. He dissents from Part II, from Part IV, and from the vacatur. In his view the district court's choice of term should be affirmed. Revocation under § 3583(e) is a distinct statutory framework. That section expressly directs the court to consider the treatment factor in § 3553(a)(2)(D) and contains no cross-reference to § 3582(a). The recognition in § 3582(a) that imprisonment is not an appropriate means of promoting rehabilitation addresses the initial decision to imprison, and it does not bar treatment considerations in choosing the length of a revocation term. Because the seventeen-month term lies within the twenty-four month ceiling and the Chapter Seven statements are advisory, he would hold that the sentence is lawful and affirm. His opinion does not suggest that the recommended range may be disregarded, and it advances no ground beyond those stated here.

##### Judge Stone-Khoury

Judge Stone-Khoury, Circuit Judge, writes no separate opinion. He joined Parts I, II, III and IV of the opinion of the court.

#### What the decision does

- Controlling holding (Proposition A, Part II; Kearse and Stone-Khoury). When a district court revokes supervised release under 18 U.S.C. § 3583(e) and requires the defendant to serve time in prison, it may not select the length of that imprisonment, or lengthen it, to promote the defendant's rehabilitation or to obtain treatment or a program for the defendant. Authority: 18 U.S.C. §§ 3582(a), 3583(e), 3553(a)(2)(D); 28 U.S.C. § 994(k); Maier, 975 F.2d at 946-47; Mistretta, 488 U.S. at 367 (confirming the statutory design only). Test: § 3582(a)'s limitation on imprisonment is read together with § 3583(e)'s incorporation of § 3553(a)(2)(D). Burden: Anderson shows the impermissible basis from the district court's own stated reasons; the term then stands only if the record as a whole shows the error did not affect the term selected (Williams, 503 U.S. at 203). Qualification: treatment remains relevant to the conditions and structure of supervision and to recommendations of care when custody rests on other grounds. Application: the express reliance on structured treatment and an institutional program in choosing seventeen months was error, and the record does not show it harmless.
- Controlling holding (Proposition B, Part III; unanimous). Chapter Seven's revocation policy statements must be considered but do not bind, so an otherwise lawful term need not fall within six to twelve months. Authority: Bermudez, 974 F.2d at 14; 18 U.S.C. §§ 3583(e), 3742; Williams and Stinson distinguished. Standard: independent review. Application: the claimed twelve-month ceiling fails.
- Remedy (Proposition C, Part IV; Kearse and Stone-Khoury). Vacate the term only, with no twelve-month or release directive, no appellate fact-finding, and no presupposition of a further term of supervised release.
- Principal contrary argument and the court's answer. The contrary argument, adopted by the dissent, is that § 3583(e) is a self-contained revocation provision that incorporates the treatment factor but not § 3582(a), so rehabilitation may properly influence how long the revocation term lasts. The court's answer is that § 3582(a) speaks to imprisonment however it is reached, that § 3583(e)'s incorporation of the treatment factor does independent work in conditions and supervision and cannot license the means § 3582(a) disclaims, and that a term under the statutory maximum does not validate an impermissible reason for its length.
- Precedential effect. This is a published opinion of a three-judge panel and is precedential under the circuit's settled practice, binding later panels unless overruled in banc or displaced by intervening Supreme Court authority. The rule in Part II (Proposition A) is circuit law on the votes of Kearse and Stone-Khoury, notwithstanding Judge Oakes's dissent; the dissent is not circuit law. Part III (Proposition B) is a unanimous application of Bermudez and changes nothing in that decision. Maier is followed for its statement of the statutory design and is not extended as a holding beyond its noncustodial setting. Mistretta is cited as description only. Williams is applied for the harmless-error standard and, with Stinson, distinguished as to Chapter Seven. Bermudez and Koehler are followed on the unavailability of a further supervised-release term. What changes within the circuit is that a court revoking supervised release may no longer lengthen imprisonment to promote rehabilitation. The observations that other lawful considerations may support a term above the recommended range, and that treatment may be recommended or addressed through conditions, are qualifications on the holding and are not applied to this record. The vacatur of Anderson's seventeen-month term and the remand instructions in Part IV are relief specific to this case, not circuit-wide rules; the remand rulings bind the district court as law of the case in this proceeding.
- Left open. The court does not decide the appropriate length of the term on remand under permissible objectives; whether any constitutional right to treatment outside prison exists; the effect of Anderson's custody status or any time already served on resentencing; whether the district court considered the Chapter Seven range at the first sentencing; or any question about the Guidelines system beyond Bermudez.
- Effect on the parties. The seventeen-month term is vacated and the case is remanded to the United States District Court for the District of Connecticut for resentencing consistent with this opinion, in the manner described in Part IV. No twelve-month ceiling, release directive or appellate finding of fact is imposed, and nothing presupposes a further term of supervised release. Violation findings and the revocation order stand. Anderson's claim that the Chapter Seven range capped her sentence at twelve months is rejected.
- Mandate and remand. The mandate has not issued. Under Federal Rule of Appellate Procedure 41(a) as in force on this date, the mandate issues twenty-one days after entry of judgment unless that time is shortened or enlarged by order, and the timely filing of a petition for rehearing stays it pending disposition. Under Rule 40(a), a petition for rehearing in a criminal case may be filed within fourteen days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order or local rule. No petition, motion for a stay, or other filing is recorded. Resentencing in the district court awaits issuance of the mandate.

#### Historical comparison

On February 17, 1994, a Second Circuit panel of Oakes, Kearse and Altimari affirmed the seventeen-month revocation sentence in United States v. Anderson, No. 93-1097. Judge Oakes wrote the opinion, joined by Judge Altimari; Judge Kearse dissented on the rehabilitation question. The majority treated the Chapter Seven recommendations as advisory and held that treatment and rehabilitation considerations could support the length of a revocation prison term, distinguishing the bar on imprisonment for rehabilitation at an initial sentencing. The simulated panel (Oakes, Kearse, Stone-Khoury) decided the same day and reached a different judgment: vacatur and remand for resentencing, two to one, Kearse writing with Stone-Khoury and Oakes dissenting. The judgment changed because the reasoning on rehabilitation under 18 U.S.C. §§ 3582(a) and 3583(e) changed. On Chapter Seven the reasoning is the same: advisory, no twelve-month ceiling. Source: United States v. Anderson, 15 F.3d 278, 281-86 (2d Cir. 1994), https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/15/278/536351/.

### [Latsis v. Chandris, Inc.](../cases/latsis-v-chandris/)

Decision date: March 24, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 93-7704  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Judgment for the defendants vacated and the case remanded for a new trial (Judges Oakes and Stone-Khoury in the majority; Judge Kearse dissenting). Opinion for publication; the opinion of the court is precedential to the extent of the holdings stated below, and the dissent is not. The mandate has not issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/latsis-v-chandris/events/1994-03-24-decision.md](../cases/latsis-v-chandris/events/1994-03-24-decision.md)

Historical panel: James L. Oakes (Senior Circuit Judge); Amalya L. Kearse (Active Circuit Judge); Richard J. Cardamone (Senior Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: James L. Oakes (Senior Circuit Judge); Amalya L. Kearse (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Richard J. Cardamone.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Appeal from: United States District Court for the Southern District of New York (Loretta A. Preska, District Judge)  

#### Decision summary

**The dispute.** Antonios Latsis was Chandris's fleet electronics superintendent. He serviced a passenger fleet of about five or six vessels, and his work included both shoreside duties and voyages. In May 1989, during a voyage of the S.S. Galileo from Baltimore to Bermuda, he developed an eye problem. The ship's physician suspected a detached retina but did not arrange immediate specialist treatment, and Latsis alleges that the delay cost him most of the central vision in his right eye. Negligence and causation are contested and were never resolved.

**How the case reached this court.** After returning to work, Latsis sailed with the Galileo to Germany and spent about six months supervising electronic work during a major conversion of the vessel, which returned to service as the Meridian. At trial in the Southern District of New York, the only question the jury decided was whether Latsis was a "seaman" entitled to sue under the Jones Act, 46 U.S.C. app. § 688. The district court instructed the jury in terms of permanent assignment or substantial work aboard vessels, but told it to exclude the German conversion period because the Galileo was out of navigation. Latsis objected to that exclusion. The jury found that he was not a seaman, and a judgment for the defendants was entered in June 1993. That judgment disposed of the action and is a final decision reviewable under 28 U.S.C. § 1291.

**What the court decided.** The court vacated the judgment and remanded for a new trial. It held that the instruction withdrawing the conversion period from the jury was legal error, and that on this record the error cannot be treated as harmless, because the evidence on how much of Latsis's time was spent aboard ship conflicted sharply (about seventy-two percent on his account, about ten percent on his supervisor's) and the verdict was general.

**How narrow the holding is.** The court did not hold that conversion or repair time always counts, that all ship repair is qualifying service, that Latsis is a seaman, or that a fixed percentage of time aboard ship decides the matter. It held only that a vessel's repair or conversion status does not categorically make a worker's continuing assignment irrelevant to seaman status for an earlier shipboard injury, and that the character and weight of that assignment are for the jury.

**The division.** Judge Oakes wrote the opinion of the court, and Judge Stone-Khoury joined it in full. Judge Kearse dissented and would affirm. The majority rested the judgment on the preserved objection alone and did not decide whether the unobjected-to portions of the charge meet the plain-error standard.

**What remains open.** Because the jury decided only seaman status, negligence, causation, and damages remain undecided and are open on retrial to the extent seaman status is established. The court made no finding that Latsis is a seaman and none on the proportion of his time spent aboard ship.

##### Judgment by component

| Component | Disposition | For | Against |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. The judgment and the preserved drydock exclusion | Judgment vacated and case remanded for a new trial | Judges Oakes and Stone-Khoury | Judge Kearse, who would affirm |
| 2. The unpreserved objections to the broader charge | No relief granted on the unobjected-to portions of the charge, and the court does not decide whether they meet the plain-error standard; the vacatur rests on the preserved objection alone | Judges Oakes and Stone-Khoury | Judge Kearse would reach the unpreserved challenge and reject it as insufficient for relief under Rule 51 |
| 3. Remedy | New trial. Negligence, causation, and damages remain undecided and are open on retrial to the extent seaman status is established. No finding that Latsis is a seaman and none on the proportion of his time spent aboard ship | Judges Oakes and Stone-Khoury | Judge Kearse, dissenting |

**Writings and joins.** Opinion of the court by Judge Oakes, in which Judge Stone-Khoury joins in full. Dissent by Judge Kearse, joined by no one. There is no concurrence and no other separate writing.

---

#### Posture and issues

**Decision under review.** A judgment of the Southern District of New York entered in June 1993 for the defendants, after a jury found that Latsis was not a seaman within the Jones Act, 46 U.S.C. app. § 688. The judgment disposed of the action and is a final decision reviewable under 28 U.S.C. § 1291.

**Preservation.** The objection to the categorical exclusion of the conversion period was specific and is preserved. Latsis did not object in the same way to the rest of the seaman-status charge, which he now says framed the question in unduly temporal terms.

**Issue 1: the preserved exclusion.** Whether the instruction directing the jury to disregard the six-month conversion assignment misstated the law of seaman status and, if so, whether a new trial is required. The correctness of the legal standard in an instruction is reviewed independently; inferences from conflicting evidence belong to the jury; and prejudice is judged under Fed. R. Civ. P. 61 and 28 U.S.C. § 2111.

**Issue 2: the unpreserved objections.** Whether the broader connection formulation in the charge warrants relief although not objected to. The governing standard is Fed. R. Civ. P. 51 and the circuit's demanding plain-error review of unobjected-to civil instructions, which requires an exceptional showing and not merely a better alternative instruction.

**Material record.** The evidence on how much of his time Latsis spent aboard ship conflicted sharply: Latsis put it at about seventy-two percent, and his supervisor at about ten percent. The conversion of the Galileo in Germany involved substantial work on propulsion, machinery, and accommodation. The parties agreed at trial that Latsis's work contributed to the function or mission of the fleet; they disputed whether his relationship to the vessels made him a seaman. The jury found that he was not a seaman and so did not reach malpractice, causation, or damages.

---

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

OAKES, Senior Circuit Judge, in which STONE-KHOURY, Circuit Judge, joins:

Antonios Latsis alleges that he lost most of the central vision in his right eye because the physician aboard the S.S. Galileo failed to arrange prompt specialist care for a suspected detached retina. His action never reached the merits. The jury was asked whether he was a seaman under the Jones Act, 46 U.S.C. app. § 688, and, having been told to disregard the six months he spent supervising the Galileo's conversion in a German yard, found that he was not. We hold that the instruction withdrawing that period from the jury's consideration was legal error, and that on this record the error cannot be treated as harmless. We vacate the judgment and remand for a new trial. Judge Stone-Khoury, who did not sit when the appeal was argued in December 1993, has participated in the decision on resubmission of the papers.

**I. The governing law.** The Jones Act gives a remedy to a "seaman" without defining the word. Two decisions of the Supreme Court in 1991 frame the inquiry. McDermott International, Inc. v. Wilander, 498 U.S. 337, 355 (1991), held that a seaman need not aid in navigation or contribute to the transportation of the vessel, but must be doing the ship's work, and that "[t]he key to seaman status is employment-related connection to a vessel in navigation." Southwest Marine, Inc. v. Gizoni, 502 U.S. 81, 88-89 (1991), added that the inquiry is of necessity fact-specific, that an occupational label such as ship repairman does not settle it, and that not all ship repairmen lack the necessary connection as a matter of law.

Our own decisions require a sufficiently substantial, more or less permanent connection with a vessel or fleet. Harney v. William M. Moore Building Corp., 359 F.2d 649, 654 (2d Cir. 1966); Klarman v. Santini, 503 F.2d 29, 33 (2d Cir. 1974); Salgado v. M. J. Rudolph Corp., 514 F.2d 750, 755 (2d Cir. 1975). Salgado's description of the seaman as aboard primarily as an aid to navigation cannot survive Wilander, but the permanence requirement is a separate element that Wilander left standing. Weiss v. Central Railroad Co., 235 F.2d 309 (2d Cir. 1956), is a reminder that a mixture of shore and vessel duties does not by itself defeat status. Useful work is therefore necessary but not sufficient. The parties' agreement that Latsis's work contributed to the fleet's function establishes only that element. It does not answer whether his employment connection to the vessels was of the kind the Jones Act protects, and that is the question the jury was asked to decide.

We review independently whether an instruction states the governing standard correctly. Where the evidence supports competing inferences, the choice between them belongs to the jury. Even a preserved error does not require a new trial unless it affected a party's substantial rights. Fed. R. Civ. P. 61; 28 U.S.C. § 2111.

**II. The exclusion of the conversion assignment.** The district court was right that the question is the worker's employment-related connection to a vessel in navigation. Its instruction nonetheless treated two different questions as one. The first is whether a worker injured while a vessel is undergoing major reconstruction is a seaman for that injury; the answer there naturally turns on the vessel's condition at the time. The second is whether a worker's overall employment relationship with a vessel or fleet is the substantial and more or less permanent connection the cases require, where the injury occurred earlier, at sea, aboard a vessel that was indisputably in navigation. The vessel's status during a later refit is pertinent to the first question. It does not make the worker's continuing assignment irrelevant to the second.

Latsis was hurt on a voyage from Baltimore to Bermuda. His claim does not depend on having been injured during the German conversion. He offers the conversion assignment for what it shows about the relationship he already had: he sailed with the vessel to the yard, supervised work directed at her return to service, and remained a fleet employee afterward. An instruction that the jury must disregard those six months made one legal characterization, that the Galileo was out of navigation during the refit, conclusive on a question to which it is not conclusive. That was error.

Neither West v. United States, 361 U.S. 118, 122 (1959), nor McKinley v. All Alaskan Seafoods, Inc., 980 F.2d 567 (9th Cir. 1992), holds otherwise. West involved a vessel withdrawn from maritime service for a complete overhaul and a shore-based repair worker injured during the overhaul, and it concerned the vessel owner's duties to that worker. McKinley, which is persuasive only, concerned a worker killed in a fire aboard a hull under conversion, and it turned on whether that vessel was in navigation when the fire occurred. Each addresses an injury sustained while the vessel was being rebuilt. Neither addresses whether the continuing employment of a worker injured earlier, at sea, may be weighed in deciding whether his connection to the vessels was an enduring one.

Chandris's strongest argument is that a major conversion takes a vessel out of navigation and that land-based ship-repair work cannot manufacture a seaman's status. The argument is serious, and Chandris is entitled to make it to the jury. It may contend that the German assignment was qualitatively shoreside reconstruction work, that it says little about any sea-based connection, and that its scale makes it a poor guide to an enduring fleet relationship. All of that goes to weight. None of it justifies telling the jury, as a matter of law, to put six months of the assignment out of mind. Our holding is correspondingly narrow. We do not hold that conversion or repair time always counts, that all ship repair is qualifying service, that Latsis is a seaman, or that a fixed percentage of time aboard ship decides the matter. We hold only that a vessel's repair or conversion status does not categorically make a worker's continuing assignment irrelevant to seaman status for an earlier shipboard injury, and that the character and weight of that assignment are for the jury.

**III. Prejudice.** The error was not harmless. Status was the only question the jury decided. The evidence on time aboard ship conflicted sharply, seventy-two percent against ten percent, so the outcome did not follow from a single undisputed fact. The excluded period was six months of a continuing assignment, directly relevant to whether his relationship to the vessels was enduring, and the verdict was general, so it does not show what the jury would have concluded had it been permitted to consider that assignment. We cannot say that the instruction did not affect Latsis's substantial rights. We need not decide which party bears the burden on prejudice, because the conclusion is the same either way: the record shows the error was prejudicial and does not show it was harmless.

**IV. The unpreserved objections.** Latsis also attacks the broader connection formulation in the charge. He did not object to it as he objected to the exclusion. Under Rule 51 a party may not assign as error the giving of an instruction unless it objected before the jury retired, stating distinctly the matter and the grounds. Review of an unobjected-to civil instruction is for plain error. Schaafsma v. Morin Vermont Corp., 802 F.2d 629, 636 (2d Cir. 1986); Abou-Khadra v. Mahshie, 4 F.3d 1071, 1078 (2d Cir. 1993). We have described such an error as one that is plain and works a miscarriage of justice, or is an obvious instance of misapplied law. The standard is not met merely because a better instruction could have been given, and the importance of a developing area of maritime law does not substitute for preservation.

Because the preserved error independently requires a new trial, we have no occasion to decide whether any unobjected-to portion of the charge meets that standard, and we do not decide it. We rest the judgment on the preserved objection alone. We neither find the original phrasing plainly erroneous nor endorse it. On retrial, the court should charge under Wilander and the circuit connection cases in a way that distinguishes work that contributes to a vessel's mission from the employment-related connection to a vessel in navigation that status requires. The charge should permit the jury to consider evidence of the continuing fleet relationship, including the conversion assignment, with each side free to argue its character and weight. And it should leave genuine factual disputes, including the proportion of Latsis's time spent aboard ship, to the jury. This guidance is tied to the preserved error and is not a ruling on the unpreserved objections.

**V. Disposition.** The judgment of the district court is vacated and the case is remanded for a new trial on seaman status under a charge consistent with this opinion. Because the jury decided nothing else, the questions of negligence, causation, and damages remain open for any necessary further trial. We express no view on whether Latsis is a seaman or on how the conflict over the time he spent aboard should be resolved.

---

#### Separate opinions

**Dissent (Judge Kearse), joined by no one.** There is no concurrence and no other separate writing. The source establishes the dissent by a statement of its grounds, reported here as established; no separate dissenting opinion text is supplied, and none is written up here.

Judge Kearse would affirm the judgment for the defendants and cannot join the vacatur, whether on the preserved exclusion or more broadly. Her dissent rests on two grounds.

First, she would hold that a new trial is not supported under the preservation rules. No relief may rest on the unobjected-to portions of the charge, because Rule 51 and the circuit's plain-error cases (Schaafsma and Abou-Khadra) require an exceptional showing that is not made, and the preserved objection to the exclusion does not by itself carry a new trial.

Second, she does not accept the majority's treatment of the conversion and navigation question. Giving weight to the Galileo's status as out of navigation during the conversion, and to the line of authority exemplified by West and McKinley, she would leave the judgment undisturbed.

Her grounds do not depend on whether the exclusion was strictly correct or merely not a ground for reversal; either would support affirmance. She would not determine seaman status, negligence, causation, or damages. She also would not join a revival of the aid-to-navigation element, a fixed time-fraction test, a rule that conversion work is always probative, or any appellate determination of seaman status, none of which the majority adopts.

The dissent has no precedential force.

---

#### What the decision does

**Controlling holding.** Judges Oakes and Stone-Khoury, on a three-judge panel, establish the following. The opinion of the court is precedential to the extent of the holdings stated here.

1. *Rule.* A vessel's repair or conversion status does not categorically make a worker's continuing assignment irrelevant to seaman status where the injury occurred earlier, at sea, aboard the vessel while she was in navigation; the assignment is evidence on whether the worker's connection to the vessel or fleet is substantial and more or less permanent, and its character and weight are for the jury.
   - *Authority:* 46 U.S.C. app. § 688; Wilander, 498 U.S. at 355; Gizoni, 502 U.S. at 88-89; Harney, Klarman, and Salgado (permanence requirement only); Weiss.
   - *Test and burdens:* employment-related connection to a vessel in navigation, plus a more or less permanent connection to a vessel or fleet; no aid-to-navigation element; no numerical threshold; independent review of the instruction, with disputed inferences left to the jury.
   - *Application:* the injury occurred at sea on a vessel in navigation, and the six-month assignment after it was relevant evidence of the continuing relationship.
