2. *Prejudice.* An instruction that removes a substantial portion of a continuing assignment from a general-verdict seaman-status finding turning on conflicting evidence is not harmless under Rule 61 and 28 U.S.C. § 2111. This is an application to this record and not a general rule.
3. *Preservation.* The judgment rests on the preserved objection alone; the plain-error question on the unobjected-to charge is not decided.

**Matters left open.** Whether an injury sustained during a major conversion would itself support seaman status, and when a vessel under conversion is out of navigation; whether conversion or repair time ever counts without more, or counts in a given case; whether any unobjected-to portion of the charge meets the plain-error standard; which party bears the burden on prejudice; whether Latsis is a seaman, and how the conflict between seventy-two and ten percent is resolved; and negligence, causation, and damages.

**Precedent and legal effect.** The holding in proposition 1 is circuit precedent on the votes of Judges Oakes and Stone-Khoury, two actual joins on a three-judge panel, and applies in the Second Circuit from March 24, 1994. The prejudice ruling decides only this record. The judgment vacating the defense judgment and ordering a new trial is party-specific relief. The retrial guidance on the charge is tied to the preserved error and is not a ruling on the unpreserved objections. The dissent has no precedential force.

The opinion follows Wilander and Gizoni and applies the circuit's permanence requirement from Harney, Klarman, and Salgado. It recognizes that Wilander displaced the aid-to-navigation element that Salgado's formulation contained, and it leaves the rest of those decisions in force. It treats West and McKinley as addressing injuries sustained during reconstruction and distinguishes them for the question decided here. It changes no rule of preservation. Rule 51 and the circuit's plain-error standard are applied as stated in Schaafsma and Abou-Khadra, and the opinion declines to extend or relax them. On remand, the district court is bound by this opinion as the law of the case on the matters decided.

**Effect on the parties.** The judgment of the district court is vacated and the case is remanded for a new trial on seaman status under a charge consistent with the opinion; negligence, causation, and damages remain open for any necessary further trial. No finding of seaman status and no finding on time aboard ship is made.

**Mandate and remand.** The mandate has not issued. Under Fed. R. App. P. 41(a) as then in force, the mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless the court shortens or enlarges the time by order, and a timely petition for rehearing under Fed. R. App. P. 40, which in a civil case between private parties is due within 14 days after entry of judgment, stays the mandate until the petition is disposed of. The date of entry of judgment is not stated, and no petition is assumed. Further proceedings in the district court follow issuance of the mandate.

#### Historical comparison

Latsis v. Chandris, Inc., No. 93-7704, 20 F.3d 45, 50-57 (2d Cir. 1994) (public reproduction: law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/20/45/523011/). Decided March 24, 1994, after argument on December 6, 1993. Panel: Oakes, Kearse, Cardamone. Judge Oakes wrote the opinion, joined by Judge Cardamone. The majority vacated the defense judgment and remanded for a new trial, finding the seaman-status charge erroneous in its treatment of the employment relationship and in categorically excluding the drydock period, without resolving disputed vessel-time facts or finding Latsis a seaman. Judge Kearse dissented, contesting reversal under the preservation rules and the drydock and navigation analysis.

Compared with the simulated judgment, the disposition, remedy, author and dissent are the same; only the lineup differs, with Judge Stone-Khoury joining Judge Oakes in place of Judge Cardamone. The reasoning reaches the same judgment on a narrower footing: the simulated majority rests on the preserved exclusion objection alone and leaves the unobjected portions of the charge undecided, while the historical majority also addressed the broader instructional framework under the preservation analysis.

### [Whalen v. County of Fulton](../cases/whalen-v-county-of-fulton/)

Decision date: March 29, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 93-7457  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Published opinion of the court, with a dissent. Appeal dismissed for want of appellate jurisdiction, two to one. No mandate has issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/whalen-v-county-of-fulton/events/1994-03-29-decision.md](../cases/whalen-v-county-of-fulton/events/1994-03-29-decision.md)

Historical panel: Thomas J. Meskill (Senior Circuit Judge); John M. Walker, Jr. (Active Circuit Judge); Constance Baker Motley (Senior District Judge, S.D.N.Y., sitting by designation).  
Simulation panel: Thomas J. Meskill (Senior Circuit Judge); John M. Walker, Jr. (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Constance Baker Motley.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

#### Decision summary

##### The case and the result

The individual officials named as defendants appealed from an interlocutory order of the United States District Court for the Northern District of New York (Frederick J. Scullin, Jr., District Judge). That order denied, without prejudice to renewal after discovery, their motion for summary judgment on the ground of qualified immunity. The record does not establish the date on which the order was entered. The officials asked for immediate review and for immunity from suit. The Whalens asked that the appeal be dismissed, or that the order permitting development of the record be left in place.

George and Elizabeth Whalen are the adoptive parents of Michael. They challenged child-welfare authorities' placement of Michael's biological sister, Elizabeth, with another family. They sued the two counties, their departments of social services, and the individual officials involved, alleging interference with association, denial of access to the courts, and violations of substantive and procedural due process, together with state-law claims. The record describes no established custodial relationship between the Whalens and the sister comparable to an existing parent-child household. No final judgment has been entered. The claims against the counties and departments and the state-law claims remain pending in the district court, and an immunity ruling for an individual official would not dispose of them. The court does not decide whether the notice of appeal was timely.

The court of appeals decided only whether it could hear the appeal now. It held that it could not. The order reserved the immunity question for decision on a developed record, so it does not conclusively determine the officials' entitlement to qualified immunity and is not an appealable collateral order. The court therefore dismissed the appeal for want of appellate jurisdiction and did not decide whether the right the Whalens assert was clearly established or whether the individual defendants are entitled to immunity.

Issues and standards of review:

1. Whether the court has appellate jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1291 and the collateral-order doctrine over an order denying a qualified-immunity summary judgment motion without prejudice to renewal after discovery. This question is decided first and independently. The legal character of the order, and whether disputed facts are material to the defense, are reviewed de novo. The officials, who seek review before final judgment, must show that the order falls within the exception to the final-judgment rule.
2. If jurisdiction existed, whether the right the Whalens assert was clearly established, a question of law, with factual disputes governed by the rules of summary judgment. This issue is not reached by the court.
3. Whether any basis exists to review, with the officials' appeal, matters affecting the counties, the departments or the state-law claims. No basis is presented, and the court does not reach the question.

Judgment: The appeal is dismissed for want of appellate jurisdiction.  
Vote: Two to one.  
Opinion and joins: Opinion of the Court by Judge Meskill, joined in full by Judge Stone. Dissenting opinion by Judge Walker, joined by no other judge. Judge Stone writes no separate opinion.

Component results:

- **Appellate jurisdiction over the order.** The order does not conclusively determine the officials' entitlement to qualified immunity and is not an appealable collateral order. For this holding: Judge Meskill and Judge Stone. Against: Judge Walker, who would exercise limited jurisdiction over the legal immunity question.
- **Disposition of the appeal.** Dismissed. For: Judge Meskill and Judge Stone. Against: Judge Walker, who would reverse in the reviewable part and remand for entry of summary judgment for the individual defendants on the federal claims resting on the asserted right to the sister's placement.
- **Qualified immunity on the merits.** The court does not decide whether the asserted right was clearly established or whether the individual defendants are entitled to immunity. Judge Walker, in dissent, would hold that no clearly established right existed. No majority reaches the question.
- **Protected interest in sibling association.** No member of the panel decides whether adoptive parents or siblings hold a constitutionally protected interest in the placement of a sibling or in sibling association generally.
- **Counties, departments and state-law claims.** Unaffected by the judgment. All three judges would leave them to the district court.

Judges Meskill and Stone agree in the judgment and in the reasoning of the controlling proposition. Judge Walker disagrees with both.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

MESKILL, Senior Circuit Judge, joined by STONE, Circuit Judge:

The individual officials named as defendants in this civil rights action appeal from an order of the United States District Court for the Northern District of New York (Frederick J. Scullin, Jr., Judge) denying, without prejudice to renewal after discovery, their motion for summary judgment on the ground of qualified immunity. Whether we may hear that appeal now is the only question before us. We hold that the order does not conclusively determine the officials' entitlement to immunity and is therefore not an appealable collateral order. We dismiss the appeal for want of appellate jurisdiction.

**I. Background**

George and Elizabeth Whalen are the adoptive parents of Michael. They challenge the decision of child-welfare authorities to place Michael's biological sister, Elizabeth, with another family. They sued Fulton County and Montgomery County, their departments of social services, and the individual officials involved under 42 U.S.C. § 1983, alleging interference with association, denial of access to the courts, and violations of substantive and procedural due process. They also plead claims under state law. The record describes no established custodial relationship between the Whalens and the sister comparable to an existing parent-child household.

The individual officials moved for summary judgment on the ground of qualified immunity. The district court denied the motion without prejudice to its renewal after discovery. The claims against the counties and departments, and the state-law claims, remain pending below, and an immunity ruling in favor of an individual official would not dispose of them. This appeal concerns only the interlocutory order. No final judgment has been entered, and none has found a constitutional right to the joint placement of siblings.

**II. Appellate Jurisdiction**

*A. The governing rule.* Our jurisdiction extends to appeals from "final decisions" of the district courts. 28 U.S.C. § 1291. An order denying summary judgment is ordinarily not final, because it leaves the case pending and may be reviewed with the final judgment. The Supreme Court has recognized a "small class" of decisions that finally determine claims of right separable from, and collateral to, the rights asserted in the action, and that may be appealed at once. Cohen v. Beneficial Industrial Loan Corp., 337 U.S. 541, 546 (1949). To come within that class, the order "must conclusively determine the disputed question, resolve an important issue completely separate from the merits of the action, and be effectively unreviewable on appeal from a final judgment." Coopers & Lybrand v. Livesay, 437 U.S. 463, 468 (1978). Cohen itself explained that the finality requirement excludes any decision that is "tentative, informal or incomplete." 337 U.S. at 546.

Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511 (1985), applied these requirements to qualified immunity. Immunity is an "entitlement not to stand trial or face the other burdens of litigation," id. at 526, and that entitlement is lost if a case is erroneously allowed to proceed. A district court's denial of a claim of qualified immunity, "to the extent that it turns on an issue of law," is therefore an appealable final decision. Id. at 530. The question the court of appeals then decides is a legal one: whether the facts the plaintiff alleges or can show describe a violation of clearly established law. Id. at 528. We have applied Mitchell with attention to its limits. Our jurisdiction is "limited to circumstances where the qualified immunity defense may be established as a matter of law," and when a factual determination is a necessary predicate to deciding whether immunity bars the suit, "review is postponed." Cartier v. Lussier, 955 F.2d 841, 844 (2d Cir. 1992); see Golino v. City of New Haven, 950 F.2d 864, 868 (2d Cir. 1991). Whether disputed facts are material to the defense is a legal question that we review de novo. Cartier, 955 F.2d at 844.

The officials ask us to review a non-final order, and so it is for them to show that it satisfies each of these requirements. We decide that question independently, and before we consider anything about the merits of the defense. The first requirement, a conclusive determination, disposes of the matter. Because the order fails it, we do not reach the requirements of separateness and effective unreviewability.

*B. What the order decided.* Whether an order conclusively determines a question depends on what the order did, not on what it is called. The words "without prejudice" settle nothing in either direction. An order that rejects an immunity defense as a matter of law does not become tentative because the district court describes the rejection as provisional, and an order that truly withholds decision does not become final because a party characterizes it as a rejection. We look to the substance.

The order before us denied the motion without prejudice and permitted the officials to renew it after discovery. By that provision the district court did not answer the question the officials put to it, namely whether, on the claims as pleaded and the facts as they stand, the individual defendants are entitled to judgment because the right the Whalens assert was not clearly established. It postponed that question until the record is fuller, and it expressly preserved the officials' ability to press the defense at that time. The immunity defense therefore remains alive and undecided in the district court. A decision that by its own terms invites renewal on a developed record is the kind of tentative and incomplete ruling that the finality requirement excludes. There is no settled legal determination for us to review. If we decided the immunity question now, we would not be reviewing the district court's answer to it. We would be supplying an answer the district court has not given.

That conclusion is consistent with Mitchell. The entitlement Mitchell protects is the right to have the legal question of immunity decided correctly before the burdens of litigation are imposed, and Mitchell permits an immediate appeal because a conclusive ruling against the defense cannot be undone if review waits. Where the district court has not ruled against the defense, and has preserved it for decision, the entitlement has not been denied. It remains to be adjudicated in the forum in which it was raised. Cartier states the point from the other direction: where a factual determination is a necessary predicate to resolving whether immunity bars the suit, review is postponed. The order here contemplates just that sequence, development of the record followed by decision. We do not decide whether the district court was right that further record is needed. That question is not before us in a posture that permits review, because the order did not conclusively decide it.

*C. Musso and Dube.* The officials rely on Musso v. Hourigan, 836 F.2d 736 (2d Cir. 1988), and Dube v. State University of New York, 900 F.2d 587 (2d Cir. 1990), in which we exercised jurisdiction although the district court had not clearly ruled on the immunity defense. In Musso, the district court denied summary judgment on a false arrest claim without expressly addressing the defendant's qualified immunity defense. We held that review was proper because declining it would risk the loss of whatever entitlement the defendant had not to stand trial, and we remanded the immunity question to the district court. Id. at 741-42. In Dube, the district court denied summary judgment in a one-sentence statement that we could not read as having addressed the defense on either constitutional claim, and we held the denial immediately appealable. 900 F.2d at 596. In each case the district court had denied the motion, and the case was to proceed toward trial with the immunity defense unaddressed. The order here differs in kind. It does not deny the defense and send the case toward trial. It withholds decision and invites the same motion to be made again on a fuller record. Neither Musso nor Dube, as we read them, involved an order that expressly preserved the defense for later decision, and neither holds that an immediate appeal lies from such an order. Nothing we say today limits the rule those cases apply to unqualified denials.

*D. The officials' functional argument.* The officials' strongest argument is a functional one. Qualified immunity protects against the burdens of discovery as well as trial. Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800, 818 (1982) ("Until this threshold immunity question is resolved, discovery should not be allowed."); Siegert v. Gilley, 500 U.S. 226, 231-32 (1991). They contend that if "without prejudice" is treated as controlling, a district court could reject the defense on a pure question of law, call the rejection provisional, and require officials to bear the very burdens the doctrine is meant to prevent. They add that, if the pleaded facts taken as true identify no clearly established right, further discovery is unnecessary and the defense should be sustained now.

We accept the premise of the first point and have framed the question with it in mind. A label cannot save an order from appeal if, in substance, the order finally rejects a legal entitlement to immunity. Our holding is that this order does not do so. The second point is an argument about the merits of the defense. It may be a substantial argument, and the officials may present it to the district court on renewal. But the strength of a defense cannot supply appellate jurisdiction that the character of the order withholds. If it could, the jurisdictional inquiry would be circular: whenever an immunity claim appeared strong enough, we would assume the authority to adjudicate it. Cohen's requirement of a conclusive determination operates independently of the apparent merits of the defense.

Nor are the officials without recourse. Nothing in our dismissal forecloses them from asking the district court to decide at once the purely legal question whether the right the Whalens assert was clearly established, to confine any discovery to facts bearing on that question, or to seek protection under Fed. R. Civ. P. 26(c). If the district court, on renewal or otherwise, rejects the defense as a matter of law, whether in terms or in substance, Mitchell and Cartier describe when an appeal lies. We decide nothing about any such ruling.

*E. The dissent.* Our dissenting colleague would take limited jurisdiction on the view that the defense can be established as a matter of law on the facts alleged. The limits our cases place on jurisdiction, that review lies where the defense may be established as a matter of law, restrict the cases that Mitchell reaches. They do not enlarge the class of orders that qualify as collateral orders, and they presuppose a district court decision against the defense that is conclusive. Whether the defense is decidable as a matter of law on the pleaded facts is precisely the question the district court left open. To answer it, we would have to decide a motion the district court has not decided. That the right the Whalens assert may prove unestablished does not turn this order into a conclusive rejection of the defense.

**III. Limits of the Holding**

First, the holding rests on the order before us. We do not hold that the words "without prejudice" invariably defeat immediate review. An order that in function finally rejects a purely legal immunity defense is reviewable under Mitchell whatever the district court calls it, and we decide nothing about such an order.

Second, we decide nothing about the merits. We do not decide whether the Whalens' allegations state a violation of any right, whether adoptive parents or siblings hold a constitutionally protected interest in the placement of a sibling or in sibling association, whether any such right was clearly established when the officials acted, or whether the individual defendants are entitled to immunity. Those questions are for the district court in the first instance and for any later review that is properly available.

Third, our decision does not endorse open-ended discovery, and it does not suggest that a district court may defer a purely legal immunity question until trial as a matter of course. Harlow and Siegert direct that the threshold question be resolved before discovery goes forward. Where facts are genuinely needed to determine what the officials did and knew, discovery may be limited to those facts. Where the question is purely legal, a plaintiff cannot overcome qualified immunity merely by predicting that discovery may persuade a court to recognize a right not previously established, because the inquiry is whether the right was clearly established when the officials acted. Harlow, 457 U.S. at 818. These observations explain the boundary of our holding. We do not apply them to the discovery that has taken or will take place in this case, and we do not review the sequencing of discovery as such.

Fourth, this is an appeal by the individual officials. We express no view on, and exercise no pendent appellate jurisdiction over, the claims against the counties and their departments or the state-law claims. They remain pending in the district court and are unaffected by this decision.

**IV. Disposition**

The appeal is dismissed for want of appellate jurisdiction. The order of the district court remains in effect, and the action continues in the district court, including discovery and renewed consideration of the immunity defense, consistent with this opinion.

#### Separate opinions

##### Dissenting opinion

Author: Judge Walker. Joined by: no other judge.

The decision record establishes this writing only in summary form, as the following statement of Judge Walker's grounds. No full text of a separate dissenting opinion is established, and none is supplied here.

Judge Walker dissents. No other judge joins him. His grounds, as established, are these. He would exercise limited jurisdiction over the legal immunity question. Under Soares v. Connecticut, 8 F.3d 917, 920 (2d Cir. 1993), review is proper where the qualified immunity defense may be established as a matter of law on the facts viewed in the Whalens' favor, or where the district court has committed fundamental error in applying the law, a rule he draws also from Cartier and Mitchell. He would not take jurisdiction unless the defense is first found decidable as a matter of law, and he would rely on no unalleged facts and resolve no disputed facts.

On the pleaded facts, which allege no custodial relationship, he would hold that no clearly established right of adoptive parents to the placement of the sister existed. He draws on Harlow, Siegert, Smith v. Organization of Foster Families for Equality & Reform, 431 U.S. 816 (1977), and Rivera v. Marcus, 696 F.2d 1016 (2d Cir. 1982), and would canvass all relevant precedent, including precedent not cited to the district court, as Elder v. Holloway, 510 U.S. 510 (1994), directs. He would resolve the question on the clearly-established prong alone, without deciding whether a protected interest exists, as this court has done in Easton v. Sundram, 947 F.2d 1011 (2d Cir. 1991), and Severino v. Negron, 996 F.2d 1439 (2d Cir. 1993). He would announce no broad constitutional rule on sibling association. He would not dismiss on the ground that the order is a provisional deferral requiring factual development.

He would reverse in the reviewable part and remand for entry of summary judgment for the individual defendants on the federal claims resting on the asserted sibling-placement right, leaving the remainder of the case, the claims against the counties and the state-law claims to the district court. His dissent is not circuit law.

Judge Stone writes no separate opinion.

#### What the decision does

- **Controlling holding (Part II of the opinion; Judge Meskill and Judge Stone).** An interlocutory denial of a qualified-immunity summary judgment motion is immediately appealable under the collateral-order doctrine only to the extent that the order conclusively determines, as a matter of law, the officials' entitlement to the defense. The rule applies to an appeal by officials from an order denying the motion before final judgment. The test is that the order must conclusively determine the disputed question, resolve an important issue completely separate from the merits, and be effectively unreviewable after final judgment (Cohen v. Beneficial Industrial Loan Corp., 337 U.S. 541, 546 (1949); Coopers & Lybrand v. Livesay, 437 U.S. 463, 468 (1978)), applied to immunity through Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511, 526-30 (1985), Cartier v. Lussier, 955 F.2d 841, 844 (2d Cir. 1992), and Golino v. City of New Haven, 950 F.2d 864, 868 (2d Cir. 1991). The burden is on the officials as appellants to bring the order within the exception. The standard of review is de novo. Applied here, the order denied the motion without prejudice and permitted renewal after discovery; it reserved the immunity question for decision on a developed record, so it is tentative and not conclusive, and the court looks to what the order did and not to its label.
- **Qualification.** The holding is confined to this order. Orders that in function finally reject a purely legal immunity defense remain reviewable whatever they are called, and the court decides nothing about them.
- **Dicta.** Part III's observations on discovery and on the officials' recourse restate Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800, 818 (1982), and Siegert v. Gilley, 500 U.S. 226, 231-32 (1991), explain the limits of the holding, and are not necessary to the judgment.
- **Status and treatment of precedent.** Published opinion. The Opinion of the Court, with the joins of Judge Meskill and Judge Stone, is precedential in this circuit on the controlling proposition stated in Part II, from its release on March 29, 1994, subject to rehearing, en banc review, and controlling Supreme Court authority. The dissent is not precedent. The decision follows and applies Cohen, Coopers & Lybrand, Mitchell, Cartier and Golino. It distinguishes Musso and Dube as concerning unqualified denials of summary judgment, and it neither overrules nor questions them. It exercises no authority to depart from any controlling decision.
- **Not decided and not precedent.** The merits of the immunity defense, the existence of any protected interest in sibling placement or association, the timeliness of the notice of appeal, pendent appellate jurisdiction over the counties and state-law claims, and whether any later ruling on renewal will be appealable. The dismissal decides only that this court lacks jurisdiction over this appeal. It carries no law-of-the-case or preclusive effect on the merits of the immunity defense, which the district court remains free to decide on renewal.
- **Effect on the parties and relief.** The appeal is dismissed. There is no remand order, because the case never left the district court. The district court's order stands, and discovery and renewed consideration of the individual defendants' immunity defense proceed there. The decision directs no entry of summary judgment, sets no scope or limit for discovery, and does not affect the claims against the counties and departments or the state-law claims.
- **Mandate status.** No mandate has issued. Under the Federal Rules of Appellate Procedure as in force on the decision date, a petition for rehearing may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order or by local rule (Fed. R. App. P. 40(a)). The mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order (Fed. R. App. P. 41(a)). If a petition for rehearing is timely filed, the mandate does not issue until 7 days after entry of an order denying it, unless that time is shortened or enlarged. The date on which judgment was entered is not established in the record, and no petition or motion is known to have been filed.

#### Historical comparison

Historically, on 1994-03-29 a panel of Judges Meskill, Walker and Motley dismissed the interlocutory appeal for lack of appellate jurisdiction. Judge Meskill wrote the opinion, joined by Judge Motley: the district court's denial of qualified-immunity summary judgment without prejudice to renewal after discovery was not a sufficiently conclusive determination under the collateral-order doctrine, and the majority did not decide the constitutional or immunity merits. Judge Walker dissented; he would have taken jurisdiction over the legal immunity question and granted qualified immunity because the asserted right was not clearly established. The provisional district-court order remained operative. The simulated judgment is the same: dismissal for want of jurisdiction, 2-1, Judge Meskill writing, Judge Walker dissenting alone. Only one panel member differs, with Judge Stone joining the majority opinion in place of Judge Motley. No changed judgment, and no different ground reaching the same judgment, was found. Source: Whalen v. County of Fulton, 19 F.3d 828, 830-34 (2d Cir. 1994) (No. 93-7457).

### [Walker v. Bates](../cases/walker-v-bates/)

Decision date: April 29, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 93-2107  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Reversed insofar as the dismissal rests on administrative cure, and remanded. Published opinion; precedential. Cite as Walker v. Bates, No. 93-2107 (2d Cir. Apr. 29, 1994). No mandate has issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/walker-v-bates/events/1994-04-29-decision.md](../cases/walker-v-bates/events/1994-04-29-decision.md)

Historical panel: Wilfred Feinberg (Senior Circuit Judge); Roger J. Miner (Active Circuit Judge); J. Daniel Mahoney (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: Wilfred Feinberg (Senior Circuit Judge); Roger J. Miner (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: J. Daniel Mahoney.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

#### The case and the result

**The dispute.** Robert Walker, an inmate at Southport, was charged in December 1990 with possessing a metal rod and excess bedding. He maintained that the property had been left in the cell by a prior occupant. At a Tier III disciplinary hearing, the hearing officer, L. Bates, refused Walker's requests to call three correction officers as witnesses and imposed 120 days in the special housing unit. The punishment began immediately. In February 1991, Donald Selsky reversed the disciplinary determination on administrative review and authorized a prompt rehearing. No rehearing occurred. Walker nevertheless remained in special housing until February 22. He then sued for damages under 42 U.S.C. § 1983. These are the properly pleaded facts, taken with the inferences from them in Walker's favor. The record does not state Bates's reasons for refusing the witnesses, does not fix how many days Walker spent in special housing before and after the reversal, and does not say who was responsible for his retention after it.

**How the case reached this court.** The district court dismissed Walker's claim against Bates under Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(b)(6) on one ground: that administrative reversal of the disciplinary determination cured the procedural defect Walker alleges. The dismissal rests on that cure theory alone, not on any finding that the witnesses Walker sought could properly be excluded. Other named defendants had been dismissed earlier, and their dismissals are not before the court. The dismissal with prejudice of the claim against Bates, the other named defendants having been dismissed earlier, is a final decision under 28 U.S.C. § 1291, and the action arises under federal law.

**The question and the standard of review.** The question is whether administrative reversal of a disciplinary determination, after the punishment it imposed has begun, defeats as a matter of law a prisoner's claim for damages for the denial of procedural due process at the hearing. Review of a Rule 12(b)(6) dismissal is de novo. A complaint may not be dismissed unless it appears beyond doubt that the plaintiff can prove no set of facts that would entitle him to relief. Conley v. Gibson, 355 U.S. 41, 45-46 (1957). On this posture the cure theory prevails only if it defeats the claim even on Walker's account of events. Whether the refusal of the witnesses was justified, whether Bates is immune, and what injury he caused are separate questions that the cure theory does not resolve.

**The parties' positions.** Walker seeks reversal and further proceedings on his witness-denial claim. Bates seeks affirmance on the ground that administrative review cured the hearing defect. He argues that the hearing and the administrative appeal are stages of a single process that worked, and that damages liability would discourage the prompt administrative correction that prison systems should be encouraged to make.

**The result.** The Court of Appeals holds that the reversal does not defeat, as a matter of law, a claim for the punishment Walker had already undergone. It reverses the dismissal insofar as it rests on administrative cure and remands for further proceedings. It decides that and nothing more, and leaves to the district court the questions the cure theory was thought to make unnecessary.

Judgment: The judgment of the district court dismissing Walker's claim against Bates is REVERSED insofar as it rests on administrative cure, and the case is REMANDED for further proceedings consistent with the opinion of the court.  
Vote: 3-0 (unanimous). By component:

1. Reversal of the dismissal insofar as it rests on administrative cure: Judges Feinberg, Miner, and Stone-Khoury in favor; none opposed.
2. Remand for further proceedings on the questions the cure theory did not resolve, with no direction as to outcome, no award of damages, and no determination that Bates is liable: Judges Feinberg, Miner, and Stone-Khoury in favor; none opposed.
3. The earlier dismissals of the other defendants are neither disturbed nor restored: Judges Feinberg, Miner, and Stone-Khoury in favor; none opposed.

Opinion and joins: Opinion of the court by Judge Miner, in which Judge Feinberg and Judge Stone-Khoury join in full. Because all three judges join the whole opinion, agreement in the judgment and agreement in the reasoning coincide.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

MINER, Circuit Judge:

Robert Walker alleges that a prison hearing officer, L. Bates, refused to let him call three correction officers as witnesses at a disciplinary hearing and then imposed 120 days in the special housing unit, that the punishment began at once, and that it was running when administrative review reversed the determination in February 1991. The district court dismissed his section 1983 claim against Bates on the ground that the reversal cured whatever was wrong with the hearing. We hold that the reversal does not defeat, as a matter of law, a claim for the punishment Walker had already undergone. We therefore reverse and remand. We decide that and nothing more, and we leave to the district court the questions the cure theory was thought to make unnecessary.

I.

A prisoner punished with segregated confinement is entitled to the process the Constitution requires before the liberty at stake is taken. To state a claim under section 1983 for denial of that process, Walker must show a protected liberty interest and the denial of constitutionally required procedure. Where New York's disciplinary system imposes special housing confinement as punishment, this circuit has recognized a protected interest and has required the procedures of Wolff v. McDonnell, 418 U.S. 539 (1974). Sher v. Coughlin, 739 F.2d 77, 81 (2d Cir. 1984); McCann v. Coughlin, 698 F.2d 112, 121-22 (2d Cir. 1983). One of those procedures is a qualified right to call witnesses and present evidence, which gives way when permitting it would be unduly hazardous to institutional safety or correctional goals. Wolff, 418 U.S. at 566. Because the right is qualified, officials must be allowed to keep hearings within reasonable limits. The Supreme Court has held that officials must at some point state their reasons for refusing a witness, that they may do so in later litigation rather than in the hearing record, and that the burden of justifying the refusal rests on them. Ponte v. Real, 471 U.S. 491, 497-500 (1985). We therefore do not read the Constitution to require contemporaneous written reasons, and nothing in this opinion imposes such a requirement.

None of these questions was decided below. The district court did not hold that Walker lacked a protected interest or that Bates had good reason to refuse the three officers, and the record does not disclose Bates's reasons. We will not supply grounds merely to sustain a dismissal that rests on a different one, and we will not foreclose grounds that Wolff and Ponte permit Bates to establish. The only ruling before us is that administrative reversal cured the defect.

II.

Bates's argument deserves a fair statement. The hearing and the administrative appeal, he says, are stages of a single process, and the process worked: the error was found, the determination was reversed, and a rehearing was authorized. Damages liability, he adds, would discourage the prompt administrative correction that prison systems should be encouraged to make. The argument has force, and Young v. Hoffman, 970 F.2d 1154, 1155-56 (2d Cir. 1992), supplies its foundation. In Young the administrative reversal came before the prisoner had served any part of the penalty. We held that the reversal was part of the process he received and cured any defect in the hearing, and that because he was never penalized he suffered no interference with a liberty interest and had no claim. Russell v. Scully, 15 F.3d 219 (2d Cir. 1994) (as modified on denial of rehearing), relied on Young in stating that an administrative appeal may correct a hearing's procedural defects.

We follow both decisions, but each rests on a premise that is absent here. In Young the sentence never operated. Walker alleges the opposite: the punishment was imposed and began at once, and it continued for a substantial period before the reversal. In that circumstance the integrated-process argument confronts a different question. The question is no longer whether a deprivation ever occurred, but whether a later correction can undo one that has been suffered. Our decisions point to the answer that it cannot, at least not automatically. In Patterson v. Coughlin, 761 F.2d 886, 893 (2d Cir. 1985), a prisoner was confined in special housing as discipline after a hearing at which he was not permitted to call witnesses, and he later obtained a stipulated order in state court under which the state agreed to restore his good time and expunge the record. We held that his section 1983 claim was not defeated by that later relief. The claim arises when the deprivation occurs without the process required beforehand, and once a cause of action for a constitutional violation has accrued, nothing the state does afterward cuts it off. Patterson involved a state-court proceeding rather than an administrative appeal, and Bates is correct that the two differ. But the reasoning on which we rely does not depend on which body acts afterward. It depends on timing: a deprivation already suffered is not undone by a later decision that it should not have been imposed.

Russell, read for what it decided, does not hold otherwise. The prisoner there had been held in special housing before and during an administrative appeal that ended in reversal. We held that the confinement itself, which the state could have imposed on administrative grounds whatever the flaws in the hearing, caused him no constitutional harm, but that the loss of privileges imposed as punishment during the appeal period could support a claim for damages. We expressly did not reach whether imposing punishment pending an administrative appeal that later corrects an error violates due process, and we resolved the case by holding that the defendant was entitled to qualified immunity. Russell therefore does not hold that a later reversal bars a claim for punishment already imposed, it left that question open, and our decision today is consistent with it.

Taking Walker's allegations as true, then, the cure theory does not defeat his claim for the confinement under Bates's order, and the dismissal cannot stand on that ground. The reversal matters, but not in the way Bates urges. It ended the operation of the order, and it may mitigate the injury Walker can attribute to the hearing. It bears on whether Bates, rather than someone else, answers for what followed it, and it bears on damages. What it cannot do is make time already spent under an allegedly unlawful punitive order disappear. Bates's concern for prompt administrative correction is met by the same distinction. The sooner a mistaken penalty is lifted, the less there is to compensate, so the rule we apply preserves the incentive. It does not support a rule under which a reversal, however late, bars the claim whatever the prisoner has already endured. We hold that administrative reversal after punitive confinement has begun is not an automatic defense to an accrued procedural due process claim for punishment already served.

III.

The holding is narrow, and we state what it does not decide. We do not decide that Walker was denied due process. He must establish a protected interest and the denial of required process, and Bates may offer safety, relevance, duplication, or other permissible grounds for refusing the officers, which the district court must consider under Wolff and Ponte. We do not hold that the refusal violated Wolff, and we do not hold that Bates is liable for any period of confinement. The complaint also places in issue the time after the reversal when Walker remained in special housing without a rehearing. Who was responsible for his retention then is left to the district court.

We do not decide that Bates lacks qualified immunity. Cleavinger v. Saxner, 474 U.S. 193, 206-07 (1985), holds that members of a prison disciplinary committee have qualified, not absolute, immunity. It does not itself decide how an administrative reversal affects liability, and we do not treat it as having done so. Whether Bates may claim the defense turns on the law clearly established when he acted. This opinion neither supplies nor assumes any clarity that the law did not already have, and the district court will decide the question on a developed record.

Finally, causation and damages are for the district court. Under Carey v. Piphus, 435 U.S. 247 (1978), the denial of process and proof of compensable injury are distinct. Actual damages require proof that the injury was caused by the denial of required process rather than by a deprivation that was justified, id. at 263-64, and the district court must distinguish detention caused by Bates's order from any independent lawful basis for confinement. A denial of procedural due process without proof of actual injury is actionable for nominal damages. Id. at 266-67. The reversal is relevant to each of these inquiries. Nothing in this opinion directs an award for every day Walker spent in special housing, and nothing makes the reversal irrelevant to damages. We apply the framework in force on the date of this decision. The dismissals of the other defendants are not before us and are not restored.

The judgment is reversed insofar as it rests on administrative cure, and the case is remanded for further proceedings consistent with this opinion.

#### Separate opinions

None. No concurring or dissenting opinion was filed. There is one writing, the opinion of the court by Judge Miner, in which Judge Feinberg and Judge Stone-Khoury join in full, so no separate position stands apart from the opinion of the court.

#### What the decision does

- **Controlling holding:** When a disciplinary determination imposing punitive special-housing confinement is reversed on administrative review after the punishment has begun, the reversal is not, as a matter of law, a defense to the prisoner's section 1983 claim that the hearing denied him required process, insofar as the claim is for punishment already served. The reversal may mitigate injury. The claim itself requires a protected liberty interest and the denial of the process the Constitution requires, including the qualified right to call witnesses under Wolff, with officials bearing the burden under Ponte of justifying a refusal. Actual damages require proof of causation under Carey. Review is de novo under Conley. Judges Feinberg, Miner, and Stone-Khoury support the proposition. The passages restating Ponte, Cleavinger, and Carey, and the observation that reversal may mitigate injury, state settled law and are not independent holdings.
- **Treatment of precedent:** This is a published, precedential opinion. It changes circuit law only to the extent of the stated proposition and decides none of the questions left open in Part III. The court follows Young v. Hoffman and distinguishes it on its premise that no penalty was served; it does not overrule, question, or limit Young. It follows Patterson v. Coughlin on the principle that an accrued claim is not cut off by later state action. It reads Russell v. Scully for what it decided and does not disturb it; Russell reserved the question of punishment imposed pending an administrative appeal. It applies Sher, McCann, Wolff, Ponte, Cleavinger, Carey, and Conley as those decisions stand. Whalen v. County of Fulton, No. 93-7457 (2d Cir. Mar. 29, 1994), Part II, concerns appellate jurisdiction over orders denying qualified-immunity summary judgment without prejudice to renewal; it is not implicated here, because the order under review is a final dismissal on a cure theory and no immunity ruling is before the court. The decision applies the law in force on April 29, 1994.
- **Effect on the parties:** The decision grants party-specific relief, reversal and remand. The dismissal of the claim against Bates is reversed insofar as it rests on administrative cure, and the case is remanded to the district court for further proceedings consistent with the opinion. The court gives no direction as to outcome on any remaining question. No damages are awarded and no finding of liability is made. The other defendants are not restored. The ruling that the cure theory does not bar the claim for punishment already served is law of the case on remand; the unresolved questions have no preclusive effect. The questions remaining for the district court are:
  - whether Walker had a protected liberty interest;
  - whether he was denied required process, including whether Bates can establish a legitimate ground for refusing the three correction officers under Wolff and Ponte;
  - Bates's personal responsibility, including for Walker's retention in special housing after the reversal;
  - whether Bates is entitled to qualified immunity, judged by the law clearly established when he acted; and
  - causation and damages under Carey, including the separation of detention caused by Bates's order from any independent lawful basis for confinement, and whether any relief is nominal or compensatory.
- **Mandate and remand:** No mandate has issued. Under Fed. R. App. P. 40(a) as in force on the decision date, a petition for rehearing may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment. Under Fed. R. App. P. 41(a) as then in force, the mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless the court shortens or enlarges the time, and a timely petition for rehearing stays the mandate until the petition is disposed of. The district court takes up the remand after the mandate issues. No other filing or next step is recorded.

#### Historical comparison

Historical result: on April 29, 1994, a divided Second Circuit panel (Feinberg, Miner, Mahoney) reversed the dismissal of Walker's section 1983 claim against Bates and remanded. Judge Miner wrote, joined by Judge Feinberg; Judge Mahoney dissented, defending the administrative-cure framework. The majority rejected a categorical rule that administrative reversal cures unconstitutional disciplinary punishment already served, distinguished the administrative-review cases, and let the claim proceed without deciding all elements of liability or damages. It awarded no specified damages and did not automatically restore every previously dismissed defendant.

Comparison: the judgment and remedy are the same, and Miner again writes. The lineup differs: Stone-Khoury sits in the seat Mahoney held, and the simulated vote is 3-0 with no dissent. The core holding is the same; the simulated opinion spells out that protected interest, Wolff and Ponte justification, qualified immunity, and damages remain for the district court. This is a changed lineup, not a changed judgment.

Source: Walker v. Bates, 23 F.3d 652, 655-59 (2d Cir. 1994) (No. 93-2107), [public reproduction](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/23/652/482392/).

### [United States v. Johns](../cases/united-states-v-johns/)

Decision date: June 13, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 92-1775  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Published opinion. Sentence vacated and case remanded for resentencing, by a vote of two to one. The mandate has not issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/united-states-v-johns/events/1994-06-13-decision.md](../cases/united-states-v-johns/events/1994-06-13-decision.md)

Historical panel: Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); Joseph M. McLaughlin (Active Circuit Judge); Dennis Jacobs (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); Joseph M. McLaughlin (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Dennis Jacobs.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

The caption also names Jeanette Kellom, Mark Lavalley and Darrell Miller. Lester Johns, Jr., the defendant, is the appellant and appeals only his own sentence. The United States is the appellee.
Appeal from: United States District Court for the District of Vermont, Franklin S. Billings, Jr., District Judge  

#### Decision summary

Lester Johns, Jr. pleaded guilty in September 1992 to a cocaine conspiracy and was sentenced to fifty-one months' imprisonment. The sentence included a two-level increase for obstruction of justice under U.S.S.G. § 3C1.1, and the district court denied him a reduction for acceptance of responsibility under § 3E1.1. The obstruction increase was imposed because of what Johns told the probation officer during the presentence investigation, where he denied some additional narcotics transactions and purchases. For purposes of the appeal, those denials are taken to have been false and material.

The court of appeals vacated the sentence and remanded for resentencing. A majority of the panel, Judges McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury, held that the obstruction increase cannot rest on Johns's unsworn presentence denials. Application Note 1 to § 3C1.1 withholds the increase from a defendant's denial of guilt, other than a sworn denial that constitutes perjury, and that protection limits the general example of false information to a probation officer in Application Note 3(h). The majority also held that the denial of the acceptance reduction cannot stand to the extent that it rested on the obstruction ruling, but that acceptance is a separate question which removal of the increase does not answer in either direction. On remand the district court must decide acceptance of responsibility independently. The court does not direct that the reduction be granted, and does not hold that it must again be denied.

Judge Altimari dissented. He would classify Johns's statements as punishable deception under Application Note 3(h), sustain the obstruction increase, and affirm the sentence.

#### The case and the result

**Decision under review.** Johns appeals the final sentence imposed after his September 1992 plea of guilty to a cocaine conspiracy: fifty-one months' imprisonment, including a two-level increase for obstruction of justice under U.S.S.G. § 3C1.1 and the denial of a reduction for acceptance of responsibility under § 3E1.1. The record before the court does not state the date on which the district court entered the sentence (Not established).

**Material record.** The prosecution identified three sales, and the sentencing inquiry also examined other narcotics dealings. During the presentence investigation Johns denied some additional transactions and purchases. The government had contrary statements and other evidence. The appeal does not depend on declaring the denials true or immaterial; their falsity and materiality are taken as established for the purposes of this appeal. The district court treated the denials as obstruction, denied the acceptance reduction, and imposed sentence. Appellate counsel first sought leave to withdraw under Anders v. California, 386 U.S. 738, 744 (1967). Replacement counsel then presented the guideline question decided here.

**Jurisdiction.** The final sentence is reviewable under 28 U.S.C. § 1291 and 18 U.S.C. § 3742(a)(1) and (2). The notice-of-appeal papers and docket receipts are not part of the record before the court, and the decision does not rest on any contested fact about timeliness.

**Issue 1: the obstruction increase.** Whether a defendant's unsworn presentence denials of his own alleged narcotics transactions, assumed false and material, may support a § 3C1.1 increase notwithstanding the commentary's exclusion of a denial of guilt. Standard of review: de novo, because the question is the meaning of the guideline and its commentary. United States v. Perdomo, 927 F.2d 111, 118 (2d Cir. 1991).

**Issue 2: acceptance of responsibility.** Whether the denial of the § 3E1.1 reduction can stand once the obstruction ruling is set aside, and what the sentencing court must do on remand. Standard of review: the correct reading of the guideline is applied independently, with appropriate deference to supported findings. 18 U.S.C. § 3742(e).

**Dates.** Plea, September 1992. Argument, April 19, 1994. Decision, June 13, 1994.

**Judgment.** The sentence is vacated and the case is remanded for resentencing, by component as follows.

| Component | Disposition | Majority | Dissent |
|---|---|---|---|
| Obstruction increase under § 3C1.1 | Vacated. The increase cannot rest on the presentence denials. | Judges McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury | Judge Altimari, who would sustain the increase |
| Denial of the acceptance reduction under § 3E1.1 | Vacated to the extent it rested on the obstruction ruling, and remanded for an independent determination of acceptance under § 3E1.1. The court does not direct that the reduction be granted or denied. | Judges McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury | Judge Altimari |
| Sentence of fifty-one months | Vacated, with remand for resentencing consistent with the opinion of the court. | Judges McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury | Judge Altimari, who would affirm the sentence |

Vote: The vote on each component is two to one. The two judges in the majority agree in the judgment and in the reasoning stated in the opinion of the court. Judge Altimari disagrees with the judgment and with the court's reasoning on the first issue.

Opinion and joins: Opinion of the court by Judge McLaughlin, joined in full by Judge Stone-Khoury. Dissent by Judge Altimari. No concurrence and no other separate writing. Judge Stone-Khoury joins the opinion of the court in full and issues no separate writing.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

McLAUGHLIN, Circuit Judge, with whom STONE-KHOURY, Circuit Judge, joins:

Lester Johns, Jr. pleaded guilty in the United States District Court for the District of Vermont (Franklin S. Billings, Jr., Judge) to a cocaine conspiracy and was sentenced to fifty-one months' imprisonment. The sentence includes a two-level increase for obstruction of justice under U.S.S.G. § 3C1.1, imposed because of what Johns told the probation officer during the presentence investigation, and the district court denied him a reduction for acceptance of responsibility under § 3E1.1. Johns appeals both rulings. We hold that the obstruction increase cannot rest on the unsworn denials at issue, because Application Note 1 to § 3C1.1 withholds the increase from a defendant's denial of guilt, and that protection limits the general example of false information to a probation officer in Application Note 3(h). We further hold that the denial of the acceptance reduction cannot stand to the extent that it rested on the obstruction ruling, but that acceptance is a separate question which removal of the increase does not answer in either direction. We vacate the sentence and remand for resentencing.

BACKGROUND

Johns pleaded guilty in September 1992. The prosecution identified three sales, and the sentencing inquiry also examined other narcotics dealings. In the presentence investigation Johns denied some additional transactions and purchases. The government had contrary statements and other evidence. Johns does not ask us to find his denials true or immaterial, and the government defends the sentence on the ground that they were materially false. We therefore take the denials to have been false and material, and we ask only whether the Guidelines permit them to be punished as obstruction.

The district court added two levels under § 3C1.1, declined any reduction under § 3E1.1, and imposed fifty-one months. Appellate counsel first sought to withdraw under Anders v. California, 386 U.S. 738, 744 (1967); replacement counsel then presented the guideline question now before us. We have jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1291 and 18 U.S.C. § 3742(a)(1) and (2).

Whether the facts described in a presentence report amount to obstruction of justice under § 3C1.1 is a matter of legal interpretation that we review de novo. United States v. Perdomo, 927 F.2d 111, 118 (2d Cir. 1991). We accept supported findings of fact and give due deference to the district court's application of the Guidelines to the facts, 18 U.S.C. § 3742(e), but a sentence imposed as a result of an incorrect application of the Guidelines must be remanded for further sentencing proceedings with such instructions as we consider appropriate. Id. § 3742(f)(1).

DISCUSSION

I. The obstruction increase

A. The guideline and its commentary

Section 3C1.1 calls for a two-level increase when the defendant willfully obstructs or impedes, or attempts to obstruct or impede, the administration of justice during the investigation, prosecution or sentencing of the offense of conviction. The word "willfully" matters. We have read the guideline to require that the defendant consciously act with the purpose of obstructing justice. United States v. Stroud, 893 F.2d 504, 507 (2d Cir. 1990).

Two notes in the commentary bear on this case. Application Note 3(h) lists, among examples of conduct to which the increase applies, providing materially false information to a probation officer in respect to a presentence or other investigation for the court. We applied the same example, under an earlier numbering, in United States v. Rodriguez, 943 F.2d 215, 217-18 (2d Cir. 1991) (citing Application Note 1(e)). Application Note 1 says something different. A defendant's denial of guilt, other than a denial under oath that constitutes perjury, is not a basis for the increase, and neither is a refusal to admit guilt or to provide information to a probation officer. The note also directs a favorable evaluation of the defendant's statements.

Commentary that interprets or explains a guideline is authoritative unless it violates the Constitution or a federal statute, or is inconsistent with, or a plainly erroneous reading of, that guideline. Stinson v. United States, 508 U.S. 36, 38, 44-45 (1993). No one suggests that either note is inconsistent with § 3C1.1 or plainly erroneous. Both bind us, and the question is how they fit together. We refer to them as Application Notes 1 and 3(h); earlier editions of the Manual numbered the probation-officer example differently, as Rodriguez shows, and the edition governing Johns's sentence is a matter the district court should confirm on remand. We decide no question that would turn on a difference between editions.

B. The government's argument

The government's argument has real force. A defendant may remain silent, but he has no privilege to lie, and we know of no decision of the Supreme Court that supplies a constitutional right to do so. A probation officer depends on accurate information, and a false answer can mislead a presentence investigation and affect the sentence. In Rodriguez a defendant told the probation officer that he had no prior record when he had several prior convictions, and we sustained the increase. We rejected the argument that a lie is immaterial because the truth was likely to be discovered, holding that the definition of a material statement "embraces all false statements that would tend to affect a defendant's sentence." 943 F.2d at 218. On those premises, the government says, Johns's statements fall squarely within Note 3(h).

We accept the premises and reject the conclusion. Johns does not claim that the Constitution entitles him to deny his conduct. His claim is textual: that the Commission, in its commentary, withheld this additional punishment from a defined class of denials. The Commission is free to protect a defendant more broadly than the Constitution requires. The observation that the Constitution does not authorize lying therefore answers a different question from whether the Commission has instructed courts not to treat a particular kind of statement as obstruction. In 1994 the Guidelines bind the sentencing court, 18 U.S.C. § 3553(b), and authoritative commentary binds with the guideline it explains. The fidelity that requires a court to apply an increase when the Guidelines call for it requires the court to withhold it when they do not. Declining to apply Note 1 because its protection exceeds the constitutional minimum would substitute judicial policy for the governing sentencing source.

C. Reading the two notes together

The two notes must be read so that each has work to do. Suppose Note 3(h) controlled whenever a statement to the probation officer was false and material. A defendant interviewed for a presentence report is asked chiefly about his offense. Nearly every false denial of guilt made in that interview can be described as materially false information given to a probation officer, since under Rodriguez a statement is material if it would tend to affect the sentence, and a denial of the conduct being sentenced hardly fails that test. Read that way, Note 1 would protect a denial of guilt only when it was true or immaterial, and a true denial was never in danger of punishment as false information. The exception would disappear in the very setting where it is most likely to matter.

The structure of Note 1 confirms the point. The Commission excluded from the protection one class of false denials, those made under oath that constitute perjury. That qualification would be unnecessary if falsity alone removed the protection. We conclude that Note 3(h) states a general example and Note 1 states a specific qualification, and that where the two meet, the specific governs. An unsworn denial of guilt does not lose that character because it is false, because it is material, or because the defendant states it in a complete sentence rather than uttering the word "no."

This reading leaves Note 3(h) a wide field. A defendant who manufactures documents, falsely implicates an innocent third party, commits perjury, or conceals prior convictions engages in conduct whose obstructive character does not depend solely on a denial of guilt. Rodriguez is an example. The lie there concerned criminal history, a collateral fact bearing on the sentence, not a denial of the offense being sentenced, and nothing we say today disturbs that decision. Our insistence in Stroud that the guideline reach conscious obstruction, 893 F.2d at 507, points the same way, because the Commission has told us that a defendant who merely denies guilt is not, for that reason alone, obstructing justice.

D. Application

Johns's statements were unsworn. As the record describes them, they denied his own alleged narcotics transactions and purchases, the conduct into which the presentence investigation was inquiring. Nothing in the record before us shows that his statements went beyond denial: it describes denials of transactions and purchases, not fabricated documents, accusations against another person, sworn statements, or misstatements about a collateral matter such as criminal history. These were denials of guilt within Note 1. That they are assumed false and material does not take them out of the note. Note 3(h) therefore does not carry the increase, and an increase imposed on this basis rests on an incorrect application of § 3C1.1.

Our colleague would classify the same statements as punishable deception under Note 3(h) and sustain the increase. We agree with him on method: the commentary is authoritative, the notes must be read together, and the question is one of text. We part ways on classification. To treat the statements as deception because they were false and material is to treat the very features that define the false denials Note 1 addresses as the features that strip them of its protection. We do not think the Commission wrote Note 1 to say so little.

E. What we do not decide

The holding is limited to the case before us. We do not decide how § 3C1.1 applies to perjury, fabricated evidence, a false accusation against another person, or deception about criminal history, and we do not hold that any of them is protected as a denial of guilt. We do not hold that every false statement to a probation officer is obstruction, and we do not hold that none is. We do not decide whether the record would support an increase on some basis other than these denials, and nothing in this opinion directs or forbids such a finding; that is a matter for the district court on a record it makes.

II. Acceptance of responsibility

Johns contends that the denial of the § 3E1.1 reduction followed from the rejected obstruction rationale and from the district court's treatment of conduct outside the offense of conviction. The government answers that a guilty plea and the absence of an obstruction increase do not establish genuine acceptance, particularly if the defendant gives false accounts of pertinent conduct. Each position is partly right.

The two adjustments answer different questions. Section 3C1.1 asks whether the defendant willfully impeded the administration of justice. Section 3E1.1 asks whether the defendant has affirmatively accepted responsibility for his offense. A defendant may avoid the first and still fail the second. In United States v. Reyes, 9 F.3d 275 (2d Cir. 1993), the district court rejected an obstruction increase and nonetheless denied the reduction, and we upheld the denial. We explained that the defendant must truthfully admit the conduct comprising the offense of conviction, that the sentencing court may require "a candid and full unraveling" of that offense, and that a guilty plea does not by itself entitle a defendant to the reduction. Id. at 278-79. The absence of punishable obstruction is therefore not affirmative acceptance, and removing the obstruction increase does not compel the reduction.

The converse is equally true. The reduction question is a separate one, and a denial of the reduction cannot rest on an obstruction premise that the Guidelines do not permit. A sentencing court may not satisfy § 3E1.1 by repeating the obstruction analysis. If the district court withheld the reduction because Johns's presentence denials were materially false statements to the probation officer, it relied on the same premise we have just set aside. Whether those denials bear on acceptance in some other way is a question under § 3E1.1 itself, to be decided on its own terms. We cannot tell from the record whether the district court also rested its ruling on a ground independent of the obstruction rationale, and we will not supply one. See United States v. Austin, 17 F.3d 27, 30 (2d Cir. 1994) (vacating where the record furnished no foundation for the denial of the reduction).

The sentencing court must also keep the offense of conviction distinct from other conduct. As we read the commentary in Austin, a defendant is not required "to volunteer, or affirmatively admit, relevant conduct beyond the offense of conviction," and acceptance is measured by the offense of conviction. 17 F.3d at 30-31. Johns denied additional transactions and purchases beyond the three identified sales. The record does not tell us whether those transactions fall within the conspiracy to which he pleaded or only within relevant conduct, or what the edition of § 3E1.1 and its commentary governing his sentence provides about false denials of conduct of either kind. Those questions determine whether and how the denials bear on acceptance, and they depend on findings that we cannot make.

On remand the district court must therefore decide acceptance independently. It must identify what Johns falsely denied, state how that conduct relates to the offense of conviction under the edition of the Guidelines governing his sentence, and identify the § 3E1.1 criterion on which it relies. We do not direct that the reduction be granted, and we do not hold that it must again be denied. The remand should trade the invalid obstruction increase neither for an automatic credit nor for an automatic renewed refusal.

CONCLUSION

The sentence is vacated and the case is remanded for resentencing. On remand the district court shall calculate the sentence without an obstruction increase resting on Johns's presentence denials, leaving intact other sentencing findings that are supported by the record and do not depend on that increase, and shall determine acceptance of responsibility independently in the manner described in Part II. We make no findings of fact, and we express no view on the ultimate acceptance determination or the resulting sentence.

#### Separate opinions

**Dissent of Judge Altimari.** ALTIMARI, Circuit Judge, dissenting. The finalized public decision records this dissent in summary form only. No separate opinion text is set out in the source, and none is written here. There is no concurrence and no other separate writing.

Summary of the dissent: Judge Altimari dissents and would affirm the sentence. His analysis is textual and rests on no constitutional ground. He agrees with the court that the commentary is authoritative, that the question is reviewed de novo, that Application Notes 1 and 3(h) must be read together, and that fabrication, false accusations, perjury and collateral lies lie outside the protection of Note 1. He parts from the court on classification. In his view Johns's presentence statements, being materially false information given to a probation officer, are punishable deception within Note 3(h), not protected denial of guilt, and the Note 1 exception does not displace the example for them. Because he would sustain the obstruction increase, he sees no basis to vacate the denial of the acceptance reduction on the ground that it depended on the obstruction rationale, and he would affirm the fifty-one month sentence in full.

The dissent is not circuit law.

#### What the decision does

##### Controlling holding

Publication and weight: Published. Propositions 1 and 2 below are the holdings of the court on the votes of Judges McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury and are circuit precedent within the stated limits.

**Proposition 1 (obstruction; Part I of the opinion of the court). Judges McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury.**

- Rule: Application Note 1 to § 3C1.1 excludes a defendant's denial of guilt, other than a sworn denial amounting to perjury, from the basis for the increase, and that exclusion limits the example in Application Note 3(h) of materially false information to a probation officer. An unsworn denial of the defendant's own alleged criminal conduct does not lose that character because it is false, because it is material, or because it is stated in a complete sentence.
- Trigger: a § 3C1.1 increase resting on a defendant's presentence statements to the probation officer.
- Test: classify the statement as a denial of guilt, which Note 1 protects, or as conduct whose obstructive character does not depend solely on denial, such as fabricated evidence, a false accusation of another, perjury or concealment of criminal history, which Note 3(h) and the guideline itself may reach.
- Standard and burdens: the classification is a legal question reviewed de novo; the commentary is authoritative under Stinson; supported findings of fact are accepted and the application of the Guidelines to the facts receives due deference under 18 U.S.C. § 3742(e). The decision does not alter the allocation of burdens at sentencing.
- Authority: U.S.S.G. § 3C1.1 and Application Notes 1 and 3(h); Stinson, 508 U.S. at 38, 44-45; Perdomo, 927 F.2d at 118; Rodriguez, 943 F.2d at 217-18 (distinguished, not disturbed); Stroud, 893 F.2d at 507; 18 U.S.C. § 3553(b).
- Application: Johns's unsworn denials of additional transactions and purchases were denials of guilt and cannot support the increase.
- Qualifications: no constitutional or right-to-lie rationale; no holding on perjury, fabricated evidence, false accusation or criminal-history deception; no holding that any or every false statement to a probation officer is or is not obstruction; no bar on a lawful obstruction finding on other conduct.

**Proposition 2 (acceptance of responsibility; Part II). Judges McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury.**

- Rule: Removal of an obstruction increase neither compels nor forecloses the § 3E1.1 reduction. The absence of punishable obstruction is not affirmative acceptance. A denial of the reduction cannot rest on an obstruction rationale the Guidelines do not allow. The sentencing court must separately identify the conduct the defendant denied, state how it relates to the offense of conviction under the edition of the Guidelines governing the sentence, and identify the § 3E1.1 criterion applied.
- Standard and burdens: the correct reading of the guideline is applied independently, with appropriate deference to supported findings. Burdens are unchanged; the opinion decides no question about the quantum of proof.
- Authority: U.S.S.G. § 3E1.1 and its commentary; Reyes, 9 F.3d at 278-79; Austin, 17 F.3d at 30-31; 18 U.S.C. § 3742(e) and (f)(1).
- Application: the denial of the reduction cannot stand to the extent it rested on the obstruction ruling, and the record does not show an independent ground; remand for an independent determination.
- Qualifications: no direction to grant or deny the reduction; no appellate fact-finding; the ultimate acceptance determination and resulting sentence are left to the district court; Austin is applied for the offense-of-conviction distinction, and the question how the commentary treats the denied transactions is not decided.

**Principal contrary argument and answer.** The government's position and the dissent treat the denials as materially false information to a probation officer under Note 3(h), relying on the absence of any right to lie and on Rodriguez. The court answers that no constitutional right is asserted, that the Commission may protect denials beyond the constitutional minimum, that reading Note 3(h) to reach every false and material denial would leave Note 1 without work to do in a presentence interview, and that Rodriguez concerned deception about criminal history, not denial of the offense being sentenced.

**Treatment of prior decisions.** Perdomo is followed on the standard of review. Stinson is applied to the authority of the commentary. Rodriguez is followed on materiality of false statements about criminal history and is distinguished, not overruled, as to denials of guilt. Stroud is applied on the requirement of willful obstructive purpose. Reyes is followed on the separateness of acceptance from obstruction. Austin is applied on the offense-of-conviction distinction for acceptance and is not extended. Anders is mentioned only for the procedural background.
