**What changes.** In this circuit, an unsworn denial of a defendant's own alleged criminal conduct made in a presentence interview is a denial of guilt within Application Note 1 to § 3C1.1 and cannot by itself support an obstruction increase under Note 3(h), whether or not it is false and material. A court may not deny the § 3E1.1 reduction on the obstruction rationale so rejected, and the reduction does not follow automatically from removal of the increase.

**Distinguishing holding from other material.**

- Party-specific relief: vacatur and remand for resentencing in this case.
- Law of the case on remand: the presentence denials cannot support the obstruction increase, and acceptance is to be determined independently.
- Dicta: the statements that perjury, fabricated evidence, false accusations and criminal-history deception are not decided; the observation that the governing edition should be confirmed; the assumption that the denials were false and material, which is a premise and not a finding.
- Separate positions: the dissent only.

**Matters left open.** The Guidelines edition governing the sentence; whether the denied transactions lie within the offense of conviction or only relevant conduct and what follows for acceptance; the district court's basis, if any, for denying the reduction apart from obstruction; whether any other conduct would support an obstruction increase; and the treatment under § 3C1.1 of perjury, fabricated evidence, false accusation of another and criminal-history deception.

##### Effect on the parties

The sentence is vacated and the case is remanded to the District of Vermont for resentencing. The district court shall calculate the sentence without an obstruction increase resting on Johns's presentence denials and shall leave intact other sentencing findings that are supported by the record and do not depend on that increase. It shall determine acceptance of responsibility independently under § 3E1.1, identifying the conduct Johns denied, how it relates to the offense of conviction under the edition of the Guidelines governing his sentence, and the criterion applied. The court does not direct a reduction, does not find facts, and does not decide whether other conduct supports an obstruction increase. See 18 U.S.C. § 3742(f)(1).

##### Mandate and remand

The mandate has not issued. Under Fed. R. App. P. 40(a) and 41(a) as in force on the date of decision, a petition for rehearing in a criminal case may be filed within fourteen days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order or local rule, and the mandate issues twenty-one days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order, a timely petition for rehearing staying the mandate until its disposition unless the court orders otherwise. Any suggestion of rehearing in banc is governed by Fed. R. App. P. 35 and the court's local rules. The opinion date is not the mandate date, and no petition, suggestion or motion is described in the record.

#### Historical comparison

Historically, on 1994-06-13 a Second Circuit panel of Judges Altimari, McLaughlin and Jacobs vacated Johns's 51-month sentence and remanded for resentencing. Judge Jacobs wrote the opinion, joined by Judge McLaughlin; Judge Altimari dissented, treating the statements as punishable deception rather than protected denial. The court held that the denial-of-guilt protection in the obstruction commentary covered the unsworn presentence denials despite their falsity and materiality, and that removing the obstruction basis required renewed, independent consideration of acceptance of responsibility without compelling credit. The simulated judgment is the same: vacatur and remand, a 2 to 1 vote, Altimari dissenting, and the same two holdings. The differences are in the panel and authorship only: Judge Stone-Khoury sat in place of Judge Jacobs, and Judge McLaughlin wrote with Judge Stone-Khoury joining. The judgment and remedy are unchanged, and no change of reasoning was found at the level the historical summary supplies. Source: United States v. Johns, 27 F.3d 31, 34-36 (2d Cir. 1994) (No. 92-1775).

### [City of New York v. United States Department of Commerce](../cases/city-of-new-york-v-department-of-commerce/)

Decision date: August 8, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 93-6183  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Judgment of dismissal vacated and case remanded to the United States District Court for the Eastern District of New York; Judge Timbers dissents; the mandate has not issued  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/city-of-new-york-v-department-of-commerce/events/1994-08-08-decision.md](../cases/city-of-new-york-v-department-of-commerce/events/1994-08-08-decision.md)

Historical panel: William H. Timbers (Senior Circuit Judge); Amalya L. Kearse (Active Circuit Judge); Pierre N. Leval (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: William H. Timbers (Senior Circuit Judge); Amalya L. Kearse (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Pierre N. Leval.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Publication status: Published. Precedential to the extent stated under "What the decision does." No reporter citation is assigned to this opinion.  
Writings: Opinion by Judge Kearse, which is the opinion of the court in the Parts and with the joins stated below; Part III.C is Judge Kearse's own view and is not the opinion of the court. Dissenting opinion by Judge Timbers. Judge Stone files no separate opinion.  

#### Decision summary

##### The dispute

Cities, states, organizations, and individual residents challenged the refusal of the Secretary of Commerce to adjust the 1990 census. Their interests include representation and the distribution of public funds. Wisconsin and Oklahoma intervened as defendants and support the Secretary, emphasizing distributive accuracy and the allocation of seats and funds. The controversy concerns whether the enumeration, supplemented by a post-enumeration survey (PES), yields a better measure of population. It does not concern whether every person could be counted without error.

The Bureau of the Census surveyed approximately 170,000 households, compared the results with the enumeration, and developed adjustment factors for 1,392 population categories. Its estimates indicated an overall undercount of approximately 5.3 million people, substantially higher for several racial and ethnic minorities than for non-Hispanic whites. These are survey estimates in the record, not findings of an independently known exact population. The Secretary acknowledged that adjusted figures would probably improve numerical accuracy nationally and in jurisdictions holding about two-thirds of the population. He nevertheless declined to adjust, stressing distributive accuracy, meaning the proportions assigned to different places, together with uncertainty in local estimates, possible changes in research findings, and the implications for later censuses. His decision was made on July 15, 1991.

##### How the case reached this court

After a thirteen-day bench trial, the United States District Court for the Eastern District of New York (Joseph M. McLaughlin, Circuit Judge, sitting by designation) dismissed the complaint. 822 F. Supp. 906 (E.D.N.Y. 1993). The district court found that adjustment produced a more accurate count for most purposes and that the plaintiffs had mounted a compelling attack on several aspects of the decision. It also found the Secretary's choice and application of his eight guidelines not arbitrary or capricious, regarded a focus on distributive accuracy as compatible with the census's representative function, and stated that disagreement over the better exercise of discretion was not enough to overturn the decision. The plaintiffs appealed and ask for reversal and the use, or further consideration, of adjusted figures for the uses challenged. The federal defendants and the intervenors ask for affirmance.

##### The questions and the result

The appeal presents two questions. The first is whether the district court correctly resolved the constitutional challenge, under Article I, § 2 and the Fourteenth Amendment, § 2, to the refusal to adjust. The second is whether 13 U.S.C. §§ 141(a) and 195 limit or require the use of sampling and adjustment, and what remedy, if any, may lawfully follow.

The court vacates the judgment of dismissal and remands. Judges Kearse and Stone vote to vacate and remand; Judge Timbers would affirm. The vacatur rests on a narrow ground. The district court asked whether the Secretary's decision was arbitrary or capricious, which is a question about administrative rationality, and did not state a constitutional standard and apply it to the reasons the Secretary actually gave. The court does not hold that adjustment was required or that the Secretary's choice was unlawful, and it does not weigh the experts or choose a statistical method. On the statutory question, Judges Kearse and Timbers hold that 13 U.S.C. §§ 141(a) and 195 do not command the Secretary to adopt adjusted figures for any census use; Judge Stone does not join that holding. All three judges agree on the limits of any relief: this court orders no adjustment, reapportionment, or reallocation, and no order directed to the President.

Judgment: Judgment of the district court dismissing the complaint vacated; case remanded to the United States District Court for the Eastern District of New York for further proceedings consistent with this opinion.  
Vote: Judges Kearse and Stone for vacatur and remand; Judge Timbers against, who would affirm and who dissents from the remand.  
Opinion and joins: Opinion by Judge Kearse, which is the opinion of the court in the Parts and with the joins shown in the table below. Part III.C is Judge Kearse's own view alone. Judge Timbers dissents. Judge Stone files no separate opinion; his vote and grounds are stated below.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

Opinion by Judge Kearse, which is the opinion of the court in the Parts and with the joins stated for each Part. Part III.C of Judge Kearse's opinion states her own view and is not part of the opinion of the court; it appears under "Separate writings" below.

##### Part I. Background (Judges Kearse and Stone)

**The decision under review.** This appeal is from a judgment of the United States District Court for the Eastern District of New York (Joseph M. McLaughlin, Circuit Judge, sitting by designation), entered after a thirteen-day bench trial, dismissing the complaint that challenged the Secretary's refusal to adjust the 1990 census. The opinion below is reported at 822 F. Supp. 906 (E.D.N.Y. 1993). The materials before this court do not show the date on which judgment was entered. The Secretary's decision under challenge was made on July 15, 1991.

**The material record.** The plaintiffs are cities, states, organizations, and individual residents; their interests include representation and the distribution of public funds. Wisconsin and Oklahoma intervened as defendants and support the Secretary, emphasizing distributive accuracy and the allocation of seats and funds. The controversy concerns whether the enumeration, supplemented by a post-enumeration survey (PES), yields a better measure of population. It does not concern whether every person could be counted without error. A 1989 stipulation required a fresh consideration of adjustment, the development of review guidelines, a survey capable of producing adjusted figures, and an explained decision. The Bureau of the Census surveyed approximately 170,000 households, compared the results with the enumeration, and developed adjustment factors for 1,392 population categories. Its estimates indicated an overall undercount of approximately 5.3 million people, substantially higher for several racial and ethnic minorities than for non-Hispanic whites. These are survey estimates in the record, not findings of an independently known exact population.

The Secretary acknowledged that adjusted figures would probably improve numerical accuracy nationally and in jurisdictions holding about two-thirds of the population. He nevertheless stressed distributive accuracy, meaning the proportions assigned to different places, together with uncertainty in local estimates, possible changes in research findings, and the implications for later censuses. The advisory panel divided evenly. The Bureau's director favored adjustment; other senior advisers opposed it. The Secretary expressly disclaimed any belief that the survey had been manipulated. The district court found that adjustment produced a more accurate count for most purposes and that the plaintiffs had mounted a compelling attack on several aspects of the decision. It also found the Secretary's choice and application of his eight guidelines not arbitrary or capricious, regarded a focus on distributive accuracy as compatible with the census's representative function, and stated that disagreement over the better exercise of discretion was not enough to overturn the decision. 822 F. Supp. at 916, 920-29.

**Relief sought.** The plaintiffs seek reversal and the use, or further consideration, of adjusted figures for the uses challenged. The federal defendants and the intervenors seek affirmance.

**Jurisdiction.** The judgment is a final judgment reviewable under 28 U.S.C. § 1291; the claims invoke 28 U.S.C. § 1331. The notice of appeal and the entry showing the date of judgment are not among the materials before the court. Jurisdiction is exercised on the premise, which the materials neither confirm nor contradict, that the notice of appeal was timely under Fed. R. App. P. 4(a).

**Issues and standards of review.** The appeal presents two issues.

1. Whether the district court correctly resolved the constitutional challenge, under Article I, § 2 and the Fourteenth Amendment, § 2, to the refusal to adjust. The governing constitutional standard is a question of law reviewed de novo. Findings of fact made after the bench trial are reviewed for clear error under Fed. R. Civ. P. 52(a). Neither standard permits this court to choose the statistical estimator it prefers. The level of deference owed to the Secretary's good-faith census judgment must be identified, not assumed.
2. Whether 13 U.S.C. §§ 141(a) and 195 limit or require the use of sampling and adjustment, and what remedy, if any, may lawfully follow. Statutory construction and jurisdiction are reviewed de novo; equitable relief is available only within an established remedial power.

##### Part II. Jurisdiction, threshold matters, and standards of review (Judges Kearse, Stone, and Timbers)

The final judgment is reviewable under 28 U.S.C. § 1291, and the claims arise under 28 U.S.C. § 1331. The plaintiffs must establish standing for the injuries they assert and for each form of relief they seek. The breadth of the coalition neither creates standing nor turns an individual representational injury into a generalized grievance. The City and State of New York and the other governmental plaintiffs assert concrete injuries to representation and to distributions of public funds that follow from the Secretary's decision. This court has held that the City, the State, and individual voters had standing to challenge census conduct on allegations of reduced federal funds and vote dilution, that such a claim is justiciable, and that it is not committed to agency discretion by law. *Carey v. Klutznick*, 637 F.2d 834 (2d Cir. 1980) (per curiam). A later decision treated that holding as settled. *Carey v. Klutznick*, 653 F.2d 732 (2d Cir. 1981). Following those decisions, the governmental plaintiffs' funding and representation injuries suffice to reach the merits, and the claim is not barred as nonjusticiable. The record before the court does not establish the standing of every other plaintiff for every form of relief; those questions are decided on the record made below, plaintiff by plaintiff and use by use, and are not decided here.

The constitutional claim must be kept apart from any claim under the Administrative Procedure Act. *Franklin v. Massachusetts*, 505 U.S. 788, 800-06 (1992), holds that the President is not an agency within the Act and that the Secretary's apportionment report is not the President's final apportionment decision. A court therefore may not use the Act to direct the President. *Franklin* does not, however, dispose of a constitutional challenge to the responsible officials' census decision, which does not depend on an APA route. Whether the Secretary's decision is final agency action reviewable under the Act as to any census use other than apportionment requires its own analysis; that question is not before the court on this record and is not decided.

The governing constitutional standard is reviewed de novo, and the district court's findings are reviewed for clear error. The Secretary's good-faith technical judgments receive respect, and a court of appeals does not find facts, weigh experts, or select the better estimator. How far administrative-law deference defines the constitutional inquiry is the question addressed in Part III.

##### Part III.A. What the claim does not establish (Judges Kearse, Stone, and Timbers)

Differential undercount among racial and ethnic groups is shown by the Bureau's estimates. That disparity does not by itself establish discriminatory purpose, and unequal effects alone do not call for racial strict scrutiny. *Washington v. Davis*, 426 U.S. 229 (1976); *Village of Arlington Heights v. Metropolitan Housing Development Corp.*, 429 U.S. 252, 265-68 (1977). The Secretary disclaimed any belief that the survey was manipulated, and nothing in the record permits an inference of racial or partisan hostility from the fact that particular States would gain or lose seats. This court makes no such finding. It does not hold that national numerical accuracy is invariably superior to distributive accuracy, or the reverse. It does not direct the use of the PES, adopt the plaintiffs' statistics, or count expert opinions. Mathematical perfection and identical accuracy in every locality are impossible, and the Constitution does not demand them.

##### Part III.B. The constitutional inquiry the district court must make (Judges Kearse and Stone; Judge Timbers dissents)

**Rule.** Where plaintiffs claim that the Secretary's refusal to adjust a census count impaired equal representation, a determination that the refusal was not arbitrary or capricious under administrative-law review does not dispose of the claim. The district court must state the governing constitutional standard and apply it independently to the reasons the Secretary actually gave. At a minimum the Secretary's choice must rest on reasons that are legitimate, genuinely held, supported by the record, and directed to the constitutional task of measuring the population for purposes of representation rather than displacing that task. The standard is a question of law reviewed de novo. The findings the district court has made remain findings and stand unless clearly erroneous.

**Authority.** The Constitution makes population the measure of representation. Article I, § 2 provides for an "actual Enumeration" made "in such Manner as they shall by Law direct," and the Fourteenth Amendment, § 2, apportions Representatives "according to their respective numbers, counting the whole number of persons in each State." Equal representation has been enforced against the drawing of districts. *Wesberry v. Sanders*, 376 U.S. 1 (1964); *Kirkpatrick v. Preisler*, 394 U.S. 526 (1969); *Karcher v. Daggett*, 462 U.S. 725, 730-31, 740 (1983). Those decisions concern the use of accepted population data and do not decide how the federal government must choose between disputed nationwide estimates of the population. This opinion therefore does not carry the burden structure of *Karcher* into the enumeration, and whether and in what form it applies is left open. But *Department of Commerce v. Montana*, 503 U.S. 442, 459-66 (1992), which recognizes both the constitutional constraints on apportionment and its unavoidable compromises, and *Franklin*, which tested a census judgment against the constitutional language and the goal of equal representation, show that a census judgment is measured against the Constitution and not merely against administrative regularity. Neither decision gives the Secretary unreviewable discretion. The arbitrary-and-capricious standard, 5 U.S.C. § 706(2)(A), asks whether an agency acted rationally within the discretion Congress conferred. It does not define what the Constitution requires of a census decision. Nor does *Carey* hold otherwise. It spoke of review for reasonableness in deciding that census methods could be reviewed at all, and it did not decide what the Constitution demands of the Secretary's choice among competing nationwide estimates after a full survey.

**Application.** The district court framed the dispositive question as whether the Secretary's decision was arbitrary or capricious. It found the eight guidelines rationally chosen and applied, regarded the focus on distributive accuracy as compatible with the census's representative function, and treated disagreement over the better exercise of discretion as insufficient. Those conclusions answer a question about administrative rationality. The opinion below does not state a constitutional standard and apply it to the reasons the Secretary actually gave. The same court found that adjustment yields a more accurate count for most purposes and that the plaintiffs mounted a compelling attack on several aspects of the decision. Those findings cannot be set aside as immaterial because the Secretary has discretion, and the opinion does not explain whether, taken with the court's other findings, they show anything more than disagreement about the wiser exercise of discretion once the decision is measured against the representational aim of the census.

**The principal contrary argument.** The Secretary, the intervenors, and the dissent contend that choosing among imperfect methods is a matter of technical judgment, that the advisory panel divided evenly, that the experts disagreed, that numerical and distributive accuracy are different things, and that the district court, after thirteen days of trial, found the guidelines rational. The court accepts that genuine technical uncertainty and the difference between numerical and distributive accuracy are real. It does not hold that adjustment was required or that the Secretary's choice was unlawful, and it does not weigh the experts. The vacatur rests on the absence of the constitutional determination, not on a view that adjustment would have been wiser.

A second objection is that this inquiry only renames stricter administrative review. It does not. Administrative review asks whether the decision is within the bounds of rational agency action. The constitutional inquiry asks whether the choice, as the Secretary actually made it, serves the constitutional task of measuring population for representation. It directs attention to the Secretary's real reasons and does not authorize a court to select or prescribe a statistical method.

**Direction on remand.** The district court should determine whether the demand that adjustment improve accuracy at every geographic level was an actual decision criterion and, if so, whether the record supports it as a technical matter; whether the competing statistical analyses support the stated rationale of distributive accuracy; whether the weight given to speculative risks to future censuses, including the possibility of manipulation that the Secretary did not believe had occurred in this survey, served the present constitutional task rather than displaced it; and whether the court's findings establish more than disagreement over the wisdom of the choice.

**Matters not decided.** The court does not decide how much justification the Constitution requires, or which party bears the burden of persuasion on the constitutional question. Judge Kearse and Judge Stone differ on both points (see Part III.C and the statement of Judge Stone's vote below). The district court should state its findings on each of the matters above so that a reviewing court can apply whatever measure of justification is held to govern.

##### Part III.C. [Judge Kearse's own view; not the opinion of the court]

Part III.C of Judge Kearse's opinion is joined by no other judge. It is set out under "Separate writings" below.

##### Part IV. The statutes (Judges Kearse and Timbers; Judge Stone does not join)

**Rule.** Sections 141(a) and 195 of Title 13 do not command the Secretary to adopt adjusted figures for any census use. The question whether the exception in § 195 forbids the use of adjusted figures for congressional apportionment is not decided.

**Authority and reasoning.** Section 141(a) directs the Secretary to take the decennial census "in such form and content as he may determine, including the use of sampling procedures and special surveys." That language, added in 1976, authorizes sampling and surveys and commits the form and content of the census to the Secretary's determination. It grants authority; it commands nothing. Section 195 provides that, "[e]xcept for the determination of population for purposes of apportionment of Representatives in Congress among the several States, the Secretary shall, if he considers it feasible, authorize the use of the statistical method known as 'sampling' in carrying out the provisions of this title." The direction concerns the authorization of sampling in carrying out the title, and it is conditioned on the Secretary's own consideration of feasibility. For apportionment it is withdrawn. Neither provision requires the Secretary to adopt a particular set of adjusted figures. The 1976 amendments reflect a purpose to permit broader use of sampling, but a broad purpose does not turn a grant of authority into a mandate. The 1989 stipulation required a fresh consideration of adjustment and an explained decision; it could not enlarge the Secretary's statutory authority or this court's remedial power.

**Application and the contrary argument.** The plaintiffs read the apportionment exception as limiting a mandate, not as prohibiting supplementation that § 141(a) permits. The intervenors read it as preserving a special restriction on apportionment. The court need not choose. Even on the plaintiffs' reading, the statutes permit the Secretary to supplement the enumeration; they do not require him to do so. A claim to an order compelling adjustment on a statutory ground therefore fails. The constitutional claim remains for the district court. Judge Stone does not join this Part.

##### Part V. Remand and limits on relief

**Part V.A. Limits on relief (Judges Kearse, Stone, and Timbers).** A defect in the reasoning of a census decision does not by itself authorize a court to reallocate seats or to disregard § 195. Each census use and each form of relief requires its own legal basis. A remedy requires an identified violation, a claimant with a record-proven injury, a lawful defendant, and an administrable correction. *Franklin* forecloses an order against the President under the Administrative Procedure Act. This court may not select adjusted totals or find facts. An adjustment whose effects would shift seats or funds among jurisdictions cannot be shaped without regard to the interests of States and localities that are not before the court. *Carey v. Klutznick*, 653 F.2d 732 (2d Cir. 1981) (other States have interests affected by an adjustment limited to some jurisdictions, and alternatives such as notice to the affected States should be considered). No immediate reapportionment is ordered.

**Part V.B. Remand (Judges Kearse and Stone; Judge Timbers dissents).** The judgment of dismissal is vacated and the case is remanded to the district court. The district court is to decide the constitutional question first, under Part III.B and on its existing findings, which remain findings, and in the manner it considers appropriate. This court directs no new census, no adoption of the plaintiffs' statistics, and no predetermined adjustment. Only if the district court finds a constitutional defect may it reach the questions of standing of other claimants and for particular relief, the proper defendants, the finality of the Secretary's decision as to uses other than apportionment, the application of §§ 141 and 195 to each affected use, and the limits stated in Part V.A. The district court is to address each of those questions before ordering any particular correction.

---

#### Separate writings

##### Part III.C of Judge Kearse's opinion: the measure of justification (hers alone; not the opinion of the court)

Author: Judge Kearse. Joins: none; no other judge joins this Part. Judge Stone does not join it, and Judge Timbers rejects it. It is not the holding of the court and is not circuit law.

Judge Kearse would hold that the Article I, § 2 claim requires a more searching justification than ordinary arbitrary-and-capricious review. Where the Bureau's own estimates, together with the district court's finding that adjustment yields a more accurate count for most purposes, show a known undercount that falls unequally on identifiable groups and places, the government must justify its refusal to correct it as essential to a legitimate governmental objective. A finding that the Secretary's choices were rational is therefore not sufficient.

She would hold that this requirement applies to enumeration, and not only to districting, because the Constitution makes the enumeration the instrument by which equal representation is carried out. If the census figures from which districts are drawn could be left knowingly skewed without a comparable justification, the requirement that districting variances be shown necessary to achieve some legitimate goal, *Karcher*, 462 U.S. at 730-31, would be hollow. *Montana* and *Franklin* allow for the unavoidable compromises of apportionment, but they treat those compromises as bounded by the constitutional task and not as discretion without a constitutional measure.

She would apply the test of legitimate, genuinely held, record-supported reasons that engage the constitutional task at this higher level, and she would send its application to the district court. She would make no appellate finding that the Secretary's reasons fail it, would not choose an estimator, and would not infer racial purpose from disparity.

##### Dissenting opinion of Judge Timbers

Author: Judge Timbers. Dissents from the judgment and from Parts III.B, III.C, and V.B of Judge Kearse's opinion. No other member of the panel is stated to join the dissent.

Judge Timbers would affirm. He agrees that the constitutional standard is a question of law reviewed de novo and that the district court's findings are reviewed for clear error. In his view the Secretary's good-faith technical judgment is entitled to substantial but reviewable deference. Distributive accuracy, uncertainty in the local estimates, and an advisory panel that divided evenly sustain the decision, and the district court's finding, after a thirteen-day trial, that the Secretary's guidelines were rational is not clearly erroneous. A court of appeals may not choose the better estimator.

He would not import into the enumeration the burden of justification that applies to the drawing of districts, and he would not infer racial purpose from the disparity in undercount. He agrees with Part IV that the statutes contain no command to adjust, and he would not decide whether § 195 forbids adjustment for apportionment.

Had he found a defect, he would have remanded to the Secretary rather than allow judicial substitution of figures, and he would have confined any relief to an identified census use, a lawful defendant, and a claimant with proven injury, with no order against the President and with attention to the absent States and localities.

##### Judge Stone's vote (no separate opinion is filed)

Judge Stone files no separate opinion. He joins the judgment and Parts I, II, III.A, III.B, V.A, and V.B of Judge Kearse's opinion. He does not join Part III.C or Part IV.

His vote to vacate rests on the proposition in Part III.B: the constitutional review of the census cannot end with the conclusion that the Secretary's decision survives ordinary administrative review, and the district court must determine whether the choice reflects a good-faith performance of the representative task, respecting genuine technical uncertainty and the difference between numerical and distributive accuracy. He would keep on the plaintiffs the burden of establishing a constitutional defect, would not add a least-restrictive-means requirement or an across-the-board burden of persuasion on the government, and would not treat unequal undercount alone as automatically shifting any burden. For those reasons he does not join Part III.C. He would reserve the construction of §§ 141 and 195 beyond what the remand requires, and for that reason he does not join Part IV.

Judge Stone's stated grounds, given without a separate opinion, are not circuit law.

---

#### What the decision does

- **Controlling holding.** This is a published opinion of a three-judge panel, and a proposition is the holding of the court only if at least two members of the panel join it. The holdings of the court are as follows.
  - Part II (threshold, justiciability, reach of the merits, the line between the constitutional claim and the APA, and the standards of review), joined by all three judges.
  - Part III.A (what the claim does not establish), joined by all three judges.
  - Part III.B, joined by Judges Kearse and Stone: where plaintiffs claim that the Secretary's refusal to adjust a census count impaired equal representation, a determination that the refusal was not arbitrary or capricious under administrative-law review does not dispose of the claim; the district court must state the governing constitutional standard and apply it independently to the reasons the Secretary actually gave; and at a minimum the choice must rest on reasons that are legitimate, genuinely held, supported by the record, and directed to the constitutional task of measuring the population for purposes of representation rather than displacing that task.
  - Part IV, joined by Judges Kearse and Timbers: 13 U.S.C. §§ 141(a) and 195 do not command the Secretary to adopt adjusted figures for any census use. Section 141(a) grants authority and commands nothing, and § 195 directs the authorization of sampling only if the Secretary considers it feasible and withdraws that direction for apportionment. Whether the exception in § 195 forbids the use of adjusted figures for congressional apportionment is not decided.
  - Part V.A (limits on relief), joined by all three judges.
- **The ground for the judgment.** The judgment vacating the dismissal rests on two votes, and the ground they share is the narrow one stated in Part III.B. Where the judges who vote for a judgment rest on different measures of the justification required, the holding is limited to the ground they share. Cf. *Marks v. United States*, 430 U.S. 188 (1977). The stricter formulation in Part III.C has one vote and is not circuit law. The dissent is not circuit law. Judge Stone's stated grounds, given without a separate opinion, are not circuit law. Part I is background and is not a holding.
- **Treatment of precedent.** The court follows *Carey v. Klutznick*, 637 F.2d 834 (2d Cir. 1980) (per curiam), and *Carey v. Klutznick*, 653 F.2d 732 (2d Cir. 1981), on standing, justiciability, and the interests of absent States. It applies *Franklin v. Massachusetts* to the limits on relief against the President. It applies *Washington v. Davis* and *Arlington Heights* to the inference of racial purpose. It treats *Wesberry*, *Kirkpatrick*, and *Karcher* as districting decisions that it does not extend to the enumeration, and it treats *Montana* and *Franklin* as showing that a census judgment is measured against the Constitution. The district court's opinion at 822 F. Supp. 906 states record rulings and is not circuit precedent. The court adopts no change to the Supreme Court's law and does not overrule any decision of this court.
- **Law of the case and party-specific relief.** The holdings of the court bind the district court on remand. The vacatur runs to the parties to this judgment, and nothing in it grants any plaintiff an adjustment or any other form of relief. The district court's existing findings of fact are not disturbed.
- **Matters left open.** The measure of justification required of the Secretary and the allocation of the burden of persuasion on the constitutional claim; whether and in what form the burden structure of *Karcher* applies to the enumeration; whether § 195's exception forbids the use of adjusted figures for congressional apportionment; whether the Secretary's decision is final agency action reviewable under the Administrative Procedure Act as to any use other than apportionment; the standing of plaintiffs other than the governmental plaintiffs and standing for any particular form of relief; and any remedy.
- **Effect on the parties.** The judgment of the district court dismissing the complaint is vacated. On remand the district court is to state and apply the constitutional standard to the Secretary's actual reasons, as described in Part III.B, on its existing findings and in the manner it considers appropriate. It may reach the questions of standing for particular relief, proper defendants, the application of 13 U.S.C. §§ 141(a) and 195 to each affected use, and the limits in Part V.A only if it finds a constitutional defect. This court orders no adjusted figures, no new census, no reapportionment or reallocation, and no relief against the President. No plaintiff is granted an adjustment, and the question whether any relief is warranted remains for the district court.
- **Mandate and remand.** The case is remanded to the United States District Court for the Eastern District of New York for further proceedings consistent with this opinion. The mandate has not issued. Under the rules in force on the decision date, a petition for rehearing may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment unless the court shortens or enlarges the time by order or by local rule, Fed. R. App. P. 40(a), and the mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless the court shortens or enlarges the time by order, Fed. R. App. P. 41(a). If a timely petition for rehearing is filed, the mandate issues 7 days after entry of the order denying the petition, unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order. Fed. R. App. P. 41(a). The period runs from the entry of judgment, and the date of entry is not stated in the materials before the court. The date of this opinion is not treated as the date of entry of judgment or as the mandate date. No petition for rehearing and no other filing is described in the materials before the court.

#### Historical comparison

On 1994-08-08 a Second Circuit panel of Judges Timbers, Kearse and Leval vacated the dismissal of the census challenge and remanded. Judge Kearse wrote the majority opinion, joined by Judge Leval; Judge Timbers dissented and would have affirmed. The historical majority required the government to justify its refusal to correct a known differential undercount as essential to a legitimate governmental objective, rather than relying on ordinary arbitrary-and-capricious review, and left the district court to apply that framework. The simulated judgment is the same: vacatur and remand, 2-1, Kearse writing, Timbers dissenting, with Judge Stone in Judge Leval's seat. The change is in the reasoning. In the simulation only Judges Kearse and Stone hold that arbitrary-and-capricious review does not end the constitutional inquiry and that the district court must apply a constitutional standard to the Secretary's actual reasons. The heightened "essential to a legitimate objective" test is Judge Kearse's alone, so the measure of justification and the burden stay open on remand. Source: City of New York v. U.S. Department of Commerce, 34 F.3d 1114, 1124-33 (2d Cir. 1994).

### [McCostis v. Home Insurance Co. of Indiana](../cases/mccostis-v-home-insurance-of-indiana/)

Decision date: August 8, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 93-9283  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Judgment of the district court vacated and cause remanded (three to none). Published, signed opinion, precedential to the extent stated under "Precedent and legal effect" below. No mandate has issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/mccostis-v-home-insurance-of-indiana/events/1994-08-08-decision.md](../cases/mccostis-v-home-insurance-of-indiana/events/1994-08-08-decision.md)

Historical panel: George C. Pratt (Senior Circuit Judge); John M. Walker, Jr. (Active Circuit Judge); Constance Baker Motley (Senior District Judge, S.D.N.Y., sitting by designation).  
Simulation panel: George C. Pratt (Senior Circuit Judge); John M. Walker, Jr. (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Constance Baker Motley.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Appeal from: United States District Court for the Southern District of New York (Loretta A. Preska, District Judge). Date of entry of the district court's judgment: Not established in the materials before the court.
Publication status: Published, signed opinion. It is the court's decision in this appeal and carries no reporter citation of its own.

#### Decision summary

Richard M. McCostis, a lawyer, appealed from a summary judgment declaring that his professional-liability insurer, Home Insurance Company of Indiana, owes him no duty to defend an action brought against him by Winston & Strawn. The district court had read a policy exclusion for the return or restitution of legal fees, costs and expenses as reaching the whole of that action.

The Court of Appeals vacated the judgment and remanded the cause by a vote of three to none. It held that, as applied to liability measured by sums that others allegedly received, the exclusion is reasonably open to more than one reading; that Home did not show its reading to be the only reasonable one; and that New York law calls for an inquiry into the parties' intended meaning before any construction against the insurer. The court did not declare that Home must defend and did not enter a declaration for either party. It also directed the district court to resolve, before any further proceedings on the merits, whether diversity jurisdiction existed when the action was filed.

##### The case and the result

**The dispute.** McCostis was a law-firm partner and outside general counsel to Barr Laboratories, and he also served Barr as a director and secretary. After Barr became a client of Winston & Strawn, McCostis allegedly took part with Winston partner Lawrence Fox in a scheme that overbilled Barr by $756,251.75. Approximately $335,000 allegedly went to McCostis, and more than $420,000 allegedly went to Fox and his designees. Winston reimbursed Barr and took an assignment of Barr's claims. Winston's twelve-count complaint against McCostis pleads federal racketeering, fraud, breach of fiduciary duty and negligent breach theories and seeks liability extending beyond the sum McCostis allegedly received. These facts are the allegations of the Winston complaint and the record of the coverage action. The overbilling is not an adjudicated fact.

Home's professional-liability policy promises a defense against covered claims even when the allegations are groundless, false or fraudulent. Its definition of damages includes monetary judgments and settlements and excludes fines, statutory penalties, sanctions, and the return or restitution of legal fees, costs and expenses (the fee-return exclusion). Home first undertook McCostis's defense and then withdrew. McCostis then sought declaratory relief.

**How the case reached the court.** The district court entered summary judgment for Home, declaring that Home owes McCostis no duty to defend the Winston action, after denying McCostis's own motion for summary judgment and receiving further submissions. It read the fee-return exclusion as encompassing the underlying action. McCostis asks for a declaration that Home must defend or, alternatively, vacatur and further proceedings. Home asks for affirmance. The declaratory judgment is a final decision appealable under 28 U.S.C. 1291. The district court's own jurisdiction, which rests apparently on diversity of citizenship, is addressed in Part I of the opinion.

**The questions and standards.** (1) Whether federal subject-matter jurisdiction is established, a matter the court must examine on its own. (2) Whether the fee-return exclusion necessarily eliminates any potential duty to defend liabilities exceeding the fees McCostis allegedly received. Review of the summary judgment and of whether the policy is ambiguous is de novo, with the material factual inferences drawn for McCostis as the nonmovant. New York law governs the policy. Home bears the burden as the summary-judgment movant and, as the insurer invoking an exclusion, must show that the complaint falls solely and entirely within it and that it admits of no other reasonable reading.

**Not before the court.** Indemnity, waiver or estoppel arising from Home's initial defense and later withdrawal, and policy terms other than the fee-return exclusion are not part of this appeal.

**Principal contrary argument.** Home contends that all demanded sums derive from improperly collected fees repaid by Winston, so relabeling restitution as racketeering, fiduciary or negligence liability cannot defeat the exclusion. The answer is in Part II.C of the opinion: Home's reading is reasonable but not the only reasonable one, and the potential coverage rests on the text and the alleged allocation of the money, not on labels.

**What the court decided.** The summary judgment declaring that Home owes no duty to defend is vacated, and the cause is remanded for an inquiry into admissible extrinsic evidence of the parties' intended meaning and for application of the New York sequence. The district court is first to resolve whether diversity jurisdiction existed, with leave to amend defective allegations under 28 U.S.C. 1653. No declaration is made for either party now, and there is no ruling on indemnity, allocation, the truth of the allegations, waiver or estoppel.

Judgment: The judgment of the district court is VACATED and the cause is REMANDED.  
Vote: Three to none.  
Opinion and joins: Opinion of the Court by Judge Walker, joined in full by Judge Pratt and Judge Stone-Khoury. No judge dissents or concurs separately.

The judges joined every component of the judgment:

1. Summary judgment declaring that Home owes no duty to defend: vacated. For: Pratt, Walker, Stone-Khoury. Against: none.
2. Remand for an inquiry into admissible extrinsic evidence of the parties' intended meaning and for application of the New York sequence. For: Pratt, Walker, Stone-Khoury. Against: none.
3. Direction that the district court first resolve whether diversity jurisdiction existed, with leave to amend defective allegations under 28 U.S.C. 1653. For: Pratt, Walker, Stone-Khoury. Against: none.
4. No declaration for either party now, and no ruling on indemnity, allocation, the truth of the allegations, waiver or estoppel. For: Pratt, Walker, Stone-Khoury. Against: none.

---

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

WALKER, Circuit Judge, for the court:

Richard M. McCostis, a lawyer, appeals from a summary judgment declaring that his professional-liability insurer, Home Insurance Company of Indiana, owes him no defense against a twelve-count complaint brought by Winston & Strawn. The district court read a policy exclusion for the return or restitution of legal fees, costs and expenses as reaching the whole of that action. We hold that, as applied to liability measured by sums that others allegedly received, the exclusion is reasonably open to more than one reading, that Home did not show its reading to be the only reasonable one, and that New York law calls for an inquiry into the parties' intended meaning before any construction against the insurer. We vacate and remand, and we direct the district court to resolve a threshold question of diversity jurisdiction.

**I. Jurisdiction**

We must satisfy ourselves of the jurisdiction of the district court as well as our own, whether or not the parties contest it. Bender v. Williamsport Area School District, 475 U.S. 534, 541 (1986). The Declaratory Judgment Act, 28 U.S.C. 2201(a), supplies a remedy and not a grant of jurisdiction. Skelly Oil Co. v. Phillips Petroleum Co., 339 U.S. 667, 671-72 (1950). The federal racketeering claims that Winston asserts against McCostis in a separate action do not make his suit against Home, which turns on the meaning of an insurance contract, one arising under federal law. The apparent basis of jurisdiction is diversity of citizenship, 28 U.S.C. 1332(a), which is assessed as of the time the action is filed. Freeport-McMoRan, Inc. v. K N Energy, Inc., 498 U.S. 426, 428 (1991) (per curiam). The materials before us do not set out the citizenship of the parties, and the name of the insurer does not establish it. See 28 U.S.C. 1332(c)(1) (citizenship of a corporation).

Section 1653 allows defective allegations of jurisdiction to be amended in the trial or appellate courts. It reaches, however, only incorrect statements about jurisdiction that actually exists, and not defects in the jurisdictional facts themselves. Newman-Green, Inc. v. Alfonzo-Larrain, 490 U.S. 826, 831 (1989). We will not resolve that distinction on this incomplete account. The judgment must be set aside in any event: on the grounds given in Part II if jurisdiction exists, and for want of jurisdiction if it does not. Before any further proceedings on the merits, the district court shall determine whether the parties were of diverse citizenship when the action was filed and shall permit any defective allegation to be amended under section 1653. If diversity existed, the proceedings go forward as set out below. If it did not, the action must be dismissed. Part II states the governing New York law on the footing that jurisdiction exists.

**II. The fee-return exclusion and the duty to defend**

*A. Governing principles.* New York law governs the policy. Erie R.R. Co. v. Tompkins, 304 U.S. 64, 78 (1938). We review a summary judgment de novo. Summary judgment is proper only if no genuine issue of material fact remains and the movant is entitled to judgment as a matter of law, Fed. R. Civ. P. 56(c), and the nonmovant's evidence is to be believed and all justifiable inferences drawn in his favor. Anderson v. Liberty Lobby, Inc., 477 U.S. 242, 255 (1986). Whether policy language is ambiguous is a question of law that we also decide de novo.

An insurer's duty to defend is broader than its duty to indemnify. Seaboard Surety Co. v. Gillette Co., 64 N.Y.2d 304, 310 (1984). The insurer must defend when the allegations of the complaint, liberally read, fall within the risk insured, however groundless, false or baseless the suit may be. Colon v. Aetna Life & Casualty Insurance Co., 66 N.Y.2d 6, 8-9 (1985). It is relieved of that duty only if there is no possible factual or legal basis on which it might eventually be obligated to indemnify. Villa Charlotte Bronte, Inc. v. Commercial Union Insurance Co., 64 N.Y.2d 846, 848 (1985). An insurer that relies on an exclusion must show that the allegations of the complaint fall solely and entirely within it and that the exclusion is subject to no other reasonable interpretation. Seaboard, 64 N.Y.2d at 311-12. Exclusions are read narrowly, and an ambiguity in the policy is construed against the insurer that drafted it, particularly in an exclusionary clause. Breed v. Insurance Co. of North America, 46 N.Y.2d 351, 353 (1978); Matthews v. American Central Insurance Co., 154 N.Y. 449, 456-57 (1897). None of this permits a court to create coverage where the language is plain. Breed itself enforced the exclusion before it because its words were clear. We do not rewrite what is clear, and we do not treat every contested allegation as an established excluded loss.

*B. The text and the demand.* The exclusion removes from the policy's definition of damages the return or restitution of legal fees, costs and expenses. The question it presents can be put concretely. Does it exclude every compensatory judgment traceable to an improperly billed fee, or only the insured's restoration of the fees and expenses he received? Both answers can be explained from the language.

In ordinary usage, return and restitution describe the giving back of what the person ordered to give it back received or holds. On that reading the exclusion denies coverage when McCostis is made to restore the fees he himself is alleged to have taken, which on Winston's allegations came to about $335,000 of the $756,251.75 overbilled. Winston's demand is not so limited. It seeks liability beyond the sum McCostis allegedly received, including the more than $420,000 that Fox and his designees allegedly kept. A judgment making one participant answer for money another retained does not return anything the first participant holds. It can fairly be described as compensation for a loss, and the policy's definition of damages includes monetary judgments and settlements.

Home's reading is not unreasonable. Winston repaid Barr and sues on Barr's assigned claims to recover improperly collected charges, so every dollar of its demand traces to a fee. On Home's view the exclusion looks to the nature of the money and not to who now holds it, and it would do little work if a lawyer could obtain coverage for the refund of a client's bill by attaching a tort label to the demand. That is a sensible account of what an insurer might intend, but a sensible reading is not necessarily the only one. The exclusion speaks of return and restitution, words naturally read in terms of what the insured received, and it does not say in terms that it reaches every compensatory recovery traceable to a fee. As applied to a demand measured by sums that others allegedly received, it is reasonably susceptible of both readings. That is an ambiguity. It is not a license to insure dishonest profit. It concerns the scope of the insurer's promise to defend against a complaint alleging several kinds of liability, not whether a policyholder may keep, or be indemnified for, what he took.

*C. Home's principal argument.* Home answers that artful pleading cannot create coverage and that recasting a demand for refunded fees as racketeering, fiduciary or negligence liability should not convert fee restitution into insured damages. As a statement of New York law that is correct, because the duty to defend turns on the facts alleged and the words of the contract, and a label alters neither. But the point is not enough to sustain Home's judgment, because the potential for coverage here does not rest on labels. It rests on the language of the exclusion, on the alleged allocation of the money, and on the breadth of the damages demand.

We hold nothing broader. The presence of a fraud, racketeering, fiduciary or negligence count does not by itself create a potential for coverage, and a negligence count does not require a defense if every fact alleged falls wholly within an exclusion correctly construed. Equally, we do not hold that the exclusion can never reach fee restitution pleaded as a tort. The task is to compare the allegations with the contract, not to count causes of action. Nor does the policy's promise to defend allegations that are groundless, false or fraudulent change the analysis. It means that the defense obligation is measured by the allegations, before and apart from any finding of wrongdoing. It does not decide whether public policy or another policy term would bar payment of a particular adjudicated liability, and we do not decide that.

*D. The New York sequence and the summary judgment.* New York prescribes the order in which an ambiguity is addressed. When policy language is susceptible of two reasonable interpretations, the parties may submit extrinsic evidence of what it was meant to convey, and resolving the ambiguity is then for the trier of fact where that evidence presents a genuine dispute. If the extrinsic evidence is conclusory and fails to resolve the ambiguity, the question remains one of law, and the ambiguity is resolved against the insurer that drafted the policy. State v. Home Indemnity Co., 66 N.Y.2d 669, 671-72 (1985). Construction against the drafter is therefore the last step and not the first. Neither side may bypass a genuine inquiry into intended meaning because an immediate legal conclusion would suit it.

Measured against that sequence, the judgment for Home cannot stand. Home had to show that the allegations fall solely and entirely within the exclusion and that the exclusion is subject to no other reasonable interpretation. The district court read the exclusion as reaching the whole action as a matter of law. Because the exclusion is reasonably open to a second reading, that conclusion treated one reasonable reading as the only one. Nothing in the materials before us shows that the parties' intended meaning has been established by admissible evidence free of genuine dispute, and Home cannot fill that gap with its own preferred reading.

For the same reason, we do not direct judgment for McCostis or declare that Home must defend. To construe the exclusion against Home now would skip the step New York requires. We do not decide whether the record contains evidence of intended meaning or what such evidence shows. That is for the district court in the first instance.

**III. Remand and matters left open**

The judgment is vacated and the cause remanded. After resolving the jurisdictional question described in Part I, the district court shall afford the parties an opportunity to offer relevant extrinsic evidence of the intended meaning of the fee-return exclusion, if it is available and admissible. If that evidence establishes the meaning without genuine dispute, the court may decide the defense question accordingly. If it presents a genuine dispute of material fact, the dispute is not to be resolved against McCostis on summary judgment. If the evidence is conclusory or fails to resolve the ambiguity, the exclusion is construed against Home, and the court will then determine, applying the defense promise, whether the complaint potentially falls within coverage.

The remand does not call for findings about McCostis's actual participation in the alleged scheme or about who ultimately retained particular sums. Those matters belong to the underlying litigation unless a distinct coverage issue properly requires them. We leave open: whether the portion of the demand measured by the sums McCostis is alleged to have received is within the exclusion standing alone; indemnity of any adjudicated liability, including any bar arising from public policy; the truth of the allegations; waiver or estoppel arising from Home's initial undertaking of the defense and later withdrawal, which is not before us on this record; the allocation of any judgment or settlement; and the application of the policy's other terms, including its exclusion of fines, statutory penalties and sanctions and whether any component of recovery, such as multiplied damages, falls within it.

*The opinion of the court is by Judge Walker, joined in full by Judge Pratt and Judge Stone-Khoury. There is no concurrence and no dissent.*

---

#### What the decision does

**Controlling holding.** Two propositions control. Both are joined by Judges Pratt, Walker and Stone-Khoury.

1. An insurer may refuse to defend on the strength of an exclusion only if the complaint falls solely and entirely within it and the exclusion is subject to no other reasonable interpretation (Seaboard; Colon; Villa Charlotte Bronte). The test compares the allegations with the contract language, not the causes of action pleaded. The burden is Home's, as insurer and as summary-judgment movant, and review is de novo. Applied here, the exclusion is reasonably open to a reading limited to the insured's own restoration of fees, so Home did not carry its burden as to a demand measured by sums others received. The ambiguity rests on the text and on the sums allegedly received by others, not on tort labels, and the exclusion is not held categorically inapplicable to fee restitution pleaded as a tort.
2. The New York sequence is text, then extrinsic evidence of intended meaning, then construction against the insurer only if that evidence fails to resolve the ambiguity (State v. Home Indemnity; Matthews; Breed). The judgment for Home is therefore vacated, and none is directed for McCostis.

**Precedent and legal effect.** This is a published opinion of a three-judge panel, joined by all three judges on each proposition above. It is precedential in this circuit on those propositions, limited to the described record: the exclusion's ambiguity as applied to alleged compensatory liability for amounts received by other participants, and the New York sequence governing the defense question. It follows Colon, Villa Charlotte Bronte, Seaboard, Breed, Matthews and State v. Home Indemnity as New York law and overrules or displaces no prior decision of this court. It is a federal court's application of New York law and binds no New York court. The portion of the demand measured by McCostis's own alleged receipts, indemnity, waiver and estoppel, and the other policy terms identified in Part III are not decided, and statements about them are not holdings. The jurisdictional direction in Part I applies settled law and is party-specific relief. The rule of Part II is a ruling for the circuit on the footing that diversity jurisdiction exists; if the district court finds it lacking, the judgment is vacated for want of jurisdiction and Part II supplies no rule. The vacatur and remand bind the parties, and the ambiguity ruling and the order of inquiry are law of the case on remand. There is no separate writing.

**Effect on the parties.** The judgment of the district court is vacated and the cause remanded for the proceedings described in Parts I and III of the opinion. The scope of the remand is limited to the threshold diversity determination, the extrinsic-evidence inquiry and the application of the New York sequence to the defense question. The district court is not directed to make findings on McCostis's participation in the alleged scheme or on the retention of particular sums, and it is not directed to enter a declaration for either party. No declaration is made for McCostis or for Home now, and indemnity, allocation, the truth of the allegations, waiver and estoppel remain unresolved.

**Mandate and remand.** No mandate has issued with this opinion, and the date of this opinion is not the mandate date. Under Fed. R. App. P. 40(a) as in force on this date, a petition for rehearing may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment unless that time is shortened or enlarged by order or local rule. Under Fed. R. App. P. 41(a) as in force on this date, the mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless that time is shortened or enlarged by order, and a timely petition for rehearing stays it pending disposition. The district court acts on remand once the mandate issues. This decision presupposes no filing under either rule.

#### Historical comparison

On 1994-08-08 the Second Circuit vacated the summary judgment for the insurer and remanded. McCostis v. Home Insurance Co. of Indiana, No. 93-9283, 31 F.3d 110, 112-13 (2d Cir. 1994). The panel was Pratt, Walker and Constance Baker Motley. Walker wrote, joined by Pratt, and Motley dissented briefly and would have affirmed for the district court's reasons. The court found the fee-return exclusion open to more than one reasonable reading where the demand exceeded what McCostis personally received, and required consideration of extrinsic evidence of intended meaning, then construction against the insurer if that evidence did not resolve it. The simulated judgment is unchanged: vacatur and remand, Walker writing. The lineup differs: Stone-Khoury takes Motley's seat, so the vote is 3-0 with no dissent. The reasoning follows the historical ground. The remedy adds a threshold direction to determine diversity jurisdiction, with leave to amend under 28 U.S.C. 1653, which the historical summary does not record. Source: 31 F.3d 110 (public reproduction, law.justia.com).

### [Pro-Choice Network of Western New York v. Schenck](../cases/pro-choice-network-v-schenck/)

Decision date: September 6, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): Nos. 92-7302, 93-7918, considered together  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded. Published opinion. No mandate has issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/pro-choice-network-v-schenck/events/1994-09-06-decision.md](../cases/pro-choice-network-v-schenck/events/1994-09-06-decision.md)

Historical panel: James L. Oakes (Senior Circuit Judge); Thomas J. Meskill (Senior Circuit Judge); Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: James L. Oakes (Senior Circuit Judge); Thomas J. Meskill (Senior Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Frank X. Altimari.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

#### Decision summary

Anti-abortion demonstrators and the organizations associated with them appealed from a preliminary injunction that protects access to medical clinics in western New York, and from the district court's later refusal to dissolve it. The Second Circuit affirmed some parts of the injunction, vacated others, and sent the case back to the district court.

The court first held that the district court could keep the New York state-law claims, and could leave the injunction in place as a whole, after the federal claim was dismissed. The court then tested the injunction term by term under Madsen v. Women's Health Center, Inc., 512 U.S. 753 (1994), which requires a content-neutral injunction that affects speech to burden no more speech than necessary to serve a significant governmental interest.

Applying that test, the court:

- vacated the fifteen-foot zones that move with each person or vehicle seeking access to a clinic, because the findings did not tie that burden on peaceful speech to the access problem;
- vacated the fifteen-foot zones fixed at entrances and driveways, and remanded for differentiated, site-specific necessity findings, while holding that a fixed zone is not categorically invalid;
- vacated the command that sidewalk counselors stop counseling when an approached person declines, because it silenced peaceful speech that obstructs no one;
- affirmed the noise term as an objective limit on noise that endangers patients' health, and directed the district court to conform its wording to Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 65(d); and
- affirmed the terms against obstruction, trespass, crowding, pushing and threats.

Judge Meskill wrote the opinion of the court. Judge Stone-Khoury joined it in full. Judge Oakes joined all of it except Part IV.B and the fixed-zone instruction in Part VII, and dissented in part on that single point: he would have affirmed the fixed zones. The vacatur takes effect only when the mandate issues, and no mandate has issued.

#### The judgment by component

The judgment is affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded. The votes differ by component.

| No. | Component | Disposition | Vote | Judges |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Retention of the pendent state claims and refusal to dissolve the injunction as a whole | Affirmed | 3-0 | Oakes, Meskill, Stone-Khoury |
| 2 | Moving fifteen-foot zones (around persons and vehicles seeking access) | Vacated and remanded | 3-0 | Oakes, Meskill, Stone-Khoury |
| 3 | Fixed fifteen-foot zones (around entrances and driveways) | Vacated and remanded for differentiated necessity findings under Madsen | 2-1 | Meskill and Stone-Khoury for the ruling; Oakes dissenting |
| 4 | Command that sidewalk counselors cease when an approached person declines | Vacated as written | 3-0 | Oakes, Meskill, Stone-Khoury |
| 5 | Noise term | Affirmed as an objective limit on noise that endangers patients' health, with remand to conform its wording to Rule 65(d) | 3-0 | Oakes, Meskill, Stone-Khoury |
| 6 | Obstruction, trespass and related physical-interference terms (including terms against crowding, pushing and threats, as the decree states them) | Affirmed | 3-0 | Oakes, Meskill, Stone-Khoury |
| 7 | Two-counselor allowance | No independent ruling; the district court is to conform it on remand to the rulings in components 2, 3 and 4 | 3-0 as to components 2 and 4; 2-1 as to component 3 | Oakes, Meskill and Stone-Khoury as to components 2 and 4; Meskill and Stone-Khoury as to component 3, Oakes dissenting |

#### Writings and joins

- Opinion of the court, by Judge Meskill. Parts I, II, III, IV.A, V and VI, and Part VII except its fixed-zone instruction: Judges Meskill, Oakes and Stone-Khoury. Part IV.B and the fixed-zone instruction in Part VII: Judges Meskill and Stone-Khoury.
- Opinion dissenting in part, by Judge Oakes, from Part IV.B and the fixed-zone instruction in Part VII. No judge joins it.
- No concurrence and no other separate writing.

#### The case and the result

**Decisions under review.** The defendants, individuals and organizations associated with anti-abortion demonstrations in western New York, appeal from the preliminary injunction reported at 799 F. Supp. 1417 (W.D.N.Y. 1992) and from the later refusal to vacate it, reported at 828 F. Supp. 1018 (W.D.N.Y. 1993). They ask for reversal of the injunction and its dissolution. The clinics and physicians ask that the injunction and its challenged restrictions be affirmed. The orders are appealable under 28 U.S.C. 1292(a)(1). The separate entry dates of the orders and the timeliness of the notices of appeal are not before the panel for decision.

**Material record.** The plaintiffs sued on September 24, 1990, pleading a conspiracy under 42 U.S.C. 1985(3) and state-law claims including trespass and claims under New York's civil-rights law. The suit concerned obstruction and harassment affecting access to medical facilities, together with advocacy on public sidewalks that is protected speech. An initial restraining order forbade obstruction of clinic access. A planned demonstration then went forward without the blockade that had been anticipated, and the order continued by agreement. The later record nonetheless showed crowding, physical interference, pushing, harassment, and contempt proceedings against several defendants. The parties disputed what that record means. The plaintiffs say it shows that ordinary prohibitions had failed. The defendants point to occasions on which the restraining order did protect access and say the record does not justify excluding peaceful speakers.

The preliminary injunction forbade obstruction and trespass, regulated health-endangering noise, and created fifteen-foot zones around entrances and driveways and around persons and vehicles seeking access. It permitted two sidewalk counselors in specified areas but required them to stop counseling when an approached person declined. The decree thus combined fixed zones, zones that move with persons and vehicles, and a command triggered by a listener's refusal.

After the Supreme Court decided Bray v. Alexandria Women's Health Clinic, 506 U.S. 263 (1993), on January 13, 1993, the district court dismissed the federal claim with leave to amend, retained the pendent state claims, and declined to dissolve the injunction. Whether the federal claim was later amended is not before the panel.

**Issues and standards of review.**

1. Whether the district court could and should retain the pendent state claims, and refuse to dissolve the injunction, after the federal claim was dismissed. The federal legal premise is reviewed independently; retention is reviewed for abuse of discretion under United Mine Workers v. Gibbs, 383 U.S. 715 (1966), and Carnegie-Mellon University v. Cohill, 484 U.S. 343 (1988).
2. Whether the fixed and the moving fifteen-foot restrictions are lawful. The legal burden is reviewed independently; findings of fact for clear error; equitable choices for abuse of discretion within the correct legal standard.
3. Whether the cease-on-refusal command, the noise term and the physical-access commands are lawful. Review is independent as to the First Amendment and Fed. R. Civ. P. 65(d), with clear-error review of findings and abuse-of-discretion review of remedial choices.

**Preservation.** Abstention and state-law remedial-authority arguments that were not properly raised in the proceedings under review cannot become grounds for reversal because the federal claim changed. The standard of review for each term is applied term by term.

**Result.** The court affirmed the retention of the pendent claims and the refusal to dissolve the injunction as a whole; vacated the moving zones, the fixed zones as presently justified, and the cease-on-refusal command; affirmed the noise term subject to conformity with Rule 65(d); and affirmed the obstruction, trespass and physical-interference terms. The case is remanded for modification of the decree and for the findings the opinion requires. The components and votes are set out in the table above.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

Meskill, Senior Circuit Judge, delivered the opinion of the court, in which Oakes, Senior Circuit Judge, and Stone-Khoury, Circuit Judge, join, except that Judge Oakes does not join Part IV.B or the fixed-zone instruction in Part VII.

##### I. Scope

The defendants oppose abortion and demonstrate outside clinics. They challenge an injunction protecting access to those clinics and the district court's refusal to dissolve it after the federal claim fell away. Two questions are presented: whether the federal court could keep the state-law case, and whether each of the decree's distinct speech and access terms survives review under Madsen v. Women's Health Center, Inc., 512 U.S. 753 (1994).

Neither the clinics' lawful medical work nor the defendants' religious and political advocacy is optional in the analysis. New York law protects against trespass and interference, the First Amendment protects peaceful communication in traditional public forums, and no special doctrine gives either side an automatic advantage. Madsen supplies the test. We apply it term by term, because a defect in one speech restriction does not defeat supported access and safety relief, and retaining jurisdiction does not validate every term.

##### II. Pendent jurisdiction

A federal court has power over state-law claims that share a common nucleus of operative fact with a substantial federal claim. Gibbs, 383 U.S. at 725-27. Power is measured at the outset. A federal claim fails to support it only if it is so insubstantial that "its unsoundness so clearly results from the previous decisions of this court as to foreclose the subject." Hagans v. Lavine, 415 U.S. 528, 536-37 (1974). Once power exists, the later loss of the federal claim does not remove it.

Whether to keep the pendent claims is then a matter of discretion guided by judicial economy, convenience, fairness and comity. Cohill, 484 U.S. at 350 & n.7. Gibbs's observation that state claims should ordinarily be dismissed when federal claims are dismissed before trial describes the usual result when those factors favor declining jurisdiction. It is not a mandatory rule to be applied inflexibly. Id. We review the federal premise independently and the decision to retain for abuse of discretion. The action was commenced before 28 U.S.C. 1367 applied to newly filed actions, so that statute is not the source of the court's power, and we do not rely on it.

The complaint pleaded a substantial federal claim under 42 U.S.C. 1985(3) when it was filed. Bray later rejected the two theories on which such a claim depended, that opposition to abortion supplies the class-based animus the statute requires and that incidental interference with interstate travel suffices. A holding that a claim fails on the merits is not a holding that prior decisions had foreclosed it when it was brought. Bray therefore removed no power. It bore only on discretion, and discretion was not abused.

The trespass and civil-rights claims arise from the same obstruction and harassment at the same facilities as the federal claim. By the time Bray was decided the district court had entered a restraining order, conducted the proceedings that produced the preliminary injunction, and handled contempt proceedings. The access dispute continued, and the state claims reach it. Judicial economy and fairness favored completing a developed case over starting it again elsewhere. Arguments based on abstention or on the limits of New York's remedial authority were not properly raised in the proceedings under review and cannot supply a ground for reversal now; any such question that was properly preserved remains open on remand.

The defendants' principal argument is that once Bray eliminated the federal theory the court should have relinquished the state controversy and dissolved the injunction. That describes one permissible exercise of discretion, not a command. Dissolving the whole decree would also have stripped away protections that are independently lawful, as Parts V and VI explain. We affirm the retention of the pendent claims and the refusal to dissolve the injunction as a whole. We do not decide whether the dismissed federal claim was or may be repleaded, and we decide no question arising under federal legislation enacted after the injunction issued.

##### III. The governing standard

An injunction that is content-neutral and that affects speech must burden no more speech than necessary to serve a significant governmental interest. Madsen, 512 U.S. at 764-66. That test is more demanding than ordinary time, place and manner review, but it is not strict scrutiny, and the fact that a decree binds defendants who oppose abortion does not make it viewpoint based.
