The question is whether each term is justified by what these defendants did, not by what they say.

The interests in unobstructed access, public safety and safe medical treatment are significant. The First Amendment does not protect trespass, physical obstruction, assault or threats. On the other side, a listener's objection to a message is not a censor's authority, and offense at political expression is not a general ground for suppressing it. Boos v. Barry, 485 U.S. 312 (1988). Those who defend a term that burdens speech must show, through findings, that it is needed. A prior violation can support necessity, but it is not a prerequisite to every preventive restriction, and a court need not wait for a completed injury before protecting access. Rule 65(d) separately requires that an injunction be specific in its terms and describe in reasonable detail the acts restrained.

##### IV. The fifteen-foot restrictions

###### A. The moving zones (Judges Meskill, Oakes and Stone-Khoury)

A zone that travels with every person or vehicle seeking access forbids speakers to stand or walk in public space near that person wherever the person goes. It burdens peaceful communication even when no doorway is blocked and no one is followed. Under Madsen it stands only if the findings connect that burden to the access problem. That some defendants obstructed patients on other occasions is not enough. A decree may reach persons acting in concert with wrongdoers, Fed. R. Civ. P. 65(d), but association does not erase the requirement that the prohibited conduct be adequately connected to the violation.

The findings describe crowding, physical interference, pushing, harassment and contempt. They justify firm conduct-based protection. They do not explain why access could be secured only by forbidding a counselor to be within fifteen feet of a moving person who is not blocked, followed or encircled. A counselor who is simply standing or walking may be forced away as the protected person moves. On this record the actual burden is insufficiently tied to access.

The plaintiffs answer that a prohibition on obstruction is hard to enforce once a crowd has surrounded a patient and that earlier violations show ordinary prohibitions failed. Difficulty of enforcement can justify a preventive measure, but only one that fits the difficulty. Madsen sustained a zone fixed at a clinic's entrances and driveway on findings that the protesters had interfered with access despite an earlier, narrower injunction. It did not sustain an exclusion that moves with each person. Nor do we treat the occasions on which the restraining order secured access as proof that conduct-based terms would suffice. Our holding rests on the absence of findings tying the moving exclusion to access.

We do not hold that every fifteen-foot zone is invalid. The moving zones are vacated. On specific findings the district court may adopt narrower protections defined by conduct, such as bars on blocking, encircling or following persons seeking access, and if the findings show those terms have failed it may consider what further restriction Madsen permits.

###### B. The fixed zones (Judges Meskill and Stone-Khoury)

A zone fixed at doorways and vehicle entrances is not invalid in principle, and its validity does not depend on a completed blockade. Entrances and driveways can require preventive clearance, and a peaceful demonstration under the earlier order does not disprove every need for it. But a fixed zone is a distinct burden and needs its own necessity findings: why excluding speakers from that area is needed to keep entrances and approaches open when the decree's obstruction and trespass commands remain in force, whether passage remains genuinely possible, and why the chosen dimension is necessary. A numerical limit does not establish tailoring.

As the findings stand, the same rationale supports the fixed and the moving zones alike. They do not separately connect a fifteen-foot exclusion at these entrances and driveways to the actual layout of those places or explain why the surviving obstruction and trespass terms cannot keep them open. We do not reject any found fact, and we respect clear-error review. We hold that the findings are legally insufficient to show that the fixed zones burden no more speech than necessary. A less restrictive alternative is not dispositive by itself; it is part of the necessity showing Madsen requires.

The plaintiffs and the dissent rely on Madsen's sustaining of a thirty-six-foot zone at a clinic's entrances and driveway, and observe that these zones are narrower. Madsen sustained its zone on findings that interference with access had continued despite an earlier injunction, and it set aside a buffer where the record showed no interference from that location. Madsen thus confirms that an entrance zone can be justified. It does not hold that any zone smaller than thirty-six feet is justified, and a comparison of numbers is no substitute for findings about this site.

We hold that no fixed zone is categorically barred, we announce no footage rule, and we do not require the district court to await another blockade. We decide nothing about whether fifteen feet, or any other dimension, could be sustained on supported findings. The fixed zones are vacated without prejudice to a renewed fixed access zone, separately justified by differentiated, site-specific findings under Madsen.

##### V. The cease-on-refusal command (Judges Meskill, Oakes and Stone-Khoury)

A person approached on a public sidewalk may decline to listen and go on freely. A decree may secure that freedom, and the freedom to leave, refuse literature and obtain treatment, by forbidding blocking, coercive following and harassment, each defined through objective conduct. It may not make a listener's refusal alone sufficient to silence otherwise peaceful advocacy in a public forum. Madsen invalidated a provision that barred approaching persons seeking clinic services unless they indicated a desire to communicate, because it burdened more speech than necessary to prevent intimidation and to ensure access. Madsen, Part III-D. Boos forecloses treating a listener's displeasure with a message as a general ground for suppression.

The command here attached whenever an approached person declined, whether or not the counselor blocked, followed, threatened or otherwise interfered. The interests in free passage and freedom from intimidation can be served by objective conduct terms, and the decree retains such terms. The command added a prohibition on peaceful speech that obstructs no one. The initial permission to counsel does not cure an unjustified prohibition on all further counseling after a refusal. We rest the ruling on Madsen's necessity requirement and on the absence of any objective conduct limit. We do not rest it on any captive-audience theory or on the emotive impact of the message.

The plaintiffs answer that patients must be able to refuse contact and obtain treatment without harassment. They must, and the surviving terms secure that freedom. The command is vacated as written. We reserve a properly supported restriction on objectively coercive following or harassment, and particular enforcement questions not presented.

##### VI. The noise and physical-access terms (Judges Meskill, Oakes and Stone-Khoury)

**Noise.** The First Amendment does not require patients at a medical facility to make Herculean efforts to escape the din of protest, and Madsen sustained limits on noise at a clinic. Madsen, Part III-B. As described, the decree regulates noise that endangers patients' health. Read that way, it is an objective prohibition keyed to the effect of sound on patients, not to the message or the speaker's viewpoint, and it does not require a protester to know any patient's private condition. Enforcement may not turn on whether clinic occupants dislike the speaker.

The defendants argue that health language is unclear and invites viewpoint-based enforcement. Rule 65(d) and the First Amendment answer that concern by requiring definite terms, and the remedy is conformity, not vacatur. We affirm the term so read and direct that on remand its wording be conformed to Rule 65(d), stating in reasonable detail, in objective terms, what noise is prohibited.

**Physical access.** The prohibitions on obstruction and trespass, and the decree's terms against crowding, pushing and threats directed at persons seeking to enter or leave, as the decree states them, regulate conduct that New York law forbids and the First Amendment does not protect. The record of crowding, physical interference, pushing, harassment and contempt supports them. They operate independently of the zones and the cease-on-refusal command. The defendants' challenge to them fails, and they remain in force.

##### VII. Disposition and remand (Judges Meskill, Oakes and Stone-Khoury, except the fixed-zone instruction, which Judges Meskill and Stone-Khoury join)

The retention of the pendent claims and the refusal to dissolve the injunction are affirmed. The moving zones and the cease-on-refusal command are vacated. The noise term is affirmed and remanded for conformity with Rule 65(d). The obstruction, trespass and physical-interference terms are affirmed. The two-counselor allowance, which operated together with the zones and the cease-on-refusal command, is to be conformed on remand to those rulings; we decide nothing about the independent validity of any limit on the number of counselors. The fixed zones are vacated and remanded for the differentiated findings described in Part IV.B. The case is remanded for modification of the decree and for the findings this opinion requires, with the district court to proceed as it deems appropriate.

#### Separate opinions

##### Opinion dissenting in part (Oakes, Senior Circuit Judge), not joined by any judge

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision. The final record states this partial dissent in summary form, and it is reported here as the record states it.*

Judge Oakes dissents from Part IV.B and from the fixed-zone instruction in Part VII. No judge joins his opinion.

Judge Oakes agrees with the court on retention of the pendent claims, on vacating the moving zones and the cease-on-refusal command, on the noise term and on the obstruction, trespass and physical-interference terms. He would conform the two-counselor allowance to the moving-zone and cease-on-refusal rulings. He dissents from vacating the fixed entrance and driveway zones and from the instruction to reconsider them.

In his view the fixed zones satisfy Madsen. They are narrower than the thirty-six-foot zone sustained around a clinic's entrances and driveway there, and they rest on the district court's findings about the obstruction, crowding and interference the defendants' conduct produced. Those findings are reviewed for clear error, and the majority does not find them clearly erroneous. He would not affirm on the bare reasonableness of a number. He would affirm on the findings, on the importance of protecting patients' access, and on the district court's remedial judgment. A limited remand for site-specific findings would be appropriate only if the findings were shown to be undifferentiated, and nothing before the court shows that. He would not apply strict scrutiny, and he would not invalidate fifteen-foot zones as a class.

#### What the decision does

**Controlling holding.** The opinion is published. It is precedential in this circuit to the extent of propositions joined by at least two judges of the panel.

- Joined by all three judges (Parts II, III, IV.A, V and VI, and the rest of Part VII): after Bray, a federal court that had power over pendent state claims at the outset may retain them in its discretion under Gibbs and Cohill, and Bray's rejection of the federal theory does not by itself remove that power; 28 U.S.C. 1367 is not the source of power in an action commenced before it applied. An injunction affecting speech at a clinic is tested under Madsen term by term. A zone that moves with each person or vehicle seeking access is not shown necessary by a record of prior obstruction by some defendants, absent findings connecting the actual burden to access. A command that ends peaceful advocacy solely because a listener has declined it burdens more speech than necessary. An objective limit on noise that endangers patients' health may stand if its wording conforms to Rule 65(d), and the decree's obstruction, trespass and physical-interference terms stand.
- Joined by Judges Meskill and Stone-Khoury (Part IV.B and the fixed-zone instruction in Part VII): a fixed zone at entrances and driveways is not categorically invalid and needs no completed blockade, but it requires its own differentiated necessity findings; on the findings in this case the fifteen-foot fixed zones are legally insufficient under Madsen. This is a majority ruling of the panel on two joins. Judge Oakes's partial dissent is not circuit law.

**Prior decisions.** Gibbs, Cohill, Hagans, Bray, Madsen and Boos are followed and applied. No decision of this court is overruled or displaced, and no earlier decision of this court on these issues is applied. The panel exercises no power to depart from any decision of the Supreme Court.

**Limits.** The holdings rest on the decree and findings as described. They set no footage rule, announce no categorical rule for or against buffer zones, and decide no question about the dismissed federal claim or its amendment, preserved state-law objections, claims under legislation enacted after the injunction, the independent validity of a limit on the number of counselors, or the timeliness of the appeals.

**Effect on the parties.** The judgment is affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded. The affirmed provisions (obstruction, trespass, physical interference, and the noise term as to be conformed) remain in force. The state-law case continues in the district court. The vacatur, the remand instructions and the modification of the decree bind the parties to this litigation. On remand the district court is bound by the rulings that the moving zones, the fixed zones as presently justified and the cease-on-refusal command are vacated and by the standards stated here. Statements describing what the district court may consider on remand are guidance on the scope of the remand and are not independent holdings.

**Scope of remand.**

1. Modify the decree to delete the moving zones and the cease-on-refusal command.
2. Conform the two-counselor allowance to those rulings and to the fixed-zone ruling.
3. Conform the noise term to Rule 65(d).
4. Reconsider any fixed access zone only on differentiated, site-specific findings applying Madsen, which may include a narrower or differently placed zone.
5. Adopt, on specific findings, any narrower protection defined by objective conduct, such as bars on blocking, encircling or coercive following.

**Mandate status.** No mandate has issued. Under Fed. R. App. P. 40(a) as in force on this date, a petition for rehearing may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order or by local rule. Under Fed. R. App. P. 41(a) as in force on this date, the mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order, and a timely petition for rehearing delays issuance as Rule 41(a) provides. No order shortening or enlarging either time has been entered, and no post-decision filing is before the court. The vacatur takes effect on issuance of the mandate.

#### Historical comparison

On 1994-09-06 the historical panel (Oakes, Meskill, Altimari) affirmed in part and reversed in part the preliminary injunction, affirmed the refusal to dissolve it wholesale after the federal claim was dismissed, and kept the developed pendent state claims after Bray. Applying Madsen provision by provision, it rejected the fifteen-foot restrictions and the requirement that sidewalk counselors cease on a listener's demand, and sustained the access, trespass and noise protections. Meskill wrote, joined by Altimari; Oakes dissented from invalidating the protective provisions.

The simulated judgment points the same way on every component, so the judgment is unchanged in substance. Reasoning and remedy differ on the fixed zones: the simulated court vacated them and remanded for site-specific findings, holding no fixed zone categorically invalid. The lineup differs: Stone-Khoury replaces Altimari, and Oakes joins the court except on the fixed zones. Meskill again writes the opinion.

Source: Pro-Choice Network of W.N.Y. v. Schenck, Nos. 92-7302, 93-7918, 67 F.3d 359, 367-77 (2d Cir. 1994).

### [Pagnucco v. Pan American World Airways, Inc.](../cases/pagnucco-v-pan-american-world-airways/)

Decision date: September 12, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): Nos. 92-9251, 92-9253, 92-9255  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Published opinion of the court, with a partial dissent. Judgments vacated in part and remanded; not otherwise disturbed. No mandate has issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/pagnucco-v-pan-american-world-airways/events/1994-09-12-decision.md](../cases/pagnucco-v-pan-american-world-airways/events/1994-09-12-decision.md)

Historical panel: Ellsworth A. Van Graafeiland (Senior Circuit Judge); Richard J. Cardamone (Senior Circuit Judge); Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: Ellsworth A. Van Graafeiland (Senior Circuit Judge); Richard J. Cardamone (Senior Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Frank X. Altimari.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Caption: Pagnucco v. Pan American World Airways, Inc. (In re Air Disaster at Lockerbie, Scotland, on December 21, 1988): the Pagnucco, Porter, and Bainbridge estate cases against Pan American World Airways, Inc., its security affiliate Alert Management Systems, and the related carrier and security defendants.  
Lower tribunal: United States District Court for the Eastern District of New York, Thomas C. Platt, Chief Judge.

#### Decision summary

A jury found that Pan American World Airways and its affiliate engaged in willful misconduct that was causally connected to the deaths of three passengers on Pan Am Flight 103, which was destroyed by a bomb over Lockerbie, Scotland, on December 21, 1988. Separate damages trials fixed the awards for the three estates. The Pan Am defendants appealed the liability determination on evidentiary grounds and challenged the loss-of-society and parental-care parts of the awards.

This opinion states the court's disposition of the appeals on a petition for rehearing submitted April 6, 1994, and takes the place of the preceding versions.

The court (Judges Stone-Khoury and Van Graafeiland, over a dissent by Judge Cardamone) vacated the determination of willful misconduct and its causal connection to the deaths, and remanded for a new trial. The reason is that the district court excluded evidence of what Pan Am's decisionmakers understood the FAA to have cleared, on the ground that an oral communication could not amount to the required regulatory waiver. The court agreed that an oral assurance could not waive a binding regulatory procedure. It held, however, that this does not make the effect of such a communication on the decisionmakers' culpable understanding categorically irrelevant under Article 25 of the Warsaw Convention, and that the exclusion of the Cozzi account, reinforced by an improper condition placed on alternative regulatory defenses, was not harmless.

On the remaining questions the judges were unanimous in result. Pan Am's request for judgment on causation is denied, and no other evidentiary ruling is held to require reversal. The Pagnucco society award is vacated and remanded for a determination of the dependency of the adult children. The parental-care components of all three awards are vacated and remanded. Punitive damages are unavailable. The society components of the Porter and Bainbridge awards are not vacated on Pan Am's challenge to the category.

The opinion decides no question of the truth of the Cozzi account, the route of the bomb, or the effect of the new trial on the 207 other passenger cases, which are not before the court.

#### The case and the result

##### Parties and posture

The Pan Am defendants seek reversal, a new liability trial, and correction of the challenged damages. The estates of Robert I. Pagnucco, Walter L. Porter, and Harry M. Bainbridge, through their representatives Judith A. Pagnucco, Molena A. Porter, and Dona Bardelli Bainbridge, seek to preserve the liability determination and the supported awards.

The decision under review consists of the final judgments of the Eastern District of New York entered after the consolidated willful-misconduct liability trial and the three separate damages trials. The entry dates of the individual judgments are not stated in the materials before the court. The court has jurisdiction over the final judgments under 28 U.S.C. § 1291. The materials before the court do not include the notices of appeal or state their filing dates. No defect in timeliness appears on those materials, and the court decides no question of timeliness.

##### Material record

On December 21, 1988, a bomb destroyed Pan Am Flight 103 over Lockerbie, Scotland, killing its 243 passengers and sixteen crew members. These cases arise from the deaths of Robert I. Pagnucco, Walter L. Porter, and Harry M. Bainbridge. Pan Am and Alert Management Systems defended the consolidated liability proceedings, and the common liability determination also governed 207 other passenger cases. The three individual damages awards were separately tried.

The estates' theory was that an unaccompanied suitcase containing an explosive device in a radio-cassette player entered the interline baggage system at Frankfurt, traveled through Heathrow, and reached Flight 103. The route and mechanism were disputed and were supported substantially by circumstantial evidence. The applicable Air Carrier Standard Security Program required positive passenger-baggage matching at the pertinent high-security airports, or physical inspection or exclusion of unaccompanied baggage. Pan Am had stopped interline matching at Heathrow and Frankfurt and used x-ray screening instead. The estates presented security warnings, evidence concerning training and implementation, and evidence that management knew the limits of x-ray screening. Pan Am contested causation and the inference of willful misconduct, and sought to prove that its personnel understood the FAA to have cleared its procedures.

The trial judge restricted that evidence on the ground that an oral communication could not constitute the required regulatory waiver. The record distinguishes an uncertain proffer concerning security official Sonesen from a specific proffer concerning executive Cozzi, who would have described the task force's instruction to obtain FAA guidance and the reported clearance on which he acted. The trial judge also conditioned certain British regulatory evidence on Pan Am's acceptance of a violation of the American rule. After a thirteen-week liability trial the jury found willful misconduct causally connected to the deaths. The damages awards were $9.225 million for Pagnucco, $1.735 million for Porter, and $9 million for Bainbridge. The jury awarded no survival damages for conscious pain and suffering. The challenges to loss of society and parental care are to be distinguished from the unchallenged financial-contribution and service components.

##### Issues and standards of review

1. Regulatory communications, culpable understanding, and alternative defenses. Evidentiary rulings are reviewed for abuse of discretion, but the legal theory of relevance on which evidence was excluded is reviewed independently, and any error warrants a new trial only if it affected substantial rights (Fed. R. Civ. P. 61; 28 U.S.C. § 2111). A claim of excluded evidence requires an adequate offer or a context making the substance apparent (Fed. R. Evid. 103(a)(2)). The estates bore the burden of proving willful misconduct under Article 25 and its causal connection to the deaths.
2. Other evidentiary objections and causation. Abuse of discretion for evidentiary rulings; independent interpretation of the rules on legal-conclusion testimony and public-record reports; sufficiency of the evidence viewed in the light most favorable to the verdict; demanding review of civil objections not preserved in the form asserted.
3. Loss-of-society damages. Independent review of whether the category is legally recoverable; dependency and amount are for the factfinder.
4. Parental-care damages and the scope of the damages remand. Independent review of the category and the instructions; substantial-rights analysis; valuation is for the factfinder.

##### Judgment by component

| Component | Disposition | For | Against or qualified |

| 2. Causation sufficiency and the other evidentiary rulings (Part III) | Pan Am's request for judgment on causation is denied; no other evidentiary ruling is held to require reversal or a direction to admit the evidence at the new trial. Unanimous in result. | Judges Stone-Khoury and Van Graafeiland (joining the reasoning of Part III in full); Judge Cardamone (concurring in the result) | None as to result |
| 3. Society award to the Pagnucco estate (Part IV.B) | Vacated and remanded for a determination of the dependency of the adult children and redetermination of that award. Unanimous. | Judges Stone-Khoury, Van Graafeiland and Cardamone | None |
| 4. Society components of the Porter and Bainbridge awards (Part IV.B) | Pan Am's challenge to the category fails, and those components are not vacated on that challenge. Unanimous. Their enforceability will conform to the result of the new trial. | Judges Stone-Khoury, Van Graafeiland and Cardamone on the disposition; Judges Stone-Khoury and Van Graafeiland on the conforming direction | Judge Cardamone, who would not order a new trial, does not join the conforming direction |

The district court will determine, in light of the new trial, which genuinely separable components remain fixed. | Judges Stone-Khoury and Van Graafeiland | Judge Cardamone does not join this direction |

The judgments are vacated in part and the cases remanded; they are not otherwise disturbed. Judge Cardamone dissents from component 1 and does not join the directions in components 4 and 6 that depend on the new trial.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

STONE-KHOURY, Circuit Judge, joined by VAN GRAAFEILAND, Senior Circuit Judge.

##### I. Background

This opinion states the court's disposition of these appeals on the petition for rehearing submitted April 6, 1994, and replaces the preceding versions. The record is summarized above, and we repeat here only what each issue requires. A jury, after a thirteen-week trial, found willful misconduct causally connected to the deaths of the three decedents, and separate trials fixed damages. The Pan Am defendants challenge the liability determination on evidentiary grounds and challenge the society and parental-care components of the awards. We take the issues in that order.

##### II. Regulatory communications, culpable understanding, and alternative defenses

**A. The Article 25 inquiry.** The Warsaw Convention, with the Montreal Agreement, makes a carrier liable for the death of a passenger caused by an accident within the Convention's terms, Art. 17, subject to a limit on liability. Article 25 withdraws that limit when the damage is caused by the carrier's willful misconduct. The estates therefore bore the burden of proving willful misconduct and its causal relation to the deaths. This court defines the term by the actor's state of mind: an act or omission done with knowledge that injury will probably result, or in a manner that implies reckless disregard of the probable consequences. Grey v. American Airlines, 227 F.2d 282 (2d Cir. 1955); Ospina v. Trans World Airlines, 975 F.2d 35 (2d Cir. 1992). A violation of safety rules, even an intentional one, is not by itself willful misconduct. Berner v. British Commonwealth Pacific Airlines, 346 F.2d 532 (2d Cir. 1965). The question is therefore not only what the security program required but what the persons directing Pan Am's operations knew and consciously disregarded. Pan Am does not challenge the basic willful-misconduct instruction, and we do not revisit it.

**B. Review.** Evidentiary rulings are ordinarily reviewed for abuse of discretion. That deference does not extend to a ruling whose premise is a mistake of law, and we review independently the theory of relevance on which this evidence was excluded. A mistaken ruling requires a new trial only if it affected the substantial rights of the party. Fed. R. Civ. P. 61; 28 U.S.C. § 2111. And the party claiming error from excluded evidence must have made its substance known to the court by an offer or in a context that made it apparent. Fed. R. Evid. 103(a)(2).

**C. Legal authorization and actual understanding.** Pan Am sought to show that its personnel understood the FAA to have cleared its substitution of x-ray screening for interline baggage matching. The district court restricted that evidence on the ground that an oral communication could not constitute the regulatory waiver the security program required.

If the question were whether Pan Am was legally authorized to depart from the written requirements, that ruling would be correct, and we say so at the outset. Pan Am remained subject to the written security requirements. An oral assurance, whatever its source, could not waive a binding regulatory procedure, and we hold nothing that would give it that effect. Our decisions on claimed official authorization are to the same effect. See United States v. Durrani, 835 F.2d 410 (2d Cir. 1987), discussing United States v. Duggan, 743 F.2d 59 (2d Cir. 1984). Those decisions address whether an official's assent excuses a violation of law. They do not decide what Article 25 asks.

Article 25 asks whether those directing Pan Am's operations acted with knowledge that their course would probably cause injury, or with reckless disregard of that probability. That is an inquiry into what those persons actually understood and why they acted as they did. That an oral waiver was legally ineffective tells us that the conduct was not excused. It does not tell us what the decisionmakers knew or believed when they made the choice. The district court's ruling treated the two as the same. It excluded evidence of the personnel's understanding because the communication could not operate as a waiver, and in doing so it denied the distinction between legal authorization and actual understanding. That premise was an error of law.

The distinction matters because a report of regulatory clearance is not necessarily offered for its truth. Offered to show its effect on the person who received it, it bears on what that person understood and why he proceeded. And where a party's state of mind is the central issue, this court has favored a liberal policy of admitting evidence directed to that state of mind, excluding it only where tangential and confusing matter clearly outweighs its relevance. Vinieris v. Byzantine Maritime Corp., 731 F.2d 1061 (2d Cir. 1984).

The Cozzi proffer illustrates the point. Cozzi, an executive, would have described the task force's instruction to obtain FAA guidance and the reported clearance on which he acted. Evidence that a responsible official was told to consult the regulator, received a report of clearance, and proceeded on that understanding has some tendency to distinguish a consciously reckless operational choice from a mistaken judgment. That tendency is what Article 25 makes consequential. The proffer was specific, it made the substance of the testimony known to the court, and the claim of error is preserved.

**D. The estates' response.** The estates have a strong answer, and we do not dismiss it. A belief that a regulator has tolerated a practice does not necessarily negate knowledge that passengers face a grave physical risk. One can knowingly endanger passengers while believing that a regulator has looked away. A jury could disbelieve Cozzi, conclude that the reliance was contrived, or find that even an honestly held understanding did not overcome knowledge of probable injury, and the weaknesses the estates identify in the offered accounts may substantially reduce their probative value. Those are reasons for the jury to test the evidence. They are not reasons to declare it legally irrelevant. Nor does Rule 403 rescue the ruling. A particularized Rule 403 objection remains available at the new trial, but it cannot sustain an exclusion whose stated foundation denied the distinction altogether. The estates are protected by Rule 105: on request the court may instruct the jury that no legally effective waiver occurred and that the evidence bears only on the carrier's state of mind.

We therefore hold that a regulatory communication's inability to create legal authorization does not make its effect on the decisionmaker's culpable understanding categorically irrelevant. The holding is narrow. We do not hold that any waiver existed, that Cozzi's reliance was genuine, or that a belief in the FAA's clearance is a defense under Article 25, and Pan Am is not entitled to an instruction that its conduct was excused. Nor do we hold that knowledge of illegality is an element of willful misconduct. Cheek v. United States, 498 U.S. 192 (1991), and Ratzlaf v. United States, 510 U.S. 135 (1994), construed particular criminal willfulness statutes. They do not establish such an element for Article 25, and we rest nothing on one.

**E. Alternative defenses.** The district court also conditioned the admission of certain British regulatory evidence on Pan Am's acceptance that it had violated the American requirement. Rule 8(e)(2) allows a party to state as many separate defenses as it has regardless of consistency. The Supreme Court has noted, in a criminal case, that the civil rules specifically authorize inconsistent pleading, and it held that a defendant who denies the offense may still be entitled to an instruction on entrapment. Mathews v. United States, 485 U.S. 58 (1988). That decision is persuasive here, not controlling. Its principle is that an asserted inconsistency between defenses is not a reason to make a party surrender one in order to present the other. Whether any particular item of British regulatory evidence is admissible depends on its own relevance, foundation, and Rule 403 balance. The condition the district court imposed was not a permissible basis for exclusion.

**F. Prejudice.** We do not minimize the estates' evidence of security warnings, prior bomb incidents, the shortcomings of x-ray detection, and the alleged failure to respond adequately to specific warnings. A jury could rely on that evidence to find willful misconduct, and nothing we say forecasts a different result. But the question is not whether another jury might find willful misconduct on this evidence. It is whether the exclusion of the defense's account of its own decisionmaking could materially have affected this jury's resolution of the central disputed element. That element was management's knowledge and disregard of probable injury. A trial built around that element is not fairly assessed by counting the remaining adverse incidents. The jury was asked to decide whether Pan Am's decisionmakers consciously disregarded a probable risk, and it did not hear, in the form properly offered, what those decisionmakers understood the regulator to have said and why they acted. The exclusion of the Cozzi account, reinforced by the improper condition on alternative regulatory defenses, was not harmless under Rule 61 and § 2111. A new trial of willful misconduct and its causal connection is required.

We do not rest that conclusion on the Sonesen proffer. The description of what Sonesen would have said was uncertain, and it cannot be treated as equally preserved or supported. Nor do we rest it on the rejection of a proposed curative instruction concerning an e-mail. That is a separate preservation question, and it does not by itself forfeit the preserved claim that Pan Am was barred from presenting other evidence of its actual understanding.

**G. The retrial.** At the new trial, evidence of the kind the Cozzi proffer described may be received on a proper foundation, with the limiting instruction described above on request, and Pan Am may present lawful alternative defenses. Each other proffer is to be evaluated separately. The district court will decide in the first instance whether the retrial can be confined to willful misconduct and its causal connection, and we leave that to it.

##### III. Causation and the other evidentiary rulings

Pan Am asks for judgment on causation. Sufficiency is assessed in the light most favorable to the verdict. The estates' theory that an unaccompanied suitcase containing an explosive device in a radio-cassette player entered the interline baggage system at Frankfurt, traveled through Heathrow, and reached Flight 103 rested substantially on circumstantial evidence, and the route and mechanism were disputed. The chain was sufficient to be evaluated by a jury. The new trial we order corrects the process by which the jury decided culpability. It does not select how the bomb traveled, and we adopt no account of its route.

The remaining evidentiary objections do not alter the result. Several objections to the estates' experts and to miscellaneous evidence of conduct were not preserved in the form now asserted. They cannot be pooled with the preserved objections as though all received ordinary review, and unpreserved civil objections receive only demanding review. Apart from the Cozzi and British-evidence rulings, no ruling is shown to require reversal on its own account, and a new trial ordered for one prejudicial exclusion does not validate unrelated proffers. Our mandate does not require the admission of every statement attributed to Sonesen, every proposed expert, or every alternative bombing scenario. Each retains its own requirements of preservation, foundation, hearsay, and cumulativeness.

For guidance at the new trial, and without deciding any particular offer, the settled rules are these. An investigative report may contain trustworthy evaluative findings under Rule 803(8)(C). Factually based conclusions are not excluded because they are opinions, and the court has both discretion and an obligation to exclude untrustworthy portions. Beech Aircraft Corp. v. Rainey, 488 U.S. 153 (1988); see Gentile v. County of Suffolk, 926 F.2d 142 (2d Cir. 1991). Admission of an evaluative finding does not carry with it every accusation embedded in the report. A security expert may explain practices and instruments but may not instruct the jury on the carrier's legal liability, because the court states the law and the jury applies it. Hygh v. Jacobs, 961 F.2d 359 (2d Cir. 1992); United States v. Scop, 846 F.2d 135 (2d Cir.), modified, 856 F.2d 5 (1988). An alternative bombing theory requires an adequate expert foundation under Rule 702. Pan Am need not establish an entire replacement account for the theory to be relevant, but unsupported possibilities do not become admissible merely because causation is disputed. An x-ray demonstration may be excluded as cumulative where the proposition it illustrates is stipulated.

##### IV. Damages

The damages questions will recur, and we decide them to guide further proceedings. Whether a category of damages is legally recoverable is a question we review independently. Whether a beneficiary was dependent, and the amount of any award, are for the factfinder.

**A. The framework.** In In re Air Disaster at Lockerbie, 928 F.2d 1267 (2d Cir. 1991), this court held that substantive federal common law governs the cause of action under the Convention and that recovery under Article 17 is for compensatory damages only. That decision binds us. The loss of the Convention's limit under Article 25 lifts the limit on compensatory recovery. It does not authorize punitive damages.

**B. Loss of society.** The decisions of the Supreme Court on loss of society in maritime settings support recognition of the category within the Lockerbie framework. Sea-Land Services, Inc. v. Gaudet, 414 U.S. 573 (1974), allowed a decedent's dependents to recover for loss of support, services, and society in a general maritime wrongful-death action, and American Export Lines, Inc. v. Alvez, 446 U.S. 274 (1980) (plurality opinion), carried the recovery for loss of society to a nonfatal injury. Mobil Oil Corp. v. Higginbotham, 436 U.S. 618 (1978), and Miles v. Apex Marine Corp., 498 U.S. 19 (1990), teach the limit on that reasoning: where Congress has prescribed the damages recoverable in a setting, courts do not supplement them. Higginbotham held that survivors could not recover loss of society under general maritime law for a death on the high seas, where the Death on the High Seas Act governs, and Miles applied the same principle of uniformity to seamen. Those limits are enforced in their own settings. They do not apply of their own force to this crash over land, and a party invoking an enacted limit must establish that it governs. To the extent Pan Am would import a high-seas pecuniary-loss limit mechanically, it has not shown a statutory basis for doing so.

We hold that, within the Lockerbie framework, a spouse and dependent children may recover compensatory damages for loss of society on proper proof. The recovery is not unrestricted. Gaudet speaks of dependents. Dependency is a factual predicate, not an incident of kinship, and it is not presumed for adult children. An appellate court cannot supply it.

The award to the Pagnucco estate includes society recovery for adult children. The record before us does not show a determination that they were dependent, and kinship does not establish it. We vacate the Pagnucco society award and remand for a determination of which adult beneficiaries, if any, were dependent and for redetermination of the award accordingly. As to the Porter and Bainbridge estates, Pan Am has not shown that the society components rest on a presumption of adult-child dependency or on any other category the law forbids, and we do not disturb them on that ground.

**C. Parental care.** Parental-care damages rest on a pecuniary foundation: the economic value of the training, nurture, guidance, and care the decedent would have provided. The ordinary expectation of such care is limited to minority. After majority, a child may recover only for a specifically proved pecuniary contribution, and this court has required children past majority to be very specific in showing that a parent's guidance had a pecuniary value. First National Bank in Greenwich v. National Airlines, Inc., 288 F.2d 621 (2d Cir. 1961). That case arose under the Death on the High Seas Act, and we follow it here for the pecuniary component of parental care under the federal common-law framework. Companionship may not be relabeled as guidance to duplicate society recovery, and the same loss may not be compensated twice.

The parental-care instructions did not separate ordinary care during minority, specifically proved pecuniary benefits after majority, and nonpecuniary companionship. The awards cannot stand on those instructions. We vacate the parental-care component of each of the three awards and remand for redetermination under corrected instructions requiring the minority limitation, a separate evidentiary basis for any claimed post-majority pecuniary contribution, and the avoidance of double counting.

**D. Scope of the damages remand.** The financial-contribution and service components were not challenged, and we do not reopen them. Because a new trial of willful misconduct is ordered, the district court should determine, in light of its result, which unchallenged and genuinely separable components may remain fixed. It should not reopen every dollar, and it should not treat existing awards as collectible on the strength of the vacated determination. We express no view on later changes in Warsaw damages law, on any unchallenged amount, or on the dependency of any adult beneficiary beyond what the record shows.

##### V. Disposition

The determination of willful misconduct and its causal connection to the deaths is vacated, and the cases are remanded for a new trial consistent with Part II. Pan Am's request for judgment on causation is denied, and no other evidentiary ruling is held to require reversal or a direction to admit evidence. The Pagnucco society award is vacated and remanded for a determination of the dependency of the adult children. The parental-care components of all three awards are vacated and remanded. The judgments are not otherwise disturbed, and the enforceability of the separable components will conform to the result of the new trial.

##### Matters left open

The court does not decide whether Cozzi's account is credible, whether Pan Am's reliance was genuine or reasonable, or what weight the account deserves. It does not decide that knowledge of illegality is or is not an element of willful misconduct under Article 25. It does not adopt any account of the bomb's route or decide the truth of any alternative scenario. It does not decide the admissibility of any particular proffer at the new trial, including statements attributed to Sonesen and unpreserved expert objections. It does not decide whether the retrial can be confined to willful misconduct and its causal connection, or what effect the new trial has on the 207 other passenger cases governed by the common liability determination, which are not before the court. It does not decide the amount of any unchallenged component, any later change in Warsaw damages law, or the dependency of any adult beneficiary not shown by the record.

#### Separate writings

**Writings of record.** The Opinion of the Court is by Judge Stone-Khoury, joined in full by Judge Van Graafeiland (Parts I through V). Judge Cardamone writes separately only in the sense described below. No other separate writing is filed.

**Judge Cardamone, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** The source establishes this writing as a partial concurrence and partial dissent, and it establishes no separate opinion text. No separate dissenting opinion is filed. The following reports his positions and the grounds the source summarizes, and is not a written opinion.

- Concurs in the result of Part III.
- Concurs in the judgment of Part IV (the vacatur of the Pagnucco society award and of the parental-care components) and in the rules there stated: that recovery is compensatory only, that society damages are available to spouses and dependent children on proof, that dependency of adult children is not presumed, and that parental care is limited to minority absent specific proof of pecuniary value.
- Dissents from Part II and from the new-trial order. He would affirm the liability determination.
- Does not join the directions in Parts IV.D and V that conform the awards to the result of a new trial he would not order.

His grounds, as summarized in the source: in his view the preserved rulings on the Cozzi proffer and on the British regulatory evidence do not warrant a new trial of liability, and neither the Sonesen proffer nor the rejected curative instruction furnishes a ground for reversal. He applies the same governing framework as the court: review is deferential but does not extend to a legally mistaken premise; Article 25 asks about the carrier's knowledge or reckless disregard; knowledge of illegality is not an element; and a belief in the FAA's clearance is not a categorical defense. He agrees with the court's rejection of the challenge to causation, and he agrees with the court's rules and judgment on society and parental-care damages. Because he would not order a retrial, he would limit the remand to the Pagnucco society award and the parental-care components, would leave liability and the unchallenged components in place, and does not join the directions that conform the awards to the result of a new trial. Whether the dissent rests on the absence of error or on the absence of prejudice from any error is not stated.

#### What the decision does

##### Controlling holdings

1. **Culpable understanding.** Rule: a regulatory communication's inability to create legal authorization does not make its effect on the decisionmaker's culpable understanding categorically irrelevant under Article 25. Authority: Warsaw Convention arts. 17 and 25; Montreal Agreement; Grey v. American Airlines, 227 F.2d 282 (2d Cir. 1955); Berner v. British Commonwealth Pacific Airlines, 346 F.2d 532 (2d Cir. 1965); Ospina v. Trans World Airlines, 975 F.2d 35 (2d Cir. 1992); Vinieris v. Byzantine Maritime Corp., 731 F.2d 1061 (2d Cir. 1984); Fed. R. Evid. 105, 403. Test and burdens: the estates must prove the treaty's willful-misconduct standard (an act or omission with knowledge that injury will probably result, or in a manner implying reckless disregard of the probable consequences) and causation; Pan Am may offer properly founded evidence of what its decisionmakers understood; on request the jury is told that no legally effective waiver occurred; Rule 403 is available on a particularized objection. Standard: abuse of discretion, independent review of the legal premise, substantial-rights review. Contrary argument: belief in regulatory tolerance does not negate knowledge of grave risk; answer: that goes to weight and to the jury's evaluation, not to categorical irrelevance. Judges: Stone-Khoury, Van Graafeiland; Cardamone dissenting.
2. **Alternative defenses.** Rule: a party may not be required to abandon one lawful defense in order to present another merely because the defenses are asserted to be inconsistent; the condition placed on the British regulatory evidence was not a permissible ground of exclusion. Authority: Fed. R. Civ. P. 8(e)(2); Mathews v. United States, 485 U.S. 58 (1988), persuasive in its criminal setting. Judges: Stone-Khoury, Van Graafeiland; Cardamone dissenting.
3. **Prejudice and remedy.** The exclusion of the Cozzi account, reinforced by the condition on alternative regulatory defenses, affected substantial rights on the central contested mental-state element, and a new trial of willful misconduct and its causal connection is required. Judges: Stone-Khoury, Van Graafeiland; Cardamone dissenting.
4. **Scope of the retrial and the other rulings.** A new trial ordered for one prejudicial exclusion does not validate unrelated proffers and does not authorize appellate factfinding about the bomb route; no judgment for Pan Am on causation. Authority: Fed. R. Evid. 702, 803(8)(C), 403; Beech Aircraft Corp. v. Rainey, 488 U.S. 153 (1988); Gentile v. County of Suffolk, 926 F.2d 142 (2d Cir. 1991); Hygh v. Jacobs, 961 F.2d 359 (2d Cir. 1992); United States v. Scop, 846 F.2d 135 (2d Cir.), modified, 856 F.2d 5 (1988). Judges: Stone-Khoury, Van Graafeiland; Cardamone concurring in the result.
5. **Society damages.** Rule: within the federal common-law framework of In re Air Disaster at Lockerbie, 928 F.2d 1267 (2d Cir. 1991), compensatory damages for loss of society are available to a spouse and dependent children on proof; dependency is a factual predicate that is not presumed for adult children; punitive damages are not available. Authority: Lockerbie; Sea-Land Services, Inc. v. Gaudet, 414 U.S. 573 (1974); American Export Lines, Inc. v. Alvez, 446 U.S. 274 (1980); limits enacted for other settings are respected in those settings (Mobil Oil Corp. v. Higginbotham, 436 U.S. 618 (1978); Miles v. Apex Marine Corp., 498 U.S. 19 (1990)). Burden: the beneficiary proves dependency; a party invoking an enacted limit must establish that it governs. Judges: Stone-Khoury, Van Graafeiland; Cardamone concurring in the judgment and the stated rules.
6. **Parental care.** Rule: parental-care damages rest on a pecuniary foundation; the ordinary expectation is limited to minority; a specifically proved pecuniary contribution after majority may be compensable; companionship may not be relabeled as guidance to duplicate society damages; no double counting. Authority: First National Bank in Greenwich v. National Airlines, Inc., 288 F.2d 621 (2d Cir. 1961); Lockerbie. Judges: Stone-Khoury, Van Graafeiland; Cardamone concurring in the judgment and the stated rules.

##### Precedent and legal effect

- **Status.** Published opinion of the court. Parts II, III and IV carry two joins (Judges Stone-Khoury and Van Graafeiland) and are precedential in this circuit to the extent of their holdings. Judge Cardamone concurs in the result of Part III and in the judgment and stated rules of Part IV; his dissent from Part II and his grounds are not circuit law.
- **Prior decisions followed.** In re Air Disaster at Lockerbie, 928 F.2d 1267 (2d Cir. 1991) (federal common law governs the Convention cause of action; compensatory damages only); Grey, Berner and Ospina (the Article 25 standard); Vinieris (liberal admission of state-of-mind evidence where intent is central); First National Bank in Greenwich (specific proof of pecuniary value of parental guidance after majority); Beech Aircraft, Gentile, Hygh and Scop (evidentiary guidance, applied as settled law).
- **Prior decisions distinguished.** Durrani and Duggan address whether an official's assent excuses a violation of law, not the relevance of a reported clearance to culpable understanding. Cheek and Ratzlaf concern particular criminal willfulness statutes and do not supply an element for Article 25. Higginbotham and Miles enforce enacted damages limits in their own statutory settings and do not apply of their own force to this crash over land. Mathews is persuasive authority in its criminal setting.
- **What changes.** No prior decision is overruled. The court holds that a regulatory communication's inability to create legal authorization does not make its effect on culpable understanding categorically irrelevant under Article 25, and that a party may not be required to abandon one lawful defense to present another. It states that society damages for adult children require proof of dependency and that parental-care damages are limited to minority absent specific proof of pecuniary value after majority.
- **Party-specific relief and law of the case.** The new trial and the damages remand bind the parties and the three cases before the court, and the rulings in this opinion govern the proceedings on remand. The court makes no ruling in the 207 other passenger cases.
- **Dicta and separate positions.** The evidentiary guidance in Part III restates settled rules and decides no particular proffer. The observations in Part II about the estates' strongest response describe matters for the jury and are not holdings about the weight of any evidence. Judge Cardamone's dissent is a separate position and not law.

##### Effect on the parties and remedy

The determination of willful misconduct and its causal connection to the deaths is vacated and remanded to the Eastern District of New York for a new trial under Part II. The Pagnucco society award and the parental-care components of all three awards are vacated and remanded for further proceedings under Part IV. Pan Am's request for judgment on causation is denied. The remaining components are not disturbed by this opinion, and the district court will determine, in light of the new trial, which unchallenged and genuinely separable components remain fixed. No award is to be treated as enforceable on the strength of the vacated determination.

##### Mandate status

No mandate has issued. This opinion is not the mandate, and the date of the opinion is not the date on which the mandate issues. The mandate is governed by Federal Rules of Appellate Procedure 40 and 41 as in force on September 12, 1994: a petition for rehearing directed to this disposition is governed by Rule 40, the timely filing of such a petition stays the mandate until it is decided, and under Rule 41 the mandate issues only after the period that rule provides and any such petition is resolved, unless the court orders otherwise. No further petition, suggestion, or other filing is assumed. The district court acts on the remand when the mandate issues.

#### Historical comparison

On 1994-09-12 the Second Circuit (Van Graafeiland, Cardamone, Altimari) issued an amended opinion on rehearing, written by Cardamone and joined by Altimari; Van Graafeiland dissented on liability. It affirmed the willful-misconduct liability verdicts and causation, affirmed the Bainbridge and Porter society awards, vacated the Pagnucco society award (adult children's dependency unproved) and the parental-care awards in all three cases (no minority limitation), and ordered a limited damages remand.

The simulated judgment issued the same day. It changes the liability judgment: the willful-misconduct and causal-connection determination is vacated for a new trial, while Pan Am's request for judgment on causation is still denied. On damages the judgments match, with materially similar reasoning on dependency and the minority limit. Only the remedy's scope differs.

Source: Pagnucco v. Pan American World Airways, Inc. (In re Air Disaster at Lockerbie), 37 F.3d 804, 814-32, esp. 828-30 (2d Cir. 1994).

### [Hathaway v. Coughlin](../cases/hathaway-v-coughlin/)

Decision date: September 29, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 93-2732  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Appeal dismissed in part, order affirmed in part, and case remanded; the mandate has not issued  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/hathaway-v-coughlin/events/1994-09-29-decision.md](../cases/hathaway-v-coughlin/events/1994-09-29-decision.md)

Historical panel: Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); Joseph M. McLaughlin (Active Circuit Judge); Dennis Jacobs (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); Joseph M. McLaughlin (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Dennis Jacobs.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Appeal from: United States District Court for the Northern District of New York (Frederick J. Scullin, Jr., District Judge)  
Opinion of the Court: Altimari, Circuit Judge, joined by McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury, Circuit Judges  
Publication status: Published; precedential opinion of the Court  
Joseph Foote is the Defendant-Appellant. The other named defendants are not parties to this appeal.

#### Decision summary

William Hathaway, an inmate, sued several prison officials and Dr. Joseph Foote, a prison general practitioner, under 42 U.S.C. 1983. He alleged deliberate indifference to his serious medical needs. At trial the jury could not agree on Foote's liability. The district court declared a mistrial and denied Foote's motions for judgment as a matter of law under Fed. R. Civ. P. 50. Foote appealed. He argued that the evidence cannot establish a constitutional violation and that qualified immunity entitles him to judgment before a second trial. Hathaway sought further trial proceedings.

The Court of Appeals held that an order denying judgment as a matter of law after a mistrial is not itself final, and that it can hear Foote's appeal only to the extent the order conclusively rejects a purely legal claim of qualified immunity. On that question the Court views the evidence and reasonable inferences in the light most favorable to Hathaway, finds no facts, and decides no question of credibility. The Court dismissed the rest of the appeal for want of appellate jurisdiction.

On the legal immunity question, the Court held three things. First, a reasonable jury, crediting Hathaway's testimony and the documented sequence of events, could find that Foote actually knew of the broken hip pins and consciously disregarded Hathaway's serious medical need. The Court did not hold that Hathaway has proved it. Second, that inference does not require expert medical testimony as a universal prerequisite, and the Court announced no categorical rule in either direction. Third, the prohibition on consciously disregarding a known serious medical need by persistent inaction was clearly established by 1980 under Estelle v. Gamble, so qualified immunity does not entitle Foote to judgment before retrial. The Court affirmed the denial of judgment insofar as it rests on qualified immunity and remanded for retrial of the claim against Foote. No damages are awarded and no ruling on damages is made. The judgments for the other defendants are not before the Court and are undisturbed.

The three judges of the panel agreed on every component of the judgment and joined the opinion in full.

##### Judgment by component

| Component | Disposition | For | Against |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Reach of the appeal | Limited to the legal question of qualified immunity, on the evidence viewed in the light most favorable to Hathaway | Altimari, McLaughlin, Stone-Khoury | None |
| 2. Remainder of the appeal | Dismissed for want of appellate jurisdiction, to the extent it seeks review of the denial of Rule 50 relief apart from that legal question or depends on crediting Foote's account | Altimari, McLaughlin, Stone-Khoury | None |
| 3. Denial of judgment insofar as it rests on qualified immunity | Affirmed | Altimari, McLaughlin, Stone-Khoury | None |
| 4. Claim against Foote | Remanded to the district court for retrial, with no ruling on damages and no disturbance of the judgments for the other defendants | Altimari, McLaughlin, Stone-Khoury | None |

#### The case and the result

##### Decision under review

After a trial in which the jury could not agree on Foote's liability, the district court declared a mistrial and denied Foote's motions for judgment as a matter of law under Fed. R. Civ. P. 50. Foote appeals. He seeks judgment ending the claim against him for insufficient constitutional proof and on the ground of qualified immunity. Hathaway seeks further trial proceedings. The materials describing the proceedings below do not state the date on which the order was entered or the date of the notice of appeal, and this decision resolves no question about the timeliness of the notice.

##### Material record

Hathaway underwent hip-fusion surgery in 1973, and pins were placed in his hip. The fusion failed, and he suffered arthritis and persistent pain. A specialist proposed further surgery in 1978, and Hathaway declined. Foote, a general practitioner at the prison, later provided pain medication, orthopedic shoes, restrictions on lifting, and placement on a lower floor. An x-ray in July 1980 showed two broken pins. Hathaway was not promptly told. He complained of pain repeatedly in the following years, with numerous visits in 1981 and 1982. He testified that a nurse told him of the broken pins in July 1981, that he then told Foote he was willing to undergo surgery, and that Foote said a referral would be made. A renewed referral to a specialist did not occur until late 1982, after correspondence about his condition and the filing of this action. The later medical assessments differed: one specialist regarded removal of the pins as not medically necessary but possibly helpful for pain, and another recommended further surgery. An operation in October 1983 removed the accessible portions of the pins and included further grafting.

An earlier appeal in this case reversed a grant of summary judgment because the medical-care claim presented factual issues. Hathaway v. Coughlin, 841 F.2d 48 (2d Cir. 1988). At the trial that followed, judgment was entered for the other defendants for lack of personal involvement. Those judgments are not before the Court.

##### Issues and standards

**Issue 1: Appellate reach after a mistrial.** Whether this Court may hear an appeal from the denial of Rule 50 relief after a mistrial, and, if so, how far. Standard: independent review of the Court's jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. 1291 and the collateral order doctrine; for a legally reachable immunity question, the evidence and reasonable inferences are taken in the light most favorable to Hathaway.

**Issue 2: Deliberate indifference, expert proof, and clearly established law.** Whether, on that view of the evidence, a constitutional violation can be found, including whether the inference of conscious disregard requires expert medical proof, and whether the law at the time of Foote's conduct (1980 through 1982) clearly established the relevant prohibition. Standard: de novo review of the legal questions; evidence and inferences viewed favorably to Hathaway under Hendricks v. Coughlin, 942 F.2d 109, 112 (2d Cir. 1991); no credibility determination.

##### Dates that bear on the law

The treatment at issue occurred from 1978 through 1983. Estelle v. Gamble was decided in 1976, Wilson v. Seiter in 1991, and Farmer v. Brennan on June 6, 1994, after the conduct and before this decision. The earlier appeal in this case was decided in 1988.

##### The result and its immediate effect

The appeal is dismissed in part, the order is affirmed in part, and the case is remanded. The Court reaches only the legal question of qualified immunity on the evidence viewed in the light most favorable to Hathaway, dismisses the remainder of the appeal, affirms the denial of judgment insofar as it rests on qualified immunity, and remands for retrial of the claim against Foote. Foote therefore faces a retrial on actual knowledge, culpability, causation, and injury.

Judgment: Appeal dismissed in part; order affirmed in part; case remanded, by the four components stated above.  
Vote: Altimari, McLaughlin, and Stone-Khoury for each component; none against.  
Opinion and joins: Opinion of the Court by Judge Altimari, joined in full by Judges McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury. There is no concurrence, dissent, or other separate writing.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

ALTIMARI, Circuit Judge, joined by McLAUGHLIN and STONE-KHOURY, Circuit Judges.

Hathaway, an inmate, brought this action under 42 U.S.C. 1983 against several prison officials and Dr. Joseph Foote, a prison general practitioner, alleging deliberate indifference to his serious medical needs. The facts bearing on this appeal are set out above. The jury could not agree on Foote's liability, the district court declared a mistrial and denied Foote's motions for judgment as a matter of law, and Foote appeals. He contends that the evidence cannot establish a constitutional violation and that qualified immunity entitles him to judgment before a second trial. Hathaway's position is that a mistrial and the denial of Rule 50 relief do not authorize an appeal that would choose among competing medical inferences.

##### Part I. The reach of this interlocutory appeal

We begin with our own power to hear the appeal. An order denying judgment as a matter of law after a jury has failed to agree does not end the litigation; it sends the case to a second trial. The existence of a Rule 50 order therefore does not itself create finality, and we rest our jurisdiction on no final judgment against Foote and on no certification. Whatever power we have comes from the collateral order doctrine as the Supreme Court applied it to qualified immunity in Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511 (1985). Mitchell holds that "a district court's denial of a claim of qualified immunity, to the extent that it turns on an issue of law, is an appealable 'final decision' within the meaning of 28 U.S.C. 1291 notwithstanding the absence of a final judgment." Id. at 530. The reason is that the entitlement "is an immunity from suit rather than a mere defense to liability" and, "like an absolute immunity, it is effectively lost if a case is erroneously permitted to go to trial." Id. at 526.

The same holding limits what we may do. The appealable issue is "a purely legal one: whether the facts alleged ... support a claim of violation of clearly established law." Id. at 528 n.9. Where the resolution of immunity turns on disputed facts, no appeal lies. See Moffitt v. Town of Brookfield, 950 F.2d 880, 884 (2d Cir. 1991). The doctrine does not permit us to decide whether Foote or Hathaway should be believed, to choose among competing medical inferences, or to review the sufficiency of the trial evidence as such. Hathaway is right to insist on that boundary. Within it, the legal question is whether the evidence, taken as the plaintiff presents it, makes out a violation of clearly established law, so we view the evidence and the reasonable inferences from it in the light most favorable to Hathaway. See Hendricks, 942 F.2d at 112. We do so only to frame the legal question. We find no fact and decide no question of credibility.

Our decision in Whalen v. County of Fulton, No. 93-7457 (2d Cir. Mar. 29, 1994), Part II, applies the same approach to a different order. We held there that an order denying a qualified-immunity summary judgment motion without prejudice to renewal after discovery, and reserving the defense for decision on a developed record, is not a conclusive determination and is not an appealable collateral order. We also made clear that the label on an order does not control and that a functionally conclusive rejection of a purely legal immunity defense remains reviewable. Whalen turned on a reservation of the defense for a later record. The order before us is of a different kind. After a full trial, the district court denied Foote's motions and declared a mistrial. The record before us contains no indication that the court reserved the defense for later decision, denied relief without prejudice to renewal, or conditioned its ruling on further development. On that record, the denial operates as a conclusive rejection of the legal question whether Hathaway's evidence, taken as true, defeats immunity, and Foote faces a second trial. Had the court reserved the defense, Whalen would govern and the premise of our jurisdiction would be absent. We decide the appeal on the footing the record presents.

We therefore hear the appeal only insofar as it presents the legal question of qualified immunity on the evidence so viewed. To the extent Foote seeks judgment on the ground that the evidence is insufficient apart from immunity, or relies on his own account of the treatment or on conflicts in the medical evidence, those contentions are not properly before us on this interlocutory appeal, and we dismiss the appeal to that extent.

##### Part II. Whether Hathaway's evidence permits a finding of a constitutional violation

Qualified immunity analysis requires a court to determine, as a necessary concomitant to deciding whether a right was clearly established, whether the plaintiff has asserted a violation of a constitutional right at all. Siegert v. Gilley, 500 U.S. 226, 232 (1991). That inquiry is part of the legal question Mitchell makes appealable, and we take it up as such, not as a free-standing review of the sufficiency of the evidence. We decide it de novo.

The Eighth Amendment, enforced here through 42 U.S.C. 1983, forbids deliberate indifference to the serious medical needs of prisoners. Estelle v. Gamble, 429 U.S. 97, 104-05 (1976). Estelle also marks what the Amendment does not reach. An inadvertent failure to provide adequate care, negligence in diagnosing or treating a condition, and medical malpractice do not become constitutional violations merely because the patient is a prisoner, and a difference of opinion over treatment does not state a claim. Id. at 105-06. A claim requires a serious medical need and a culpable state of mind. See Wilson v. Seiter, 501 U.S. 294 (1991) (applying the deliberate-indifference standard of Estelle to conditions of confinement, medical care included). Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825 (1994), decided after the treatment at issue but before this decision, states the standard that governs the merits today. The official must both be aware of facts from which the inference could be drawn that a substantial risk of serious harm exists and draw the inference. Id. at 837. Actual knowledge may be shown by inference from the circumstances, and a factfinder may conclude that an official knew of a substantial risk from the very fact that the risk was obvious. Id. at 842. An official who actually knew of the risk may nonetheless escape liability if he responded reasonably. Id. at 844-45. The standard asks what the official actually knew and did. It is not met by showing that he should have known. See id. at 837, 842-45.

Viewed in the light most favorable to Hathaway, the record shows a serious and persistently painful condition, a failed hip fusion with arthritis. The condition changed in July 1980, when an x-ray showed two broken pins, and Hathaway was not promptly told. For two years he returned repeatedly, complaining of pain. In July 1981, after a nurse told him of the broken pins, he told Foote he was willing to undergo surgery, and Foote said a referral would be made. The renewed specialist referral did not come until late 1982, after correspondence about his condition and after he had sued. A jury crediting that testimony could find that Foote knew the hardware had broken, knew the pain continued, knew that Hathaway, who had declined surgery in 1978, now wished to proceed, and, having said a referral would be made, did not make it for more than a year. A jury could further find that this was not an exercise of medical judgment about a changed problem but a conscious decision to let an ineffective course continue. That is evidence from which actual awareness and disregard may be inferred.

Foote's response is substantial, and we do not discount it. Hathaway had a complicated, longstanding orthopedic condition. He had himself declined surgery in 1978. He received continuing treatment: medication, orthopedic shoes, restrictions on lifting, and a lower-floor placement. Specialists who later examined him did not agree, one regarding removal of the pins as not medically necessary though possibly helpful for pain and another recommending further surgery. A physician is not deliberately indifferent because conservative treatment fails to eliminate pain, and a delay that amounts to negligence is not a constitutional wrong. Estelle, 429 U.S. at 105-06. A jury could find on this evidence that Foote continued to exercise professional judgment, relied on specialist advice, understood Hathaway's complaints differently, or concluded that the risks and limited likely benefits of surgery favored continued conservative care. It remains open to Foote to make each of those showings at retrial.

But the existence of competing inferences defeats Foote's request for judgment; it does not support it. On the question we may decide, the evidence is taken in the light most favorable to Hathaway, and a court may not select the professional-judgment inference simply because some care was provided. Treatment of some kind does not conclusively defeat a finding of deliberate indifference, because care directed to the condition as it stood in 1978 does not establish that the changed problem disclosed in 1980 was met with judgment rather than disregard. Hathaway's 1978 refusal of surgery is relevant but not dispositive. The breaking of the pins, and his testimony that he then expressed willingness to proceed, bear directly on the premise on which reliance on that refusal rests. The 1983 operation neither proves nor disproves that Foote's earlier choices were constitutionally deficient, and we do not convert the later decision to operate into a retrospective constitutional prescription. Equally, nothing in this opinion permits a jury to find deliberate indifference merely because treatment was inadequate or unsuccessful. The question remains what Foote actually knew and whether he consciously disregarded it.

We therefore hold only that a reasonable jury, crediting Hathaway's testimony and the documented sequence, could find actual awareness and disregard. We do not hold that Hathaway has proved it. Foote's actual knowledge, his culpability, causation, and injury remain for decision on remand.

The appeal also presents whether this inference can rest on the evidence described without expert medical testimony. We decline to announce a categorical answer in either direction. Some questions in prison medical litigation cannot be answered by lay jurors, such as the evaluation of a treatment choice or the causal connection between a delay and an injury, and expert proof may be required for them. The inference at issue here is of a different kind. It rests on a sequence that lay jurors can follow without medical training: a known failure of surgical hardware, withheld information, persistent complaints of pain, a patient's changed position, and a promised referral not carried out for more than a year. We hold only that the evidence described can support the requisite inference without a universal expert-testimony prerequisite. We do not hold that expert evidence is unnecessary in prison medical cases, and we leave open whether it will be needed on causation or injury at retrial.

##### Part III. Whether the law was clearly established when Foote acted

Foote argues that, whatever the evidence shows, the law did not clearly establish in 1980 through 1982 that his course of treatment was unlawful. Two features of the inquiry control. First, the relevant date is the date of the conduct, not the date of our decision, and notice created by a later decision cannot make earlier conduct unlawful. Our method asks whether the right was defined with reasonable specificity, whether Supreme Court or circuit decisional law then supported its existence, and whether, under preexisting law, a reasonable official would have understood that his acts were unlawful, all measured as of the time of the conduct. See Jermosen v. Smith, 945 F.2d 547 (2d Cir. 1991). Whether a right was clearly established at a particular time is a question of law, which we decide in light of all relevant precedents. Elder v. Holloway, 510 U.S. 510, 514-16 (1994). Second, the right must be defined with particularity, because the contours of the right must be sufficiently clear that a reasonable official would understand that what he is doing violates it. Anderson v. Creighton, 483 U.S. 635, 639-40 (1987).

Defined at that level, the right at issue is not a right to a particular operation on a particular date, and it is not a general entitlement to adequate medical care. It is the prohibition on consciously disregarding a known serious medical need by persistent inaction, in the circumstances Hathaway's evidence would establish.
