That prohibition was clearly established by 1980. Estelle, decided in 1976, held that deliberate indifference to the serious medical needs of prisoners violates the Eighth Amendment, and it distinguished that wrong from negligence, malpractice, and disagreement over treatment. 429 U.S. at 104-06. This circuit had applied the principle before the conduct at issue. See Williams v. Vincent, 508 F.2d 541 (2d Cir. 1974) (allegations supported an inference of deliberate indifference rather than an exercise of professional judgment where physicians may have chosen an easier and less efficacious treatment); Todaro v. Ward, 565 F.2d 48 (2d Cir. 1977) (applying Estelle; repeated instances of delayed or denied care may show deliberate indifference). A reasonable physician in 1980 through 1982 would have understood that the Eighth Amendment forbade knowingly leaving an inmate's serious and painful condition unaddressed after learning that it had changed, as distinct from treating it with ordinary professional judgment or with negligence. Our later decisions describe the right in the same terms. See Kaminsky v. Rosenblum, 929 F.2d 922 (2d Cir. 1991). Those decisions confirm what Estelle already provided and are not the source of notice.

Two sources are not. Farmer supplies the standard governing the merits at the time of decision, but it cannot create notice retrospectively for conduct that preceded it, and we do not use it for that purpose. Our 1988 decision in this case likewise postdates the conduct. It held only that the medical-care claim presented factual issues that precluded summary judgment, and it supplies no notice.

Foote's principal contrary argument is that the specific course he followed was not clearly unlawful. To the extent the argument is that the law gave no clear guidance on when a physician must refer a patient who earlier refused surgery for removal of broken pins, it defines the right at the level of the particular medical decision. The question is not whether the law required a given operation by a given date. It did not, and we recognize no such right. The question is whether the law forbade a physician from consciously disregarding a known serious medical need, and it did. Whether Foote's conduct fell within that prohibition, or instead reflected judgment or negligence, depends on facts the jury has not found. On the evidence viewed in the light most favorable to Hathaway, immunity does not entitle Foote to judgment before retrial.

##### Part IV. Disposition

The appeal is dismissed to the extent it seeks review of the denial of Rule 50 relief apart from the legal question of qualified immunity or depends on crediting Foote's account. The order is affirmed insofar as it rejects Foote's claim of qualified immunity on the evidence viewed in the light most favorable to Hathaway. The case is remanded for retrial of the claim against Foote. We award no damages and make no ruling on damages; the first jury's inability to agree supplies no basis for one. The judgments for the other defendants are not before us and are undisturbed, and no claim against any other official is revived. Liability, if any, must rest on Foote's own conduct. On retrial the jury must be instructed that negligence and disagreement over treatment are insufficient, that inadequate treatment is not by itself deliberate indifference, and that the question is Foote's actual awareness and disregard.

#### Separate opinions

None. There is no concurrence, dissent, or other separate writing. The opinion of the Court, written by Judge Altimari, is joined in full by Judges McLaughlin and Stone-Khoury.

#### What the decision does

##### Controlling holding

The controlling propositions, each carrying the joins of Judges Altimari, McLaughlin, and Stone-Khoury, are these.

1. **Reach after a mistrial (Part I).**
   - Rule: the denial of Rule 50 relief after a mistrial is not itself final, and appellate power exists only to the extent the ruling conclusively rejects a purely legal qualified-immunity defense. Review is confined to whether the evidence, viewed favorably to the plaintiff, shows a violation of clearly established law. The remainder is dismissed.
   - Authority: 28 U.S.C. 1291; Mitchell, 472 U.S. at 526, 528 n.9, 530; Moffitt, 950 F.2d at 884; Whalen Part II; Fed. R. Civ. P. 50.
   - Test: independent review of jurisdiction, with the plaintiff-favorable view of the evidence used only to frame the legal question.
   - Burdens: the Court examines its own jurisdiction independently, and the holding allocates no burden of proof to either party.
   - Qualification: an order that reserves the defense or denies relief without prejudice to renewal is governed by Whalen and would not support the appeal.
   - Application: the order denied the motions after a full trial and mistrial, with no indication of reservation, so the legal immunity question is reviewable and the rest is dismissed.
   - Supporting judges: Altimari, McLaughlin, Stone-Khoury.

2. **Constitutional violation on the plaintiff-favorable evidence (Part II).**
   - Rule: a serious medical need together with actual awareness and disregard is required. Negligence and disagreement over treatment are not enough. Knowledge may be inferred from the circumstances. On the evidence described, the combination of knowledge of the broken pins, prolonged pain, withheld information, and failure to act on a promised referral permits a finding of deliberate indifference, and treatment of some kind does not conclusively defeat it.
   - Authority: Estelle, 429 U.S. at 104-06; Wilson, 501 U.S. 294; Farmer, 511 U.S. at 837, 842-45; Siegert, 500 U.S. at 232; Hendricks, 942 F.2d at 112.
   - Test and burdens: de novo review of the legal question, plaintiff-favorable inferences, no credibility determination. The plaintiff bears the burden of proving actual awareness and disregard at trial, and the holding decides only that a reasonable jury could so find.
   - Qualification: no holding that Hathaway has proved the violation. Actual knowledge, culpability, causation and injury remain for retrial.
   - Application: the July 1980 x-ray, the nondisclosure, the repeated complaints, the July 1981 exchange with Foote, and the delayed referral permit the inference, while the continuing treatment and the specialists' differing views are competing inferences for the jury.
   - Supporting judges: Altimari, McLaughlin, Stone-Khoury.

3. **Expert proof (Part II).**
   - Rule: there is no categorical rule requiring or dispensing with expert testimony. The lay-comprehensible sequence described here can support the inference of conscious disregard without a universal expert-testimony prerequisite.
   - Authority: Estelle; Farmer, 511 U.S. at 842.
   - Qualification: expert proof may be required for questions a lay jury cannot evaluate, such as a treatment choice or causal connection, and the need for it on causation and injury at retrial is left open.
   - Supporting judges: Altimari, McLaughlin, Stone-Khoury.

4. **Notice and qualified immunity (Part III).**
   - Rule: for conduct in 1980 through 1982, Estelle, applied in this circuit before that conduct, clearly established the narrowly defined prohibition on consciously disregarding a known serious medical need by persistent inaction. Farmer and the 1988 decision in this case create no notice. The right is not a general medical-care duty and not a right to a particular operation on a particular date.
   - Authority: Estelle, 429 U.S. at 104-06; Williams v. Vincent, 508 F.2d 541; Todaro v. Ward, 565 F.2d 48; Jermosen, 945 F.2d 547; Elder, 510 U.S. at 514-16; Anderson, 483 U.S. at 639-40.
   - Test: right defined with reasonable specificity, supported by decisional law at the time of the conduct, and understood by a reasonable official as unlawful.
   - Application: on Hathaway's evidence, a physician who knew of the broken pins and the changed circumstances and persistently failed to act would have been on notice.
   - Supporting judges: Altimari, McLaughlin, Stone-Khoury.

##### Principal contrary arguments and answers

Hathaway contends that no appeal lies because the Court would be choosing among medical inferences. The Court agrees as to everything outside the legal immunity question and dismisses it, and it reaches only the legal question on the plaintiff-favorable view. Foote contends that continuing treatment, Hathaway's earlier refusal of surgery, and specialist advice show at most disagreement or negligence, and that the course was not clearly unlawful. The Court answers that these are competing inferences for the jury, that the changed circumstances after July 1980 alter the premise of the earlier refusal, and that the right is defined at the level of the prohibition on conscious disregard, not at the level of a particular operation.

##### Matters left open

Whether Foote in fact knew of the broken pins and consciously disregarded the need; causation and compensable injury; damages; whether expert proof will be required on causation or injury at retrial; whether any constitutional right to a particular operation on a fixed date exists (none is recognized here) or any general rule dispenses with medical experts; how the appeal would be resolved if the order had reserved the defense, a matter governed by Whalen; the timeliness of the notice of appeal; any review of the dismissed contentions on the merits; and the judgments for the other defendants. This opinion decides no question that depends on the facts a jury may find at retrial.

##### Precedent and legal effect

This is a published opinion of the Court, precedential on the propositions stated in Parts I through III, each of which carries three joins. It follows Mitchell v. Forsyth and Moffitt v. Town of Brookfield on the reach of interlocutory review of qualified immunity. It applies Whalen v. County of Fulton, Part II, without narrowing or extending it, and distinguishes it on posture: Whalen concerned a without-prejudice denial of summary judgment that reserved the defense for a developed record, while this appeal concerns a denial of Rule 50 relief after a full trial and mistrial with no reservation shown. It applies Estelle v. Gamble, Wilson v. Seiter, Farmer v. Brennan, Siegert v. Gilley, and Elder v. Holloway as binding Supreme Court authority, and it applies the circuit's method for clearly established law in Jermosen v. Smith. It changes no earlier circuit law and overrules nothing. Farmer is treated as the present merits standard and not as a source of notice.

Circuit-wide precedent is limited to the propositions in Parts I through III: the reach of the appeal and dismissal of the remainder; the sufficiency of the described combination of evidence to permit a finding of deliberate indifference, with no categorical expert-proof rule; and the notice supplied by Estelle for the narrowly defined prohibition. Party-specific relief is the affirmance of the denial of immunity-based judgment on the evidence viewed favorably to Hathaway, the dismissal of the remainder, and the remand for retrial of the claim against Foote. That ruling rests on the evidence at the first trial viewed in Hathaway's favor; it does not bind a factfinder or determine what the evidence at retrial will show. The mandate of the 1988 decision in this case is neither extended nor reduced. There is no concurrence or dissent, and nothing in this opinion is dicta beyond the statements that the Court expressly reserves.

##### Effect on the parties

The appeal is dismissed in part, the order is affirmed in part, and the case is remanded to the district court for retrial of the claim against Foote on actual knowledge, culpability, causation, and injury, with the jury instructed as stated in Part IV. No damages are awarded and no ruling on damages is made. The judgments for the other defendants are not before the Court and are undisturbed, and no claim against any other official is revived. Costs are not addressed.

##### Mandate and remand

The mandate has not issued. Under Fed. R. App. P. 40 and 41 as in effect on the date of this decision, a petition for rehearing may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order, and the mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order; a timely petition for rehearing stays the mandate until the petition is disposed of. The remand proceedings in the district court await the issuance of the mandate. No petition or other filing has been made or is assumed.

#### Historical comparison

Hathaway v. Coughlin, 37 F.3d 63, 66-70 (2d Cir. 1994) (public reproduction), decided September 29, 1994. Panel: Altimari, McLaughlin, Jacobs. Altimari wrote, joined by McLaughlin; Jacobs dissented, stressing the treatment given, specialist decisions, and the line between disagreement over care and deliberate indifference. The court affirmed denial of Foote's post-mistrial motion insofar as the qualified-immunity issue was reviewable, holding that the evidence, viewed favorably to Hathaway, permitted a finding of deliberate indifference despite some treatment, so immunity did not require judgment for Foote. Further trial proceedings remained possible. The simulated judgment is the same on immunity and retrial, so the judgment is not changed. What differs is the lineup (3-0, no dissent, Stone-Khoury on the panel in place of Jacobs) and the stated reasoning and remedy: express partial dismissal for want of jurisdiction, no categorical expert-proof rule, Estelle as the source of notice, and retrial instruction directions, none of which the historical summary records.

### [Scanio v. United States](../cases/scanio-v-united-states/)

Decision date: October 11, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 94-2002  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Published. Order of dismissal vacated and matter remanded, two to one (Pierce and Stone; Van Graafeiland dissenting). No mandate has issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/scanio-v-united-states/events/1994-10-11-decision.md](../cases/scanio-v-united-states/events/1994-10-11-decision.md)

Historical panel: Ellsworth A. Van Graafeiland (Senior Circuit Judge); Lawrence W. Pierce (Senior Circuit Judge); Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: Ellsworth A. Van Graafeiland (Senior Circuit Judge); Lawrence W. Pierce (Senior Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Frank X. Altimari.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Appeal from: United States District Court for the Western District of New York (Michael A. Telesca, Chief Judge)

#### Decision summary

Charles D. Scanio was convicted in 1988 of structuring a currency transaction and was sentenced to one year of imprisonment followed by two years of supervised release. He later filed a motion under 28 U.S.C. § 2255 attacking the conviction and alleging that his trial counsel was ineffective. The district court dismissed the motion in a one-page order because, on its calculation, Scanio's term of supervised release had expired at midnight on Friday, November 26, 1993, so that he was no longer in custody when the motion was docketed on Monday, November 29, 1993. Counsel's reported account is that he went to the courthouse on November 26 and was told that the clerk's office was closed by order of the chief judge for the extended Thanksgiving holiday.

The court of appeals vacated the dismissal and remanded, by a vote of two to one. Because different Parts of the opinion command different majorities, the result is best understood Part by Part:

- **Part II (Judges Stone and Pierce).** The dismissal cannot stand on a record that does not establish when the term of supervised release legally began and ended. The district court relied on an inaccurate label ("probation") and on the date of Scanio's physical release from a halfway house, November 27, 1991, without showing how that date relates to the legal expiration of the one-year term of imprisonment. The court does not decide when the term began or ended. This Part supports the vacatur.
- **Part III (Judges Stone and Van Graafeiland).** Rule 6(a) of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure, and the next-business-day principle it embodies, does not by its own force continue a sentence that has fully expired. Custody under § 2255 as it stood in 1993 was a status, not a period for an act. Judge Pierce does not join this Part, and Judge Van Graafeiland, who joins it, dissents from the judgment. The vacatur rests on Part II and not on Part III.
- **Part IV (Judges Stone and Pierce).** The court does not adopt a rule that a motion is lodged on the day counsel unsuccessfully tried to file it. If the district court reaches that question on remand, it must do so on notice to both parties and on findings, with the burden on the movant.
- **Part V (Judges Stone and Pierce).** The court does not decide the merits of the ineffective-assistance claim, whether Scanio was in custody on November 29, 1993, or whether to treat the motion as an application for a writ of error coram nobis.

Judge Van Graafeiland would affirm.

#### The case and the result

##### Decision under review

Scanio appeals from a one-page order of the district court dismissing his motion under 28 U.S.C. § 2255, filed November 29, 1993, on the ground that he was no longer in custody when the motion was docketed. The record before the court does not state the date on which the order was entered. The motion challenges the federal conviction and alleges that Scanio's trial counsel rendered ineffective assistance. The merits of that claim are not before this court.

##### Material record

In 1988 Scanio was convicted of structuring a currency transaction under 31 U.S.C. §§ 5324(3) and 5322(a) and was sentenced to one year of imprisonment followed by two years of supervised release. The conviction was affirmed in United States v. Scanio, 900 F.2d 485 (2d Cir. 1990). He began imprisonment on November 30, 1990, was transferred to a Salvation Army halfway house on July 29, 1991, and was released from that facility on November 27, 1991, immediately before Thanksgiving. The district court treated supervised release as beginning on November 27, 1991 and expiring at midnight on November 26, 1993. The record account is that counsel went to the federal courthouse on Friday, November 26, 1993 to file the motion and was told that the clerk's office was closed by order of the chief judge for the extended Thanksgiving holiday; counsel filed on Monday, November 29, the first day the office reopened. The district court's order referred to "probation," although the sentence imposed was supervised release.

No finding states whether counsel had the completed motion on November 26, what he presented, what the closure order said, or what other means of filing were available. No finding explains how the November 27, 1991 release relates to the legal expiration of the one-year term of imprisonment.

##### Jurisdiction

The dismissal is a final order on a collateral motion, appealable under 28 U.S.C. § 1291. The notice of appeal and docket receipts are not among the materials before the court, and no finding is made about the date of the notice; the appeal was argued by both sides on the custody question and nothing before the court indicates untimeliness. Scanio asks for vacatur and consideration of his collateral challenge; the United States asks for affirmance for want of custody at filing.

##### Issues and standards of review

1. Custody at the time of the motion, and the legal commencement and expiration of the supervised-release term. Custody jurisdiction and the construction of the sentence are reviewed independently; findings of fact that the record supports are reviewed for clear error.
2. Whether Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(a), in light of the clerk's-office closure, carries custody to the next business day. The interpretation of Rules 6(a) and 82 and of § 2255 is reviewed independently.
3. Whether counsel's attempt to file on November 26, 1993 bears on the date of filing. The governing filing rules are reviewed independently; the court does not find disputed facts about the attempt, and a remand on this question requires that it be properly reachable and material.

##### Judgment

The order dismissing the motion is **vacated** and the matter is **remanded** for further proceedings, by a vote of two to one. Pierce and Stone vote to vacate and remand; Van Graafeiland dissents and would affirm.

By component:

| Component | Holding or disposition | Judges in the majority on that component | Other judges |
|---|---|---|---|
| Custody calculation (Part II) | The dismissal cannot stand on a record that does not establish when the term of supervised release legally began and ended. | Pierce and Stone | Van Graafeiland dissenting |
| Rule 6(a) (Part III) | Rule 6(a) and the next-business-day principle do not, by their own force, continue a fully expired sentence. | Stone and Van Graafeiland | Pierce does not join this Part |
| Closure, attempted filing and further proceedings (Part IV) | No constructive-lodging rule is adopted on this record; the question is committed to the district court on notice to both parties and on findings, if it is reached. | Pierce and Stone | Van Graafeiland dissenting from the vacatur and remand (he also would not adopt such a rule on this record) |
| Matters not decided (Part V) | The ineffective-assistance merits, any coram nobis vehicle, and the other questions listed in Part V are not decided. | Pierce and Stone join Part V | All three judges agree that the ineffective-assistance merits and coram nobis are not reached |

##### Writings and joins

- **Opinion of the court, by Stone, Circuit Judge.** Part I (background, jurisdiction, review): Stone and Pierce. Part II (custody and the legal term): Stone and Pierce. Part III (Rule 6(a)): Stone and Van Graafeiland. Part IV (closure, attempted filing, remand): Stone and Pierce. Part V (matters not decided) and the closing decretal paragraph: Stone and Pierce.
- **Pierce, Senior Circuit Judge.** Joins Parts I, II, IV and V. Does not join Part III, because he would give the closure effect in deciding whether the motion was filed while the term was still running. He has written no separate opinion.
- **Van Graafeiland, Senior Circuit Judge,** dissenting from the judgment and from Parts II and IV, and joining Part III. His dissent is summarized below under Separate writings.
- No concurring opinion is issued.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

The opinion is the opinion of the court only in the Parts and on the joins stated above: Parts I, II, IV and V and the closing decretal paragraph for Judges Stone and Pierce, and Part III for Judges Stone and Van Graafeiland.

STONE, Circuit Judge:

Charles D. Scanio appeals from an order of the United States District Court for the Western District of New York (Michael A. Telesca, Chief Judge) dismissing his motion under 28 U.S.C. § 2255 on the ground that he was no longer in custody when the motion was docketed on Monday, November 29, 1993. The district court treated Scanio's two-year term of supervised release as having begun on November 27, 1991 and as having expired at midnight on November 26, 1993, the Friday after Thanksgiving, when the clerk's office was closed by order of the chief judge. We hold in Part II that the record does not establish when that term legally began and ended, so that the dismissal cannot stand as entered. We hold in Part III that Rule 6(a) of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure does not, by its own force, continue a sentence that has fully expired until the next day on which the clerk's office is open. We decline in Part IV to adopt on this record a rule treating a motion as lodged on the day counsel unsuccessfully tried to file it, and direct instead that the question be taken up on notice to both parties, and on findings, if it is reached. The order of dismissal is vacated and the matter is remanded. We express no view on the merits of Scanio's claim of ineffective assistance of trial counsel.

##### I. Background, jurisdiction and review

*Joined by Judges Stone and Pierce.*

In 1988 Scanio was convicted of structuring a currency transaction, in violation of 31 U.S.C. §§ 5324(3) and 5322(a). He was sentenced to one year of imprisonment, to be followed by two years of supervised release. This court affirmed the conviction. United States v. Scanio, 900 F.2d 485 (2d Cir. 1990). Scanio began serving his term of imprisonment on November 30, 1990. On July 29, 1991 he was transferred to a halfway house operated by the Salvation Army, and on November 27, 1991, immediately before Thanksgiving, he was released from that facility.

The district court treated the term of supervised release as running from November 27, 1991 to midnight on November 26, 1993. The account in the record of what followed is short. Counsel went to the federal courthouse on Friday, November 26, 1993 to file Scanio's motion and was told that the clerk's office was closed by order of the chief judge for the extended Thanksgiving holiday. Counsel returned and filed on Monday, November 29, the first day the office reopened. The motion, which attacks the federal conviction, alleges that trial counsel was ineffective. The district court dismissed it in a one-page order because Scanio was no longer in custody when it was docketed. The order refers to "probation." The sentence imposed was supervised release.

Several matters are not established. No finding states whether counsel had the completed motion with him on November 26, what he presented at the clerk's office, what the closure order provided, or what other means of filing, if any, were open that day. And the order identifies no sentencing or release document showing how the November 27 release relates to the legal expiration of the one-year term of imprisonment that began on November 30, 1990.

The order is a final disposition of a collateral motion and is appealable under 28 U.S.C. § 1291. The notice of appeal and docket receipts are not among the materials before us, and we have made no finding about the date of the notice. The appeal was argued by both sides on the custody question, nothing before us suggests that it is untimely, and we proceed on that footing. Whether the district court had power to hear the motion is a question of law, as is the construction of the sentence, and we review both independently. Findings of fact that the record supports are reviewed for clear error. The meaning of Rules 6(a) and 82 and of § 2255 is likewise reviewed independently. Whether and when a paper is treated as filed is a legal question, but the facts on which the answer might turn are for the district court; an appellate court does not find them or supply a finding that is missing.

##### II. Custody and the legal term of supervised release

*Joined by Judges Stone and Pierce. Judge Van Graafeiland dissents from this Part.*

Section 2255 is a remedy for a prisoner in custody under sentence of a federal court. We have treated that requirement as jurisdictional. United States v. Brilliant, 274 F.2d 618, 620 (2d Cir. 1960). Custody is measured when the motion is made. A person who has completely served his sentence when he moves is not in custody because of the lingering consequences of the conviction. Maleng v. Cook, 490 U.S. 488, 491-92 (1989). But a person who is in custody when he moves does not lose his case because he is released while it is pending. Carafas v. LaVallee, 391 U.S. 234, 238 (1968). Custody does not require confinement; restraint short of imprisonment may suffice. Jones v. Cunningham, 371 U.S. 236 (1963). A term of supervised release imposed as part of a sentence can therefore be custody under that sentence. Equity cannot supply the condition. The importance of a claim, and sympathy for the movant, do not enlarge the class of persons whom the statute reaches.

Scanio therefore had to be under the sentence on the day his motion was made. Whether he was depends on when his imprisonment legally ended, when supervised release legally began, and when the two-year term expired. Section 3624(e) of Title 18 makes the legal commencement of supervised release the operative fact. Those dates are fixed by the sentence and by the legal arrangement under which Scanio was released, not by the date on which he walked out of a particular building. Nor may a rule developed for another kind of sentence be carried over without examination. Stabile v. United States Bureau of Prisons, 834 F.2d 305 (2d Cir. 1987), concerned the relation between parole supervision and a sentence; it does not by itself resolve how a term of supervised release, which is imposed separately from the term of imprisonment, is to be calculated.

The district court's order does not meet that standard. It rests on a label and on a date. The label is inaccurate: "probation" is not the term imposed, and the differences among probation, parole and supervised release matter to how each is calculated. A misdescription of that kind would not by itself require vacatur if the calculation beneath it were sound and shown. But the order does not show it. Its other foundation, the physical release from the halfway house on November 27, 1991, is an event whose legal significance the order does not explain. It may mark the point at which imprisonment legally ended and supervision began. It may instead record an interim or early release that left the term of imprisonment legally running to a later date, in which case supervised release began later and expired later. The record does not show how the release date relates to the day on which the one-year term legally expired, taking account of any credit and the terms of the placement, or how the first and last days of the two-year term were counted. We cannot assume that every early physical release shortens the total sentence, and the district court could not assume it either.

The gap is material. On the district court's own calculation the term ended three days before the motion was docketed. A difference of a few days in the legal commencement of supervised release, or in the way the term is counted, could decide whether the motion was made in custody. The missing determination depends on documents and findings that an appellate court cannot create.

The strongest contrary position, which our dissenting colleague adopts, is that custody is tested when the motion is made, that supervised release begins when imprisonment ends, that time in a halfway house is part of the term of imprisonment so that the November 27 release started the two-year term, and that the reference to "probation" is therefore an immaterial slip. If the sentence and release documents show that imprisonment legally ended on November 27, 1991, that conclusion will follow, and the district court's calculation will stand. But it is a conclusion to be reached from the legal arrangement, and the order did not reach it that way. We do not hold that the halfway-house placement, or the release from it, has any particular legal significance. We hold that its significance must be determined and that a dismissal resting on an unexamined inference from the date of physical release cannot be sustained.

We therefore hold that a district court that dismisses a § 2255 motion for want of custody must ground its determination in the legal commencement and expiration of the term, established from the sentence and the legal release arrangement, and not in an inaccurate label or an unexamined inference from the date of physical release. Where the record does not establish those dates and the difference is material, vacatur and remand for a supported determination is the proper course. We do not decide when Scanio's term began or ended.

##### III. Rule 6(a)

*Joined by Judges Stone and Van Graafeiland. Judge Pierce does not join this Part. Judge Van Graafeiland joins this Part although he dissents from the judgment; the vacatur rests on Part II and not on Part III, and Part III states the rule that governs the remand.*

Scanio argues that, because the clerk's office was closed on November 26 by order of the court's own chief judge, Rule 6(a) should carry his time to the following Monday, so that the motion docketed on November 29 was made while he was still in custody.

Rule 6(a) prescribes how to compute a period of time prescribed or allowed by the rules, by court order or by statute when its last day falls on a weekend or legal holiday and, since 1985, when the act to be done is the filing of a paper and the last day is one on which weather or other conditions have made the clerk's office inaccessible. The Federal Rules, however, do not extend or limit the jurisdiction of the United States district courts. Fed. R. Civ. P. 82.

Scanio is right that calling a requirement jurisdictional does not by itself place it beyond ordinary computation rules. In Union National Bank v. Lamb, 337 U.S. 38, 41 (1949), the Supreme Court applied the liberality of Rule 6(a) to the statutory time limit for taking an appeal to that Court, and in Simon v. Commissioner, 176 F.2d 230, 232 (2d Cir. 1949), this court applied the rule to the three-month statutory period for seeking review of a Tax Court decision. Both decisions concern periods within which an act must be done. They show that a deadline is not immune from Rule 6(a) because it is jurisdictional. They do not show that a status the statute requires continues because a clerk's office is closed.

Custody is a status. Section 2255, as it stood in 1993, did not fix a period within which a motion had to be made; the statute asked whether the movant was in custody under the sentence. There is no period for Rule 6(a) to compute. The question is not whether a deadline was met on the next business day but whether a sentence that had fully run was still running. A rule that kept a person in custody solely to preserve access to a forum would alter the statutory condition itself, not the manner of satisfying it, and would use a rule of procedure to enlarge the court's power, which Rule 82 forbids. Maleng and Carafas point the same way: the consequences of a completed sentence do not substitute for custody, and the principle that release after a proper commencement does not defeat the case presupposes custody when the case was commenced.

We hold that Rule 6(a), and the next-business-day principle it embodies, does not by its own force continue a fully expired sentence. We need not decide whether the closure on November 26 was a condition that made the clerk's office inaccessible within the meaning of the rule, because even if it was, the rule governs the computation of periods and not the duration of a sentence.

Scanio's response is that the court's own closure should not cost him a channel that was open to him while he held the required status. The equities of that argument are real. But equity cannot lengthen a sentence, and the closure did not extend Scanio's term. This Part decides only that Rule 6(a) cannot supply the custody the statute requires. It does not decide whether the motion was made while custody existed. That question is distinct, and Part IV addresses it.

##### IV. The closure, counsel's attempt to file, and further proceedings

*Joined by Judges Stone and Pierce. Judge Van Graafeiland dissents from this Part and from the vacatur and remand; he also would not adopt a constructive-lodging rule on this record.*

Scanio's reliance on the closure and on Rule 6(a) is preserved. A different theory, that a completed motion actually presented at the clerk's office on November 26 should be treated as lodged on that day, is suggested by counsel's reported visit, but it is not shown to have been presented in those terms to the district court or developed in the submissions here. The theory would not extend custody. It would identify November 26, when on the district court's own calculation the term was still running, as the day on which the motion was made, and so would preserve the requirement that custody and filing coincide.

The law recognizes that the legally operative filing event need not always be the later docket entry. In Houston v. Lack, 487 U.S. 266 (1988), the Court held that a pro se prisoner's notice of appeal is filed when it is delivered to prison authorities for forwarding to the court. Houston rests on the prisoner's dependence on those authorities, and it does not control here. Scanio had counsel, who was not dependent on any prison mail system and who, on the account we have, went to the courthouse himself. Houston is nonetheless instructive, because it shows that deciding when a paper was filed is a different question from enlarging a court's power. Extending the principle to a represented movant whose completed motion was refused at the clerk's office solely because of an official closure would be a new rule. It would have to be justified openly and bounded by administrable limits, and it could not be adopted without a determination that the question is properly reached and without giving both parties a fair opportunity to address it.

The record does not permit that now. There is no finding that counsel had the completed motion on November 26, that he presented it at the place designated for filing, that the closure alone prevented its receipt, or that no other means of lodging it was available. An appellate court does not find those facts, and the government has not had an opportunity to meet the theory in the form in which it would have to be decided. We therefore do not adopt a constructive-lodging rule, and we do not extend Houston. We do not assume that a visit to the courthouse, an intention to file, or papers prepared elsewhere amounts to a filing. We hold only that the question, if it must be reached, should be taken up on notice to both parties and on findings if the facts are disputed.

The remand follows from the structure of the case. The district court must first determine, from the sentence and the legal release arrangement, when Scanio's term of supervised release legally began and expired. If the term was running when the motion was docketed on November 29, 1993, the motion was made in custody, and Scanio's later release does not defeat it. Carafas, 391 U.S. at 238. The district court may then address the motion's remaining requirements and its merits, on which we express no view. If the term had expired before the motion was docketed, the district court shall give both parties notice and an opportunity to be heard on whether the closure and counsel's attempt to file may bear on the date of filing and on whether that question is properly before it. If it reaches the question, it shall determine, with the burden of proof on the movant, whether counsel had and actually presented the completed motion at the clerk's office on November 26 while custody remained, whether the closure alone prevented its receipt, what other means of filing were available, and when filing first became possible. If the district court concludes that the theory is not properly before it, is unavailable under governing law, or is not supported by proof, and that the term had expired before the first effective filing, it shall dismiss for want of custody, because equity cannot replace the jurisdiction that custody supplies. Any ruling on the legal question is reviewable in the ordinary course, and we decide nothing about it today.

##### V. Matters not decided

*Joined by Judges Stone and Pierce. All three judges agree that the ineffective-assistance merits and coram nobis are not reached.*

We do not decide the merits of Scanio's claim of ineffective assistance of trial counsel, and nothing in this opinion promises collateral relief. We do not decide that Scanio was or was not in custody on November 29, 1993, or when his term of supervised release began or ended. We do not decide whether the November 26 closure fell within the inaccessibility provision of Rule 6(a), or whether a constructive-lodging rule is available for a represented movant. We do not convert the motion into an application for a writ of error coram nobis. That remedy requires its own showing under United States v. Morgan, 346 U.S. 502 (1954), and cannot be granted by relabeling an unsuccessful § 2255 motion.

The order dismissing the motion is vacated, and the matter is remanded to the district court for further proceedings consistent with this opinion.

*Closing decretal paragraph joined by Judges Stone and Pierce.*

#### Separate writings

The source for this decision establishes no concurring opinion and no separately written opinion by any judge. It establishes the following positions, which are reported here as the source states them and are not written up as full opinions.

##### Dissent of Judge Van Graafeiland (summary only)

Judge Van Graafeiland, Senior Circuit Judge, dissents from the judgment and from Parts II and IV, and joins Part III. No separate opinion text for the dissent is supplied; what follows is the summary of it.

Judge Van Graafeiland would affirm. In his view custody is tested when the motion is filed, and the controlling fact is the legal commencement of the supervised-release term. Under 18 U.S.C. § 3624(e) supervised release begins on release from imprisonment, and under 18 U.S.C. § 3624(c) time in a halfway house is part of the term of imprisonment, so the two-year term began with Scanio's release from the halfway house on November 27, 1991 and ended before the motion was docketed. The district court's reference to "probation" was a misdescription that does not matter to that conclusion.

He would hold that Rule 6(a) computes periods for acts, that § 2255 as then in force prescribed no filing period to compute, that Rule 82 bars enlarging jurisdiction by rule, that Lamb and Simon concern time periods and not a custodial status, and that collateral consequences of an expired sentence do not satisfy the custody requirement, relying on Brilliant, Carafas, Maleng and, for non-physical restraint, Jones. He would not adopt a constructive-lodging rule on this record, would not extend Houston to a represented movant or rest on assumed facts about tender, and would order no hearing. He would not reach the ineffective-assistance merits or coram nobis; his affirmance would be without prejudice to any separate vehicle Scanio may be able to establish under Morgan, as to which he expresses no view.

##### Position of Judge Pierce on Part III (no separate opinion)

Judge Pierce, Senior Circuit Judge, has written no separate opinion. He joins Parts I, II, IV and V and does not join Part III. He does not accept the conclusion in Part III that the closure and Rule 6(a) cannot be given effect in computing whether the motion was filed while the term was running. He would give the closure effect in deciding that question. That disagreement does not affect Parts I, II, IV and V, which he joins, and he would not rest the judgment on any holding that Rule 6(a) cannot reach the closure.

#### What the decision does

##### Controlling holdings

1. **Custody and the legal term (Part II; Stone and Pierce).**
   - Rule: a § 2255 movant must be in custody under the challenged federal sentence when the motion is made; supervision after release, including supervised release, can be custody; collateral consequences of a fully expired sentence cannot substitute; a case properly commenced in custody survives later release; equity cannot supply the condition.
   - Authority: United States v. Brilliant, 274 F.2d 618, 620 (2d Cir. 1960); Carafas v. LaVallee, 391 U.S. 234, 238 (1968); Maleng v. Cook, 490 U.S. 488, 491-92 (1989); Jones v. Cunningham, 371 U.S. 236 (1963); 28 U.S.C. § 2255; 18 U.S.C. § 3624(e).
   - Test: the legal commencement and expiration of the term must be established from the sentence and the legal release arrangement, not from an inaccurate label or an unexamined inference from the date of physical release.
   - Standard of review: independent as to custody jurisdiction and construction, clear error as to supported findings.
   - Application: the order rested on the label "probation" and on the November 27, 1991 release; neither establishes the legal term; the margin (three days on the district court's own calculation) makes the gap material; vacatur and remand for a supported determination follows. The Part does not decide when the term began or ended.

2. **Rule 6(a) (Part III; Stone and Van Graafeiland).**
   - Rule: Rule 6(a) computes periods of time; it does not by its own force continue a terminated custodial status, and Rule 82 forbids using the rules to extend jurisdiction.
   - Authority: Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(a), 82; Union National Bank v. Lamb, 337 U.S. 38, 41 (1949), and Simon v. Commissioner, 176 F.2d 230, 232 (2d Cir. 1949), followed and distinguished as decisions about periods for acts; Maleng; Carafas.
   - Test: whether the question is the computation of a period for an act or the continuation of a status the statute requires.
   - Application: custody under § 2255 as it stood in 1993 was a status, not a period; the next-business-day principle cannot lengthen a sentence that had fully run. The Part does not decide whether the November 26 closure was a condition making the clerk's office inaccessible, or whether the motion may be treated as made earlier by another route.
   - Two votes command this proposition. It is joined by a judge who dissents from the judgment, and the vacatur rests on Part II and not on Part III. Part III states the rule that governs the remand. Judge Pierce does not join it.

3. **Attempted filing and further proceedings (Part IV; Stone and Pierce).**
   - Rule: the date on which a paper is legally filed is a question distinct from enlarging a court's power; Houston v. Lack, 487 U.S. 266 (1988), is analogous and does not control a represented movant. No new filing rule is adopted.
   - Trigger for further proceedings: the term had expired before the November 29, 1993 docketing.
   - Procedure and burden: notice and an opportunity to be heard for both parties; if the question is reached, the movant must prove that counsel had and actually presented the completed motion at the designated place on November 26 while custody remained, that the closure alone prevented receipt, what other means of filing were available, and when filing first became possible.
   - Application: the record contains no findings on those predicates, and an appellate court does not find them.

##### Precedential reach and treatment of precedent

- **Publication.** The opinion is published. It is the opinion of the court in the Parts and on the joins stated above.
- **Part II (Stone and Pierce).** Precedential on two joins. A district court that dismisses a § 2255 motion for want of custody must ground the determination in the legal commencement and expiration of the term, established from the sentence and the legal release arrangement. Limited to a record in which the order rested on an inaccurate label and an unexamined inference from the date of physical release. It does not decide when Scanio's term began or ended, and it does not hold that a halfway-house placement or release has any particular legal significance. Brilliant, Carafas, Maleng and Jones are followed; Stabile is treated as a parole decision that does not by itself resolve a supervised-release calculation.
- **Part III (Stone and Van Graafeiland).** Precedential on two joins. Rule 6(a) and the next-business-day principle do not by their own force continue a fully expired sentence; Lamb and Simon are followed as decisions about periods for acts and distinguished as to a continuing status. The Part is joined by a judge who dissents from the judgment, and the vacatur rests on Part II and not on Part III; Part III states the rule that governs the remand. It does not decide whether the November 26 closure made the clerk's office inaccessible within Rule 6(a), and it does not decide whether the motion may be treated as made earlier by another route. Judge Pierce does not join it.
- **Part IV (Stone and Pierce).** Precedential on two joins only for the procedure directed on remand: notice and an opportunity to be heard for both parties, findings on the tender predicates with the burden on the movant, and dismissal if custody had expired and the theory is unavailable or unproved. No constructive-lodging rule is adopted, and Houston v. Lack is neither extended nor limited beyond its stated pro se prisoner setting.
- **Dicta and separate positions.** The statements in Part V of what is not decided are not holdings. The dissent and Judge Pierce's stated non-join are not circuit law.
- **Party-specific relief and law of the case.** The vacatur and remand bind the parties in this litigation. The directions in Parts II to IV govern the remand and are the law of this case on the issues they address.
- **Earlier law.** No earlier decision of this court is overruled or limited. The Supreme Court decisions cited are followed as binding authority according to their scope.

##### Relief and effect on the parties

The order of the district court dismissing the § 2255 motion is vacated, and the matter is remanded to the United States District Court for the Western District of New York for further proceedings consistent with the opinion. The remand is limited. The district court is to determine the legal commencement and expiration of the term of supervised release from the sentence and the legal release arrangement. If the term was running when the motion was docketed on November 29, 1993, it may proceed to the motion's remaining requirements and merits. If the term had expired, it is to give both parties notice and an opportunity to be heard on the closure and counsel's attempt to file, make the findings described in Part IV if it reaches the question, and dismiss for want of custody if the theory is not properly before it, is unavailable, or is not proved. No view is expressed on the merits of the ineffective-assistance claim, and no coram nobis relief is granted or directed.

##### Mandate and remand

No mandate has issued. Under Fed. R. App. P. 40(a) and 41(a) as in force on the date of decision, a petition for rehearing may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order or by local rule, and the mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order. A timely petition for rehearing stays the mandate until the petition is disposed of, and if the petition is denied the mandate issues 7 days after the order denying it unless that time is shortened or enlarged. The remand takes effect on issuance of the mandate. No petition for rehearing, application to stay the mandate, or other filing is recorded as of the date of this decision.

#### Historical comparison

On October 11, 1994, the Second Circuit (Van Graafeiland, Pierce, Altimari) affirmed dismissal of Scanio's section 2255 motion for lack of custody at filing, 2-1. Judge Altimari wrote, joined by Judge Van Graafeiland; Judge Pierce dissented, questioning the custody calculation and the rejection of the holiday closure computation principle. The majority treated the supervised-release term as expired before the November 29, 1993 filing and held that Rule 6(a) computation cannot extend custody or enlarge section 2255 jurisdiction. No hearing was ordered.

The simulated judgment changes the outcome: it vacates and remands rather than affirms. The Rule 6(a) rule is essentially unchanged, so the changed judgment rests on the custody calculation, held unsupported on this record, and on the remedy, not on a different view of Rule 6(a).

Source: Scanio v. United States, 37 F.3d 858, 858-65 (2d Cir. 1994).

### [In re Secured Equipment Trust of Eastern Air Lines, Inc.](../cases/in-re-secured-equipment-trust-of-eastern-air-lines/)

Decision date: October 17, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): Nos. 94-5015, 94-5017  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Published; precedential. Judgment of the district court reversed and remanded, by a vote of two to one. The mandate has not issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/in-re-secured-equipment-trust-of-eastern-air-lines/events/1994-10-17-decision.md](../cases/in-re-secured-equipment-trust-of-eastern-air-lines/events/1994-10-17-decision.md)

Historical panel: Amalya L. Kearse (Active Circuit Judge); Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); Allen G. Schwartz (Active District Judge, S.D.N.Y., sitting by designation).  
Simulation panel: Amalya L. Kearse (Active Circuit Judge); Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Allen G. Schwartz.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

*The opinions below are simulation text. They are simulated judicial opinions based on the finalized decision and are not verbatim historical reporter text.*

#### Decision summary

The question was whether the Secured Equipment Trust, created by Eastern Air Lines to raise $500 million through certificates secured by part of its aircraft fleet, is a "business trust." If it is, it counts as a "person" against whom an involuntary Chapter 11 case may be commenced under 11 U.S.C. § 303(a). The bankruptcy court dismissed the involuntary petition on the ground that the Trust was not a qualifying business trust, and the district court affirmed.

The court of appeals reversed. Judge Kearse, writing for the court and joined by Judge Stone, held that the Trust is a business trust. The Code does not define the term, and the court answered the question as one of federal law by examining the whole arrangement: the trust's formation, its purposes, its investment structure, the operations its instruments authorize, and the operations its trustee has in fact carried on. The court rested its holding on the certificate-financed capital-raising structure, the Trust's holding of the equipment, the commercial leasing and management powers the indenture conferred from the outset, and the large-scale exercise of those powers. It held that neither a fixed return to the holders nor secured-financing economics alone takes a trust out of the category, and that the absence of a residual profit interest in the holders does not defeat classification.

The court limited its holding. It did not hold that post-default management alone is dispositive, that eligibility follows from financial size, or that every collateral or indenture trustee is a business trust. It also stressed that eligibility decides very little: no order for relief is directed, no priority is altered, and the remaining requirements of section 303 and any properly presented grounds for dismissal or abstention go back to the bankruptcy court.

Judge Altimari dissented, joined by no other judge. He would have held that the Trust is not a business trust and would have affirmed the dismissal.

#### The case and the result

**The dispute and the record.** Eastern Air Lines, Inc. created the Trust to raise $500 million through certificates secured by part of its aircraft fleet. In form the transaction was a sale of certificates, a purchase of aircraft with the proceeds, and a lease of the aircraft back to Eastern; the parties agree that its economic substance was a secured financing. The certificates were issued in three series with different priorities, interest rates, and maturities. First Fidelity Bank served as collateral trustee, and separate trustees represented the holders of the series. Eastern's rent was set to service the certificates' principal, premium, and interest; the indenture returned any excess to Eastern and provided that after full payment the equipment would be reconveyed to Eastern and the Trust dissolved. On default, the collateral trustee was authorized to possess, hold, lease, and sell the equipment.

Eastern entered Chapter 11 on March 9, 1989 and stopped making timely payments. A January 1991 stipulation returned aircraft and $230 million in equipment proceeds to the Trust, and the trustee thereafter maintained, marketed, leased, and sold aircraft with retained legal, accounting, and aviation help, taking possession of sixty-seven commercial jets and 165 engines, of which forty-seven jets remained under management two years later.

**The petition and the proceedings below.** On March 28, 1991, LNC Investments, Charter National Life Insurance Company, and Magten Asset Management Corporation, holders of $54.2 million in second- and third-priority certificates, filed the involuntary petition; the Resolution Trust Corporation later joined. The matter was transferred from New Jersey to the Southern District of New York. The collateral trustee and United Jersey Bank sought dismissal; the petitioning holders and Shawmut Bank Connecticut, National Association, the third-series trustee, opposed it. The bankruptcy court (Burton R. Lifland, Bankruptcy Judge) dismissed the petition on the ground that the Trust was not a qualifying business trust, in a decision reported at 153 B.R. 409 (Bankr. S.D.N.Y. 1993). The district court (John E. Sprizzo, District Judge) affirmed. The petitioning holders seek reinstatement of the involuntary case; the Trust and the trustees who sought dismissal seek affirmance.

**Jurisdiction.** The district court's final disposition of the bankruptcy appeal is reviewable under 28 U.S.C. § 158(d). The materials before the court disclose no question about the timeliness of the appeals.

**Issues.** The first issue is eligibility: whether the Trust is a business trust, and so a "person" against whom an involuntary case may be commenced under 11 U.S.C. § 303(a). The standard of review is that the bankruptcy court's legal conclusions are reviewed independently and its findings of fact for clear error. In re Ionosphere Clubs, Inc., 922 F.2d 984, 988-89 (2d Cir. 1990). The petitioning parties must establish that the Trust is an eligible debtor. The second issue concerns reach and remedy. Eligibility is the only question that was decided below and the only one decided here. Other section 303 conditions, creditor qualification, grounds for an order for relief, priorities, and plan treatment were not resolved through the eligibility ruling.

**Judgment.**

1. Eligibility ruling (the Trust is not a business trust): reversed. Judges Kearse and Stone for reversal; Judge Altimari would affirm.
2. Dismissal of the involuntary petition: the district court's judgment affirming the dismissal is reversed, and the case is remanded for further proceedings on the remaining requirements of section 303 and any properly presented grounds for dismissal or abstention. Judges Kearse and Stone for; Judge Altimari against.
3. Relief and collateral questions: no order for relief is directed, and no priority is altered. The court decides nothing about creditor qualification, the grounds for an order for relief, priorities, or plan treatment. No judge on the panel would decide those matters.

Vote: two to one.

**Opinion and joins.** Opinion of the court by Judge Kearse, joined by Judge Stone in full. Dissenting opinion by Judge Altimari, joined by no other judge.

**Where the judges agree and differ.** All three judges apply the independent standard of review, place the burden on the petitioning parties, read the undefined term from the Code and the whole arrangement, and reject a categorical residual-profit requirement; all three would decide none of the questions reserved in Part V of the opinion of the court. Judges Kearse and Stone agree on the judgment, on the holding stated in Part III, on the limits stated in Part IV, and on the remedy. Judge Altimari disagrees on the judgment, on the weight of the post-default operations, and on the remedy. Each proposition of the opinion of the court has two joins.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

Before KEARSE, ALTIMARI, and STONE-KHOURY, Circuit Judges.

KEARSE, Circuit Judge, joined by STONE-KHOURY, Circuit Judge:

**I. The statutory question and the standard of review.**

The district court's final disposition of the bankruptcy appeal is reviewable here under 28 U.S.C. § 158(d), and the materials before us disclose no question about the timeliness of the appeals. We review the bankruptcy court's legal conclusions independently and its findings of fact for clear error. Ionosphere Clubs, 922 F.2d at 988-89. The meaning of the statutory term "business trust" and its application to the instruments that created this Trust are legal questions, and a legal definition does not become insulated from review merely because its application depends on a detailed financial arrangement. The facts that matter come from the indenture and from the bankruptcy court's own account of what the trustee did, and we disturb no finding of fact. The petitioning parties bear the burden of establishing that the Trust is an eligible debtor.

Section 303(a) permits an involuntary case to be commenced only under Chapter 7 or 11, and only against a person, except a farmer, a family farmer, or a corporation that is not a moneyed, business, or commercial corporation, that may be a debtor under the chapter invoked. Under 11 U.S.C. § 101(41) a person includes a corporation, and under § 101(9)(A)(v) a corporation includes a business trust. The Code does not define "business trust." No decision of the Supreme Court or of this court that controls the construction of the term has been identified, and decisions classifying entities under other statutes do not replace the Code's own text. The question is open in this circuit.

**II. The test.**

Three considerations guide the construction. First, Congress placed the business trust within the definition of corporation. That placement distinguishes an entity that conducts organized business through trustees from an ordinary donative or custodial trust whose incidental transactions merely preserve property. Second, the Bankruptcy Act's predecessor definition reached a business conducted through trustees where beneficial ownership or interest was represented by certificates or other written instruments, and the reports accompanying the 1978 Code describe its corporation definition as similar to the Act's. That continuity supports attention to collective investment, trustee management, and commercial operations. It does not mean that every instrument labeled a trust, or every holder of a security interest, is an eligible debtor. Third, lower-court decisions persuade but do not bind us. In re St. Augustine Trust, 109 B.R. 494, 496 (Bankr. M.D. Fla. 1990), correctly directs attention to the trust documents and the whole arrangement rather than to the bare fact that some business-like activity occurs. We agree with that much, and we adopt no enumerated list of characteristics as a checklist.

Whether a trust is a business trust under the Code is a question of federal law, answered by examining the whole arrangement: the trust's formation, its purposes, its investment structure, the operations its instruments authorize, and the operations its trustee has in fact carried on. The presence of some business-like conduct does not alone decide the question, and the absence of any single feature does not alone defeat classification.

**III. Application.**

On this record the Trust is a business trust.

The Trust was formed to raise capital from investors, and it did so on an institutional scale: $500 million through certificates in three series differing in priority, rate, and maturity, with a collateral trustee and separate series trustees. This was not a family settlement that happened to sell an asset. The investors' interests are represented by certificates, the form of beneficial interest that the predecessor definition singled out, and their capital was committed to an organized commercial transaction, the purchase of a fleet of aircraft and its lease to Eastern. The equipment was held in the Trust, to be reconveyed to Eastern after full payment. Leasing equipment for rent is itself a commercial activity, and the rent was the means by which the investors' return was produced.

The Trust's opponents emphasize that the holders' return was fixed and that any surplus reverted to Eastern. We accept those facts, and we accept the parties' agreement that the transaction was economically a secured financing. Neither fact removes the Trust from the statute. Many businesses earn specified rather than residual returns, and a contractually fixed return is no less an investment return because the investor lacks unlimited upside. Nothing in the Code's inclusion of business trusts makes participation in residual profit the boundary of the category, and the predecessor definition's focus on trustee-conducted business and certificated interests points the other way. The question is whether a financing enterprise conducted in this institutional form can be a business trust, not whether its participants must pretend that the underlying obligation was something other than what the parties agree it was. We hold that neither a fixed return nor secured-financing economics alone excludes a trust from the category, and that the absence of a residual profit interest in the holders does not defeat classification.

Nor was the trustee a mere custodian. The same indenture that fixed the holders' return authorized the collateral trustee, on default, to possess, hold, lease, and sell the equipment: in substance, to lease and dispose of an aircraft fleet on the certificateholders' behalf. Those powers were conferred when the Trust was formed, and they show what institution the parties created. We do not suggest that later distress allows a trustee to manufacture eligibility; a trust that was not a business trust at its formation does not become one because its trustee finds itself with assets to manage. Here, however, the original arrangement contained substantial continuing commercial leasing and management powers, and the trustee exercised them. After the January 1991 stipulation returned aircraft and $230 million in proceeds, the trustee maintained, marketed, leased, and sold aircraft with retained legal, accounting, and aviation professionals; it took possession of sixty-seven commercial jets and 165 engines and was still managing forty-seven jets two years later. We do not rest on the size of the collateral pool or on the number of professionals as independently making the Trust a business. Those facts matter because they make concrete the institutional function the instrument assigned from the start and show that the authorized powers were real and continuing, not remote contingencies.

We hold, accordingly, that this certificate-financed, trustee-operated equipment enterprise is a business trust within 11 U.S.C. § 101(9)(A)(v) where its original arrangement includes substantial continuing commercial leasing and management powers, as it does here, and where those powers have in fact been exercised, as they were here. The certificated capital-raising structure, the Trust's holding of the equipment, the original leasing and management powers, and the exercise of those powers together satisfy the statutory category.

**IV. The principal contrary argument.**

The strongest argument for affirmance is that the Trust does not conduct an enterprise for its own account. It holds collateral for a debt, returns any surplus to Eastern, and liquidates after default to enforce the security rather than to pursue a mission of its own. Professional management after default is common to secured financings, and a rule that treated it as sufficient would turn ordinary security trustees into involuntary debtors at the instance of junior holders.

The concern is real, and our holding is drawn to respect it. A trustee whose role is simply to hold a lien and distribute receipts does not become a business trust because it employs counsel or sells collateral after default. We do not hold that post-default management alone is dispositive, that eligibility follows from financial size or from the fact that a trustee performs some commercial transaction, or that every collateral or indenture trustee is a business trust. What distinguishes this Trust is the combination we have described: an organized certificate investment, an integrated equipment transaction, and substantial continuing commercial powers, authorized at the outset and exercised on a large scale. That description explains why the category is satisfied on this record. It is not a checklist in which every characteristic must appear in every future case. Nor were the Trust's leasing and sale powers less commercial because they were conferred to protect the holders' return.

The Trust's opponents also point out that the series trustees had already filed claims in Eastern's bankruptcy and that ordinary proceedings could address actionable trustee misconduct. The desirability of consolidating disputes, and the existence of another forum, do not rewrite the definition of the proposed debtor, and we place no weight on them in construing the statute. Eligibility, moreover, decides very little. It does not establish that an order for relief must issue, and it does not allow junior certificateholders to erase negotiated priorities or to obtain relief merely because the equipment lost value. The bankruptcy court must still decide whether the remaining requirements for an involuntary case are met, any properly presented grounds for dismissal or abstention, and the treatment of claims under their governing sources. The availability of those protections is not a substitute for reading the eligibility provision; it confirms that classifying the debtor need not predecide every later dispute.

**V. Matters not decided.**

We announce no universal profit-purpose prerequisite and no universal checklist for all trusts, and we do not decide whether trusts with no profit purpose fall outside the Code. We do not decide what weight, if any, decisions in other statutory settings that speak of participants sharing in an enterprise's gains carry in construing § 101(9)(A)(v); our holding rests on the Code, its predecessor, and this record. We express no view on creditor qualification, the grounds for an order for relief, priorities, or plan treatment, or on whether section 303(a)'s separate exception for a corporation that is not a moneyed, business, or commercial corporation applies to the Trust.

**VI. Disposition.**

The judgment of the district court is reversed, and the case is remanded to the district court for remand to the bankruptcy court to determine the remaining requirements of section 303 and any properly presented grounds for dismissal or abstention, consistent with this opinion. See 28 U.S.C. § 2106. We direct no order for relief and alter no priority.

#### Separate opinions

**Dissenting opinion, Judge Altimari, joined by no other judge.**

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision. The finalized record states this dissent in summary form, and it is reported here as the record states it, in the third person.*

Judge Altimari would affirm the dismissal and does not join the holding, the application, or the disposition in the opinion of the court. He proceeds from the same standard of review, the same allocation of the burden, and the same inquiry into the whole arrangement. On that inquiry he would hold that the Trust is not a business trust. The Trust was formed to secure a debt; its holders receive a fixed contractual return; any surplus returns to Eastern; and on payment the equipment is reconveyed and the Trust dissolved. The possession, leasing, and sale that followed Eastern's default are incidental to protecting and recovering on the collateral, and the volume of that management does not convert a collateral-holding arrangement into a business trust. A contrary rule would expose ordinary collateral and indenture trustees to involuntary petitions by junior holders.

He would not make profit generation a universal prerequisite, would not fix the status of all liquidating trusts, would not treat the multi-factor tests applied by bankruptcy courts as binding, and would confine the holding to this indenture. On his view the dismissal stands and no remand is needed, and the remaining section 303 predicates, priorities, plan consequences, and the broader questions concerning nonprofit and liquidating trusts remain undecided.

#### What the decision does

- **Controlling holding.** A certificate-financed, trustee-operated equipment enterprise is a business trust within 11 U.S.C. § 101(9)(A)(v) where its original arrangement includes substantial continuing commercial leasing and management powers and where those powers have in fact been exercised. The certificated capital-raising structure, the Trust's holding of the equipment, the original leasing and management powers, and the exercise of those powers together satisfy the statutory category. Neither a fixed return nor secured-financing economics alone excludes a trust from the category, and the absence of a residual profit interest in the holders does not defeat classification. The limits are stated in Parts IV and V of the opinion: post-default management alone is not dispositive; eligibility does not follow from financial size or from a trustee's performance of some commercial transaction; not every collateral or indenture trustee is a business trust; there is no universal profit-purpose prerequisite and no universal checklist; and the question whether trusts with no profit purpose fall outside the Code is not decided. The statement that a trustee who merely holds a lien and distributes receipts is not a business trust marks the limit of the holding and is not an independent rule for such trustees.
- **Legal effect and treatment of precedent.** The opinion is published and precedential. The holding, stated in Part III and limited by Parts IV and V, is the holding of the court on the votes of Judges Kearse and Stone, and it is binding circuit precedent on the classification of a certificate-financed, trustee-operated equipment enterprise whose original arrangement includes substantial continuing commercial leasing and management powers that have been exercised. The standard of review follows Ionosphere Clubs. The decision in St. Augustine Trust is treated as persuasive authority and followed only in its direction to examine the trust documents and the whole arrangement. No earlier decision of this court fixed a definition of business trust under the Code, and none is overruled or displaced. The reversal and remand are party-specific relief. The determination that the Trust is a business trust is the law of the case on remand as to eligibility. Nothing in the decision has preclusive effect on any other section 303 question, on priorities, or on the treatment of any claim. The dissent is not circuit law.
- **Effect on the parties.** The judgment of the district court affirming the dismissal of the involuntary petition is reversed. No order for relief is directed, and no priority is altered. Creditor qualification, the grounds for an order for relief, priorities, and plan treatment are not decided, and the Court expresses no view on whether section 303(a)'s separate exception for a corporation that is not a moneyed, business, or commercial corporation applies to the Trust.
- **Relief and next stage.** The case is remanded to the district court for remand to the bankruptcy court to determine the remaining requirements of section 303 and any properly presented grounds for dismissal or abstention, consistent with the opinion. See 28 U.S.C. § 2106.
- **Mandate and remand.** The mandate has not issued. Under Federal Rule of Appellate Procedure 41(a) as in force on the date of this decision, the mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order, and the timely filing of a petition for rehearing under Rule 40 stays it until the petition is decided. A petition for rehearing may be filed within the time Rule 40(a) fixes, which is generally 14 days after entry of judgment unless an order or local rule alters it. No petition and no other filing is recorded in this decision. Proceedings in the bankruptcy court on remand await issuance of the mandate and the district court's remand.

#### Historical comparison

Historically, on 1994-10-17, a Second Circuit panel of Judges Kearse, Altimari, and Schwartz affirmed the dismissal of the involuntary petition, 2 to 1. Judge Altimari wrote, joined by Judge Schwartz; Judge Kearse dissented. The majority treated the Trust as an arrangement preserving collateral for Eastern's secured financing, not an eligible business trust, regarded the post-default operations as incidental, and declined to make profit generation a universal prerequisite. In re Secured Equipment Trust of Eastern Air Lines, Inc., 38 F.3d 86, 89-93 (2d Cir. 1994). The simulated panel (Kearse, Altimari, Stone) reached the opposite judgment: it reversed and remanded for the remaining section 303 questions, 2 to 1, with Judge Kearse writing for the court, joined by Judge Stone, and Judge Altimari dissenting. The judgment changed, and the reasoning changed with it, since the simulated court found a business trust on the certificated structure and the original leasing and management powers actually exercised. Both decisions declined to adopt a universal profit-purpose rule, and neither decided the remaining conditions for relief or priorities.

### [DeWeerth v. Baldinger](../cases/deweerth-v-baldinger/)

Decision date: October 27, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): Nos. 93-7144, 93-7146  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Published opinion of the court; precedential. This is the court's amended disposition of the appeals. The version of these appeals dated May 16, 1994 is the version under reconsideration, and nothing is stated here about its content. Reversed; the earlier final judgment is reinstated; remanded only for ministerial steps. No mandate is entered by this opinion.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/deweerth-v-baldinger/events/1994-10-27-decision.md](../cases/deweerth-v-baldinger/events/1994-10-27-decision.md)

Historical panel: Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); John M. Walker, Jr. (Active Circuit Judge); Richard Owen (Senior District Judge, S.D.N.Y., sitting by designation).  
Simulation panel: Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); John M. Walker, Jr. (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Richard Owen.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling.
