It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Court below: United States District Court for the Southern District of New York, Vincent L. Broderick, District Judge.

#### Decision summary

##### The case and the result

**The dispute.** Gerda Dorothea DeWeerth inherited a Monet painting in 1922; her father had acquired it in 1908. The painting was moved for safekeeping during the war and disappeared in 1945 from a castle where American soldiers had been quartered. That chronology does not identify who took the painting or establish that a particular soldier did. Wildenstein & Co., Inc. handled the painting in the 1950s, and Edith Marks Baldinger bought it in 1957 in good faith. DeWeerth located the painting in 1982, demanded its return, and Baldinger refused.

**How the case reached this court.** In the ensuing diversity action the district court found for DeWeerth and rejected the limitations and laches defenses on the record before it. DeWeerth v. Baldinger, 658 F. Supp. 688 (S.D.N.Y. 1987). This court reversed, predicting that New York would require reasonable diligence in locating stolen property in addition to demand and refusal. DeWeerth v. Baldinger, 836 F.2d 103 (2d Cir. 1987). Supreme Court review was denied on June 13, 1988, and the judgment became final. In Solomon R. Guggenheim Foundation v. Lubell, 77 N.Y.2d 311, 317-21 (1991), the New York Court of Appeals rejected a due-diligence addition to the limitations rule, while leaving diligence and prejudice relevant to laches. DeWeerth unsuccessfully asked this court to recall its mandate, then moved in the district court under Fed. R. Civ. P. 60(b). The district court reopened the judgment, and Baldinger and Wildenstein appealed. Wildenstein is a third-party defendant, and its position is distinct from the ownership dispute between DeWeerth and Baldinger.

**The decision under review.** Baldinger and Wildenstein appeal from the order of the district court granting relief under Rule 60(b) from a judgment that had become final, and from the resulting judgment. The order is reported at 804 F. Supp. 539 (S.D.N.Y. 1992); the papers before the court do not state its date of entry. The district court reasoned principally that the New York Court of Appeals' rejection of this court's predicted rule created extraordinary circumstances under Rule 60(b)(6), and alternatively that Baldinger's continued possession of the painting implicated Rule 60(b)(5). Baldinger and Wildenstein seek reversal and preservation of the final judgment. DeWeerth seeks affirmance.

**Jurisdiction.** The order granting post-judgment relief and the resulting judgment support appellate jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. 1291. The papers before the panel do not include the notices of appeal or the docket entries showing when they were filed. The panel proceeds on the assumption that the appeals were timely noticed, an assumption no paper before it contradicts, and its holding on jurisdiction is limited to that assumption.

**Issues and standards of review.**

1. Whether the district court had authority to entertain the Rule 60(b) motion after the mandate and the denial of recall. Baldinger challenges that authority. Review of the mandate's legal scope and of the district court's authority is independent.
2. Whether the later New York decision supplies extraordinary circumstances under Rule 60(b)(6). The appeal challenges the reopening directly. The ultimate Rule 60 ruling is reviewed for abuse of discretion, which includes correction of an erroneous legal premise; the state-law and Rule 60 framework is reviewed independently. The movant bears the burden of establishing extraordinary circumstances.
3. Whether the judgment has prospective application within Rule 60(b)(5). The meaning of the clause is a legal question reviewed independently, and the ruling is reviewed for abuse of discretion within the correctly identified category. The movant must first identify a qualifying prospective operation.

The original title, limitations and laches merits are not before the court on direct review.

**What the court decided.** The court held that the district court had authority to entertain the motion, but that the later New York decision, standing alone on this record, supplies neither the extraordinary circumstances that Rule 60(b)(6) requires nor a prospective operation within Rule 60(b)(5). The order granting relief under Rule 60(b) and the resulting judgment are reversed, and the earlier final judgment is reinstated.

Judgment: The order granting relief under Rule 60(b) and the resulting judgment are reversed, and the earlier final judgment is reinstated. By component:

1. Authority to entertain the motion after the mandate and the denial of recall: the district court had authority. Altimari, Walker and Stone (3-0).
2. Rule 60(b)(6): the later New York decision, without an independently extraordinary circumstance on this record, does not justify reopening the final judgment; the relief is reversed. Altimari, Walker and Stone (3-0).
3. Rule 60(b)(5), prospective application: continued possession under this completed judgment is not prospective operation within the clause; the alternative ground is reversed. Altimari, Walker and Stone (3-0).
4. Remedy: the earlier final judgment is reinstated and the case is remanded only for ministerial steps to give it effect. Altimari, Walker and Stone (3-0).

Vote: 3-0 on each component. No judge dissents or concurs separately.  
Opinion and joins: Opinion of the court by Judge Walker, joined in full by Judge Altimari and Judge Stone-Khoury. There is no concurrence and no dissent.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

WALKER, Circuit Judge, joined by ALTIMARI and STONE-KHOURY, Circuit Judges.

These appeals ask whether a federal judgment in a diversity action, made final by appellate review and the denial of Supreme Court review, may be reopened because the state's highest court later rejected the state-law rule that this court had predicted would decide the case. The district court reopened the judgment, reasoning principally that the New York Court of Appeals' correction of our prediction created extraordinary circumstances under Rule 60(b)(6), and alternatively that Baldinger's continued possession of the painting implicated Rule 60(b)(5). We hold that the district court had authority to entertain the motion. We also hold that the later state decision, standing alone on this record, supplies neither the extraordinary circumstances that Rule 60(b)(6) requires nor the prospective operation to which Rule 60(b)(5) is addressed. We therefore reverse and reinstate the earlier final judgment. The record is summarized in the decision summary above.

**I. Authority to entertain the motion.** Baldinger first argues that the district court lacked authority to consider DeWeerth's motion because our mandate ended the litigation and because we declined to recall it. The argument fails. A mandate governs the record and the issues that were before the appellate court, and a district court does not disregard it by asking whether a later event warrants relief under Rule 60(b). The Supreme Court so held in Standard Oil Co. of California v. United States, 429 U.S. 17 (1976), where it ruled that a district court may entertain a Rule 60(b) motion without first obtaining leave of the appellate court. Our own decisions are consistent. In Eutectic Corp. v. Metco, Inc., 597 F.2d 32, 34 (2d Cir. 1979), and Fine v. Bellefonte Underwriters Insurance Co., 758 F.2d 50, 52-54 (2d Cir. 1985), we held that a district court may not review or alter what this court actually decided and that Rule 60(b) may not serve as a substitute for appeal. Those decisions protect matters the mandate disposed of. They do not make every later Rule 60 motion jurisdictionally impossible. DeWeerth's motion rests on an event that occurred after our judgment became final, a decision of the New York Court of Appeals, and the district court could consider it.

Nor does the denial of the motion to recall the mandate change the analysis. That denial was unexplained, and we draw no inference from it about the reasons for it. It did not decide that no ground later presented could satisfy Rule 60, and we need not declare whether or when repeat motions remain entertainable. The power to consider a motion, however, is not an entitlement to the relief it seeks. That power did not carry with it authority to vacate a final judgment unless the requirements of the Rule were met. We turn to them.

**II. Rule 60(b)(6).** Rule 60(b)(6) permits relief from a final judgment for "any other reason justifying relief from the operation of the judgment." We review the grant of such relief for abuse of discretion, but a district court necessarily abuses its discretion when it bases a ruling on an erroneous view of the law. Cooter & Gell v. Hartmarx Corp., 496 U.S. 384, 405 (1990). We consider independently what the Rule requires, the scope of our mandate, and the state-law framework. As the party seeking relief from a final judgment, DeWeerth bore the burden of establishing the circumstances the Rule demands.

Those circumstances must be extraordinary. The Supreme Court has explained that there must be an end to litigation someday, and it has described the case in which relief was warranted, Klapprott v. United States, 335 U.S. 601 (1949), as one of extraordinary circumstances, in which conditions beyond the movant's control had kept him from defending the action. Ackermann v. United States, 340 U.S. 193, 198-99, 202 (1950). This court has applied the same standard. We have said that relief under clause (6) is generally reserved for extraordinary circumstances or for a judgment that would work an extreme and undue hardship, that a post-judgment change in the law may in special circumstances be extraordinary, and that the motion may not be used as a substitute for appeal. Matarese v. LeFevre, 801 F.2d 98, 106-07 (2d Cir. 1986). We have also said that a change in decisional law is not, by itself, grounds for relief under clause (6). Travelers Indemnity Co. v. Sarkisian, 794 F.2d 754, 757 (2d Cir. 1986). Read together, those decisions leave a narrow door open and do not make later legal disagreement a routine means of reopening a final judgment. A panel of this court could not disturb either decision, and we do not try to.

We begin with what is not disputed. Under Erie Railroad Co. v. Tompkins, 304 U.S. 64 (1938), and Guaranty Trust Co. v. York, 326 U.S. 99, 109 (1945), a federal court exercising diversity jurisdiction applies the substantive law of the state, including its law of limitations, so that the outcome of the litigation is substantially the same as it would be in the state's courts. When the state's highest court has spoken, its construction governs the question in litigation still pending, and a federal court's confidence in an earlier prediction does not outrank it. Guggenheim is such a decision. It rejected the due-diligence requirement this court predicted in 1987, while preserving diligence and prejudice as considerations bearing on laches. 77 N.Y.2d at 317-21. We accept Guggenheim as the authoritative statement of New York law on this point, and we accept DeWeerth's contention that it rejected, rather than adjusted, the premise on which our judgment rested. Nothing in this opinion defends a federal court's power to make state policy or to hold a litigant to a rule the state does not have.

The question before us is a different one. It is not what New York law requires, which is now settled. It is whether an authoritative correction of the legal premise of a completed federal litigation, without more, permits reopening the final judgment under the federal rule that governs such relief. We conclude that it does not on this record.

DeWeerth litigated ownership and the timeliness of her claim. She obtained a favorable decision at trial, received appellate review of the legal question on which the case turned, and Supreme Court review was denied. The defect she identifies is not a denial of a hearing, a fraud in obtaining the judgment, or a continuing decree that operates unlawfully because circumstances have changed. It is an erroneous prediction of state law, later shown to be erroneous by the state's highest court. That is the ordinary form of legal error, and the prospect that a court's reading of the law will later be repudiated by another court is a feature of all litigation. A change in decisional law does not alone satisfy clause (6), Sarkisian, 794 F.2d at 757, and the Supreme Court has made the same point about the finality of judgments in the strongest terms. The consequences of a final judgment are not altered by the fact that the judgment may have been wrong or rested on a legal principle subsequently overruled in another case, and the doctrine of finality serves public interests beyond any individual judge's determination of the equities in a particular case. Federated Department Stores, Inc. v. Moitie, 452 U.S. 394, 398, 401 (1981). Moitie concerned an unappealed judgment; this judgment was reviewed on appeal, which makes the finality interest no weaker. The importance of this painting to DeWeerth and the clarity of the New York court's later statement are real, but they do not change the category of the claimed error. A later corrective precedent does not become an exceptional circumstance because its effect on a particular litigant is described as a severe legal error.

The interest in finality on which we rest is concrete, but it is not an interest in proof of reliance. It lies in the completed adjudication of competing claims to a chattel and in the legal settlement of entitlement to it. The papers before us describe no particular expenditure or transaction by Baldinger in reliance on the judgment, and we assume none. Nor does our conclusion rest on the idea that DeWeerth, by invoking a federal forum, consented to the application of an erroneous statement of state law. A diversity litigant is entitled to state substantive law and does not consent to judicial invention. Our holding rests instead on the federal rule that governs when a completed federal judgment may be reopened. In a case still open to decision, Erie requires the application of Guggenheim. In a case that has been finally decided, Rule 60 asks for more than a later decision showing that the prediction was wrong.

We give the district court's equitable judgment the respect it is due, and we recognize that Judge Broderick was closely familiar with the equities. But the discretion Rule 60(b)(6) confers is discretion to determine whether extraordinary circumstances exist. It is not discretion to treat the ordinary occurrence of a later corrective decision as extraordinary. The order rested principally on the authoritative correction, and no independently extraordinary circumstance appears in the record before us. Because the correction alone does not satisfy the Rule, the order rested on an erroneous view of what clause (6) requires.

DeWeerth offers two principal responses. She argues first that Guggenheim did not announce a new policy but explained the rule New York had always applied, as older demand-and-refusal decisions such as Gillet v. Roberts, 57 N.Y. 28 (1874), and Goodwin v. Wertheimer, 99 N.Y. 149 (1885), show, so that leaving our judgment in place treats her differently only because her suit was in federal court. We assume the premise but reject the conclusion. The difference she identifies arises because her case was decided on a prediction rather than by the state's highest court. Erie accepts that a federal court must sometimes predict state law, and every prediction carries the risk of later repudiation. If repudiation alone reopened judgments, the exception would consume the rule of finality throughout diversity jurisdiction, and the opportunity to correct an erroneous prediction on direct review is the system's answer to that risk, not a general exemption from finality once review is complete.

She relies second on Pierce v. Cook & Co., 518 F.2d 720, 722-23 (10th Cir. 1975) (en banc), which granted relief where federal and state judgments arising from the same accident diverged after the state's highest court changed the controlling rule. Pierce does not bind this court, and its circumstances differ. The divergent judgments there concerned persons injured in a single accident, and the court itself recognized that a change in law is ordinarily not extraordinary. Guggenheim arose from a separate dispute among different parties over a different work, and no judgment concerning this painting or this transaction in another forum conflicts with the judgment here. We do not adopt Pierce as the law of this circuit. We also do not decide whether such a relationship among judgments would warrant relief in a case that presented it.

We leave open what this holding does not reach. We do not hold that a later state decision can never contribute to extraordinary circumstances, and we do not announce a rule that Erie always compels reopening. Circumstances involving an independently extraordinary defect in the proceedings, a materially different relationship among judgments, or another recognized ground under Rule 60 are not before us and are left open. We hold only that, on this record, the later authoritative rejection of a state-law prediction does not justify reopening this fully litigated final judgment. Because the order cannot stand on the ground it rested upon, we do not reach whether the motion was made within a reasonable time.

**III. Rule 60(b)(5).** Rule 60(b)(5) provides relief where "it is no longer equitable that the judgment should have prospective application." The district court alternatively relied on that clause. We interpret the clause independently and review the ruling for abuse of discretion within the correct category. A movant must first identify a qualifying prospective operation before changed circumstances can justify relief.

The clause reaches decrees that are executory or that involve the supervision of changing conduct or conditions, which are provisional and remain open to adaptation as events change. It does not reach restraints that protect rights fully accrued. United States v. Swift & Co., 286 U.S. 106, 114 (1932). A judgment is not prospective within the clause merely because its consequences continue. Twelve John Does v. District of Columbia, 841 F.2d 1133, 1138-39 (D.C. Cir. 1988) (persuasive); see Sarkisian, 794 F.2d at 757 n.4 (doubting that the preclusive nature of a dismissal with prejudice is a prospective effect under the Rule).

The judgment here adjudicated competing claims to title and possession of a painting, and it was complete when entered. Baldinger's continued possession is a consequence of that adjudication, not a decree requiring the court to administer future conduct. The record establishes continued possession. It does not establish ongoing judicial supervision, an injunction, or any executory obligation. DeWeerth answers that the judgment continues to determine who may retain the painting despite what the state now says is the rule. The same is true of every judgment awarding property or money. Treating that consequence as prospective operation would subject final property and money judgments to continuing reconsideration whenever the law later changes, and it would let a movant avoid the extraordinary-circumstances requirement of clause (6) by invoking clause (5). We hold that continuing possession under this completed judgment is not prospective operation within the clause. We decide nothing about a genuinely continuing injunction or other executory decree, or about what showing would warrant relief from one. The order under review rested on the prospective-application clause, and we address no other clause of Rule 60(b)(5).

**IV. Limits of the decision and disposition.** Our decision is limited to the order under review. We do not decide the merits of the original limitations defense, laches, diligence, prejudice, the adequacy of the search for the painting, or who took it in 1945; the reinstated judgment is the product of the earlier proceedings, and this appeal is not a new direct appeal on the original property claim. The order under review is a ruling on a Rule 60(b) motion, and no question concerning an independent action to relieve a party from a judgment is presented by the papers before us; we express no view on one. Wildenstein's appeal challenges the same reopening and falls with it. We decide nothing about the merits of any claim between Baldinger and Wildenstein beyond the effect of reinstating the judgment.

The order granting relief under Rule 60(b) and the resulting judgment are reversed, and the earlier final judgment is reinstated. The case is remanded to the district court only for such ministerial steps as are needed to give effect to this disposition. The district court is not to reweigh the equities, to redecide the Rule 60 motion, or to decide laches or diligence on remand.

#### Separate opinions

None. No judge dissents or concurs separately.

#### What the decision does

- **Controlling holding:** This is a published, precedential opinion of the court, and each holding below was joined by all three judges.
  1. A district court may entertain a Rule 60(b) motion resting on a later event without leave of the court of appeals; it may not relitigate a matter the mandate actually disposed of, and an unexplained denial of recall does not settle every ground later presented. Authority to entertain the motion is distinct from entitlement to relief. Authorities: Standard Oil; Eutectic; Fine.
  2. Rule 60(b)(6) requires extraordinary circumstances, which the movant must establish. The later authoritative rejection of a state-law prediction, without an independently extraordinary circumstance on this record, does not justify reopening a fully litigated final diversity judgment. This is not a rule that a later state decision can never contribute to extraordinary circumstances. Authorities: Ackermann; Matarese; Sarkisian; Moitie; Erie and York distinguished as to pending cases.
  3. The prospective-application clause of Rule 60(b)(5) reaches executory or supervisory decrees, not a completed adjudication of entitlement whose consequences continue. Continuing possession under this completed judgment is not prospective operation. Authorities: Swift; Twelve John Does (persuasive).
- **Treatment of precedent:** The opinion follows Standard Oil, Eutectic, Fine, Ackermann, Matarese, Sarkisian and Swift as binding authority, applies Erie and York to distinguish pending from finally decided cases, treats Guggenheim as controlling New York substantive law, and treats Pierce and Twelve John Does as persuasive only. It does not adopt Pierce as circuit law and does not disturb Matarese. It changes no earlier rule of this circuit. It does not revisit the 1987 decision: in any case still open to decision, Guggenheim states the New York rule, and in this closed litigation the final judgment stands.
- **Limits:** The opinion is limited to a fully litigated and reviewed final diversity judgment, a motion resting on the later authoritative rejection of the state-law prediction on which it depended, and a completed adjudication of entitlement to property. The matters described as left open are not holdings. The reinstated judgment retains whatever preclusive and law-of-the-case effect it had before the Rule 60 order; this opinion decides nothing further about either.
- **Effect on the parties:** The order granting relief under Rule 60(b) and the resulting judgment are reversed. The earlier final judgment is reinstated. The relief is party-specific, and the reinstatement binds the parties to these appeals. Wildenstein's appeal falls with the reopening it challenged, and nothing is decided about any claim between Baldinger and Wildenstein beyond the effect of reinstating the judgment.
- **Mandate and remand:** The case is remanded only for ministerial steps to give effect to that disposition, and not for any reweighing of equities or decision of laches or diligence. This opinion does not enter a mandate, and its date is not a mandate date. The papers before the court supply no mandate date for any earlier version of these appeals, and none is assumed. As the rules stood on the date of decision, a petition for rehearing under Fed. R. App. P. 40(a) could be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment, unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order or local rule. Under Fed. R. App. P. 41(a), the mandate issues 21 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order; a timely petition for rehearing stays the mandate until its disposition unless the court orders otherwise, and if a petition is denied the mandate issues 7 days after entry of the order denying it unless that time is shortened or enlarged. The ministerial proceedings on remand follow the issuance of the mandate. No filing or later proceeding is assumed.

#### Historical comparison

On October 27, 1994, a Second Circuit panel of Judges Altimari, Walker and Owen filed an amended opinion by Judge Walker, joined by Judge Altimari. It reversed the Rule 60(b) order and restored the earlier final judgment. The majority separated the district court's authority to consider later events from satisfaction of Rule 60, held that the later authoritative New York decision did not supply extraordinary circumstances to reopen this final diversity judgment, and held that continued possession was a consequence of the adjudication, not prospective operation under Rule 60(b)(5). Judge Owen dissented, stressing the district judge's equitable discretion. The simulated judgment is unchanged: reversal and reinstatement on the same grounds. Only the lineup differs, with Judge Stone-Khoury in Owen's place and joining Walker, making the result 3-0 with no dissent. Source: DeWeerth v. Baldinger, 38 F.3d 1266, 1271-78 (2d Cir. 1994), Nos. 93-7144, 93-7146.

### [Young (Ramadan) v. Selsky](../cases/young-v-selsky/)

Decision date: November 21, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 93-2567  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Reversed and remanded, by a vote of 2 to 1. Published; precedential opinion of the court on two joins. No reporter citation is assigned. The mandate has not issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/young-v-selsky/events/1994-11-21-decision.md](../cases/young-v-selsky/events/1994-11-21-decision.md)

Historical panel: Wilfred Feinberg (Senior Circuit Judge); Ellsworth A. Van Graafeiland (Senior Circuit Judge); Ralph K. Winter, Jr. (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: Wilfred Feinberg (Senior Circuit Judge); Ellsworth A. Van Graafeiland (Senior Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Ralph K. Winter, Jr.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

(Short title: Young (Ramadan) v. Selsky. This decision concerns the claims against Donald Selsky.)
Court below: United States District Court for the Northern District of New York (Neal P. McCurn, District Judge), acting on the recommendation of Magistrate Judge Gustave J. DiBianco.

#### Decision summary

##### The case and the result

Jerry Young, also known as Ramadan, was an inmate at Eastern Correctional Facility when the events at issue occurred. Between February and May 1992, five disciplinary hearings involving him were held at that facility. Young alleges that he was denied requested witnesses, including thirty-one inmates and two correctional officers, and that he received confinement in special housing and losses of privileges. Whether any witness should have been called, why any request was refused, and whether any sanction was lawful have not been established, and this decision does not decide them.

Donald Selsky is the Director of Special Housing and Inmate Disciplinary Programs of the New York Department of Correctional Services. Acting as the commissioner's designee, he reviewed on administrative appeal the serious disciplinary determinations at issue and affirmed all five dispositions. In July 1992 Young filed this action under 42 U.S.C. 1983, seeking damages, expungement of the disciplinary records, and restoration of the affected rights. A separate action against officials who conducted the hearings is not before the court.

The district court dismissed, on the ground of absolute immunity, the damages claims under section 1983 against Selsky. The papers before the panel do not include the notice of appeal or the docket entry fixing the date on which judgment was entered, and no entry date is stated. The material on which the district court relied came largely from other lawsuits. It described Selsky's centralized review role, his large caseload, and asserted insulation from institutional pressures. The record on appeal also includes evidence that Selsky advised hearing officers during disciplinary proceedings and that he works within a correctional supervisory structure whose officials have operational interests in disciplinary outcomes. Appointed counsel for Young contended that the district court had an incomplete picture of Selsky's function, and the State agreed that further materials bearing on that function might be considered.

One issue is presented: whether the function Selsky performs in reviewing New York prison disciplinary determinations entitles him to absolute immunity from damages under section 1983. The court held that, on this record, Selsky, who bears the burden as the proponent of the immunity, has not shown that his review function warrants absolute rather than qualified immunity. The dismissal on absolute-immunity grounds is reversed and the case is remanded to the district court. Qualified immunity remains available to Selsky on its own terms, and the decision does not decide qualified immunity, his personal involvement, causation, the merits of the due process allegations, or any protected liberty interest.

Jurisdiction rests on 28 U.S.C. 1291, because the dismissal of the damages claims against Selsky is a final judgment. Whether a function warrants absolute immunity is a question of law reviewed independently, and unresolved questions of conduct and injury remain for the proper factfinder. The law applied is the law in force on the date of decision.

##### The principal contrary argument and the answer

The principal contrary argument, pressed by Selsky and reflected in the dissent, is that a statewide appellate reviewer who is distant from the underlying incidents, who decides a very large number of appeals, and whose decisions can be corrected in the state courts performs a function so like that of an appellate judge that he must be protected from repeated damages suits by dissatisfied prisoners. The opinion of the court answers that the immunity belongs to the function and must be established by the official who claims it; that centralization and volume speak mainly to harassment, which qualified immunity, the personal-involvement requirement and the ordinary limits on claims already address; that *Cleavinger* rejected the same reasoning for prison disciplinary decisionmakers; and that this record does not show insulation from the Department's institutional interests, adversary presentation, or a body of precedent at the review stage.

##### Judgment, vote, and writings

Judgment, by component:

1. **Dismissal of the damages claims against Selsky on the ground of absolute immunity: reversed.** For reversal: Judge Feinberg and Judge Stone. Against: Judge Van Graafeiland, who would affirm. Vote: 2 to 1.
2. **Remand:** the case is remanded to the district court for further proceedings consistent with the opinion of the court. For: Judge Feinberg and Judge Stone. Against: Judge Van Graafeiland, who would affirm and so would order no remand on immunity.
3. **Questions not decided and left to the district court:** qualified immunity; Selsky's personal involvement; causation; the witness-related due process claims under *Wolff v. McDonnell*; any protected liberty interest; and the relief Young seeks. No judge on the panel decides any of these questions.
4. **Relief:** No judge on the panel directs an award of damages, expungement, or restoration. The opinion makes no direction as to costs.
5. **Mandate:** has not issued (see "Mandate and remand" below).

Opinion and joins:

1. Opinion of the court by Judge Feinberg, joined in full by Judge Stone.
2. Dissent by Judge Van Graafeiland, joined by no other judge. He joins no part of the opinion of the court.
3. There is no concurrence and no other separate writing.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

**Opinion of the court by Judge Feinberg, joined by Judge Stone.**

Jerry Young, also known as Ramadan, who was an inmate at Eastern Correctional Facility when the events at issue occurred, appeals from a judgment of the United States District Court for the Northern District of New York (Neal P. McCurn, District Judge) that dismissed, on the ground of absolute immunity, his claims for damages under 42 U.S.C. 1983 against Donald Selsky. Selsky is the Director of Special Housing and Inmate Disciplinary Programs of the New York Department of Correctional Services and, as the commissioner's designee, reviewed on administrative appeal the five disciplinary determinations that Young challenges. The district court acted on the recommendation of Magistrate Judge Gustave J. DiBianco. The question is whether the function Selsky performs in reviewing serious prison disciplinary determinations is one for which the law grants absolute, rather than qualified, immunity from damages. We hold that Selsky, on whom the burden of establishing the immunity rests, has not shown on this record that it is. We therefore reverse and remand.

##### I. Background

Between February and May 1992, five disciplinary hearings involving Young were held at Eastern Correctional Facility. Young alleges that at those hearings he was denied witnesses he had requested, including thirty-one inmates and two correctional officers, and that he received confinement in special housing and losses of privileges. We have no findings on whether any requested witness should have been called, on why any request was refused, or on whether any sanction was lawful, and nothing in this opinion decides those matters. Selsky affirmed all five dispositions. In July 1992 Young filed this action under section 1983, seeking damages, expungement of the disciplinary records, and restoration of the rights he lost. A separate action against officials involved in the hearings is not before us and plays no part in our decision.

The district court dismissed the damages claims against Selsky on the ground that his review function is protected by absolute immunity. The materials on which that ruling rested came largely from other lawsuits. They describe Selsky's centralized, statewide role in reviewing disciplinary determinations, the large number of determinations he reviews, and what was asserted to be his insulation from institutional pressures. The record on appeal contains more. It includes evidence that Selsky advised hearing officers during disciplinary proceedings and that he works within a correctional supervisory structure whose officials have operational interests in how disciplinary proceedings come out. Counsel appointed to represent Young on appeal contended that the district court had been given an incomplete picture of Selsky's function, and the State agreed that the court might consider further materials bearing on it. The appeal was submitted on the papers on April 26, 1994, without oral argument, and appointed counsel later submitted supplemental materials.

The State's agreement has a limit. It allows us to consider the materials for what they show about the nature of Selsky's function. It does not make testimony in unrelated lawsuits indisputable proof of every fact asserted in it, and an appellate court does not find facts. We do not find how often, or in what manner, Selsky advised hearing officers. As will appear, our conclusion does not depend on such a finding.

##### II. Jurisdiction and Standard of Review

The dismissal of the damages claims against Selsky is a final judgment, and we have jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. 1291. Whether the function an official performs warrants absolute immunity is a question of law, and our review is independent. We do not resolve any question of conduct or injury that must be decided by a factfinder. The official who asserts absolute immunity bears the burden of showing that it is justified for the function in question. *Burns v. Reed*, 500 U.S. 478, 486-87 (1991). As the Supreme Court has put it, "[t]he proponent of a claim to absolute immunity bears the burden of establishing the justification for such immunity." *Antoine v. Byers & Anderson, Inc.*, 508 U.S. 429 (1993).

##### III. Discussion

###### A. The governing principles

Section 1983 contains no express immunity. The Supreme Court has treated qualified immunity as the ordinary protection of officials who exercise discretion, and it has reserved absolute immunity for functions that warrant it. Absolute immunity attaches to a function, not to an office or a title. The immunity of judges, *Bradley v. Fisher*, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 335 (1872); *Pierson v. Ray*, 386 U.S. 547 (1967), protects the exercise of judicial judgment under conditions that make damages actions an unnecessary and harmful way of controlling error. In *Butz v. Economou*, 438 U.S. 478, 512-14 (1978), the Court extended comparable protection to officials who perform adjudicative functions within federal agencies, because their proceedings closely resemble judicial proceedings and are surrounded by comparable safeguards. *Butz* did not make a judge of every official who reviews another's decision, and the Court has cautioned against extending absolute immunity further than its justification requires. *Burns*, 500 U.S. at 486-87.

*Cleavinger v. Saxner*, 474 U.S. 193, 202-06 (1985), is the decision closest to this one. It concerned the members of a prison disciplinary committee. The Court, drawing on *Butz*, looked to the considerations characteristic of the judicial process: the need to assure that the decisionmaker can perform his functions without harassment or intimidation; the presence of safeguards that reduce the need for private damages actions as a means of controlling unconstitutional conduct; insulation from political influence; the importance of precedent; the adversary nature of the process; and the correctability of error on appeal. Concluding that the committee members lacked the independence and the procedural safeguards that characterize courts, the Court held that they were entitled to qualified immunity only. *Cleavinger* teaches that neither the label attached to a prison disciplinary decision nor its importance to institutional order decides the question. What decides it is whether the person claiming the immunity has shown that he performs a function that is judicial in the respects the immunity exists to protect.

###### B. Application to Selsky's review function

Selsky's position has force. He reviews, for the whole state system, determinations made at many institutions. He is not ordinarily involved in the incidents that give rise to them. He decides a very large number of appeals, and an official so situated is exposed to suit by those who do not prevail. Administrative review and review in the state courts under Article 78 are available to correct error. And absolute immunity cannot depend on whether the official decided correctly. Our conclusion does not suggest that any of the five dispositions was wrong. The question is whether, on this record, Selsky has carried the burden of showing that his function has the characteristics that justify absolute immunity. We conclude that he has not.

First, insulation from institutional pressure. Independence is the premise on which the immunity of judges and agency adjudicators rests: a decisionmaker who need not fear the consequences of an unwelcome ruling may decide without regard to them. Selsky is an official of the Department that operates the facilities in which the determinations he reviews are made. He works within a correctional supervisory structure, and the officials of that structure have operational interests in how disciplinary proceedings come out. The record includes evidence that he advises hearing officers in the course of disciplinary proceedings. Centralizing review in an office removed from any single facility relieves some of the local pressures that concerned the Court in *Cleavinger*, but it does not show that the reviewer is insulated from the Department's own institutional interests, which a statewide office shares with the facilities it oversees. An official who advises the tribunals whose decisions he later reviews, to whatever extent he does so, is not shown to stand apart from the hearing process in the way an appellate court stands apart from the trial court. We do not find that Selsky gave such advice in any of Young's five cases, and we do not decide the extent of any advisory role. It is enough that the evidence is directly relevant to the comparison with a court, and that the party who bears the burden of establishing insulation has not established it.

Second, adversary presentation, precedent, and the other safeguards of adjudication. The materials on which Selsky relies describe the centralization of review, the volume of determinations, and the asserted insulation of his office. The State has not shown that review proceeds through an adversary presentation in which opposing positions are put to a neutral decisionmaker, that Selsky's determinations are issued in a form and with an effect that makes them precedent constraining later reviewers, or that the process contains safeguards of the kind that reduce the need for private damages actions as a means of controlling unconstitutional conduct. Those are the features that make the work of a court something other than the work of an administrator, and showing them is the proponent's burden.

Third, correctability. That error in a disciplinary determination may be corrected by administrative review and by proceedings in the state courts is a consideration to be weighed, and we weigh it. It is not sufficient by itself. If the availability of review were enough, absolute immunity would become the rule for every official whose decisions can be reviewed, which is the opposite of the caution the Supreme Court has urged. *Burns*, 500 U.S. at 486-87. Nor does later correction undo a deprivation already suffered. In *Walker v. Bates*, No. 93-2107 (2d Cir. Apr. 29, 1994), Part II, this court held that administrative reversal of a disciplinary determination after punitive confinement has begun is not, as a matter of law, a defense to a section 1983 claim for punishment already served. *Walker* did not concern absolute immunity, no reversal occurred here, and we do not extend its holding beyond its terms. We note it only to explain why the possibility of later correction cannot bear the weight Selsky would place on it as a substitute for ordinary damages review.

Fourth, harassment. The concern that an official who decides many appeals will be sued by many disappointed inmates is genuine, and the State properly presses it. It is the consideration to which Selsky's centralized role and large caseload mainly relate. But it cannot alone establish absolute immunity. Qualified immunity protects an official who could reasonably have believed his conduct lawful and permits the early disposal of insubstantial claims. A plaintiff must also show the defendant's personal involvement in the alleged violation and causation, and the ordinary rules of pleading and evidence, together with the court's authority to dismiss meritless claims, remain in force. The frequency with which unsuccessful appellants may sue cannot suffice, for the same could be said of the disciplinary committee members in *Cleavinger*, whom the Court nonetheless held to qualified immunity. We add what is not our reasoning. It is not that inmates must have someone to sue, and it is not that the size of Selsky's caseload shows indifference to the merits of the appeals he decides.

The authorities on which Selsky relies do not alter this analysis. *Dorman v. Higgins*, 821 F.2d 133, 137 (2d Cir. 1987), granted absolute immunity to a probation officer for preparing a presentence report because in doing so the officer acts as an arm of the court, in work integral to the judicial process. Selsky is an official of a correctional department, and his office is not part of any court. *Shelly v. Johnson*, 849 F.2d 228 (6th Cir. 1988), does not bind us and concerns a materially different state arrangement for adjudicating prison discipline; Selsky has not shown that the features that mattered there are present in New York's system. *Richardson v. Selsky*, 5 F.3d 616 (2d Cir. 1993), resolved the claims against Selsky and others on the ground of qualified immunity and did not decide whether his review function is absolutely immune. It cannot be read to establish a categorical immunity for that function.

###### C. Holding and its limits

We hold that, on the record before us, Selsky has not carried his burden of showing that his review of prison disciplinary determinations has the independence from institutional pressure and the safeguards that justify absolute immunity. Administrative review by Selsky does not acquire absolute judicial immunity merely because it is called an appeal. Qualified immunity remains available to him on its own terms. The dismissal of the damages claims on the ground of absolute immunity must therefore be reversed.

The holding is narrow. It rests on the failure of the proponent's showing and not on any finding of fact that this court could not properly make on appeal. It is confined to the structure of review shown on this record and states no rule about facts not presented. It does not hold that no official who reviews prison discipline, in New York or elsewhere, can have absolute immunity. It does not hold that Selsky's affirmance of a disciplinary determination was itself a violation. Whether Young can establish a protected liberty interest under the law then governing, a violation of the process actually owed, Selsky's personal involvement, and causation are questions for the district court. In deciding them the district court will apply *Wolff v. McDonnell*, 418 U.S. 539 (1974), with the limits on the right to call witnesses that *Wolff* recognizes where institutional safety or correctional goals require them. An official may affirm a disciplinary determination after a reasonable assessment that legitimate security grounds justified the refusal of witnesses. Whether Selsky could reasonably have understood the challenged determinations to be lawful, under the law as it stood when he acted, is a question of qualified immunity that we do not decide on this record, for it would require us to resolve contested questions about what he did and why.

###### D. Remand

On remand the district court should permit development of the record concerning the five disciplinary appeals, the reasons for the rulings on requested witnesses, Selsky's personal involvement, qualified immunity, causation, and the relief Young seeks. We direct no award of damages in any amount and no expungement of any disciplinary record. Young is entitled to a forum in which to try to prove his claim, not to an award from this court. The district court's dismissal addressed the claims for damages. Our reversal does not bar any claim to relief other than damages against Selsky, but neither do we decide that such relief is available. An immunity from damages does not by itself resolve whether other relief may be had, and relief directed to the future requires an appropriate defendant and an injury that is live and capable of remedy. Those questions are for the district court.

The judgment of the district court dismissing the damages claims against Selsky on the ground of absolute immunity is reversed, and the case is remanded for further proceedings consistent with this opinion.

#### Separate opinions

##### Dissent of Judge Van Graafeiland (joined by no other judge)

Judge Van Graafeiland dissents and would affirm the dismissal of the damages claims against Selsky. In his view, Selsky's statewide review of disciplinary determinations is sufficiently adjudicative and appellate in character that absolute immunity is warranted to protect that function from repeated damages suits by prisoners dissatisfied with the outcome of their appeals. He joins no part of the opinion of the court. He would order no remand on immunity.

The grounds of the dissent are reported here at the level just described, and this account goes no further than they do. There is no concurrence and no other separate writing.

#### What the decision does

- **Controlling holding.** Absolute immunity under section 1983 turns on the function performed, not the title of the office or the label "appeal." The official who claims it bears the burden of showing that the particular function has the independence from institutional pressure and the procedural safeguards that characterize adjudication. The test is the set of considerations identified in *Butz* and applied in *Cleavinger*: the need to protect the decisionmaker from harassment or intimidation; safeguards that reduce the need for private damages actions; insulation from political or institutional influence; the importance of precedent; the adversary nature of the process; and the correctability of error on appeal. The burden is on the proponent of the immunity. The authorities for the rule are *Bradley v. Fisher*, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 335 (1872); *Pierson v. Ray*, 386 U.S. 547 (1967); *Butz v. Economou*, 438 U.S. 478, 512-14 (1978); *Cleavinger v. Saxner*, 474 U.S. 193, 202-06 (1985); *Burns v. Reed*, 500 U.S. 478, 486-87 (1991); and *Antoine v. Byers & Anderson, Inc.*, 508 U.S. 429 (1993). On the record presented, Selsky has not carried that burden as to his review of prison disciplinary determinations. Administrative review by Selsky does not acquire absolute judicial immunity merely because it is called an appeal; qualified immunity remains available to him on its own terms. In application, the record does not show insulation from the Department's institutional interests, adversary presentation, or a body of precedent at the review stage; the centralization and volume of his review bear mainly on harassment, which qualified immunity addresses; and the availability of later correction does not carry the immunity.
- **Limits of the holding.** The holding rests on failure of the proponent's showing, not on any finding that the advisory-role evidence is true. It is confined to the structure of review shown on this record and states no rule about facts not presented. It is not a rule that no official reviewing prison discipline in any system can have absolute immunity. It does not decide qualified immunity or the merits on this record. It does not extend *Walker v. Bates* beyond its own question.
- **Binding and non-binding parts.** The opinion is published and is precedential as to the holding stated in the opinion of the court, on the joins of Judge Feinberg and Judge Stone. The dissent has no precedential force and states Judge Van Graafeiland's position only. The reversal and remand of this judgment, and the direction that no damages or expungement is awarded now, are party-specific. The observations about the *Wolff* limits on witnesses, about relief other than damages, and about the character of *Walker* describe matters left to the district court and are not holdings. On remand, the ruling that Selsky has not shown absolute immunity on the record presented governs the further proceedings in this case, and the opinion states no rule about facts not presented.
- **Treatment of prior authority.** The opinion applies, as binding Supreme Court authority, *Butz*, *Cleavinger*, *Burns* and *Antoine*, and follows *Bradley* and *Pierson* on the judicial function. It distinguishes *Dorman v. Higgins* (a probation officer acting as an arm of the court), treats *Shelly v. Johnson* as persuasive authority from another circuit that concerns a materially different arrangement, and reads *Richardson v. Selsky* as a qualified-immunity decision that did not decide absolute immunity. It refers to *Walker v. Bates*, Part II, only for its own holding on the cure question and does not extend it. No earlier circuit holding is overruled or displaced.
- **What the circuit's law now is.** The circuit now holds that the official who reviews New York prison disciplinary determinations bears the burden of showing a function with the independence and safeguards of adjudication, and that Selsky's statewide review has not been shown on this record to carry absolute immunity.
- **Effect on the parties.** The judgment dismissing the damages claims against Selsky on the ground of absolute immunity is reversed. Young may go forward in the district court on those claims. No damages, expungement or restoration is directed. A claim to relief other than damages is neither barred nor sustained by this decision. The opinion makes no direction as to costs.
- **Matters left open.** The opinion of the court does not decide: whether Selsky is entitled to qualified immunity; whether he was personally involved in, or caused, any violation; whether any sanction implicated a protected liberty interest under the law then governing; whether the refusal of any requested witness violated the process owed under *Wolff*; whether Young may recover damages in any amount; whether and against whom any relief other than damages is available; the extent and character of any advisory role Selsky played with hearing officers; any question about the structure of review on facts not presented on this record; and whether any official who reviews prison discipline in another system has absolute immunity.
- **Mandate and remand.** The case is remanded to the United States District Court for the Northern District of New York for further proceedings consistent with the opinion of the court. The scope of remand is the development and decision of the five disciplinary appeals, the reasons for the rulings on requested witnesses, Selsky's personal involvement, qualified immunity, causation, any protected-interest question, and the relief Young seeks. The mandate has not issued, and the date of the opinion is not the date of the mandate. Under Fed. R. App. P. 40(a), a petition for rehearing may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment, the United States not being a party, unless that time is shortened or enlarged by order. Under Fed. R. App. P. 41(a), the mandate issues only after the time for seeking rehearing has expired, and a timely petition for rehearing stays it until the petition is disposed of. No filing after the decision is recorded.

#### Historical comparison

Historically, on 1994-11-21 a Second Circuit panel of Judges Feinberg, Van Graafeiland and Winter reversed the absolute-immunity dismissal of the damages claims against Donald Selsky and remanded. Judge Feinberg wrote the opinion, joined by Judge Winter; Judge Van Graafeiland dissented, weighting the adjudicative appellate function and protection against repeated prisoner suits. The court applied functional immunity analysis and held that Selsky's administrative appellate position did not establish the judicial independence and safeguards absolute immunity requires, leaving qualified immunity, the merits, personal responsibility and relief to further proceedings, with no automatic damages award or expungement. The simulated judgment matches on disposition, 2 to 1 split, author, dissenter, remedy and functional-immunity ground; the only difference is that Judge Stone joins Feinberg's opinion in Winter's place. The judgment is unchanged, and no change of reasoning is found. Source: [Young v. Selsky, 41 F.3d 47 (2d Cir. 1994)](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/41/47/564093/), at 53-61.

### [United States v. Shaoul](../cases/united-states-v-shaoul/)

Decision date: December 1, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 94-1093  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Published opinion of the court; judgment of conviction and order denying a new trial affirmed; mandate not issued  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/united-states-v-shaoul/events/1994-12-01-decision.md](../cases/united-states-v-shaoul/events/1994-12-01-decision.md)

Historical panel: J. Edward Lumbard (Senior Circuit Judge); Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); José A. Cabranes (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: J. Edward Lumbard (Senior Circuit Judge); Frank X. Altimari (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: José A. Cabranes.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Appeal from the United States District Court for the Southern District of New York (Dominick L. DiCarlo, Chief Judge of the Court of International Trade, sitting by designation).
Opinion by Circuit Judge Altimari, joined in full by Senior Circuit Judge Lumbard and Circuit Judge Stone-Khoury. There is no concurrence and no dissent.

#### Decision summary

Jack Shaoul, an antiques dealer, was convicted by a jury of one count of conspiracy and ten counts of mail fraud arising from two insurance claims. He asked the court of appeals to overturn the conviction on two grounds. First, after the verdict the government learned that one juror was the uncle of the wife of an assistant United States attorney in the same district, a prosecutor who had no part in this case, and that the juror had not recalled the relationship when questioned before trial. Shaoul argued that this relationship alone required a new trial. Second, he argued that the jury was not properly instructed that it had to be unanimous about the facts supporting a conviction, and he raised two further objections to the charge.

The court rejected both grounds and affirmed in full, by a vote of 3 to 0. On the juror, Shaoul's lawyer had told the district court that the juror acted in good faith and then chose not to question him, so the claim that the juror answered dishonestly failed. The court also held that a juror's indirect family relationship to a prosecutor in the district who had nothing to do with the case does not, by itself, establish disqualifying bias, and that the record showed no actual bias and no reason to order a hearing. On the instructions, no objection had been made at trial, so the court reviewed only for plain error and found none. The court expressly left open several related questions, which are identified in the opinion and in the holdings below.

#### The case and the result

##### Posture

Jack Shaoul appeals from a final judgment of conviction after a jury trial on one count of conspiracy and ten counts of mail fraud, and from the denial of his motion for a new trial based on a juror's indirect relationship to an assistant United States attorney who had no part in the case. The district court also rejected his challenge to the jury instructions. It imposed concurrent forty-month prison terms, two years of supervised release, a $50,000 fine, the costs of imprisonment and supervision, and restitution of $13,356.90. The appeal raises no independent challenge to any component of the sentence.

##### The material facts

Shaoul, an antiques dealer, submitted insurance claims arising from two alleged losses. The first was a reported theft of paintings and other items from his car in January 1991. His employee Edward Purcell testified that a painting attributed to Albert Pinkham Ryder remained in the shop and that Shaoul told him to conceal it, and that he photographed it beside a current newspaper after the reported theft. Louis Liskin described a backdated letter reciting a purchase interest, intended to support an inflated valuation, and testified that he understood the painting was not authentic. The second claim followed a genuine water leak at the shop in February 1991. Witnesses described moving previously broken glassware into the wet area and further damaging a purported Winslow Homer watercolor that had been identified as inauthentic. The valuation documents included an invoice for a sale that the named gallery owner testified had not occurred. Purcell and Liskin testified under nonprosecution agreements, which bore on their credibility but did not require the jury to disregard them, and other witnesses and documents corroborated them.

Before deliberations the court gave a general instruction on unanimity, explained that the government need not prove every alleged overt act, and twice directed unanimity on the material falsity of the relevant representations. When the jury asked whether all of the overt acts had to be proved, the court repeated that one was sufficient. Defense counsel did not object to the original charge or to that answer. After the verdict the government learned that a juror was the uncle of the wife of an assistant United States attorney. That assistant worked in the same district and had no involvement in this prosecution, and the juror had not recalled the relative's employment during voir dire. The government made the juror available at sentencing. Counsel expressly conceded the juror's good faith, declined to examine him, and argued that the relationship alone required a new trial.

##### Jurisdiction

The court has jurisdiction over the final judgment under 28 U.S.C. section 1291; the district court's jurisdiction rested on 18 U.S.C. section 3231. The materials before the court do not show the date of entry of judgment or of the notice of appeal, and no question about the timeliness of the appeal is among those raised.

##### Issues and standards of review

1. Whether the denial of a new trial under Fed. R. Crim. P. 33 on the ground of the juror's undisclosed relationship was an abuse of discretion. Review is for abuse of discretion, with independent review of the legal tests for nondisclosure and implied bias. The movant bears the burden of establishing the factual or categorical ground on which relief rests. Counsel's concession of good faith and decision not to examine the juror are preserved facts of record, and the legal consequence of the known relationship is open to decision.
2. Whether the unobjected-to unanimity instruction, the answer to the jury's question about overt acts, and the remaining charge objections show reversible error. Because no objection was made when the court could correct the charge or its answer, review is for plain error under Fed. R. Crim. P. 30 and 52(b) and *United States v. Olano*, 507 U.S. 725 (1993): error, plainness, effect on substantial rights, and the discretionary inquiry into fairness, integrity and public reputation. The defendant bears the burden on prejudice.

##### Judgment

The judgment of conviction and the order denying a new trial are affirmed.

- Component 1, juror relationship and denial of a new trial (Part II): Affirmed. Judges Lumbard, Altimari and Stone-Khoury concur. No judge dissents.
- Component 2, unanimity, the overt-act answer and the remaining charge objections (Part III): Affirmed. Judges Lumbard, Altimari and Stone-Khoury concur. No judge dissents.
- Remedy: No new trial, no remand, no juror hearing and no change to the sentence. The vote on the judgment as a whole is 3 to 0.

##### Writings and joins

Opinion of the court by Judge Altimari, joined in full by Judge Lumbard and Judge Stone-Khoury. Every Part is joined by all three judges; there is no Part-by-Part variation. There is no concurrence and no dissent.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

ALTIMARI, Circuit Judge, joined by LUMBARD, Senior Circuit Judge, and STONE-KHOURY, Circuit Judge:

**I. Overview.** Shaoul asks for a new trial on two grounds. He says a juror's undisclosed family tie to the prosecuting office required one, and he says the jury was not properly instructed on unanimity and related matters. We reject both and affirm. The facts are set out above.

**II. The juror.**

*A. The record and how we review it.* Rule 33 permits a new trial when the interest of justice requires one. We review its denial for abuse of discretion, but we decide without deference the legal rules the district court used to judge a nondisclosure or a claim of implied bias.

Counsel's course at sentencing shapes what we can decide. The government produced the juror. Counsel did not merely fail to object; he expressly conceded good faith and gave up the chance to question the juror. The Supreme Court has distinguished forfeiture, the failure to assert a right in time, from waiver, the intentional relinquishment of a known right. *Olano*, 507 U.S. at 733. Counsel's election was of the second kind as to the factual inquiry. Shaoul's legal contention, that the known relationship by itself required a new trial, is a question of law that remained open whatever counsel chose to do about the facts, and we decide it on the merits. We do not decide whether counsel's election would also bar that contention, and we do not treat it as a bar. The election does determine the record. The record shows a particular relationship, an honest failure to recall it, and nothing about the juror's actual state of mind.

*B. Nondisclosure.* A party who seeks a new trial because a juror answered a voir dire question incorrectly "must first demonstrate that a juror failed to answer honestly a material question on voir dire, and then further show that a correct response would have provided a valid basis for a challenge for cause." *McDonough Power Equipment, Inc. v. Greenwood*, 464 U.S. 548, 556 (1984). The burden is the movant's at each step. We apply that test to deliberate and inadvertent nondisclosures alike, and our decision in *United States v. Langford*, 990 F.2d 65, 68 (2d Cir. 1993), does not delete its first requirement: the juror there was treated as having answered dishonestly, and the court then found the second requirement unproved.

The first requirement is dispositive here. Counsel told the district court that the omission was made in good faith. A juror who honestly does not recall a relationship has not failed to answer honestly. Because the first requirement fails, we do not ask whether a truthful answer would have supported a challenge for cause.

The strongest form of Shaoul's argument is that an undisclosed tie to the prosecuting office calls for relief even when the omission was innocent. *United States v. Colombo*, 869 F.2d 149 (2d Cir. 1989), does not support that argument. The juror there was alleged to have deliberately concealed her relationship to a government attorney, and the court reasoned that her willingness to lie about it exhibited an interest strongly suggesting partiality, a reasoning that distinguished the cases in which nondisclosure was honest or not shown to be deliberate. *Id.* at 152. Nothing of that kind is present here. The nondisclosure route fails on the record counsel chose to make.

*C. Implied bias.* Shaoul's remaining argument does not depend on dishonesty. He contends that the relationship, once known, implies bias as a matter of law. An honest answer is not immunity from bias that is actually shown, or in exceptional circumstances inferred, and *McDonough* did not foreclose those avenues. *See* 464 U.S. at 556-57 (Blackmun, J., concurring). The question is whether this relationship, standing alone, establishes disqualifying bias. It does not.

The tie is attenuated twice over. It runs from the juror through his niece to her husband, not to the assistant himself, and the assistant, though employed by the office that prosecuted, had no role in the case. Employment by the sovereign that prosecutes does not by itself imply bias. In *Dennis v. United States*, 339 U.S. 162, 172 (1950), the Supreme Court held that federal employees were not challengeable for cause solely by reason of that employment, while stressing that the way remains open in every case to bring a contention of bias "from the realm of speculation to the realm of fact," that the trial court has a serious duty to determine whether actual bias exists, and that its rulings on such challenges receive broad discretion. *Id.* at 168. The juror here was not employed by the government at all. If a government employee is not presumed biased in a government prosecution, the uncle of the wife of an employee who had no part in the case is not presumed biased either.

*Colombo* is consistent with that conclusion. It said that relatives of attorneys actually involved in the investigation and prosecution should not sit as jurors. 869 F.2d at 152. The line it drew was between those who conduct the prosecution and others. That line follows the source of potential partiality, which is a connection to the people conducting the case, not an arbitrary degree of kinship or common employment in a large office. We read *Colombo* to require no more, and we decline to extend it to every relative of every lawyer in the prosecuting office. The Fourth Circuit's decision in *Garland v. United States*, 182 F.2d 801, 802 (4th Cir. 1950), which rejected a challenge based on a juror's sister's marriage to an uncle of the prosecuting assistant, is not binding on us. We take it for no more than persuasive support, and we do not read it or anything we say here to make family relationships categorically irrelevant.

We therefore hold only that a juror's indirect family relationship to an assistant United States attorney in the prosecuting district, who had no involvement in the prosecution, does not by itself establish disqualifying implied bias. A closer relationship, a tie to someone who took part in the case, exposure to information about the prosecution, or evidence of actual partiality may require a different inquiry and a different result, and we do not decide those questions. Nor does our conclusion rest on the strength of the evidence of guilt. That evidence would be no answer to a showing that the jury was partial. The claim fails because the dishonesty and the bias on which it depends have not been established.

*D. Actual bias and a hearing.* Actual bias is a question of fact that the party asserting it must establish, and the trial court is the primary judge of a juror's impartiality. *Dennis*, 339 U.S. at 168. The district court had no finding or testimony of partiality before it because counsel chose to ask no questions, and kinship alone cannot supply what the record lacks. The only evidence is the relationship and the juror's honest failure to recall it.

Nor is there a basis for a remand for a hearing. The government made the juror available at sentencing, and counsel, who had conceded good faith, declined to question him. Where counsel expressly declined to develop a matter and no one has alleged bad faith or any other factual basis for thinking the juror biased, we will not order the inquiry counsel passed up. The district court did not abuse its discretion in denying the motion.

**III. The instructions.**

*A. Review.* Shaoul did not object to the charge, or to the answer to the jury's question, when the court could have corrected either. Rule 30 requires an objection that states distinctly the matter and the grounds, and the court was likewise given no chance to correct its answer to the jury. His claims are therefore reviewed for plain error under Rule 52(b). *Olano* requires an error, meaning a deviation from a legal rule; that the error be plain, meaning clear or obvious; and that it affect substantial rights, which ordinarily means it prejudiced the outcome, with the burden on the defendant. 507 U.S. at 733-35. Only then may the court correct the error, and it should do so only if the error seriously affects the fairness, integrity or public reputation of judicial proceedings. *Id.* at 736. We read the charge as a whole and do not judge a sentence in isolation. *United States v. Locascio*, 6 F.3d 924, 942 (2d Cir. 1993).

*B. Unanimity and the overt-act answer.* Jurors must agree on the factual basis of a conviction at least where that basis divides into distinct conceptual groupings. *United States v. Schiff*, 801 F.2d 108, 114 (2d Cir. 1986). In this circuit a general instruction requiring unanimity ordinarily ensures that agreement, and a court need not give a more specific instruction unless the complexity of the evidence or other factors create a genuine danger of jury confusion. *Id.* at 114-15; *United States v. Natelli*, 527 F.2d 311, 325 (2d Cir. 1975); *United States v. Harris*, 8 F.3d 943, 945 (2d Cir. 1993); *United States v. Peterson*, 768 F.2d 64, 68 (2d Cir. 1985).

The charge here gave the general instruction and, on the mail-fraud counts, twice directed unanimity on the material falsity of the representations. Shaoul argues that because several overt acts and several alleged falsehoods were in play, different jurors could have settled on different predicates, and that the answer to the jury's question, which told the jurors that proof of one overt act sufficed, did not say that they must agree on which. That possibility arises whenever an indictment alleges more than one act or statement, and it is the reason for the general instruction rather than a reason to displace it. To displace the ordinary rule Shaoul had to show complexity or some other circumstance creating a genuine danger that jurors would convict on incompatible factual bases. The case involved two insurance claims by one defendant, tried together on a single conspiracy count and ten mail-fraud counts. Nothing in the record described to us shows that the jury could have reached its verdict by combining findings that were mutually exclusive, and the charge did not authorize it to do so.

The jury's question, as the record describes it, asked whether all of the overt acts had to be proved. That is a question about the government's burden and not a report of disagreement over which act occurred. *See United States v. DiLapi*, 651 F.2d 140, 145-46 (2d Cir. 1981) (notes asking whether every alleged act had to be proved did not show uncertainty about unanimity). The answer was accurate: one overt act was sufficient. It corrected a misunderstanding of the government's burden and did not tell the jury that it could convict without agreement. It was given against a unanimity direction that had been delivered and never withdrawn. That distinguishes a case in which jurors are told they need not agree on which of several specified acts was committed. *See DiLapi*, 651 F.2d at 146. Read together, the charge and the answer do not present an error that is plain.

We do not decide whether jurors must agree on a particular overt act in a conspiracy prosecution, or whether a materially different and more complex prosecution would require a particularized unanimity instruction. The charge as given presents no plain error on either view of those questions. We add a word about practice. Where the evidence permits jurors to select among distinct acts or statements, a specific direction can add clarity, and it may be sound practice to give one. *See Natelli*, 527 F.2d at 325. But a course that is advisable is not thereby required, and its omission without objection is not plain error. *Peterson*, 768 F.2d at 68; *cf. United States v. Remington*, 191 F.2d 246, 250 (2d Cir. 1951) (describing a requested instruction on agreement as to time and place as correct, but not treating its refusal as reversible where its substance was probably covered by the unanimity instructions). Because Shaoul has not shown an error that is plain, we do not reach the remaining requirements of *Olano*.

*C. The remaining objections.* Shaoul also objects that the court's more particular description of the alleged falsehoods broadened the indictment, and that a sentence reciting a stipulation that mailings occurred diluted the mailing element. A constructive broadening of an indictment differs from a clarification of the falsehoods it charges, and Shaoul's objections do not show that the jury was permitted to convict of an offense different from the one charged. A stipulation that mailings occurred establishes that fact but does not remove the separate requirement that each mailing be incident to an essential part of the scheme. *See Schmuck v. United States*, 489 U.S. 705, 710-11 (1989). The sentence reciting the stipulation is read within the charge as a whole, *Locascio*, 6 F.3d at 942, and so read it did not remove that requirement. Neither objection shows an error that is plain. As with the unanimity claim, our conclusion rests on the charge and the governing error standard, not on the strength of the government's proof.

**IV. Conclusion.** The conceded honest omission defeats the nondisclosure claim; the juror's indirect relationship to an uninvolved prosecutor does not by itself establish disqualifying bias; the record shows no actual bias and no ground for a hearing; and the charge presents no plain error. The judgment of conviction and the order denying a new trial are affirmed.

#### Separate opinions

None. There is no concurrence and no dissent, and no separate writing.

#### What the decision does

**Controlling holdings.** The opinion is precedential on four propositions, each joined by all three judges.

1. Part II.B, joined by all three judges: a conceded honest voir dire omission fails the first requirement of *McDonough*, and *Langford* does not remove it.
2. Part II.C, joined by all three: a juror's indirect relationship to an assistant United States attorney in the prosecuting district, who had no involvement in the case, does not by itself establish disqualifying implied bias. The test and the burden are the movant's. Closer ties, participation, exposure to case information, and evidence of partiality are reserved.
3. Part II.D, joined by all three: no actual bias is shown on a record in which counsel conceded good faith and declined to examine the juror, and no hearing is warranted.
4. Parts III.B and III.C, joined by all three: the unobjected-to charge, read as a whole with the accurate answer to the jury's question, presents no plain error on unanimity, the overt-act answer, the description of the falsehoods or the mailing stipulation, and the court does not hold that a particularized unanimity or overt-act instruction is required.
