**Treatment of precedent and limits.** The opinion follows and applies *McDonough*, *Langford*, *Colombo*, *Dennis*, *Olano*, *Schiff*, *Natelli*, *Harris* and *Peterson* and overrules none of them. It adopts no new rule beyond the four propositions above. It does not hold that the claim of juror bias is waived, does not hold that kinship to a prosecuting-office lawyer is irrelevant, does not decide whether jurors must agree on a particular overt act, and does not decide whether a more complex prosecution would require a particularized unanimity instruction. Its observations about closer relationships and about trial practice are not holdings. *Garland* is treated as persuasive only.

**Effect on the parties.** The affirmance is party-specific relief: it leaves Shaoul's conviction and sentence in place. It creates no law of the case beyond this appeal, and it is not a decision on any sentencing question, since none was raised. The judgment of conviction and the order denying a new trial are affirmed in full. The court orders no remand, no evidentiary hearing on the juror, no new trial and no sentencing relief.

**Mandate and next step.** The mandate has not issued. Under the Federal Rules of Appellate Procedure in force, a petition for rehearing may be filed within the time Rule 40 allows after entry of judgment. Rule 41 provides that the mandate issues after that time expires if no petition is filed, that a timely petition stays the mandate until it is disposed of unless the court orders otherwise, and that the time may be shortened or enlarged by order. No petition, motion or other filing is assumed, and no mandate date has been fixed.

#### Historical comparison

Historically, United States v. Shaoul, No. 94-1093, 41 F.3d 811, 815-18 (2d Cir. Dec. 1, 1994), affirmed the conviction. Judges Lumbard, Altimari and Cabranes sat; Judge Cabranes wrote for a unanimous panel, with no separate opinions. The court held that the conceded honest voir dire omission did not satisfy McDonough, that the juror's indirect relationship to an uninvolved prosecutor did not independently require disqualification, and that the charge, including the material-falsity direction and the overt-act answer, showed no plain error. No new trial or sentencing relief was granted.

The simulated judgment is unchanged: affirmed in full, 3 to 0, no separate writing, no remand, hearing or sentencing relief, decided the same day. What differs is the panel and authorship: Judge Stone-Khoury sits in place of Judge Cabranes, and Judge Altimari writes. The simulated reasoning reaches the same grounds; the historical summary does not say whether the original separately addressed waiver, actual bias, a hearing, or the reserved open questions, which the simulated opinion does.

Source: United States v. Shaoul, 41 F.3d 811, 815-18 (2d Cir. 1994) (public reproduction at law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/41/811/564137/).

### [United States v. Duncan](../cases/united-states-v-duncan/)

Decision date: December 2, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 93-1402  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Published opinion. Judgment of the district court affirmed in all respects. The mandate has not issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/united-states-v-duncan/events/1994-12-02-decision.md](../cases/united-states-v-duncan/events/1994-12-02-decision.md)

Historical panel: Wilfred Feinberg (Senior Circuit Judge); Lawrence W. Pierce (Senior Circuit Judge); Roger J. Miner (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: Wilfred Feinberg (Senior Circuit Judge); Lawrence W. Pierce (Senior Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Roger J. Miner.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Appeal from the final judgment of the United States District Court for the District of Connecticut (T. F. Gilroy Daly, District Judge), entered after a jury trial.

#### Decision summary

Vinal S. Duncan was convicted by a jury on four counts: a conspiracy under 18 U.S.C. section 371 (Count One), bank fraud under 18 U.S.C. section 1344 (Count Two), conspiracy to make corrupt payments (Count Four), and corrupt payments under 18 U.S.C. section 666(a)(2) (Count Five). The district court imposed concurrent prison terms, the longest of sixty-five months, and, on the counts governed by the Sentencing Guidelines, applied a four-level increase for Duncan's role as an organizer or leader and a separate two-level increase for perjury.

The Court of Appeals affirmed in all respects. It held, on each of four questions, as follows.

1. Expert testimony. An Internal Revenue Service agent, Thomas Mulligan, testified about financial records, false returns, and the movement of funds. The court held that the challenged parts of his testimony stayed within factual explanation and that admitting them was not an abuse of discretion. In the alternative, the court held that any error was harmless.
2. Ex post facto challenge. Duncan argued that his bank-fraud conduct was completed in 1983, before section 1344 took effect on October 12, 1984. The court rejected the challenge because the April 1985 sale of the second parcel to the bank was an execution of the charged scheme after the statute took effect.
3. Count One. The count stands on the jury's express finding of a bank-fraud object and of an overt act after October 12, 1984. The court did not decide whether the IRS object of the conspiracy is time-barred.
4. Leadership adjustment. The four-level increase under section 3B1.1(a) of the Sentencing Guidelines was not clearly erroneous, and no resentencing is required.

Judges Feinberg, Pierce, and Stone were for affirmance on every component, and none was against. Judge Feinberg wrote the opinion of the court, joined in full by Judges Pierce and Stone. There is no concurrence or dissent. The mandate has not issued.

#### The case and the result

**Posture and issues.** Duncan appeals from a judgment of conviction on Counts One, Two, Four, and Five. He seeks reversal or resentencing. The United States seeks affirmance.

**The bank transactions.** Duncan was a director of Security Savings & Loan Association and a member of its loan committee. In 1983 he and other committee members arranged to purchase two parcels on Meriden Road through a nominee, concealing their beneficial ownership from the remaining directors. They used a mortgage financed by the bank, leased property to the bank, and later approved sales of the parcels to the bank without disclosing their interests. The first parcel was sold for $200,000 in October 1984 and the second for $135,000 in April 1985. The transactions produced $135,000 above the initial purchase amount, and the beneficial owners received distributions by checks of less than $10,000.

**The Taft Group.** In 1986 Duncan, Richard Barbieri, and John Corpaci formed the Taft Group. It provided benefits to Waterbury Mayor Joseph Santopietro and his associates in return for favorable official treatment. Barbieri and Corpaci pleaded guilty, cooperated, and described Duncan's knowledge and participation. One transaction was a payment of $63,000 made through a lease of uninhabitable office space. At trial Duncan disputed guilty knowledge and intent. He did not deny that the transactions and corrupt payments occurred.

**The agent's testimony.** Thomas Mulligan, an agent of the Internal Revenue Service, testified about the financial records, the tax system, false returns, and the movement of funds. He had taken part in the investigation and had reviewed the records. Duncan objected that portions of the testimony supplied legal conclusions.

**The verdict.** The jury convicted on Counts One, Two, Four, and Five. Its verdict specifically identified a bank-fraud object of the Count One conspiracy and an overt act after October 12, 1984, the date on which section 1344 took effect.

**Jurisdiction.** The district court had jurisdiction under 18 U.S.C. section 3231. Appellate jurisdiction rests on 28 U.S.C. section 1291 and, for the sentencing challenge, 18 U.S.C. section 3742. The papers fixing the date of entry of judgment and the date of the notice of appeal are not among the materials before the court. Neither party presents a question about timeliness, and the court decides none.

**The issues and the standard of review for each.**

1. Whether portions of Mulligan's testimony crossed from expert explanation into legal instruction, and if so whether the error was harmless. Admission of expert testimony is reviewed for abuse of discretion, with independent review of the boundary between permissible assistance and an impermissible legal conclusion. Preserved nonconstitutional error in a criminal trial is reviewed under Fed. R. Crim. P. 52(a) and Kotteakos v. United States, 328 U.S. 750 (1946).
2. Whether section 1344 was applied to an offense completed before the statute took effect. Ex post facto and statutory questions receive independent review, and the court takes the verdict and the evidence as they stand.
3. Whether the Count One conviction survives the argument that the IRS object of the conspiracy is untimely, given the verdict's express identification of a bank-fraud object. The legal effect of the alternative bases is reviewed independently.
4. Whether the four-level leadership adjustment is supported. The role finding is reviewed for clear error and the meaning of the guideline independently.

The relevant dates are the effective date of section 1344 (October 12, 1984), the two sales (October 1984 and April 1985), the formation of the Taft Group (1986), the argument (April 13, 1994), and this decision (December 2, 1994).

**Judgment.** The judgment of the district court is affirmed in all respects. By component:

1. Expert testimony. The admission of the challenged testimony was within the district court's discretion; alternatively, any error was harmless. Affirmed. For: Judges Feinberg, Pierce, and Stone. Against: none.
2. Ex post facto challenge to the bank-fraud convictions. The challenge is rejected. Affirmed. For: Judges Feinberg, Pierce, and Stone. Against: none.
3. Count One. The count is sustained on the jury's express bank-fraud finding, and the limitations argument directed at the IRS object is not decided. Affirmed. For: Judges Feinberg, Pierce, and Stone. Against: none.
4. Leadership adjustment. The four-level increase under section 3B1.1(a) is affirmed, without resentencing. For: Judges Feinberg, Pierce, and Stone. Against: none.

Duncan's issues are the four above; none is directed specifically at Counts Four and Five apart from the evidentiary objection in component 1. The convictions on Counts One, Two, Four, and Five and the sentence stand.

Opinion and joins: One opinion of the court, by Judge Feinberg, joined by Judge Pierce and Judge Stone as to every Part (I.A, I.B, I.C, II, III, and IV). No concurring or dissenting opinion is filed, and no judge qualifies a join.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

Opinion of the court by Judge Feinberg, joined in full by Judges Pierce and Stone.

##### I. The testimony of Agent Mulligan

*A. The governing line.* Rule 702 of the Federal Rules of Evidence admits the opinion of a qualified witness when specialized knowledge will assist the jury, and Rule 704(a) provides that an opinion is not objectionable merely because it embraces an ultimate issue the jury must decide. Neither rule makes a witness a source of law for the jury. We have held that Rule 704 was not intended to allow experts to offer opinions embodying legal conclusions. United States v. Scop, 846 F.2d 135, 139-42 (2d Cir.), modified, 856 F.2d 5 (1988); see United States v. Bilzerian, 926 F.2d 1285, 1294 (2d Cir. 1991); Hygh v. Jacobs, 961 F.2d 359, 363-65 (2d Cir. 1992) (civil action; expert testimony that expresses a legal conclusion must be excluded). The line separates a factual conclusion that helps the jury understand the evidence, even on a matter the jury must decide, from an opinion that instructs the jury on the governing law or announces that the accused committed the charged offense. Scop also holds that an expert may not rest an opinion on his own assessment of other witnesses' credibility. Mulligan was not simply assessing the credibility of witnesses from their trial testimony, so that part of Scop is not at issue, and we do not extend it.

Three points about the line decide this appeal. First, it is functional. What matters is what the testimony conveys to the jury, not whether the witness says the word "guilty." A label that names an offense can carry an official legal judgment, and the fact that it names an uncharged offense, such as money laundering here, does not make it admissible. Second, the witness's status is not an exemption. That an agent took part in the investigation and reviewed the records bears on his foundation and on the context in which the jury hears him. See United States v. Schwartz, 924 F.2d 410, 425-26 (2d Cir. 1991) (agent testified both as an expert and as a witness to the investigation; his opinion explaining why the investigation continued did not offend Scop). It does not authorize him to apply the criminal law for the jury. Schwartz examined the testimony in context and holds nothing broader. Third, the rule does not bar what Rule 702 invites. An agent may organize a complicated financial record, identify discrepancies in filings, explain why a return or filing was false, trace the movement of funds, and describe how false filings and transactions bore on the administration of the tax system.

*B. Application.* Duncan's objection is aimed at references to money laundering and to filings that obstructed the IRS. He argues that they carried an official legal judgment even though Mulligan never said that Duncan was guilty. The government answers that Mulligan organized a complicated record and explained, from his investigation and his review of the documents, why particular filings were false, and that he did not tell the jury that Duncan was guilty of bank fraud or of either conspiracy.

Duncan is right on the principle. Omitting the word "guilty" would not save testimony that in substance told the jury that the transactions were legally money laundering or that the filings legally amounted to a charged offense. But considered in the context of the testimony of which they formed a part, the references he challenges did not do that. They explained what the documents showed, how the funds moved, and how false filings affected the working of the tax system. They did not instruct the jury on the elements of any count, and they did not announce that Duncan had the criminal intent the government had to prove or that he had committed any charged offense. An agent's statement that falsified filings interfered with the assessment and collection of tax describes an effect on tax administration that lies within the knowledge of an IRS agent. A statement that the accused thereby committed the charged conspiracy would cross the line. The challenged testimony stayed on the permitted side, and admitting it was not an abuse of discretion. We rest on what the testimony did in this trial. We do not hold that a government witness may use legal labels because he investigated the case, knows the participants, or names an uncharged offense, and testimony of this kind will not always survive scrutiny.

*C. Harmlessness, in the alternative.* Even if some phrase went beyond explanation, the judgment would stand. Duncan objected, so a nonconstitutional error is reviewed under Rule 52(a) of the Federal Rules of Criminal Procedure, which directs that an error not affecting substantial rights be disregarded, and under Kotteakos v. United States, 328 U.S. 750 (1946). The question under Kotteakos is whether we can say "with fair assurance, after pondering all that happened without stripping the erroneous action from the whole, that the judgment was not substantially swayed by the error." This court has put the same inquiry as whether it is highly probable that the error did not contribute to the verdict, United States v. Castro, 813 F.2d 571, 577 (2d Cir. 1987), and as whether the error had a substantial and injurious effect or influence in determining the jury's verdict, United States v. Teitler, 802 F.2d 606, 616 (2d Cir. 1986).

Three features of the inquiry matter here. The burden does not rest on Duncan. Because his objection was preserved, the government, as the beneficiary of the error, must persuade us that it was harmless, and it cannot do so by observing that he has not proved prejudice. Cf. United States v. Olano, 507 U.S. 725 (1993) (for forfeited error under Rule 52(b), the defendant bears the burden of persuasion on prejudice, a burden shifting that distinguishes Rule 52(b) from Rule 52(a)). Nor does the civil harmless-error rule, Fed. R. Civ. P. 61, or any civil authority applying it, supply the measure in a criminal trial. Second, the sufficiency of the remaining evidence is not the test. A verdict that enough evidence could support may still have been swayed by an error, and the question is the error's actual influence in this trial. Third, the court looks at the whole record, including how the challenged material figured in the case.

Measured that way, any error was harmless. The challenged references were a limited part of testimony that otherwise explained documents and transactions. Duncan did not deny at trial that the transactions and corrupt payments occurred; he disputed knowledge and intent. Whatever their precise legal flavor, the references characterized what the financial records showed. They did not supply proof of what Duncan knew or intended, and the testimony did not announce it. The proof on those contested questions came from Barbieri and Corpaci, participants who pleaded guilty, cooperated, and described Duncan's knowledge and participation, and from the documentary record of the transactions themselves. Compare United States v. Castillo, No. 92-1279 (2d Cir. Jan. 28, 1994) (Part II.B) (officers' repetition of a sole eyewitness's account of a firearm, used in summation to corroborate that contested fact, was not harmless under Kotteakos). The error in Castillo bore on a contested fact and was used to prove it; the references here did not bear on knowledge and intent. We are satisfied that the verdict was not substantially swayed by them. We reach that conclusion on the influence of the challenged material, not on the sufficiency of the other evidence and not on any failure by Duncan to show prejudice.

##### II. Section 1344 and the Ex Post Facto Clause

Duncan argues that the conduct underlying his bank-fraud convictions was completed in 1983, before 18 U.S.C. section 1344 took effect on October 12, 1984, so that Count Two, and the bank-fraud object of Count One, punish a completed pre-enactment offense. We review the constitutional and statutory question independently. We do not substitute a different factual theory for the one the jury's verdict and the evidence support.

The rule. The Ex Post Facto Clause, U.S. Const. art. I, section 9, cl. 3, forbids applying section 1344 to an offense completed before the statute took effect, and a conviction may not rest on conduct that predates the effective date. United States v. Torres, 901 F.2d 205, 226 (2d Cir. 1990) (ex post facto error where the instruction and verdict form let the jury apply a statute on the basis of conduct before its effective date). The Clause does not bar application of a statute to criminal activity that continues after enactment. United States v. Ferrara, 458 F.2d 868, 874 (2d Cir. 1972) (conspiracy and overt acts continued long after the amendment). Section 1344 punishes one who knowingly executes, or attempts to execute, a scheme or artifice to defraud a financial institution. Two qualifications limit the reach of a continuing-scheme theory. Later acts of concealment after the central objectives of a crime or conspiracy have been attained do not extend it, United States v. Fletcher, 928 F.2d 495, 499 (2d Cir. 1991); United States v. Knuckles, 581 F.2d 305, 313 (2d Cir. 1978), and the lingering effects of a completed wrong do not either. An agreement whose object includes receipt of anticipated profit, by contrast, continues until the profit is realized. Fletcher, 928 F.2d at 500; Knuckles, 581 F.2d at 313.

The contentions. Duncan characterizes the wrong as the appropriation of a corporate opportunity in 1983, which later transactions and concealment could not turn into a new federal crime. The government's charged theory was broader in a way that matters legally: a purchase through a concealed nominee, followed by a profitable resale to the bank.

The evidence fits the government's theory. The parcels were bought through a nominee, with ownership hidden from the other directors and with financing from the bank, and the participants then approved the bank's purchases without disclosing their interests. The premium of $135,000 over the purchase amount was the product of those sales, and the beneficial owners received it. The resale to the bank was the means by which the scheme drew money from the bank. It was not a later effort to conceal a crime already complete, and it was not merely a lingering effect of the 1983 acquisition. The second parcel was sold to the bank in April 1985, months after section 1344 took effect, and that sale was an execution of the scheme within the statute. For Count One the jury also expressly found an overt act after October 12, 1984. We rest on the April 1985 sale and on that finding. We do not rely on the October 1984 sale, because the materials before us do not place it before or after October 12. We do not rely on any later agreement or effort to conceal the scheme, and we hold neither that concealment extends a fraud indefinitely nor that every later economic effect is an execution. Because the convictions do not depend solely on the 1983 acquisition and a post-enactment execution is shown, Duncan's temporal objection fails.

##### III. Count One and the IRS object

Duncan argues that the IRS object of the Count One conspiracy is untimely and that this defect undermines the whole count. The jury's verdict, however, specifically identified the bank-fraud object and found an overt act after October 12, 1984. We hold that this express finding supplies a valid basis for Count One independent of the IRS object, and we therefore do not decide whether the IRS object is time-barred.

The rule. Griffin v. United States, 502 U.S. 46, 49-60 (1991), distinguishes a theory the evidence fails to support from a theory the law forbids. Jurors are equipped to decide whether the facts support a theory, so a general verdict on a multiple-object conspiracy stands if the evidence supports one object, even if it is inadequate as to another. When a possible ground is legally unavailable, the verdict may have to be set aside because one cannot tell whether the jury relied on it. Griffin is therefore not a rule that one valid object always cures another that is legally unavailable, and we do not read it that way. The question is whether a valid basis is shown by what this jury actually decided, without presuming how a silent verdict was reached.

Application. This is not a silent general verdict. The jury identified the bank-fraud object of the conspiracy under 18 U.S.C. section 371 and found an overt act after the statute's effective date. Part II holds that a bank-fraud object reaching the post-enactment sale to the bank is legally valid. A valid basis for Count One is therefore ascertainable from the verdict itself. Duncan answers that an untimely IRS object taints a general conspiracy conviction. That would matter if the verdict could have rested on the IRS object alone, or if the bank-fraud basis could not be told apart from it. Neither is so here. We leave undecided the limitations argument directed at the IRS object, and we leave undecided any broader proposition that Griffin cures every legally invalid alternative.

##### IV. The leadership adjustment

Section 3B1.1(a) of the Sentencing Guidelines provides a four-level increase for a defendant who was an organizer or leader of criminal activity that involved five or more participants or was otherwise extensive. Duncan does not challenge the size or extensiveness predicate. He denies that he was a leader. The unchallenged perjury adjustment is not before us.

The standard. A district court's finding of a defendant's role under section 3B1.1 is a factual determination reviewed for clear error. United States v. Garcia, 936 F.2d 648, 656 (2d Cir. 1991); United States v. Beaulieau, 959 F.2d 375, 380 (2d Cir. 1992). We interpret the guideline independently. We follow United States v. Castillo, No. 92-1279 (2d Cir. Jan. 28, 1994) (Part VI), a published decision of this court, for the standard, the burden, and the inquiry: the government must establish the organizing or supervisory facts by a preponderance of the evidence; possession of proceeds is not by itself a basis for the adjustment; and the court looks to the discretion exercised, the nature and degree of the defendant's role in planning or organizing the offense, and the control and authority held over other participants. See also Beaulieau, 959 F.2d at 380 (control and authority over others, discretion, and role in planning). Castillo was a case under subsection (c), is confined to its record, and states no general rule about role enhancements, and we rely on it for no more. Under that inquiry a corporate title cannot substitute for actual authority, planning, or direction. Nor does the possibility that others also led weaken a finding about Duncan: there can be more than one leader or organizer of a criminal enterprise. Garcia, 936 F.2d at 656.

The contentions and application. Duncan argues that other participants controlled the deals and that his position does not establish criminal leadership. The government points to repeated planning, central involvement in the real estate ventures, the testimony of the participants who pleaded guilty, and the sham office lease as showing actual direction beyond a title. The question is whether the evidence permitted the sentencing court to find that Duncan organized or led, not whether we would rank the participants differently. It did. Duncan did not merely hold a position. In the bank transactions he and other committee members arranged the purchase of the parcels through a nominee, hid the beneficial ownership from the remaining directors, and later approved the sales to the bank without disclosing their interests. In the Taft Group he joined Barbieri and Corpaci in forming the enterprise in 1986, and Barbieri and Corpaci described his knowledge and participation in the arrangements by which benefits went to the Waterbury mayor and his associates, including the $63,000 payment made through the lease of uninhabitable office space. The sentencing court was entitled to credit that account over Duncan's denial that he led, and the fact that Barbieri, Corpaci, and others also exercised authority does not negate his. We see no indication that the finding rested only on a title or on Duncan's receipt of proceeds. The finding is not clearly erroneous, and the adjustment stands. No resentencing is required.

##### Matters left open

The opinion does not decide: the timeliness of the appeal, whose filing dates are not before us; whether the IRS object of Count One is time-barred; whether Griffin cures every legally invalid alternative; whether any government witness may ever use a legal label, or whether Scop's credibility holding extends to an agent who also testifies to the investigation; whether concealment or later economic effects can ever extend a section 1344 offense; the date of the October 1984 sale relative to October 12, 1984; whether the size or extensiveness predicate of section 3B1.1(a) is met, which is unchallenged; the perjury adjustment; and any rule for role enhancements other than the standard, burden, and inquiry stated in Castillo, Part VI.

#### Separate opinions

None. No concurring or dissenting opinion is filed, and no judge qualifies a join.

#### What the decision does

**Controlling holdings, by Part and joins.**

- Part I.A and I.B (Feinberg, Pierce, Stone): Rules 702 and 704(a) admit an investigating agent's explanation of records, fund movements, false filings, and their effect on tax administration, but not instruction on the governing law or a pronouncement of guilt, and the agent's role is no exemption (Scop, Bilzerian, Hygh, Schwartz). The test is functional, applied to the testimony in context; review is for abuse of discretion with independent review of the boundary. Applied: the challenged references stayed within factual explanation.
- Part I.C (Feinberg, Pierce, Stone), alternative and independent ground: preserved nonconstitutional evidentiary error in a criminal trial is reviewed under Rule 52(a) and Kotteakos; the government bears the burden; the question is the error's influence in this trial, not sufficiency and not the civil rule. Applied: any error was harmless.
- Part II (Feinberg, Pierce, Stone): the Ex Post Facto Clause bars applying section 1344 to an offense completed before October 12, 1984 but not to a scheme executed after; concealment and lingering effects do not alone extend a crime. Applied: the April 1985 sale to the bank executed the charged scheme.
- Part III (Feinberg, Pierce, Stone): a jury's express identification of a bank-fraud object and a post-enactment overt act supplies a valid basis for the conspiracy count independent of the challenged IRS object; Griffin is not a universal one-valid-object cure.
- Part IV (Feinberg, Pierce, Stone): role findings under section 3B1.1(a) are reviewed for clear error and the guideline independently; the government's burden is a preponderance; title or receipt of proceeds alone does not suffice; evidence of planning, concealment from the other directors, formation of the Taft Group, and the sham lease supported the finding.

**Precedent and legal effect.** This is a published opinion of the court, joined by all three judges, and it is precedent in this circuit to the extent of the holdings stated in Parts I through IV and summarized above. It follows Scop, Bilzerian, and Hygh, Kotteakos and Rule 52(a), Torres and Ferrara, Griffin, and Castillo, Part VI (for the standard, burden, and inquiry on role findings only). It cites Castillo, Part II.B, only by comparison. The harmlessness holding is an alternative ground, independent of the ruling that there was no error. No earlier decision of this court is overruled, displaced, or narrowed.

The statements that the October 1984 sale and later concealment are not relied on, and the matters left open above, mark the limits of the holdings and are not rulings. The affirmance of Duncan's convictions and sentence is party-specific relief. The characterization of the agent's testimony is a ruling on this trial record and is not a rule for other records.

**Effect on the parties.** The judgment of the district court is affirmed. There is no new trial, no resentencing, and no remand. Duncan's convictions on Counts One, Two, Four, and Five and his sentence stand.

**Mandate and remand.** The mandate has not issued, and the date of this opinion is not the date of the mandate. Under Fed. R. App. P. 40(a), a petition for rehearing in a criminal case may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment, unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order or by local rule. Under Fed. R. App. P. 41(a), as amended effective December 1, 1994, the mandate issues 7 days after the time for filing a petition for rehearing expires, unless a petition is filed or the court shortens or enlarges the time by order; if a timely petition is filed, the mandate issues 7 days after entry of an order denying it, unless the court shortens or enlarges the time by order. The periods run from entry of judgment, and this decision assumes no filing by any party.

#### Historical comparison

Historically, on December 2, 1994, Judges Feinberg, Pierce and Miner affirmed the convictions and sentence in a unanimous published opinion by Judge Pierce, with no separate opinions. United States v. Duncan, No. 93-1402, 42 F.3d 97, 101-07 (2d Cir. 1994). The simulated panel (Feinberg, Pierce, Stone) also affirmed unanimously on the same date, with Judge Stone replacing Judge Miner and Judge Feinberg writing. The judgment is unchanged; the differences are in reasoning. Both approved the agent's testimony, rejected the ex post facto objection, sustained Count One on the identified bank-fraud object, and upheld the leadership finding. The historical alternative harmlessness discussion used Civil Rule 61 and civil prejudice authorities. The simulated opinion applies Rule 52(a) and Kotteakos, puts the burden on the government, and rejects the civil rule as the measure. Its ex post facto holding rests on the April 1985 sale and the jury's post-enactment overt-act finding alone.

### [American Geophysical Union v. Texaco, Inc.](../cases/american-geophysical-union-v-texaco/)

Decision date: December 23, 1994 (simulated decision)  
Docket(s): No. 92-9341  
Decision type: Panel decision  
Disposition: Determination that the copying is not fair use AFFIRMED; case REMANDED to the district court for further proceedings on matters not submitted. Opinion for publication; no reporter citation has been assigned. The order under review is interlocutory (certified under 28 U.S.C. § 1292(b)), and the mandate has not issued.  
Final decision record: [years/1994/cases/american-geophysical-union-v-texaco/events/1994-12-23-decision.md](../cases/american-geophysical-union-v-texaco/events/1994-12-23-decision.md)

Historical panel: Jon O. Newman (Chief Judge, active circuit service); Ralph K. Winter, Jr. (Active Circuit Judge); Dennis Jacobs (Active Circuit Judge).  
Simulation panel: Jon O. Newman (Chief Judge, active circuit service); Ralph K. Winter, Jr. (Active Circuit Judge); Alex-Lamar Stone-Khoury (Active Circuit Judge).  
Stone replaces: Dennis Jacobs.  
Assignment basis: Stone takes the third-listed seat of the published panel, a mechanical convention applied uniformly to these eighteen cases and fixed before any vote modeling. It is not based on an expected vote, author, or outcome; it does not reproduce a historical assignment order; and it does not remove the replaced judge from the roster.

Appeal from: United States District Court for the Southern District of New York (Pierre N. Leval, District Judge), 802 F. Supp. 1 (S.D.N.Y. 1992).

#### Decision summary

Texaco Inc. had a researcher, Dr. Donald H. Chickering II, keep complete photocopies of eight articles from the Journal of Catalysis in his files. A group of scientific and technical publishers sued Texaco over unauthorized photocopying. The district court held that the copying of those eight articles is not fair use and certified that ruling for interlocutory appeal. The Second Circuit panel, by Chief Judge Newman for a unanimous court (Judges Winter and Stone joining in full), affirmed that limited determination and remanded the case for further proceedings.

The court weighed the four statutory fair-use factors together rather than counting them. It gave Texaco credit on the first factor (modestly, at most) because the copying served research, and on the second factor because the articles are predominantly factual. It found the third factor favored the publishers because each article was copied in full. It held that the fourth factor, the effect on the potential market, weighed most heavily against Texaco and decided the balance on this record. In doing so it adopted a three-condition test for when lost licensing revenue counts as market harm. Texaco, which bears the burden on the defense, did not carry it.

The holding is confined to the eight retained complete copies on the stipulated record. The court did not decide damages, the scope of any injunction, the legality of other scientists' copying, or whether Texaco's library qualifies under 17 U.S.C. § 108. There is no concurrence and no dissent.

#### The case and the result

**Decision under review.** The decision under review is an opinion and order of the district court dated October 26, 1992, 802 F. Supp. 1, holding that Texaco's copying of eight articles from the Journal of Catalysis, retained in the files of one of its researchers, is not fair use, and certifying that ruling for interlocutory appeal under 28 U.S.C. § 1292(b). Id. at 29. The order is not a final judgment. It does not decide the legality of every photocopy made by every scientist, the damages for any claimed infringement, or the scope of an injunction. Texaco seeks reversal of the fair-use ruling. The publishers seek affirmance as to the selected copying practice.

**Material record.** The American Geophysical Union and eighty-two other publishers sued Texaco over unauthorized photocopying of scientific and technical articles. Texaco employed approximately 400 to 500 researchers nationally and maintained a substantial research library at Beacon, New York. Its subscriptions to the Journal of Catalysis rose from one to two in 1983 and to three in 1988. The parties narrowed the first trial to a written record on the fair-use defense, randomly selected one researcher, Dr. Donald H. Chickering II, a chemical engineer conducting catalysis research, and focused on eight articles from the Journal retained in his files. He or other Texaco employees made complete copies for his use. Six articles came to his attention through circulated issues and two through references in other publications. The copies were retained principally for possible later consultation rather than immediate use in an experiment, and he did not ultimately use five of them. Academic Press generally owned the copyrights in the individual articles through assignments from authors, who were not paid for publication. The articles are predominantly factual scientific writings. The record also addressed the Copyright Clearance Center's licensing arrangements, the feasibility of licensing institutional copying of this kind, and the limited evidence of added subscription demand if photocopying were unavailable. The district court found that convenient and reasonably priced licensing procedures were available to Texaco for this reproduction. 802 F. Supp. at 18, 25.

**Jurisdiction and dates.** The district court had jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1338(a). The appeal comes to this court on the district court's written certification under § 1292(b), which permits a court of appeals, in its discretion, to allow an appeal from a certified order on application made within ten days after the order's entry. The papers before the panel do not include the application, the order allowing the appeal, or docket entries bearing on its timeliness, so the appeal is decided as certified and presented, and nothing outside the certified order is reached. Relevant dates: district court opinion and certification, October 26, 1992; argument, May 20, 1993; resubmission on the papers and decision, December 23, 1994.

**Issue and standard of review.** One issue: whether Texaco's complete photocopies of the eight retained articles are fair use under 17 U.S.C. § 107. The ultimate fair-use determination is a mixed question of law and fact, reviewed independently, and subsidiary findings of fact are reviewed for clear error. Twin Peaks Productions, Inc. v. Publications International, Ltd., 996 F.2d 1366, 1374 (2d Cir. 1993). Texaco bears the burden on its affirmative defense. The fair-use defense was tried and decided on the stipulated record, which also fixes the reach of the question: eight articles in one researcher's files, with damages and the scope of relief not submitted.

**Judgment.**

1. Fair use of the eight retained complete copies. The determination that the copying is not fair use is AFFIRMED. For: Newman, Winter, Stone. Against: none.
2. Remedy and remand. The case is REMANDED to the district court for further proceedings on matters not submitted. No damages are set, no injunction is directed, and no licensing system is ordered. For: Newman, Winter, Stone. Against: none.
3. Reasoning. The opinion of the court, Parts I through V, is joined in full by all three judges. There is no concurrence and no dissent.

**Opinion and joins.** Opinion of the court by Chief Judge Newman, joined in full by Judges Winter and Stone. No separate writing.

#### Opinion of the court

*Simulated judicial opinion based on the finalized decision.*

NEWMAN, Chief Judge, for the court, in which WINTER and STONE, Circuit Judges, join.

This certified interlocutory appeal asks whether complete photocopies of eight scientific articles, made for one Texaco researcher and kept in his files, are a fair use under 17 U.S.C. § 107. The district court held that they are not. We affirm that limited determination, on the stipulated record and for the reasons below, and we decide nothing beyond it.

**I. The record and the question**

The publishers sued Texaco over photocopying of scientific and technical articles. The parties narrowed the first trial to a written record on fair use and selected one researcher at random, Dr. Chickering, a chemical engineer doing catalysis research. The record concerns eight Journal of Catalysis articles copied in full and kept in his files, chiefly for possible later consultation, five of which went unused. The articles are predominantly factual, and Academic Press generally owns the copyrights through assignments from unpaid authors. The stipulation frames a representative inquiry. It does not make one researcher's files a legislative record about scientific institutions generally.

**II. The governing framework**

Section 106(1) gives the copyright owner the exclusive right to reproduce the work in copies. Section 107 provides that fair use of a work for purposes such as scholarship or research is not an infringement, and it directs courts to consider the purpose and character of the use, the nature of the work, the amount and substantiality of the portion used, and the effect of the use on the potential market for or value of the work. The task "is not to be simplified with bright-line rules." Campbell v. Acuff-Rose Music, Inc., 510 U.S. 569, 577 (1994). Each factor is explored and the results are weighed together in light of copyright's purposes. Id. at 578. They are not four votes to be counted.

Research is a listed purpose and it counts, but it is not an automatic exemption. A commercial setting does not by itself make a use unfair, because the listed purposes are generally pursued for profit. Id. at 584-85. Transformation strengthens a claim of fair use but is not absolutely necessary to one. Id. at 579; see Sony Corp. of America v. Universal City Studios, Inc., 464 U.S. 417, 449-51 (1984) (complete copying of whole programs for private viewing held fair). Fair use is an affirmative defense, so Texaco bears the burden, and a proponent will have difficulty carrying it without favorable evidence about the relevant markets. Campbell, 510 U.S. at 590; Harper & Row, Publishers, Inc. v. Nation Enterprises, 471 U.S. 539, 561 (1985). We review the ultimate determination independently and the subsidiary findings for clear error. Twin Peaks, 996 F.2d at 1374. Because the stipulated record and the district court's findings suffice to evaluate each factor, no remand for further factfinding is needed. Harper & Row, 471 U.S. at 560.

Section 108 does not alter the inquiry. Subsection (f)(4) preserves the right of fair use under § 107, so neither compliance nor noncompliance with a library provision decides this appeal. We express no view on whether Texaco's library qualifies under § 108.

**III. The four factors**

*A. Purpose and character.* Texaco's strongest argument is that these were copies for a scientist's actual research, not for sale, public distribution, or a competing publication. Scholarship and research are within the statute's list, and our decisions give uses of that kind real weight under the first factor. Wright v. Warner Books, Inc., 953 F.2d 731, 736 (2d Cir. 1991); New Era Publications International, ApS v. Carol Publishing Group, 904 F.2d 152, 156 (2d Cir. 1990). That Dr. Chickering did not put five of the articles to use in an experiment does not deprive the copying of a research purpose. Reviewing the literature, identifying possible future work, and keeping references at hand are ordinary parts of research. Nor does Texaco's profit motive make a scientist's work something other than research.

The first factor also asks what the copy does. These copies added nothing to the articles. Each reproduced an article whole, to be read and consulted as an authorized copy would be. Wright and New Era concerned new works of biography that used the plaintiffs' writings, and they do not fix the weight of this factor for nontransformative duplicates. The district court, deciding before Campbell, treated the copying as neither transformative nor noncommercial and resolved the factor for the publishers. 802 F. Supp. at 13. On independent review, crediting the research purpose fully and giving the commercial setting no presumptive force, we regard the factor as mixed and, at most, modestly favorable to Texaco. Labels such as "archival" or "institutional" do not decide it. A researcher's working file is still his file, and what matters is the reproduction function it serves.

*B. Nature of the works.* Published scientific articles are protected in their expression, but the facts and discoveries they report are not. Feist Publications, Inc. v. Rural Telephone Service Co., 499 U.S. 340, 344, 349-50 (1991). There is a greater need to disseminate factual works than works of fiction or fantasy, Harper & Row, 471 U.S. at 563, and the district court so found, 802 F. Supp. at 17. This factor favors Texaco, and the weight is real. It does not, however, make every complete copy of a factual publication fair, and it says little about substitution.

Texaco adds that scientific authors seek dissemination, not royalties. Copyright does not protect only works written for pay. The authors assigned their rights, and the publishers perform the institutional work of assembling and disseminating the journals. That work remains a legally recognized interest. Conversely, the point gives publishers no right to monopolize facts, to prevent independent experiments, or to collect a fee whenever a researcher learns from an article. The law protects expression and permitted uses separately, and we hold the publishers only to the former.

*C. Amount and substantiality.* The relevant works are the individual articles, each separately authored and owned, not the bound volume. Comparing eight articles with a two-hundred-page volume does not make the copying small. 802 F. Supp. at 17. Complete copying does not automatically defeat fair use, Sony, 464 U.S. at 449-50, and a scientist may need an entire article to follow its methods or results. That explains why excerpts would be less useful to him. It does not show that the full amount is needed for a use different from the article's own function, because the retained copy performs the whole article's ordinary reference function. Necessity to a researcher's preferred convenience is not, by itself, necessity enough to displace the reproduction right. The third factor favors the publishers.

*D. Effect on the potential market.* Two markets are in play and must not be confused.

The first is the market for subscriptions. The district court did not rest principally on lost subscriptions, 802 F. Supp. at 19-21, and neither do we. The record contains limited evidence that Texaco would have bought more subscriptions without photocopying, and nothing justifies assuming that it would have bought one for every researcher. We give no weight to a speculative subscription loss, and the thinness of that proof does not affect the result.

The second is the market for authorized reproduction. The fourth factor looks beyond the defendant's own copying to whether unrestricted and widespread conduct of the same sort would substantially and adversely affect the potential market for the original. Campbell, 510 U.S. at 590; see Harper & Row, 471 U.S. at 566-68. The potential markets that count are those that owners would in general develop or license others to develop. A use such as criticism, which owners are unlikely to license, falls outside that notion. Campbell, 510 U.S. at 592. An owner also cannot manufacture a market injury by announcing a fee for conduct that would otherwise be fair, for then every fair use would vanish whenever permission was offered for sale. But a licensing practice that owners actually serve for a particular kind of reproduction is a cognizable market, and copies that substitute for what it supplies injure it.

We therefore hold that lost licensing revenue is cognizable under the fourth factor only where each of three conditions is met. First, the market is for the kind of use at issue, a reproduction that serves as a substitute for an authorized copy, and not for a use that the law leaves free of permission, such as criticism, commentary, or the use of facts. Second, the licensing mechanism is shown by evidence to be actually operating for that kind of use, with permission available at a reasonable price and with reasonable convenience, and is not merely announced. Third, the copying in question in fact substitutes for what the mechanism supplies. The existence or availability of a fee is never proof of harm by itself. The harm is the substitution, shown by these conditions together, and the burden on the fair-use defense remains with Texaco. We need not decide when a mechanism not yet operating is a cognizable potential market.

The conditions are met here. The district court found that convenient and reasonably priced licensing procedures were available for this reproduction. 802 F. Supp. at 18, 25. We accept those findings. They concern a mechanism operating for the very activity at issue, institutional photocopying of journal articles, and not a fee announced to defeat an otherwise fair use. The copies are of the kind that mechanism supplies. They are complete, retained, working duplicates of articles, made at the employer's expense, that serve the same reading and reference function as authorized copies, and the researcher could consult them in place of further authorized copies. The publishers do not claim a right to control criticism or the use of facts. The relevant loss is not a subscription for every employee. It is the substitution of unlicensed duplicates for authorized reproductions in a market connected to the statutory reproduction right. Texaco's showing, which centers on the thinness of the subscription loss, does not answer the licensing evidence.

Texaco's principal contrary argument is circularity. It contends that a permission market depends on the legal ruling it is offered to support, and that ordinary research photocopying may produce public benefits far exceeding any demonstrated subscription loss. The objection is sound as far as it goes, and we accept its premise: a newly asserted price cannot make an otherwise fair use unlawful, and a bare offer of a license establishes no protected entitlement. But circularity arises when the existence of the market is the question being decided. Here the record shows a functioning, workable mechanism for this kind of copying, and the question is whether duplicates that substitute for what that mechanism supplies remain fair. That is a different question from whether owners may charge for uses the law protects without permission, and Campbell places those uses outside the licensing market. The public benefit of research is real. It is weighed in the first and second factors, and it does not erase the market effect shown.

*E. Weighing.* The second factor, and modestly the first, favor Texaco. The third favors the publishers. The fourth weighs most heavily on this record and decides the balance. We do not reach that conclusion by counting factors or by judging scientific work unworthy, and we do not hold the fourth factor supreme in every case. We hold that the combination of complete retained substitute copies and a demonstrated, workable licensing mechanism for that very reproduction defeats Texaco's defense, even after full credit to the genuine research purpose and the factual character of the articles. Texaco has not carried its burden.

**IV. Remaining contentions**

Texaco relies on Sony and on Williams & Wilkins Co. v. United States, 487 F.2d 1345 (Ct. Cl. 1973), aff'd by an equally divided Court, 420 U.S. 376 (1975). We accept Sony's lesson, that completeness and the absence of transformation neither decide nor foreclose fair use, and we apply it. But Sony found no meaningful likelihood of harm to a market, 464 U.S. at 450-51, and here an operating mechanism for the very reproduction is shown. The equally divided affirmance in Williams & Wilkins supplies no controlling reasoning from the Supreme Court. The Court of Claims decision is persuasive authority, not binding, and it differs from this case in institutional purpose, in the copying system, and in licensing-market conditions. It cannot be dismissed merely because it is older, and it does not govern materially different copying.

The older inquiry in Folsom v. Marsh, 9 F. Cas. 342, 348 (C.C.D. Mass. 1841), into the nature and objects of the use, the quantity and value of the material used, and its effect on the original, and the emphasis on productive use in Leval, Toward a Fair Use Standard, 103 Harv. L. Rev. 1105 (1990), are persuasive materials that accord with our emphasis on substitution. Neither binds us, and we rest nothing on them.

**V. Scope and disposition**

We decide the certified question for the eight retained complete copies on the stipulated record, and no more. We do not hold that all corporate research copying is commercial and therefore unlawful, that all complete copies fail, that transformation is indispensable to fair use, or that a license offer alone establishes a protected market. We do not hold that the journal issue rather than the article supplies the measure of the amount copied. We do not decide the legality of other scientists' copying, or of temporary, spontaneous, critical, or otherwise justified uses of scientific literature, and our holding does not establish that a scientist must purchase permission for every such use. We do not decide any period in which the asserted licensing opportunity was not genuinely available, or when a mechanism not yet operating is a cognizable market. We do not decide damages, including whether a license fee could measure damages for earlier conduct, a proposition that would require its own proof. Cf. Business Trends Analysts, Inc. v. The Freedonia Group, Inc., 887 F.2d 399, 404-05 (2d Cir. 1989) (rejecting a hypothetical saved-cost measure under 17 U.S.C. § 504(b) on the record there). We do not decide the scope of any injunction, and a ruling on fair use does not automatically determine it. Our holding applies the ordinary law of copyright to a commercial defendant. It is not a judgment about the relative worth of publishers and scientists.

The determination that the copying of the eight articles is not fair use is affirmed, and the case is remanded to the district court for further proceedings consistent with this opinion.

#### Separate opinions

None. There is no concurrence and no dissent.

#### What the decision does

**Controlling holdings.** The opinion is published and precedential for the four propositions below, each carrying three joins (Newman, Winter, Stone), and only to the extent of their stated scope: the eight retained complete copies on the stipulated record.

1. Framework, burden and review (Part II). Fair use is decided by weighing the four statutory factors together in light of copyright's purposes, with no bright-line rule. Research is a listed purpose that counts but is no exemption. A commercial setting and the absence of transformation are not disqualifying. Texaco, as proponent of an affirmative defense, bears the burden, and will have difficulty carrying it without favorable evidence on the relevant markets. The ultimate question is reviewed independently and subsidiary findings for clear error. Authority: 17 U.S.C. §§ 106, 107, 108(f)(4); Campbell; Harper & Row; Sony; Twin Peaks. Newman, Winter, Stone.
2. Factors one to three (Parts III.A to III.C). The research purpose and the factual character of the works favor Texaco, the first factor only modestly at most because the copies are nontransformative duplicates; the reproduction of each article in full favors the publishers. Newman, Winter, Stone.
3. Fourth factor and the licensing mechanism (Part III.D). Loss of licensing revenue is cognizable market harm only where the market is for the kind of use at issue (a reproduction that substitutes for an authorized copy), the licensing mechanism is shown by evidence to be actually operating for that kind of use at reasonable price and convenience, and the copying in fact substitutes for what the mechanism supplies. The existence of a fee never proves harm by itself. Applied: the retained complete copies, which serve the reading and reference function of authorized copies, substitute for reproductions obtainable through the mechanism the district court found operating, so the fourth factor weighs most heavily against Texaco and decides the balance on this record. Newman, Winter, Stone.
4. Scope and remedy (Parts IV and V). The holding is limited to the eight retained copies on the stipulated record. Newman, Winter, Stone.

The three-condition test for cognizable licensing-market harm is circuit precedent for fourth-factor analysis of reproduction that substitutes for authorized copies, and it is bounded by the qualifications in Parts III.D and V.

**Treatment of precedent.** Followed or applied as binding authority: Campbell, Harper & Row, Sony and Feist (Supreme Court); Twin Peaks (standard of review); Wright and New Era (scholarship and research as listed purposes that count under the first factor). Wright and New Era are not extended to fix the weight of the first factor for nontransformative duplicates. Treated as persuasive only: the Court of Claims decision in Williams & Wilkins, Folsom, and the Leval article. The equally divided affirmance in Williams & Wilkins carries no controlling Supreme Court rationale. No decision of this court is overruled, and the opinion exercises no overruling power.

**Dicta and matters left open.** Dicta and matters left open are not holdings, including the statements that § 108 is not decided, that damages and injunction scope are reserved, and that a mechanism not yet operating is not addressed.

**Effect on the parties.** The determination that the copying is not fair use is affirmed. The affirmance and remand bind the parties, and the determination as to the eight articles is the law of the case in the further district court proceedings. The case is remanded to the district court for further proceedings on matters not submitted, including damages, the scope of any injunction, copying beyond the eight articles, and market conditions in any other period. The opinion sets no damages, directs no injunction, and orders no licensing system.

**Mandate and remand.** The order reviewed is interlocutory and rests on certification under 28 U.S.C. § 1292(b). The mandate has not issued, and this opinion's date is not the mandate date. Under the rules in force on this date, including the amendments effective December 1, 1994, a petition for rehearing may be filed within 14 days after entry of judgment unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order or by local rule (Fed. R. App. P. 40(a)). The mandate issues 7 days after the time for filing a petition for rehearing expires, or, if a timely petition is filed, 7 days after entry of an order denying it, unless the time is shortened or enlarged by order, and a timely petition stays the mandate (Fed. R. App. P. 41(a)). A party may move to stay the mandate pending a petition for certiorari on a showing that the petition would present a substantial question and that there is good cause for a stay (Fed. R. App. P. 41(b)). No petition, motion, or other filing is recorded, and none is assumed. The date on which the mandate would issue is not computed here.

#### Historical comparison

Historical disposition: in the amended opinion on denial of rehearing, December 23, 1994, the Second Circuit affirmed the interlocutory rejection of fair use for the stipulated copying of retained complete articles, leaving damages, injunction and remaining district-court questions open. Panel: Newman, Winter, Jacobs. Newman wrote, joined by Winter; Jacobs dissented, stressing the scientific purpose, an individual researcher's files, and the circularity risk of using an emerging permission market to defeat fair use. The majority weighed all four section 107 factors, giving weight to the copies' substitute function and the evidence of an institutional copying-license market.

Comparison: the judgment is unchanged (affirmance and remand on the same limited question). The lineup changed: Stone replaces Jacobs, and the court is unanimous. The reasoning reaches the same judgment on the same grounds, with the simulated majority stating an explicit three-condition licensing-market test and answering the circularity objection itself.

Source: American Geophysical Union v. Texaco Inc., 37 F.3d 881, 885-905 (2d Cir. 1994) (No. 92-9341) (amended on denial of rehearing Dec. 23, 1994).

**Castillo (origin year 1994).** The simulated judgment of 1994-01-28 vacated the firearm conviction under 18 U.S.C. 924(c) and remanded that count for a new trial. It also vacated the aggregate sentence and remanded for resentencing in light of the eventual disposition of that count, with the section 3B1.1(c) adjustment left in place. It left the convictions on the three other counts undisturbed and affirmed the Rule 33 ruling on timeliness alone. The Court's order of 1994-10-03 denied certiorari on a petition that, as the Standing State records, sought review of a judgment leaving Castillo's conviction and sentence in place. The denial therefore disposes only of that petition. It does not review or change the simulated judgment, and it is not transferred to this case. The Court supplied no explanation, and a denial approves none of the Second Circuit's reasoning, historical or simulated. Relief and further proceedings are unchanged: the simulated mandate has not issued, and the live review status of the simulated judgment stays open (Section 7).

**Latsis (origin year 1994).** The simulated judgment of 1994-03-24 vacated the judgment for the defendants and remanded for a new trial on seaman status, with negligence, causation and damages open; Judges Oakes and Stone-Khoury formed the majority and Judge Kearse dissented. The defendants are the parties aggrieved by that vacatur, and they are the petitioners. The grant of 1994-10-17 fits that judgment: the relief under review is the same vacatur and new trial. The petition's question concerns what employment-related connection to a vessel in navigation seaman status requires, which the simulated opinion addressed in rejecting a categorical exclusion of the conversion assignment. The petition did not raise Rule 51 or plain error, which the simulated opinion left undecided. A grant states no merits ruling and no reasoning, and it does not change the simulated judgment, the relief ordered, or the mandate status (the final record states the mandate has not issued). The petition's filing date, and so its timeliness, are Not established.

**DeWeerth (origin year 1994).** The simulated judgment of 1994-10-27 reversed the Rule 60(b) order and the resulting judgment, reinstated the earlier final judgment, and remanded only for ministerial steps; DeWeerth is the party that loses under it. The denial of 1994-11-14 states no holding, and a denial is not approval of the circuit's reasoning. It leaves the relief ordered unchanged and decides nothing about the Rule 60(b)(6) and 60(b)(5) questions. Because the petition's filing date and the Second Circuit decision it targeted are Not established, the denial is not recorded as filed against the 1994-10-27 simulated disposition. The mandate and the ministerial remand are a research task (Section 7).

### Selected cases awaiting argument or decision

**Latsis v. Chandris, Inc., No. 93-7704 (decided 1994-03-24; origin year 1994).** Supreme Court: Chandris, Inc., et al. v. Latsis, No. 94-325, 513 U.S. 945. Stage: certiorari granted 1994-10-17; granted review awaiting briefing and argument. No argument date is established.

- **Accepted question:** Not established as the Court's own statement. The compatibility record describes the petition's question as what employment-related connection to a vessel in navigation seaman status requires. The circuit decision addressed two issues: whether the instruction directing the jury to disregard the six-month conversion assignment misstated the law of seaman status and required a new trial, and whether the unobjected-to parts of the charge warranted relief.
- **Simulated judgment under review:** The judgment for the defendants is vacated and the case remanded for a new trial on seaman status under a charge consistent with the opinion. The judgment rests on the preserved objection alone; the plain-error question was not decided. The court made no finding that Latsis is a seaman and none on the time he spent aboard ship. The simulated mandate has not issued.
- **Principal arguments, as the circuit decision states them:** Latsis was injured at sea on a voyage from Baltimore to Bermuda aboard a vessel in navigation, and he offered the later six-month conversion assignment as evidence of the continuing relationship he already had with the vessels; the evidence on his time aboard ship conflicted sharply (about seventy-two percent on his account, about ten percent on his supervisor's). The defendants argued that a major conversion takes a vessel out of navigation and that land-based repair work cannot create seaman status, relying on West v. United States and McKinley v. All Alaskan Seafoods, and that the assignment was shoreside reconstruction work that says little about an enduring fleet connection. The majority held that these points go to weight for the jury and not to exclusion. Judge Kearse, dissenting, would have affirmed on the preservation rules and on the vessel's out-of-navigation status during the conversion.
- **Status:** No Supreme Court merits ruling in this case is admitted as of 1994-12-23. Pending mandate and remand work is carried in Section 7.

Anderson (No. 93-1097): a certiorari denial entry of 1994-10-03 (Anderson v. United States, No. 94-5768, 513 U.S. 913) carries no "reported below" citation and cannot be tied to this case, so it is not admitted. All other selected cases have no admitted review event; unchanged matters for Castillo and DeWeerth are in Section 7.
